# The Skyland Chronicles: The Last Skylander

_Source manuscript, extracted from the MOBI upload. This file is reference input only — never edited by the rewrite pipeline._



---

## Chapter 1 — CHAPTER ONE Journey begins {#chapter-1}

CHAPTER ONE

Journey begins

I looked across the ballroom and cast a flirtatious glance at the tall man perusing the refreshments. He must have felt my eyes upon him for he suddenly stood up straight and scanned the crowd until he found me. I looked into his eyes for just a moment too long before I cast my eyes downward with a coy smile. I lingered for a heartbeat before I looked back at the man. He was still watching me intently and I quickly looked away again. I kept him in my peripheral vision and I made sure that my body was facing him and I kept my weight on my right leg to keep my hip cocked so that the slit of my dress revealed my boots and thigh. If he did not approach me after being that obvious then he was not worth my time at all. Sure enough, the man picked up an additional fluted glass of sparkling wine and carefully made his way around the couples swirling across the dance floor. I pretended to admire a large painting on the wall while I waited for him to approach and I heard him stop just behind me and to my right.

"I find it to be appalling that a lady as beautiful as you stand without drink," the man schmoozed and I turned around to face him.

I looked up at him and then cast my eyes down. Men did enjoy timid women.

"Thank you," I responded and accepted the glass.

"To a joyful evening," he toasted.

"To a joyful evening," I agreed and gently clinked my glass against his before we both took a sip of the wine.

It was white, crisp with a hint of mineral. It was drinkable, especially since there were no fermented buffalo milk around.

"Gereon," he introduced himself and offered me his hand.

"Mary," I responded and shifted my glass to my left hand before reaching for his wrist. But I managed to catch myself and with a silent plea to the Spirits that he had not noticed my lapse I turned my motion to gently place the tips of my fingers in his palm. He bowed low over my hand and his lips just barely brushed my glove. What a gentleman.

"I am pleased to have met another Skylander," Gereon said with a grin.

So my disguise had worked. Most Skylanders sported colours of hair that was very unusual among the Outsiders. Even so I had been born with a full head of chocolaty brown hair. But for this evening I had dyed bright crimson and violet highlights to make sure that my place of birth would be unmistakable.

"As am I," I smiled warmly. "It has been many years since I have met one of my own kind."

Gereon's face flashed past a pained expression.

"So it is. There are so many fewer of us now than before. How old were you during the Fall, if you don't mind my asking?"

"Oh, it is quite alright. I was nine years old." That was a lie. I had been thirteen. Or maybe fourteen. I had never known exactly how old I was. "How old were you?"

"I was just twenty when I shipped out to Utopeia to a bright and glorious future. How different it all turned out, eh? All thanks to the Traitor, cursed be her name."

"Cursed be her name," I echoed even though the words turned my stomach. "I still remember Utopeia," I continued wistfully. "It's a proper shame that it was not meant to be."

Gereon peered at me with a slight smile. "Who says it wasn't?" He finally asked smugly.

I let my eyes widen just a tiny bit. "What do you mean?" I breathed, captivated.

Gereon leaned closer conspiratorially and I did not miss the casual glance he sent down my cleavage. Mother had been correct, those were great assets when dealing with men. And some women.

"There is a new settlement being constructed for us Skylanders," Gereon whispered excitedly.

I gasped and covered my cleavage daintily with a gloved hand. "I had heard the rumours, but never would I have imagined..."

"It's true," Gereon said, visibly excited. "And I happen to know the location!"

I smiled brightly. "You must tell me, Gereon! I would love to be amongst my own kind after this long amongst these... Savages. I would be forever beholden to you," I added with a heated glance and a smile full of promise.

I saw his tongue dart out to wet his lips and a final glance at my chest broke the last of his resistance.

"I have a map," he whispered urgently. "You should travel with me, Mary," he said insistently. "I had been planning to leave the evening before yesterday but then I was invited to this little gathering so I decided to postpone my journey. What do you say, Mary?" He said rakishly, confident that I had taken the bait hook, line, and sinker.

"That would be lovely, Gereon! Such a gentleman to offer his protection to a lone woman. How could I ever repay you?" I swooned.

"No need, Lady Mary. Anything for a fellow Skylander in search of companionship," Gereon winked.

Companionship indeed. I clapped my hands together and giggled. "When do we leave?" I asked excitedly.

"I was planning on leaving tomorrow," he stated before his face fell. "Unless you can't be ready by then, in which case I am more than willing to wait a little longer."

I put on a crestfallen expression. "Oh, so soon? I couldn't live with myself if I kept you waiting, Gereon!"

I blinked back tears.

"I understand if you would need a day or two to get everything in order," Gereon started.

"No!" I wailed, not too loud so that I did not draw attention to us. "It will take me far longer to purchase a horse!"

Gereon blinked. "A horse? That is all you need?"

I nodded morosely.

"Bah! Easily remedied. I have an extra horse. I had been intending to use it as a spare horse but you can ride it. The journey will be a little longer since we won't be able to switch horses when they get tired but that will be fine. The trip will be much more enjoyable in the company of such a beautiful woman as you."

I smiled hesitantly and let a glimmer of hope show in my eyes.

"Believe me, it is no bother. I would rather travel slowly with you than swiftly alone. The nights out there are long and cold," Gereon grinned rakishly.

I threw my arms around his neck with a girlish squeal and made sure to press the length of my body into his. "Thank you, thank you, thank you!" I pushed back from him and affected a startled but excited expression. "I must go pack!" I exclaimed and started walking away but Gereon grabbed my elbow with a chuckle. I looked at him quizzically.

"I haven't told you where to meet," he grinned.

I tried to force a blush, but did not know if I succeeded so I cast my eyes down in shame.

"Western gate, by dawn," he informed me gently.

"By dawn?" I whined and let my shoulders droop.

"By dawn," Gereon winked. "The road is long ahead and we should get as much out of the sunlight as we can."

"Alright, fine. By dawn, then," I graced him with a quick smile and then I was heading out of the ballroom.

I smiled to myself. I had him. Hook, line, and sinker. I decided to head back to the inn where I was renting a room to get some early shut eye. I had never been an early riser so I might as well get as much rest as I could.

I spotted Gereon through the open gate and true to his word he held the reins of two horses. They were sad excuses for horses and I was certain that Gereon had been swindled when he bought them. I feigned a big yawn that turned into an actual yawn and I stretched my arms over my head when Gereon spotted me. He grinned widely and tipped his hat in greeting.

"Lady Mary, good morning to you. I hope you slept well?"

"Hardly any at all, Master Gereon, I was too excited to rejoin the rest of our people," I said and giggled nervously before brushing past him to gush over the horses. "Such magnificent beasts!" I said while taking in every detail about them with a critical eye. At least one of them was in okay health, the other would go lame within a few days if it carried any load of significance.

"Magnificent, sure," Gereon agreed. "But so primitive and uncivilised. But they will suffice for now until we arrive at our destination. There's bound to be Skylander magic there to replace the animals."

I turned to face Gereon. "Magic," I whispered. "I hardly remember it. Do you truly think that there will be magic there?"

"Of course. Do you know how to ride?"

I widened my eyes and shook my head.

"Not to worry," he reassured me, "you just sit tight and I will lead both horses. Do you need help getting up?"

Gereon eyed my outfit dubiously, but not without approval. I was not dressed for a cross-country trek. Not at all. While my skirts were not voluminous, no skirt of any kind was ever suitable for riding, and my corset was more fit for a ball than anything else. I had chosen it for a reason other than comfort though. I needed to lull Gereon into a sense of comfort, and what better way to do that than to trick him into believing that I was some ditsy grown up on hyperbolic stories of the great homeland?

"Yes, please," I admitted bashfully.

"Alright, please forgive me for being a little forward," Gereon muttered and stepped awfully close to wrap his hands around my hips. He pulled back in surprise when his fingers made contact with cold indigo crystal. He pinched a loop of fabric between thumb and finger and lifted my skirt to the side to reveal my big revolver.

"It belonged to my father," I told him with big eyes. "It is the only thing I have left from him. I think that he can rest easy in the afterlife knowing that his old gun will protect me out there," I whispered.

Gereon shrugged and shifted his grip around my holster. His fingers dug into my hips and he heaved. Luckily for him I was a short girl and he could hoist me into the saddle with a barely suppressed grunt of effort. I ended up sat sideways in the saddle and the horse did not like it and pranced and tossed her head. I suppressed my instinct to soothe the horse and coo comforting nothings at her. Instead I just fluidly followed along with the movements to maintain my balance and let Gereon calm the mare down. Eventually she settled down and Gereon swung himself into the saddle. I was impressed with how natural his movements were. For one grown up in the Skylands he seemed well versed in the art of riding.

"Are you ready?" He asked me over his shoulder.

"I am. Off we go!" I exclaimed and pointed at the horizon.

Gereon clicked his tongue and dug his heels into the sides of his gelding and we started our journey. I let out a cry of surprise and exuberance when my mare lurched into action and I made a show of gripping the saddle horn with a white-knuckled grip. I pretended to gradually grow accustomed to the the horses movements until I could relax a little more. Gereon kept eyeing me out of the corner of his eye until he seemed content that I would not fall off my horse.

"So, do you know how to shoot that thing?" He asked casually.

"I just point it at the bad guys and pull the trigger, right?" I said with a ditsy giggle.

"That's about it, yes," he agreed, "though there's a bit more nuance to it than that. I can teach you, if you want to."

"I would love that! Mother did not approve of girls shooting and she forbade father from teaching me," I grumped affectionately.

"Well she did have a point back then, but the world has changed. The Skylands fell, Utopeia was destroyed. There's no place for people like us no more. We all gotta learn how to take care of ourselves."

"Indeed. I would be delighted if you would teach me, Gereon."

"My pleasure."

"Do you think that you could teach me magic as well? I remember most of us were able to do at least something with magic, but my parents died before I was old enough to be taught."

Gereon smirked and raised his palm. A small sphere of Soulfire erupted above his skin and I forced a gasp.

"Amazing!"

Gereon closed his fist, snuffing out the magic, with a smug look on his face. "I ain't no Mage, but I do know a few useful spells I can teach you."

I clapped my gloved hands and squealed. This act was getting tiresome so I pretended that my mare had stumbled and I nearly fell from the saddle. My hands slapped down on the saddle horn.

"Whoa, easy there, girl," Gereon reassured me and slowed down a bit.

"Don't slow down, I'll just hold on. The faster we ride, the sooner we arrive and can get off of these horses!"

"A woman of my mind," Gereon chuckled. "Hold on, little lady!"

And then he spurred the horses on carefully, glancing back at me to make sure that I would not fall off. I made it look like I was holding on for dear life but I could easily maintain my awkward sitting position and soon enough we were cantering along, the miles slowly trickling away beneath our hooves. We slowed down by midday to eat and Gereon spread his coat on the grass for me to sit on. He really was a gentleman.

"How are you holding up?" Gereon asked me and took a bite of beef jerky.

"It's wonderful, though my bum is a bit sore," I admitted reluctantly.

"Yeah, that will last for a couple of days," he chuckled, obviously remembering his first few times on a horse.

We ate in silence for a few minutes, admiring a herd of whitetail deer across the open plain. This place reminded me so much of home, or at least how it had been when I was a child. There were more trees here and the terrain was dotted with steep cliffs and ravines instead of the gently rolling hills.

"Were are we going, exactly?" I finally asked.

"Due west, into the mountains."

"That's it? Nothing more specific?"

Gereon chuckled. "Of course. I have a map. But we travel due west for a couple of days before we start to use it to home into our destination."

I nodded and swallowed my bite of lunch. "Great! Shall we get going?"

The rest of the day went by smoothly, yet slowly. I couldn't wait to get off the horse and make camp and when we did Gereon was a perfect gentleman and insisted that he pitch the tent himself while I rested my bruised bottom. I let him be the gentleman and I cooked a stew in the little pot he had retrieved from his pack. The stew finished just when he added the finishing touches on the tent and he sat down heavily next to me and wiped his brow. I handed him a bowl without speaking and we ate in comfortable silence. When we finished Gereon glanced at the low sun.

"There's still some light left. Would you like to practice with that piece of yours?"

I didn't, but I had to keep this charade up so I nodded. Gereon showed off by using magic to line up a group of wooden chunks on top of a fallen tree. I reached down and drew my revolver, fighting my instincts to maintain an awkward grip. I raised it with one arm to aim at one of the blocks and I held it straight before me and leaned my head back to glare down the sights. Gereon chuckled good-naturedly.

"Hold on, let me give you some pointers. First, plant your feet about shoulder width. Right foot a little behind you and angled," he tapped my boot with his own lightly. "Slide your hand up the grip, all the way."

I did and slipped my finger inside the trigger guard. "Ah! Never touch the trigger until you are ready to shoot. Maintain trigger discipline," he cautioned sternly. I obeyed with silent approval. He sure did know his guns, I had to give him that. I made my hand shake as if overpowered by the weight of the revolver.

"Grab it with both hands for stability. That is quite a large gun for a girl of your size, Mary. Lean into it. Don't hold your arms straight, keep them slightly bent to absorb the kick. This bad boy will kick something fierce when he speaks. Now focus on the front sight and line them up. Breathe calmly. Exhale slowly and then squeeze, don't pull, squeeze the trigger."

I took my time, and then fired. I missed by a long shot, on purpose. I huffed and stomped my foot.

"Relax, no one hits on the first try. Do it again."

I missed again and this time I made myself stumble from the recoil. Gereon swooped in to catch me.

"Easy there, I got you. Want to try again?"

I nodded, putting on a determined face.

"Alright, here's what we'll do. You just assume position here," he guided me by my shoulders to stand in front of him. "And then I'll help you with aiming, so that you get a feel for it, okay?"

I nodded and he put his arms around me, his large hands enveloping my own cradling the gun. He surrounded me, strong arms around my shoulders and despite myself I found myself relaxing into him, leaning into his chest.

"Now just focus on the front sights and align it with the rear. Make sure they're level and flush. See, if you tilt the weapon like this," he nudged it a little to the left, "then the left rear sight obscures part of the front sight. That means you are not holding your weapon level. That is the most important part. Always align your weapon." He shifted out grip to the proper alignment. "Now that it is level, we aim by moving our whole body, like this."

He panned us back and forth, holding the weapon level the whole time.

"Now," he whispered and his warm breath blanketed my face, "aim at the wooden block and fire when you are ready. Remember, breathe."

I did as I was told and fired. The wood frayed at the edge and the block slowly slid off the fallen tree trunk.

"I hit it!" I exclaimed.

"You sure did," Gereon said with a wide smile, stepping away from me.

I could not suppress a disappointed sigh that his warmth was no longer there.

"You're a natural, Mary, with just a little bit of regular practice and you'll be a deadeye in no time."

"Oh stop, you flatterer," I smiled and swatted at his shoulder with the back of my hand.

"Does it work?" He asked with a mischievous wink.

"Maybe," I smiled coyly.

He took a small step closer, towering over me, smiling down at me. "There's more where that came from, pretty lady."

I tilted my face up to him, licking my lips expectantly. And then yawned widely. I gasped and covered my mouth with my hand. "Excuse me! I must be more tired than I expected after the days ride."

Gereon winced. "You should retire into the tent then. I'll sleep under the stars tonight. We have more time for 'practising' tomorrow."

There were no doubt in my mind about what he meant when he said 'practice'.

"Oh, no!" I exclaimed. "I can't first force myself on your journey and then kick you out of your own tent!"

"Nonsense, Mary, I'm glad to let you have it."

"But," I started but he cut me off.

"It wouldn't be proper," he said firmly.

I huffed and stared him down. "I decide what is proper, Gereon! I won't be able to sleep knowing that you are out here, freezing. You said it yourself that the nights out here get cold. Now you come into that tent with me and sleep comfortably in the warmth, okay?"

Gereon could barely suppress his smirk. "Yes, ma'am."

I gave him a final playful glare and turned around to walk toward the tent, knowing full well that his eyes would be glued to my behind.

"Just, no funny business, mister. I don't think my bum can tolerate any more abuse this night," I complained and rubbed my pretend sore ass.

Gereon chuckled at the thinly veiled promise behind my words. I crawled into the tent and opened the sleeping roll that he had thoughtfully prepared for me. Gereon backed into the tent, his own sleep roll in his arms and he spread it out next to mine in the cramped interior of the tent.

"See," he said with a disarming smile, "plenty of room between us. No risk of any 'funny business', as you put it."

"Thanks," I smiled and then started to work with the brass latchings that stretched down the front of my corset. Gereon could not help but stare but I did not care. It was a relief to get out of the damned thing. While there were corsets that were comfortable to wear, this was one that was designed for form over function. It was pretty though. I put it aside and idly caressed the moulded leather before I slid my legs into my sleeping bag while making sure that my skirt was on the outside. When I was mostly inside the sleeping roll I undid the knot that kept the skirt closed and pulled it all over my head and then sank lower into the roll until it was up past my lower ribs.

"Aren't you getting into bed?" I asked Gereon teasingly and rested my head on my hand, elbow to the ground. He had just been staring at me while I undressed and he was obviously frazzled.

"Ah, yes. Certainly," he stammered. "Are you just going to watch me?"

I was a bit surprised by his sudden uncertainty but I just smiled coyly. "Like you just stared at me?"

To his credit, he blushed and put his hat on the ground and shrugged out of his coat. He unbuttoned a few buttons of his shirt and then pulled a sheaf of paper. He put it into his hat.

"Is that the map?" I asked curiously.

"Yes, I keep it in my shirt for safety. Of all of our supplies, that map is the most important. As long as we have that we can reach our destination eventually.

"Good thinking," I complimented him.

Gereon stripped out of his shirt and I could not help but admire his physique. He was not muscled like a warrior but he had the sinewy look of a scholar turned worker. It was obvious that he was experienced in manual labour, but his fingers had ink stains and his eyes glowed with intelligence. He could have been one of the commanders of the Fist of Heaven if it hadn't been for his too yellow Skylander hair. He crawled into his bedroll and blew out the lantern.

"Good night, Mary."

"Good night."

I was ready for tomorrow. Time to rest.

For the next two days we rode farther from the Cities into relative wasteland. I knew that there were settlements around the mountain range we were heading toward but nothing in the scale of the Cities. Out here there were little law and order, mostly lone farmers and their families spread out amongst the odd village. The mountains loomed in the distance and I judged it to be another week of journey to get there since the quality of the roads had deteriorated with the distance from the Cities and soon we would have to ride cross country. I doubted that the mare I was on would last that long, she was already breathing hard. Luckily I had managed to keep Gereon away from me at night, still complaining that the saddle was making me sore. In fact, my side-saddle riding did chafe me since I was not used to riding in this manner. Gereon were holding the reins of my mare instead of tying them to his saddle horn as he had done previously after I made my mare toss her head and almost collide with his horse. He had explained that it was safer if he just let go and let the mare carry me away for a bit before she would tire and he could catch up and calm her down. I had smiled widely and trusted his judgement. I looked behind us down the paltry road. We were alone. My mare suddenly screeched and lurched to the side, yanking the reins out of Gereon's grip and bolting onto the plain.

"Hey!" He shouted and I heard him urge his gelding to follow. I let the mare run, only occasionally shifting my weight to steer her. She was not as well trained as the horses I usually rode but she did listen to my commands eventually. When we were a suitable distance from the rode I flailed my arms and screeched before pitching backwards over the saddle to land in a tumbling roll in the grass.

"Mary!" I heard Gereon scream and he drew his horse up beside me. His feet thudded into the grass and then he knelt beside me. "You okay?"

"Yes," I breathed, trying to get my bearings. "She just bolted! Why would she do that?" I said with panic in my voice.

"Maybe she saw a snake or something," Gereon mused while his fingers poked and prodded me, searching for broken bones.

"A snake?" I squeaked in fear.

"Or just a branch!" Gereon quickly amended. "Horses are skittish by nature and sometimes confuse branches for snakes. You're fine, Mary, you're fine. I don't think you broke anything."

I clung to his shoulders and my hand snaked down his chest, feeling the map through his shirt. "No, I don't think I did. I'm bruised but I think I will be fine."

Gereon scanned the horizon. "I should bring your horse back and then we should camp here for a bit, have a rest."

"That would be lovely," I smiled and fanned my face with my hand.

With a last worried glance Gereon left me to lead my mare back. She had stopped running almost as soon as I fell off and he didn't have to go far. He hitched the horses to a nearby tree and then returned to join me.

"How are you feeling?"

"A little rattled," I admitted. "I was not prepared for her to bolt just like that."

"Here," he said and handed me a metal flask. "Drink this. It'll help calm you down."

I accepted the flask with shaking fingers and took a heavy swig. Sweet liquid poured into my mouth and then the potent alcohol burned my lips. It was nothing like the fine whiskeys my mother had favoured, but I did appreciate the bite of alcohol.

"What is that?" I asked, grimacing through the burn in my throat.

"Southern brandy," Gereon answered and took a much larger swig than I had. He sighed contentedly and offered me the flask and I shook my head. "Good stuff. Say what you will about these lands, but they do make way better alcohol than what we had in the Skylands."

I shook my head. "I was too young to drink when we left for Utopeia," I reminded him.

"As was I," he admitted. "But that did not stop me," he said with a mischievous grin. "Ready to get back on the road? I promise to hold the reins tighter," he asked while standing up and offering me his hand.

I took his hand in mine and he pulled me to my feet with a rakish grin. He turned on his heel and started walking back toward the horses. In a single smooth movement I drew my revolver, cocked the hammer and aimed at the back of his head. His head had just started to turn toward the crystal tinkling of the hammer and I saw his bemused smile and confused frown when I squeezed the trigger. The weapon bucked in my hand, his hat flew off his head along with most of his brain. His body toppled bonelessly into the grass and lay still. The horses screamed and pranced but the hitching held them fast. I holstered my revolver and considered the fresh corpse. I had never killed someone before. I had seen war and plenty of gore in my short life. That did not phase me at all. I had seen countless people die when I relived my mothers life. She had killed millions. Granted, most of those kills had not been face to face but I had lived through her memories. Felt them. Felt her emotions when she killed innocent women whose only crime was cooking Stardust for a criminal gang. I had been her when she murdered a male prostitute that was blackmailing Uncle Kenny. I had seen my own father burn to death in magic fire through her eyes. Even so I had believed that I would feel something when I took my first life myself. Remorse, regret, disgust? Anything. But I felt only relief. I had set out on my journey almost a decade ago when I had relived my mothers life for the first time. That night when her spell beast had delivered the memory core on my pillow a year after mom had been killed in Utopeia and mother had killed herself to become a liche before flying away on my dragon brother to slay an evil monolith. I had seen my brother give his life to help mother take revenge for mom. I had felt his pain when the magic he drank consumed him from within. Two and a half million Skylanders had died that day, but thousands had survived the battle for Utopeia. Scattered into the Elweyr forest. And I had sworn on that day that I would find them all, one by one, and kill them. Every last one of them. At some point in my life, I would be the last Skylander. I had promised the shades of my mothers and father that it would be so, and I knew that they would smile at me if they had still been alive. And so it all started with Gereon. I had heard the rumour of a new Skylander settlement, but I did not know where. But now I did. I rolled Gereon onto his back and ripped his shirt open to steal the map from his cooling corpse. I unfolded it and spent a few minutes deciphering it. When I was certain that we had been on the right track I folded it back up again and I tucked it into the left cup of my corset for safe keeping. Standing up straight I cracked my back and groaned in relief when a series of pops traversed my spine. Riding side-saddle had always messed me up. Time to get to work cleaning this mess up. I removed the saddles from both horses before I stripped them completely naked. Their fur still held obvious chafe marks from saddles and buckles but in a couple of weeks they would appear as wild horses. Surprisingly unskittish, but wild. When the horses was free I brushed them both and patted them before sending them on their way with solid smacks to their rumps. They sprang into action and did not look back once. I went through the saddlebags but there were no valuables there except for a roll of cash that I pilfered and a few pieces of beef jerky. After having my first decade in this life eating only the Skylander fungus even beef jerky tasted great to me. I piled the saddles onto the ground and with a wave of my hand I used magic to lift Gereon onto them along with all of the crap that I didn't need from the packs. Finally I climbed out of my skirt and dropped it on Gereon's still chest. Out of my own pack I pulled a pair of snug trousers and put them on. Then I retrieved a power stone, similar to the one mother used to have on a choker around her neck. The one that contained the pattern and power to manifest her armour. I had one as well, a perfect replica of hers. Uncle Kenny had helped me build it. I activated the powerstone before I dug through my pack to slip the armour stone around my neck. Relief filled me when I felt its familiar constricting embrace around my throat. I waited impatiently for the powerstone to manifest one of my own creations. After my mothers had died and the Clans had consolidated into a single unit to embrace the magical technology the Skylanders had brought development had boomed. The Clans had gone from herding nomads to an industrial powerhouse within a decade. Somehow without losing connection to their roots as a horse people. But one of the young Bizhjerai boys had been captivated by a contraption that one of the visiting Fists of Heaven had brought. It had been a prototype, and they had called it a 'bicycle', because it had two wheels. The soldier from the mercenary company had lamented that there were no steam engines small and powerful enough to power it and that he had been forced to use a pair of paddles with his legs to drive himself forward. But the young Bizhjerai boy, Lukas, had instantly realised that magic would solve that problem. Which is why he came to me. My mother had discovered that magic usage was rare on the Outside, that the knowledge had been lost during the Apocalypse. But it had been brought back and regained a foothold among the Clans. Uncle Kenny was the most skilled known Mage, and I had made sure to learn all that he had to teach. Lukas and I had modified the bicycle, strengthened it and designed a magical engine. I had also spent a lot of time on the procedure to materialise magic. I had made a discovery in how to speed the process up without sacrificing too much efficiency. Back in the day, when constructing things out of magic, they had often been materialised in complete units and only moving parts were kept separate to assemble later. But I had discovered that the rate of materialisation was directly proportional to the materialisation spell itself. Instead of having one big spell manifesting one big object, I used a spell that spawned a multitude of materialisation spells that each materialised a component. A second spell mounted them together and fused parts as necessary. It was a more complex blueprint and a way more complex spell to construct but it reduced the manifestation time by a large factor. Within minutes my bike had formed, almost as quick as my mother had been able to manifest her Valkyrie armour. My own Valkyrie armour, or rather my Angel armour, took significantly less to manifest. I put the drained powerstone back into my pack and withdrew a much larger one that I slid into the slot on the bike frame. I only had the one power core for my bike and I knew that it would drain before I arrived at my destination. There were no magical founts nearby to recharge it in. I had charged this in Dun Traelak before I stowed away on one of Uncle Raistlin's airships. Luckily, mothers memories had supplied me with the solution. I had adapted mom's magic-seeking spell-bugs to have increased storage capacity. Out of my pack I pulled a cluster of crystals as big as my head. I called it my nest. I shook it violently, channelling some of my personal power into it to wake it up. As if I had kicked a bee hive crystals rained from it, igniting into fat spell-bugs that buzzed into a cloud on shimmering wings. I pulsed a location to them, a distance to the north-west where I planned to make camp, and the swarm spread out in that direction. The magical field was weak out here but while I watched the swarm fade into the distance I thought about the analogy that Lukas had used when he pitched his idea to me. 'If the school of fish is sparse, cast a wider net.' I straddled my bike and with a thought the magical engine whirred into life with a soft hum and an ear-jarring squeal. I winced. I hadn't yet figured out how to manifest lubricant for the gears and axis. With a wave of my hand I set fire to the pile of crap, saddles and Gereon before I kicked my bike into gear and whirred across the plain. I felt good about my day. I was one corpse closer to a better world. I could travel way faster than a horse, and way smoother. And longer without resting my mount. The bike was superiour to a horse in almost every way.

Except that it was dead. It lacked personality and a will of its own. It lacked a Soul. If given a choice for a leisure trip, I would pick a horse any day. But when I had to get somewhere fast... The bike just trumped horses in utility. Soon magical bikes and other non-horse powered machines would make horses entirely obsolete, and that would be a sad day indeed. My power core was about a full when I pulled over for the night and I had a dry meal of beef jerky and dried Skylander fungus. Even after the fall of Utopeia the farmers of Dun Traelak had kept cultivating it in the caves that had been excavated into the mountain. It was simply too easy to grow and nourishing not to. Combined with savoury meat in a stew it was actually quite pleasant. I watched the sun set and the stars reverse-fade into existence while I made plans. I was on the road toward my destiny, toward the completion of my oath to kill them all. To finish what my mothers had started. But I knew little as to how. I had pulled some strings back home to secure a favour that I could call in, and even more strings with Uncle Raistlin for aid from the Fists of Heaven. He had promised me one of his most promising Captains, a woman named Ioana, when I sent the word. But he had not been happy about that. I would have to make it up to the cantankerous old man. Suddenly nervous, those two favours were all that my plan hinged on, I removed my corset and made sure that the map was secured. Then I squeezed my breasts with both hands until I felt the hard lumps within. The two spell stones that I had implanted into my own flesh and then healed the wounds with Blood Magic. The only place where the Skylanders could not sense them with their Magesense was beneath the skin of the living. The natural magical barrier that resided there would obscure their senses. Reassured that I still had the spells that would send signals to my friends I crawled into my bedroll and fell asleep under the stars.


---

## Chapter 2 — CHAPTER TWO Arrival {#chapter-2}

CHAPTER TWO

Arrival

I rode my bike for another day, my swarm of magic-collecting bugs trailing after me to catch up in the night and recharge my power core. I arrived at my destination by noon the day after. I had managed to cover a whole week of riding in just two days on my bike. I grinned smugly as I gunned the engine to speed toward my destination. It appeared to be nothing but a small camp of modest dwellings and when I drew closer I saw that it was a single long house with a bunch of tents and lean-tos surrounding it. Was this all of the might the remaining Skylanders could scrape together? This paltry gathering was what remained of the once mighty Skylands? They deserved to be put our of their misery, to go out in a blaze of glory instead of weakly petering out like an old man dying in bed in his own filth. I rolled up to the camp amidst the sound of hammer on nail and saws chopping up wood. I was accosted by two men whose hands rested on their revolvers, ready to draw.

"State your name," the one on the left commanded.

"Mary Laier."

"You a Skylander?" The one on the right sneered. I decided that I did not like him.

"Yes," I said with a gesture at the unnatural streaks in my hair and the obviously magic-crafted bike.

"Lots of people out there with Skylander tech. Those Bizhjerai savages stole a bunch of knowledge from us during the war."

"And the Traitor and her cocksucking friend taught them even more."

I decided that I did not like the first guy either. "No, I don't have anyone that can vouch for me. Everyone I knew from the Skylands are dead. But I was taught by the great Mathias Varl himself before he passed away."

"Oh yeah?" The first one questioned and stepped closer. "Can you prove it?"

"I sure can," I agreed and scooped a book out of my pack. It was made from soft, paper-like materialised magic and the text written within glowed with an inner light. "See the dedication," I told them and opened the first page.

"To my lovely student. Signed Mathias Varl." The second guy read and glanced at his friend. "Checks out."

Uncle Kenny would be so pissed when he discovered that I had stolen the tome his old teacher had gifted him with. Kendall had always admired the man even though they ended up fighting on opposite sides of the war.

"Alright, surrender your weapons to us and make yourself useful. They'll be returned to you when you've proven yourself to be trustworthy."

I did not like that one bit, but I did not need my weapons to be lethal. I had trained for many years to make sure of that. I unbuckled my weapons belt and handed it over to the first man before I dug in my pack and withdrew my disassembled and tightly wrapped rifle. It was my mothers rifle, the one Kendall had made for her when she left on the First Expedition. It had served her well for many years and she had killed many Skylanders with it. I wanted to continue that tradition.

"Hey, this here looks like the Traitor's gun," the man said and spun it in his fingers before showing it to his friend.

"Yeah, it sure does. Just like all the stories describe it. But isn't that supposed to be locked away in Dun Traelak?"

"It's a replica," I interjected with affected boredom in my voice.

"Why'd you carry a replica of the Traitor's gun, girl? You one of them Skylanders' gone native?" He peered at me suspiciously. He had obviously been an Undercity dweller when he was still on the Skylands. It was obvious from his accent.

"It was effective, was it not? I am not too proud to realise what is a good idea and use it, even if it was an enemy that thought of it."

"Hah!" The other one laughed. "Spoken like a true Skylander, pragmatic to the core. I like you, girl."

"Well I don't," the other one grumped. "I'll be keeping my eye on you, lass."

"Yeah, whatever," I scoffed and rolled my bike into camp.

"Go see Lord Micah," the nicer of the two called after me. "Him's the tall feller with the pinkish hair, he insists on meeting all new arrivals and he'll put you to work."

I waved over my shoulder and rolled through town. Though calling it a town might have been stretching it. There could only have been somewhere between fifty and a hundred tents and I could see less than a hundred people. I had thought that all Skylanders would gather here. I had hoped to use this place as bait to draw them all here so that I could kill them all in one fell swoop and save me the bother of hunting them down. The fourth time I nearly ran someone over I lowered the stand for my bike and shut it all down. I extracted the power core and quickly disassembled the bike, reabsorbing the magic into my powerstone and stowing it away. Then I set off on foot to find this Lord Micah. I was glad that I had bought sturdy boots for this trip since my feet slipped in the thick mud with each step. I was glad that I hadn't worn the high-heeled boots I had taken to wearing. The same kind of boots that Mother used to wear. I had never really been able to pull it off with the same grace that she had. I had even seen her fight in them once. Mother had always been graceful, always in control of her body no matter what. Even after her arm had been destroyed and replaced with a crystal replica she had retained her grace in its use. But even though I wasn't as graceful as her, I still wore those boots at every opportunity. Because she had. And because I was short and the high heels gave me a much needed boost to my stature. When I couldn't see anyone with pinkish hair in town I asked a woman with a fiery red mop on her head that reminded me of mom. She pointed out at the fields behind the town and muttered that Micah was probably out there before hurrying off. I mumbled a thank you at her back and started walking again. Soon I heard the sound of axes biting into wood and the grunts of men at work. I found a group of shirtless men chopping up a felled tree, muscles coiling beneath sweat-slicked skin.

"Hey there, fellers," I called out, mimicking the accent of the guards.

One of the men glanced at me and did a double-take before a sly grin spread across his features.

"Well what do we have here?" He asked loudly and the nearest men stopped working and smiled at me.

"Don't mind me," I smiled back. "I'm just enjoying the show. Carry on gentlemen." I leaned casually against a felled tree and tilted my head and watched them. A few of them smiled and attacked the tree with renewed vigour but the man who had first looked up at me flung his axe onto his shoulder and sauntered up to me.

"I haven't seen these fellers work this hard since... Hell, ever. I should have you, and those," he said with a vague gesture at my boobs, "here all day to keep them motivated. Name's Arthur. Arthur Swanson, pleased to meet'ya." He said heartily while shifting the grip on his axe to his other hand to offer me his grimy paw. I shook it like a warrior, wrist to wrist.

"Mary Laier. Pleasure is all mine, big guy. You the boss around here?"

"Aye. I have the dubious honour of being the foreman of this here lumberin' crew. What can I do you for?"

"I'm new in town, was told to find a Lord Micah."

"Ah, I see. Micah does like to meet every newcomer though I wouldn't call him 'Lord' to his face. He does not like that the people have started blathering such shite."

My estimation of this Micah character rose a notch. Arthur turned his head over his shoulder. "Hey, Micah!" He bellowed. "Newcomer here to meet ya'!"

A mountain of a man rose to his full height to show a vast muscled back covered in scars. He turned around to show a craggy face that seemed carved from granite. His face was as scarred and grizzled as his back and a nasty burn scar covered the left side of his jaw and neck. He would have been utterly imposing if it hadn't been for his hair. To call it 'pinkish' was an understatement. It was like saying that the Skylands had 'taken in a little water' when it plummeted into the ocean. His hair was a vivid cerise colour and it shone in the midday sun. Sweat ran in rivulets down his skin and my estimation of him rose yet another notch. I preferred when the guy in charge got his hands dirty. It showed that he did not have an inflated view of himself.

"Oh, yeah?" Micah thundered and it was obvious that he had been in the military. With a voice like that he must have started out as a drill Sergeant. When he stalked closer I could feel his presence like a physical force towering above me. He was almost a third again my height and more than twice my width and his form blocked out the sun.

"Back to work, Arthur, if you would be so kind. I'll handle this. Old Marnie need that roof on her cottage or her pneumonia will get worse."

"Yes, Micah. Of course," Arthur said and hurried back to work.

"I'm Micah," the big man said and offered me his hand.

"Mary Laier," I said and put my hand in his and tried not to wince as it vanished into his gigantic hand. But his calloused grip was surprisingly gentle.

"You don't look old enough to have been more than a child when Utopeia fell."

I shook my head. "No, I was very young. We barely got out alive."

His chest rumbled. "I still remember that day as if it was yesterday. The Traitor stared me right in the eyes, her savage beast towering behind her. I thought I was about to die."

"You faced Kyra Zorantis in battle?" I breathed. I had relived that battle so many times throughout the years since that all of it was etched into my brain as if I had been there myself. In none of them were this man featured. Granted, mothers memories had been smothered in a thick red haze of rage and grief.

"I wouldn't call it a battle. I shot her in the heart but her body just bled magic instead of blood and she didn't even seem to notice. She gave me this as a parting gift," he said and indicated the burn scar. "Then she just floated over my steaming body and left me for dead. Would have taken her but a gesture to make sure that I actually was. Damn woman ripped Utopeia apart almost by her own damn self. And to think that I once respected her. Heck, I even trained her back in the day."

That was news to me.

"I see you are surprised by this fact. I was her drill Sergeant when she was in the Guard, and when I was promoted to Captain of my own precinct I asked for her to be assigned to me personally. One of the best Guards I ever served with, honest to a fault and would never take money on the side."

My mouth was hanging open by that point.

"I see that my words surprise you, but it's the truth. I trained her, I trusted her. Too bad she had to go mercenary like so many others, and then she gone and went full native and killed our people. Cursed be her name."

"Cursed be her name," I muttered in reflex.

"But you didn't come here to listen to an old man reminisce about our people's worst enemy. Why are you here and how can you be useful?"

I drew a deep breath to steady my nerves. I had not been prepared for that diatribe about mother.

"I heard that a new settlement was being built, just for Skylanders. I've longed to be with my people for years ever since my teacher died."

"Teacher? What about your folks?"

"They died at Utopeia. Well, only father did. Mother died shortly thereafter in the Elweyr forest of an infection. I would have died to if Mathias Varl hadn't found me there."

Micah stroked his chin. "Varl, eh? Good man. Too bad he died, we could have really used him around here. You said he taught you?"

"He sure did."

"So you know stuff about construction and shield magic?"

I nodded enthusiastically. Now that Mathias was dead I was probably the second most skilled Shield Mage in the world. Second only to Uncle Kendall. All of the others died either on the Hand of Glory, in Utopeia or in the Fall of the Skylands.

"He taught me so much."

Micah stared at me for a long time, still stroking his craggy chin. "How did you learn of our location? Has the rumour mill already started? Do we have to start working on our defences or did you learn it from one of our own?"

"I heard it from a fellow Skylander, he let me copy his map. He left a few days before me, saying that he couldn't wait to get here and that if I found a swift horse I would catch up to him."

"Maybe he's here already, who was this fellow?"

"Gereon. I don't know his last name." Maybe he had told me. If he had, I had already forgotten.

"Gereon. Good kid. His father served under me at Utopeia. That crimson beast ripped him to shreds right before my eyes. I haven't seen him around, maybe he's here, maybe he's still on the road. Maybe he died getting here. A lot of people die on those roads out there. No civilisation for leagues in these parts."

"Perhaps. I hope he's alright though, he was a nice man," I said honestly. He had been. But he was a Skylander. Even though Skylanders were born innocent just like everyone else, we could not help but destroy eventually. Including me. Not even mother could escape that fate. She had been the most perfect Skylander of them all, and she ended up destroying her home city, killing millions. "So, Micah. Tell me about this place."

"Oh, we don't have time for that now. Tomorrow night I'll hold another introductory speech for all the newcomers. I have them once a week so that I don't have to repeat myself too often."

"Fair enough," I agreed. "Then tell me how I can be useful."

"A woman who is not unwilling to get her hands dirty. I approve. But you've walked through town. Why don't you tell me what needs doing?" He said with a playful glint in his eyes.

"Well, first of all you seem to be lacking in raw building materials, hence you would not be chopping wood. There is only one actual building in town, and that supports my hypothesis. When I walked through town I saw only a handful of cooking fires with what mostly appeared to be broth in the cauldrons so there is a food shortage as well. As you mentioned earlier, defencive structures are sorely lacking even though most of the people are armed with at least a melee weapon. Also it was absolute pain to walk through the mud. Planks or something laid out would make it easier to manage logistics."

"All sound observations, Mary, you have a keen eye. And where in all of this mess would you believe yourself to be most useful?"

"That depends. Mathias and I designed a new spell for materialising magic. I could set up a bunch of those to create generic building materials that can then be assembled into housing or whatever. I could also do the same for defencive structures. Dragon teeth traps, a palisade to start with before migrating to a wall. Canno's and mortars to defend the choke point of the valley mouth."

Micah considered that while scratching at the back of his head.

"What about the food issue?"

"I can hunt, but that is not my forte. And out there I would be just one gun. Any of the other projects would be a better use of my abilities." Despite myself I was getting excited for the project. I had always enjoyed building and solving problems.

"One thing you missed, Mary, was our most glaring problem. Manpower. We aren't that many, yet, though there's a steady trickle of newcomers every day. But each and every one of them is another mouth to feed and another house that needs building. We have food stores to last us for a while but all of the workers are needed to gather raw materials and for construction."

I blinked. "Those were the two problems I suggested a solution for," I pointed out cautiously.

"And both of them require a lot of magic power. Do you sense much magic around here?"

Now that he mentioned it, I didn't. Micah sighed deeply.

"When I came here with the first convoy I was following a rumour that there would be a magic fount here that we could build on. Like the Skylands, but on a smaller scale. But I haven't found it yet. I fear that it might have dried up ages ago. I read that it should be possible to open it again but that knowledge died with the Traitor and whatever tome she learned it from is kept by the Bizhjerai bastards."

"Then you are in luck, Micah, for Mathias Varl found a copy of that exact same tome in one of the southern Cities," I lied.

Micah's eyes lit up with newfound hope. "Don't tell me that you know the spell to open up magic founts," he hissed.

"Today is your lucky day, Micah. I sure do. I can even find it."

"What? That's insane. Only the Traitor's bitch could do that," he scoffed dubiously.

"Perhaps. But don't you think that if some backwater savage without training could figure it out, shouldn't it have been ease itself for a Mage of Mathias Varl's calibre?"

"You do have a point, young woman. You do have a point. Alright, here's the deal. As far as I know, even if you manage to open up a fount, it will take at least a few days to erect the required infrastructure to utilise it?"

I nodded that he was correct. Growing a focusing tower and a collector nexus here would take more than a week.

"And in that time we will need workers to build, to gather materials, and to hunt."

I nodded again.

"What we don't have, are enough workers for all of these tasks."

I nodded to keep him talking and finally give me some orders.

"That is your task. You're a problem solver. Use your spells with whatever magic power you can scrounge up to automate something that will free up workers for tasks that can't be automated with the meagre magic we have at our disposal."

I inclined my head. It was an interesting problem, one that I would love to solve. The more central a position I could acquire here, the easier it would be to topple this place from the inside when the time was right.

"Got it. I'll get right on that, Micah."

"I have faith in you, Mary. You strike me as a bright young woman. You remind me of my daughter."

The pain in his eyes when he said that last part told me exactly what had happened to her.

"Excellent!" He exclaimed enthusiastically and I found myself warming to this old veteran. He started to turn away but then stopped himself. "Oh, and seek out Ms Vorka. She's in charge of housing. She will show you where you can hang your hat at night." He shot a glance at my head. "Not that you are wearing a hat... Anyway, pleasure meeting you Mary, and I hope that you can deliver on your promises." He almost smiled, as close as his stony face could come to a smile these days I suspected, and then returned to chopping wood. I leaned back onto my tree trunk to think. My mind was already working the problems. My eyes drifted toward the working, sweating, men. Might as well let my eyes enjoy the show while I mulled over my options.

I decided that defence could wait. We were well enough equipped to fight off small attacks with what we had and given our limited resources we would not be able to construct defences adequate to fend of medium or greater sized attacks. Just slightly reducing the level of annihilation we would suffer would just be a waste of time. So I turned my mind to the construction of new dwellings. The most pressing concern appeared to be manpower, and a lot of it was currently wasted on chopping down trees to build with. It would be much more efficient to just use materialised magic and grow the buildings out of nothing. But to do that, we would need a vast source of magic power. A fount, like the one that was the core of Bizhjerai power. But to do that I needed to find a ley-line crossing and open it up and use the initial power surge to construct a collector tower and a receiver here at camp. That would take time. And in that time our food stocks would deplete and people would start to go hungry. An intermediary solution would be needed to by me some time to work on the magic power supply lines. I tapped my bottom lip. A lot of people were wasted chopping wood. With some magical tools I could probably reduce their number and the then freed workers could focus on hunting. I decided that was what I was going to do first. I could make a magic powered lumber mill and power it with the magical energy in my bikes power core for a short while. If I sent magic collecting swarms wide and far it would stretch the magic supply for a bit longer. But it would still run out eventually. I started walking among the working men, watching them work, trying to figure out where to apply power to multiply their efforts. After an hour I had identified the three most time consuming tasks: felling the trees, splitting the trunks into planks and clearing away the branches. I pulled out the powerstone for my bike, the one that contained the schematics as well as the power required to manifest it. I pulled the schematics out of it, copied it into an invocation-time spell and started butchering it. I ripped away everything that had anything to do with the bike until I was left with just the engine. It was still too large for my purpose so I spent an hour and a half shrinking it down into something that could be managed with brute strength. After that was done it was ease itself to add a blade surrounded by a spinning chain with crystal teeth that would cut through the wood as if it was nothing. I stored the schematics in a spare power stone before I set to materialising it. Once it was done I plucked it out of the air and dismissed the spell framework with a wave of my hand. I caught a guy staring at me with slack jaw and he bashfully snapped his mouth shut and got back to work but he kept glancing at me out of the corner of his eye. I brought my machine over to the guys chopping down a poplar tree.

"Gentlemen, may I?"

They exchanged confused glances but stepped back from the tree. I started my machine and pressed it firmly against the side of the tree. Sawdust sprayed when the crystal teeth bit into the wooden fibres and in just a minute or two the tree creaked and fell. The men whooped with joy and attacked the branches with their axes, debranching the tree in no time. Micah walked up to me.

"Impressive."

"Thank you, Micah."

"With machines like these we will only need a fraction of the men to keep up with demand."

"That was my intention," I said smugly.

"How long will the power last?"

"Depends. Not too long. I plan on getting on to that problem next," I told him. "But first I'll build a similar machine for splitting logs. That will free up even more men to work on other things. If we use the children to clear the branches away that will free up even more workers," I commented.

Micah stared at me with an expressionless face. "I'll see it done." Then he walked away with no further comment.

I nodded firmly at his back. He didn't mince words. I liked that. The machine for splitting logs was more complex than I had anticipated and night was falling by the time I finished. It was not as efficient as I had hoped since it was too big to be portable. Instead the men had to either chop the trunks up into smaller segments to be split into shorter planks or they required almost a dozen men just to wrest the large trunks into position to be split. At least the splitting itself was quick enough. Though the power levels in my power core was already getting disturbingly low. Before I headed out to find something to eat I dispatched my entire magic collection swarm into the night, but I was certain that I would need to increase their number drastically to keep up with demand. I went in search of food and approached one of the big cauldrons that I had seen earlier in the day. I was greeted by a kindly old woman with a thinning head of azure hair. She smiled at me. "You're that young lass that arrived earlier today," she stated.

"Yes, ma'am," I agreed and accepted the bowl of stew she handed me.

"My grandson worked with the lumbering," she confided with a wide toothless smile. "He absolutely hated it."

"Sorry to hear that," I responded mechanically and took a bite of the stew. It was horrible. But filling, I hoped.

"Don't be, sweetheart. He told me about the machine you whipped out of thin air. He won't be going back there tomorrow. He said he was going hunting. He has always loved the wilds," the woman said and smiled dreamily. "I still remembered that first day when we stepped out into the wilderness outside of Utopeia. I had never seen him that happy before."

I nodded, using my mouthful as an excuse not to respond. I wasn't interested in her, or her grandson. I scarfed down my meal, managing to dodge any further conversations, and went in search of Ms Vorka who would assign me to my sleeping quarters. Night had fallen, but I still had work to do. I needed to become a trusted member of this little community so that I could sabotage it from within. Plans were already swirling in my head and a grim smile stretched my lips.


---

## Chapter 3 — CHAPTER THREE Planning {#chapter-3}

CHAPTER THREE

Planning

I rose with the dawn the next day and stretched languidly inside my ratty tents It was barely large enough to cover me while I slept and there was no way I could stand up straight inside it even though I was quite a short woman. I glared balefully at my uncomfortable leather corset and decided that I would not wear that today. I was among Skylanders now, might as well wear Skylander garb. I reached up and touched the powerstone on the choker that was all I wore. Power billowed out of it to surround my body and in short order I was clad in clothes similar to what mother favoured. I frowned down at myself. Perhaps that was not prudent. I had already made a guard suspicious by carrying a 'replica' of her gun. I should not dress like her, not here. Grumbling under my breath I reached up to my powerstone and changed the clothing settings. Slowly my outfit dissolved and reformed into something that was not quite so obviously inspired by the Traitor. I ended up in something similar to what a working woman would wear on the Skylands. An indigo blouse, a simple corset over that followed by tight leggings and a short utility skirt. Despite the mud outside I went with a pair of boots with a three inch heel. At least the heel was wide and gave plenty of traction, and it gave me a much needed boost to my general lack of stature. I crawled out of the tent, careful to not get mud on the knees of my leggings and stood up to greet the day. I cursed under my breath. I did not enjoy waking up early. I preferred to stay up late and sleep in. Most of the best thinking was done late at night. But I had a lot of work to do today if I wanted to worm my way into an irreplaceable status here in the camp. First I visited the logging camp and I was a bit surprised that there were only two men there. I had thought that they would start work early what with the severe lack of building materials. Both men grabbed a tree-felling machine each and got to work and in short order they had each felled a tree. They kept going, probably waiting for the others to start splitting the trees. I nodded to myself and turned full circle and scanned the horizon to see if I could spot my magic collecting bug swarms. They should return any moment now. I lamented that my magesense was not as powerful as mothers had been. She had been able to sense anything within a mile in great detail, but she had been far more powerful than me. Even before the epic duel with the liche Grasax she had been more powerful than me. I knew that I could never compete with her. She had been more powerful, more experienced, and a more skilled fighter. No matter how much I lamented it, I would never be her equal as a fighter. No one would. Yet I was the product of not just one great and powerful mother. I was the product of two. Anjali had not been as skilled a warrior as Kyra, nor had she been as powerful a Mage. But she had been a brilliant craftswoman and used her magic to construct spells that confounded even the great Kendall. That is were I not only matched Kyra, but surpassed her. Mother had had the raw strength to shatter the Skylands. She wielded her power like a sledgehammer, powerful enough to crush her opponents. I used it like a rapier, to dismantle my foe with the least amount of effort. I applied force to the weakest link to bring the whole machinery to a halt. A simple thrust to the heart. What both of my mothers had failed to realise whas that magical construction was the way forward. Mother had been too stuck in the thought patterns of the Skylanders to understand, and mom had been too much a Bizhjerai to realise that construction could be used in battle. But I had seen it, just like Kendall had so long ago. It wasn't our magic that made Skylanders powerful. A Mage in combat might be able to vanquish any foe he faced with ease, but he was still just a single man on the battlefield. Even if he killed a hundred men in battle, that wouldn't cause the win. The true strength of magical construction was to create tools for ordinary men. Tools that could multiply the effects of an individuals actions. Like the tree felling machines. Instead of five men chopping the tree down with axes, a single man could now fell a tree in mere minutes. Sure, I could blast that tree down in seconds, but that would waste my powers. My machines could work without me. If I spent an hour creating a machine then a man could fell trees for years without me and I could optimise something else instead of being stuck felling trees. Over time, the investment of my time added up and made it all worth it. But I digress. My machines would be worthless without an additional source of power, and to do that I needed to find a ley-line crossing. I had forced myself to relive mother's memories so many times that her memories were almost my own. I remembered how mom had struggled with designing the scouting spell bugs and how mother had helped her. I remembered how they had taken my dragon brother out to test them and I remembered their jubilation and exuberance when they had finally gotten it to work. The spell was embedded in my head and I could cast it almost without effort. But not without taking my time doing it. It used the old mechanism for materialising magic and each little bug required a physical spell stone core to give it momentum. It took me most of the morning creating a handful of them but it was with pride that I watched them buzz away on the horizon in spiralling search patters. They would return to me if and when they found a source of magic worth opening. Satisfied with my morning, I went in search of food. My belly grumbled and I cursed myself for having skipped breakfast. It was something I preferred to do since I never woke up hungry, but every time I did it I cursed myself before noon because I was starving. Grumbling under my breath, I walked back into the mass of tents. The lunch was as disappointing as the dinner yesterday had been. It was mostly vegetables that had been boiled beyond recognition. I really hope that hunting party that had been sent out this morning returned with some meat for dinner. I forced down a second helping and went to see if my nest of power collector bugs had return. They had, and they had brought back a surprising amount of power. I only used a small portion of it to refuel the tree felling and log splitting machines and saved the rest for bootstrapping the collector tower. That would require a significant investment of power for the initial collector lens. Once that was done the rest of the tower could be built using the power supplied by the focusing towers. Now that I had optimised the raw materials gathering I turned my attention to the next issue. Defences. While I did not have enough magical power to actually do anything about it, I did have time to plan. And an hour of planning was worth several hours of actual work. I spent an hour climbing the mountain side so that I could get a top-down view of the valley. I could have used my Angel armour to fly up here, but I was loathe to waste magical power in the current supply situation. Besides, I had always enjoyed using my muscles. Even back on the Skylands when I had been I kid I had loved to climb the buildings on the Surface whenever father brought me up there. Thinking of father no longer hurt. It had for a long time after his death and it wasn't until Uncle Kenny had brought me and grandmother to the Bizhjerai lands that the pain slowly started to fade. I still missed him something fierce, for I had loved him with all of my heart, but thinking of him now only brought me a wistful happiness. When I had been a kid I had not believed in an afterlife, and to an extent I didn't now either, but the custom of the Clans was to treat your ancestors as if their Spirits were still watching over you. Even it was not true, I still found some comfort in that it could be true. I knew that if it was then he would be watching over me and I knew that he would be proud over what I had become. He had been a lowly mercenary on the Skylands, a killer for hire. A daughter of someone like him had little prospects on the Skylands. Their fate was to either be a merc like him, a Stardust slinger or a prostitute. Instead I had become the daughter of the mighty Bàs Marbhadair and Anjali the Red Terror, brother of Malkareth the Great. I had been taught by Alissa Skylander and Kendall the Builder and was the protege of Marie, the Chief of Chiefs. And now I had taken on the mission that mother had died starting. The eradication of all Skylanders and the people that had forced father into the life he had never chosen. But in order to destroy them, I had to first protect them. I had to create a safe haven for them to flock to. And so I studied the valley below me. It appeared to have been carved by a great glacier that had melted before completing its final slide toward the lowlands. The valley mouth was narrow and easily defended before it flared into a wide channel through the mountain. If we built a fort at the valley mouth then it would be nearly impenetrable. Almost identical to Dun Traelak. In my minds eye I saw a long wall spanning the valley mouth, studded with cannons and mortars. The cliff walls on either side would hold several batteries of artillery and roost for Skylanders equipped with Valkyrie or Angel armour to swoop down to harass the enemy or reinforce the wall. I saw several sally ports were APCs could glide into the fray to flank any siege forces or put down the survivors of an attack that had been quashed by the rain of death from above. But such defences were useless until we had enough people to man it. As soon as we had a source of magic to sustain construction I would start, even if we could not defend the wall in its entirety. It would take a long time to build and I should get started as soon as possible. I could make this place as difficult to take as Dun Traelak, even harder actually, since it would be much easier to plan for anti-air weapons. Mother had had that trouble when she modernised Dun Traelak, the existing infrastructure had made it difficult to fit everything inside the ancient fortress. I was building from scratch. Excitement shot through me and I rubbed my hands together. I couldn't wait to get started. It was almost noon when I returned to the camp and I scarfed down another portion of tasteless stew. Something poked my shoulder and I turned around to face whoever wanted to talk to me, but there were no one there. I glanced around in confusion, trying to figure out if someone was playing a prank on me but then I noticed the little bug hovering beside me. It was one of my scouting bugs and I eagerly reached out and caught it my fist. Images bloomed in my mind and I suddenly knew where there were a crossing of ley lines. I wanted to go and set it up straight away, but I forced my excitement down and waited for more scouting bugs. In the meantime I built blueprints for manufacturing spells that would produce everything from gun parts to ammunition and crystal bricks for the walls. The traditional Skylander way of building buildings was to materialise magic to grow the building as a single unit, but that forced you to use only a single large materialisation spell. By producing smaller, more modular, bricks instead allowed me to mass produce them in parallel and they could later be fused by magic into a single piece. Way faster and with the same end result. It would cost more magical power though, but I believed it was worth it. Once enough ley-lines had been opened, magical power was almost a non-issue. I sat in a sunny spot with my back to a tree while I worked on my schematics, scribbling away in my spell book. Suddenly a shadow fell over me and I glanced up to see Micah towering above me.

"I see that you have completed the task I gave you," he greeted me.

"I have," I agreed.

"You have not asked me what to do now," he observed stonily.

"You seemed to prefer when we identified a problem by ourselves and work to solve it."

Micah grunted. "What problem are you working on?"

"Defences."

"We don't have spare manpower to construct any defences," Micah pointed out.

"That is true. But I am just planning. An hour spent planning is worth several hours of needless work. I am waiting for the rest of my scouting spells to return with locations of suitable ley-line crossings. Might as well use the time wisely."

"I approve. Any preliminary reports?"

"One scouting bug have returned, there are a pair of ley-lines crossing further up into the valley. Since that scout returned first that should be the closest."

Micah nodded and sat down next to me. "Show me your plans for the defences."

And I did. Micah lit up when we were talking war and it was clear that he had been a career military man. We drew in the dirt and I pointed out how I had imagined the wall and the position of gun batteries and roosts in the cliffs. Micah grunted and altered my sketch.

"Shouldn't have batteries," he pointed out and replaced my batteries with a longer, thinner line of artillery.

"But that would make ammunition logistics much easier," I protested.

"True," he agreed. "But it also allow the enemy to easier map our defences and can take out a whole cluster of guns with a single well placed volley. A thin line is more resilient. We will deal with logistics."

I thought about that for a moment. It made perfect sense. "What about having your line of artillery, but every stretch of ten guns share a local ammunition storage?"

"Yes, that should make logistics simpler. But a Skyship would be able to locate the magazine and destroy it with a bombardment. Even if the artillery survives, they are useless without ammo."

"Flak cannons?" I asked even though I already knew what he was going to say.

"That would dissuade lone Skyships, but if there is a small fleet of them? They will just take out the flak cannons first with long range bombardment cannons and then move in for the kill."

Eventually we settled for a multi-tiered solution with each piece of artillery having its own small storage of ammunition backed up by my more centralised but still distributed magazine. Those would in turn be served by a central, very defended armoury.

Next we went over the wall itself and Micah erased it all and ignored my indignant sputtering. He redrew it in a new jagged shape instead of my smooth wall. It did not look at all like the straight wall of Dun Traelak.

Micah seemed to pick up on my confusion for he started drawing arcs in front of the wall.

"What is that you are drawing now?" I asked.

"Firing arcs," he grunted and his sketch suddenly made sense.

The salients and buttresses he had drawn would allow fighters on and inside the wall to overlap their fields of fire on an advancing enemy, harassing them from all sides.

"But all of this is probably useless," Micah finally sighed and rubbed his eyes.

"What? Why?"

"Because I was there at the battle for Dun Traelak and the Traitor managed to snatch victory from us by pulling that dragon horde out of her ass."

"You where there?" I gasped. "But I thought that there were no survivors!"

"We were not many that made it out alive. Out of the thousands that marched on Dun Traelak, less than three hundred made it back. I only survived because I was cut off at the rear when those flying bastards started dropping down on us. I saw that it was a lost cause and retreated to defend Utopeia."

"And why did that lead you to the conclusion that all of this," I said with a gesture at our earthen sketch, "is useless?"

"Because we could have won that battle and saved our people as soon as we battered down the shield that protected Dun Traelak. Had we just stayed back and shelled the fortress into rubble and burying the Traitor and her bitch inside we would have been long gone before the dragons arrived. Even if they had caught us and slaughtered us on the plains on our way back we would still have won. Dun Traelak would have been gone, the Traitor dead. The spine of the Clans would have been shattered and we would have been able to defeat them at our leisure. Utopeia would still stand. The Skylands would not have Fallen."

The bitter words rolled off his tongue in a resentful stream and I could tell that the words had festered inside him for many years.

"But our commanders... No, we commanders. For I was one of them. We were to stuck on the idea of capturing Dun Traelak, to use it as a foothold against the Clans and claim the magical power network the Traitor had established. We could not subside on the artificial ley-line from the Skylands forever. It was bleeding our homeland dry."

We were both silent for a long while.

"But we have nothing that anyone else would want to take from us. No resources, no goods. Not even quality land. The people out here have no use for magic, they have their steam power instead, primitive as it are. We have nothing for them to take. Except our lives. If we are attacked, it will be a war of extermination. And thus, all of this," he said bitterly and made an encompassing gesture, "will be useless. They will just grind us to dust from afar until we are all dead."

"Then we build a shield," I said with finality.

"That did not help Dun Traelak. It did not help Utopeia. It did not even help the Skylands. A shield is, like a wall, a temporary counter-measure. It will fall eventually."

"So are you saying that we should just hope that we are not attacked?"

"Not at all. We should still do all of this. But we must figure out a better way to counter long range bombardment."

"I'm on it," I responded instantly, though I had no idea where to start.

Micah chuckled. "Glad to hear it. Always good to have more minds working on the same problem. I will make sure to gather all of you in the next few days so that you may exchange ideas." He glanced up at the sun and I was surprised to see that it was setting. We had been talking defences all afternoon.

"You should make sure to eat something and rest early."

When I just looked askance at him he smiled grimly.

"Because you are waking up early tomorrow to hike up to that ley-line crossing. We need magical power and soon, Mary."

I sprang to my feet and nodded eagerly. When I left Micah behind to get some food I was already thinking about tomorrow. I had to solve this defence problem to prove to Micah that I was better than the other people working on the same problem. I had to earn more responsibilities so that I could design as many faults in the systems as I could. My mind was spinning with thoughts of vengeance and sabotage and sleep did not claim me until way past midnight.


---

## Chapter 4 — CHAPTER FOUR North {#chapter-4}

CHAPTER FOUR

North

Dawn found me yawning and bleary eyed. I had not had much sleep in the night and the last thing on the list of things I wanted to do right now was to hike up the valley. But I did want to open that ley-line crossing, so I just had to simply buckle up and do it. Fortunately Ms Vorka had been an even earlier riser than I so I had a belly full of bread and cheese and a satchel of provisions that would last two days.
