Smothering a yawn I started walking toward the logging area where I would just keep going north.

"Mary? Hold up!"

I turned around at the unexpected voice and was astonished to see four people jogging up to me. I stopped and waited for them. When they drew closer my eyebrows started climbing my forehead. They were all decked out in light armour and armed with at least a side-piece each. All of them had some sort of bladed sidearm as well except for the huge man in the front who carried a heavy pole-ax.

"Yes, how can I help you?" I asked the group when they came within talking distance.

"We are to go with you," the tall man said with a genuine smile.

I was impressed, and a little annoyed, that his eyes did not dip toward my cleavage. He was big, almost as big as Micah, but that was were the similarities ended. Micah was stony, cold and efficient where this boy was soft, smiling and with a mischievous spark in his green eyes. His curly hair was just barely long enough to brush the lobes of his ears and it was a deep green colour with turquoise highlights. A Skylander through and through. He rested his pole-ax against his shoulder and extended his hand.

"I'm Zandor," he said with another dazzling smile and I shook his hand, wrist to wrist. He was about my age, possibly a little older, and he was trying desperately to grow a beard. His chin and cheeks wasn't cooperating fully and the result was that his face looked stained a deep green. He really should shave that thing off entirely and he would be really handsome.

"This is Wylah," he said, introducing the willowy woman next to him. She was a head taller than me and had a slim body with whipcord muscles that spoke of endurance and viper-like speed. I shook her wrist and her impassive face showed a slight smirk. Her eyes were a dark brown and her hair matched them almost perfectly if it hadn't been for the streak of silvery hair that grew from her fringe. She made me think of a badger with that stripe.

"Felaan," the other male of the group said softly and offered me his hand. His skin was swarthy, and his hair was a shade darker with golden streaks shot through it. His complexion did not look like a Skylander, but it was not too far of a stretch. While most Skylanders were pale there were quite a few that had darker complexions. I had read in one of Grasax' old tomes that the people that founded the Skylands were refugees from a country consisting of a mix of people from all over the ancient world.

"And this shy kitten over here is, Valia," Zandor introduced the last member of their group.

Valia gave me a shy smile and offered me her hand and she surprised me with an unexpectedly firm grip around my wrist. She was only a hand taller than me and her hair shone like the sun. It wasn't just blond, it was outright golden. Metallic sheen and all. Her eyes was a pale platinum, equally metallic. I suffered a sudden desire to run my fingers through her golden locks to feel if it felt like normal hair or if it would feel like cold metal. I restrained myself.

"What brings me the pleasure of your company?" I asked more cheerfully than I felt.

"We've been assigned to go with you," Zandor beamed. "We haven't scouted the valley much so we don't know what's up there. It could be dangerous," he shrugged. "I think it'll just be some animals, maybe a bear or wolves. Nothing the five of us can't handle."

Great. While I didn't truly mind the company, it would be nice to have some alone time to plan my treachery in peace. But that could wait. It would take a long time for the Skylanders to assemble here.

"Alright, let's go," I said and started turning around to lead the way.

"Aren't you bringing any weapons?" Zandor asked.

"The guards took them when I came here, said that I had to prove my trustworthiness before they were returned to me," I shrugged.

Zandor sighed heavily. "That was Brengar, right? He's such an asshole."

"I don't know his name. And what do you mean?"

"He tries to pull that shit on all of the newcomers. There is no policy of disarming newcomers," Zandor explained wearily. "Let's go wake that bastard up and force him to give you back your weapons."

I nodded dumbly. I had been played for a fool, and I did not like it.

"Why can't he just obey orders?" Zandor asked no one in particular. "I guess he was just born an idiot and never grew out of it. Can't stand that guy."

None of the others answered him aside the occasional grunt of agreement and I quickly understood that Zandor was the talker of the group.

"Wake up, Brengar, you old fool!" Zandor said loudly to a tent and kicked a foot that extended past the flap. A grunt and curse preceded a rustling of cloth and Brengar stuck his head outside, blearily blaring at Zandor.

"I believe you have something that belongs to my friend," Zandor said with a nod in my direction.

"Policy is for newcomers to..."

He didn't get further before Zandor cut him off. "That's bullshit and you know it, Brengar. Give her her weapons. Now."

"Policy for newcomers is to..." Brengar tried again.

"Do you really want me to go and wake Micah up? Again?"

Brengar stared at the big youth for a long while before he cursed and ducked back into his tent. A moment later my weapons belt sailed out to splash into the mud, followed by my disassembled and wrapped rifle.

"That motherfucker," Zandor grumbled and took a step toward the tent. Felaan and Wylah instantly had him by the arms and were pulling him back from the tent. It was clear to me that they would have no chance against the big man if he had really tried to get away from them. He let them lead him away while I picked up and belted my weapons around my hips.

"Don't mind Zandor," Valia whispered next to me. "He is a good guy, but Brengar somehow manages to get on his nerves. I have never seen him angry apart from when he is dealing with that old fool."

Valia seemed genuinely worried that I would hold Zandor's sudden anger against him.

"It's fine. I wouldn't mind beating Brengar up myself," I winked at the shy girl and she grinned back. We rejoined the others and Zandor opened his mouth, undoubtedly to apologise. I held up my hand to forestall it.

"Thank you all for getting me my kit back. I felt naked without my guns," I said.

"We wouldn't want you naked, now would we?" Zandor said and froze. His face slowly turned crimson. "I mean," he sputtered, "not that I wouldn't want to see you naked. Shit! I mean, I would not presume to..."

"Zandor," Wylah interrupted wearily. "Stop talking. For your own sake."

"Yes. Of course. Yeah," he said and then stalked away toward the logging camp.

I exchanged an amused glance with Wylah who rolled her eyes and followed her big friend. The rest of us hurried to catch up and walked in silence for a while.

While we walked I used magic to unwrap and assemble my rifle before I slung it over my shoulder. Wylah also carried a rifle while Valia carried twin revolvers and a long dagger that was almost a sword. Felaan had a hatchet in his belt and a revolver and Zandor had his pole-ax, a revolver and a side-sword.

"You know magic?" Zandor asked with wide eyes, his earlier embarrassment apparently forgotten.

"Yeah," I shrugged. "I thought that was obvious since we are going north to open up a ley-line crossing."

"Oh, yeah. I guess it would be. I hadn't considered that. What's your speciality?"

"Construction, mainly, but I have also trained in magical combat."

He gave me a once over. "You don't look old enough to have been trained in the Skylands," he observed. "I thought all construction Mages died in Utopeia."

"Mathias Varl didn't," I pointed out.

"No way! You were trained by Mathias Varl?"

"Yup. He found me in the woods after Utopeia fell and my parents were killed. He took me in and taught me everything he knows."

"Then you must be the most powerful construction Mage in the world," Felaan commented softly. He never seemed to raise his voice above the level of a whisper.

"No, I am not."

"No? Everyone else died, right?"

"Kendall is still alive, isn't he?"

Wylah spat on the ground. "Unfortunately he is."

I winced when she spat. Uncle Kenny was the nicest man in the world. "Despite what he has done, despite helping the Traitor, he is still a brilliant Mage," I said resolutely.

"It sounds as if you respect him," Valia whispered.

"Of course I do. He is brilliant, regardless of what he has done."

"I would love to see you fight him," Zandor said with a faraway look in his eyes.

"I wouldn't," I said honestly. "If I ever saw him, I would run as fast as I could away from him." That was not a lie. Uncle Kenny would be incredibly pissed off that I ran away from Dun Traelak in defiance of his wishes.

"What? But he's the enemy!" Zandor protested.

"He's one of the most powerful Mages in the world. He would whoop my ass up and down this valley if I tried to fight him." Again, not a lie. Kendall had rarely raised his hand against me. Only when I had really, really deserved it. And he had been crying every time. And that was what had hurt the most. Not the spanking. His disappointment and the knowledge that it was me who had caused his pain. That had spurred me into trying to be a better girl.

"We are Skylanders," Zandor said vehemently, "and we do not flee from any fight!"

"And that is why the Skylands Fell, why Utopeia lost its army outside of Dun Traelak and why Utopeia was crushed. They picked a fight with a stronger opponent and refused to back down."

"The Clans were not stronger!" He protested. "They were just savages. If it hadn't been for the Traitor and her damned dragons they wouldn't have stood a chance."

"You are correct. But they did have Kyra Zorantis. They did have dragons. That made them stronger. Allies are part of your strength, Zandor. There is no shame in not participating in a fight that you can't win. In fact, it is foolish to fight if you are not certain that you can win. If you have other options, of course."

Zandor opened his mouth, closed it again before opening it. Finally he scowled and pressed ahead.

I felt suddenly bad for being so harsh with him, but his naivety had chafed on my nerves. He was an idealist, not a realist. But that did not give me the right to shatter his illusions so brutally. I glanced at the others who just shrugged.

"He'll be alright," Felaan said. "He doesn't like being proven wrong and he often reacts like that. He just needs to mull it over and then he will be back to apologise to you."

That was the longest sentence I had heard from the dark man so far and I smiled at him. I hoped that he was correct about his friend. Despite his naivety the big guy was growing on me. His cheer and optimism was a nice break from my own vengeance-filled thoughts and dark attitude.

I decided to trust the quiet man and we forged on in silence. It was not until nightfall that we caught up to Zandor. He stood in a small clearing in the surprisingly thick woods with a sheepish expression on his face.

"Sorry, guys," he started while staring at his feet. "I've been an ass today, especially to you Mary. I apologise for getting upset, and for my... Nakedness comment... Earlier."

I couldn't let the big man suffer when he wore that expression on his face. "It's alright, Zandor."

He threw my a cautious glance and I tried to exude sincerity. Zandor perked up and spread his arms wide. "This is a fine place to set up camp tonight," he announced. "I found a stream a little way to the west so we can make a soup!"

The rest of us agreed and Felaan and Zandor, who were carrying the tents, started pitching them while Wylah set off to fill our little pot with water. Valia and I started gathering rocks to build a fireplace.

"So you guys have known each other a long time?" I asked her casually.

"Yeah. But we didn't really become close friends until a few months ago."

"How did you all meet?"

"We all lived around the same town. When we heard about this place we decided to travel here together and try to make a life among our own kind."

I placed my rock on the ground and stood up straight to stretch my back. "Was it really that bad for you where you were?"

"Mostly it was fine," Valia said softly. "But we were always the outsiders, you know? The word spread about the war with the Clans. I'm not trying to justify what happened, but history is written by the victors, right?"

"I hear you," I grumbled. Though I didn't. I had been there, smack dab in the middle of the winning side. It was true that the Clans hated the Skylanders almost as much as I did, but the Clans valued honesty. They would not smear the name of their enemies. These rumours must have originated somewhere else. And then it struck me. Of course. It must have been Uncle Raistlin. The old mercenary captain could never tell a story without embellishing it and as a far ranging merchant his stories would spread like wildfire.

"So, what did they do to you?" I finally asked while picking up another large rock.

"People are usually pleasant, on the surface, as long as you have something that they want. For trading, I mean. But we were always shunned and avoided if people could get away with it. A whole lot of little things like that."

I put my rock next to the others, completing the circle of the fire break.

"Alright," I said and dusted off my hands. "Firewood next?"

"Yes, I saw a bunch of fallen branches down the path we took, I'll show you."

I followed Valia in silence. She didn't strike me as the talkative type. I appreciated that.

Then she surprised me by asking a question. "Why did you come here?"

"After Varl died I decided to travel the world, make use of my skills as a Construction Mage. But everywhere I went people were suspicious of Skylanders and our strange magic. Few wanted to hire me, so I kept moving. I had to resort to hunting for food. There were too few that wanted to deal with me."

"And then you heard about this place," Valia concluded for me and indicated the scattered branches she had seen earlier.

"Not quite," I admitted reluctantly. "Before I met Gereon, the Skylander that told me about this place, I was heading north." I paused for effect. "For Dun Traelak."

Her gasp was assurance enough that she had bought my ruse. My cover should not be too perfect or it might attract attention.

"You were going to work with the enemy?"

"I didn't think I had a choice. They are the only one who I know work with Skylander Magic. Both Kendall the Builder and Alissa Skylander are esteemed members of Clan Bizhjerai."

Valia shot me a weird look and I replayed what I had said mentally before cringing inwardly. I shouldn't have used their Bizhjerai names! I should have called Kendall the 'Traitor's bitch' or something similar and Alissa by just her first name, or 'Turncloak'. Only Clansmen called her 'Alissa Skylander'.

"At least that is what a south-ranging Azwaili trader told me," I said with a sheepish shrug before bending over to pick up another branch to put in Valia's waiting arms.

"I guess that makes sense," Valia said dubiously.

"On my way north I met this Gereon and I instantly rerouted here. I hope that he makes it here okay, he left several days before me but he doesn't seem to have arrived yet."

"I hope so too," Valia whispered. "But I wouldn't count on it. If he aren't here already then he has most likely perished on the road. A lot of the others here lost someone on the road."

I nodded and placed another branch on the growing stack in her arms. I filled my own arms with wood and then we returned to the campsite to dump it all in a pile next to the fire break.

"Any of those branches good for kindling?" Felaan asked us.

"No need," I said and waved my fingers. "Magic."

"Right. Then let's build a fire, I guess," Zandor said and started stacking branches. A few of them were too long to fit properly and he snapped them with his huge hands as if they were twigs. I would have had to brace the end against the ground and put all of my weight on it to break a branch of that size. When he was done he leaned back and gave me an excited look. With a flourish I cast a tongue of flame at the logs and maintained it until they were thoroughly ignited.

"I wish someone had taught me to do that," Zandor sighed wistfully.

"I'll teach you," I said before I knew it and I zipped my lips shut. But the damage was done. Why had I offered to teach him magic? Never help the enemy!

"Really?" His green eyes were round and full of hope.

Too late to back out now. "Yeah," I promised, "when I have solved the current tasks Micah has set before me and the camp have some breathing room. Then I will teach you to conjure some flame."

"Can you teach me too?" Wylah asked without expression and I nodded.

"What is using magic like?" Valia asked me.

I had to frown deeply to think about it. Using magic had become second nature to me, as easy as breathing. What did it actually feel like?

"It is sort of like using a limb, one that you don't really know that you have. And you can use it in almost infinite ways. Like making signs with your hands," I said and twisted my fingers to cast a shadow in the shape of a bunny on the ground. "And the more complex your sign is, the more spectacular results you can achieve."

"That makes no sense," Zandor complained.

I shrugged in response. "It will once you try it. The first few spells are the most difficult, like when you learn to hold a pen. But when you finally learn to hold it and move it, you can draw almost any form without much training as long as you know what you are drawing."

I was rather proud over that analogy, though my new friends seemed dubious. But it was true. It had taken me days to even manage to extend a trickle of magic, and almost a week to actually do something with it. After that it took only a single day for me to learn how to cast a light spell, and half a day after that I was hurling fireballs all over the courtyard of Dun Traelak. I couldn't help but smile at the memory of Kendall and Alissa running around to put the fires out and absorb my stray attacks with shields while shouting for me to stop and calm down. That had been less than a year after my mothers had died, a few weeks before mother's memory core had arrived on my pillow by way of spell-bug. The water in the pot had started steaming gently and Zandor dropped a handful of jerky and a generous helping of oats into it.

"Were you all at Utopeia when the Clans came?" I asked into the silence and then wondered why the hell my brain had thought that was a good conversation starter.

Wylah, Valia and Felaan nodded, but it was Wylah who spoke. "Our parents were settlers, Undercity born and bred."

"You all made it out alright?" I asked.

"Almost," Felaan answered. "My elder brother died in the breach in the walls. I heard later that the Red Terror slew him personally. Valia lost her family in the city."

The quiet girl nodded sadly.

"Both of my parents died in the Traitor's rampage, along with my sister," she said tonelessly. "Father threw me into a ruin when he saw her coming. Knocked me senseless but I could still see. The Traitor ripped my sister limb from limb before she used magic to punch his head into oblivion. Mother got in the way of her pet dragon. At least she died quickly."

I remembered those people. I had seen their faces in mothers memories. Heard their screams while she slew them one by one. For the first time I felt a tiny twinge of compassion for my mothers victims and I hastened to squash it.

"What about you?" Felaan said softly and stirred the pot.

I cleared my thoughts and recalled my cover story. "It was awful. As soon as the first mortar shells fell my father pulled me out of bed and we tried to get out. His plan was to flee into the Elweyr forest and hide until the Clans left. What his plan was after that, I do not know. Whatever route we took we were cut off by the Guard and they wouldn't let us pass. They said that their orders were to keep the streets clear of civilians so that the military could move unobstructed. They kept us like that until the Clans broke through and started pillaging the city. A group of Azwaili found us and dad told us to run," I tried to squeeze out a tear and was surprised when I actually succeeded. "He didn't make it. But he bought enough time for mother and me to stay ahead of the Clansmen and get out of Utopeia. Our house was in the area that was destroyed by wild magic when the Hand of Glory was destroyed. He saved our lives. Mother died in the forest a few days later of an infection. I hadn't even noticed that she had been wounded in Utopeia. I would have died too if Mathias Varl had not stumbled upon me."

Zandor reached out and patted my knee in comfort and I couldn't help but smile at him. He wore his heart on his sleeve and seemed to be a caring guy.

"What about you, Zandor?"

He shook his head. "I wasn't in Utopeia. I was in a raiding party in the Azwaili lands. I missed it all, I wasn't there and everyone died. I could have made a difference!" He smacked his fist onto his knee.

I could only stare in surprise. He was not old enough to have been in the military back then. He couldn't be much older than I and I had just been a little girl.

"No, you couldn't," Valia whispered consolingly. "You are just one man, Zandor. The Traitor slew hundreds, maybe even thousands. I'm glad that you were not there, for if you were then you would be dead now. In vain," she added the last part firmly and Zandor nodded, still without looking up. I could tell that this was an argument they'd had many times before.

"You were in the Guard, Zandor?" I asked and accepted the bowl of soup Felaan handed me. "You don't look much older than I."

"I... I might have lied about my age to join the Guard." He look up and glared at each of us defensively. "I was an orphan, they would never have let me go to Utopeia unless I could be of use."

I held up my hand in surrender. "Hey, I'm not judging. If you hadn't then you would still have been on the Skylands when it Fell."

He nodded sagely and took a bite of the soup. We were all starving from the long day of hiking and we basically inhaled our soup and then had seconds. Almost as soon as Valia put her spoon down into her empty bowl she yawned wide enough to make her jaw crack.

"You guys all look beat," I said, "I'll take first watch. Who wants second?"

"Zandor," Felaan and Valia said at the same time.

The big man threw up his hands in surrender. "Every time," he grumbled.

"That is because you can fall asleep anywhere at a moments notice," Felaan pointed out.

"That's true," Zandor agreed. "It is a skill that have taken me many years to cultivate. Not my fault that you seem incapable of doing likewise."

The others rolled their eyes.

"Guys," Wylah said and waited for all of us to turn our attention to her. "We only have space in the tents for four." She looked at me. "Usually Valia and I sleep in one and Zandor and Felaan the other. But now we are five..."

"Say no more," I said. "I will sleep under the stars tonight since I didn't bring a tent for myself. I'll suffer for my own poor planning."

"But it will be freezing out here," Zandor protested. "I will sleep out here. I am larger than you and it will take longer to cool me down." Then he put on a slightly disgusted expression. "Though then you would have to sleep with Felaan..."

Felaan scowled and opened his mouth but I spoke before the two men could start a bickering session, as men were wont to do.

"I can sleep outside. I'm a Mage, remember? I can keep myself warm with magic, no big deal." I looked at each of them in turn. "Now that that is settled, I suggest that you all get some shut eye. I will wake Zandor in an hour and a half."

After a few moments they all agreed and we exchanged our good nights before they crawled into their beds. Finally alone I could relax. I pointedly turned my back to the fire so that I did not ruin my night vision and I resigned myself to boring guard duty.

It turned out to be just as boring as I had anticipated and each moment felt like a lifetime. When the moon had finally cleared the tree tops I poked my head into the tent that Zandor shared with Felaan and toed his foot. The big man jerked with a deep snort and then sat up to rub his eyes. Felaan grumbled when he was jostled but merely turned in his sleep roll and went back to sleep. A few moments after I had withdrawn from the tent Zandor stepped out and my eyes nearly bulged out of their sockets. His shirt was undone and what was revealed by the pale moonlight was glorious. He didn't have chiselled muscles beneath smooth skin like the men in romance novels did. Instead his waist was thick and his chest bulged with solid flesh and was covered in a rug of curly green hairs. His entire torso radiated strength and power and masculinity that had been hidden beneath his light chest plate. I just wanted to curl up against him, to feel enveloped and protected by his solid power. Cradled gently by this behemoth of a man. I shook myself out of it. I hadn't come here to find a man. I had come here to kill them all. I forced my eyes upward toward his face and was surprised by what I hadn't seen before. He had struck me as so youthful earlier in the day. Perhaps it was the incredibly sparse beard that he so valiantly attempted to grow but I had completely missed the solid square of his jaw or the powerful sweep of his cheek bones. If he just shaved that beard, and never wore a shirt, he would be the epitome of my dreams.

I forced myself to give him a firm nod before retreating to the other side of the fire where my own bedroll was. I set my corset to dissolve along with my boots and socks before pulling off the latter and sliding into my bedroll. At times like this I was glad that I had a choker like my mother had. No need to carry spare clothing on hikes like this. I erected my shield and altered it to keep heat inside before I cast a tiny spell to maintain a comfortable temperature within my little cocoon. I fell asleep within the span of a few breaths.


---

## Chapter 5 — CHAPTER FIVE Power unleashed {#chapter-5}

CHAPTER FIVE

Power unleashed

The next morning we ate our breakfast cold and quickly broke camp. We should arrive at our location today and since I was fairly certain where we would end up I felt confident enough to release my swarm.

"What is that?" Zandor asked in wonder when he saw me shake the little spell bugs out of their nest.

"They are spell-bugs that will travel wide to absorb magic for me. They will meet us at the ley-line crossing," I explained.

"That sounds like the spy-bugs that the Red Terror used," Felaan pointed out with narrowed eyes.

"It is a brilliant spell, and they served her well. I am not ashamed to recognise a great tool even if my enemy uses it," I shrugged. I meant it too. I used many Skylander spells every day. The booming Bizhjerai economy was built on Skylander spells.

"I suppose," Felaan acceded reluctantly.

"How do they work?" Zandor asked with interest.

We set off on our hike while I tried to explain to him. He seemed utterly lost when I described the individual spells that comprised the bug and I had to concede that it was a fairly advanced spell even for a skilled Mage. I had seen them created since I was a little girl and I found them no more peculiar than lighting the fire. I tried with an analogy.

"Alright, so a Mage draws power from their surrounding, right?"

"Right."

"And the density of the general magic field varies."

"Of course."

"So the power available to a Mage is dependent on the immediate surrounding magical field."

"Still with you."

"These bugs sort of serve as an extension of myself. I send them far afield into the magical field to gather power outside of my immediate range. They work sort of like a sieve. It scoops all the magic up and leaves the rest behind and then they return it all to me."

"A sieve," Zandor said thoughtfully before barking a laugh. "I get it! A sieve. Mary Laier, the Siever! Brilliant!"

I froze, keeping a smile on my lips. I cursed whatever stray thought had made me choose my fake last name. It sounded like he had said 'Mary Liar, Deceiver'. How ironic if that would bring someone to think about treachery and then connect the dots between my fake first name and actual name. If they figured out that I was actually Marissa Zorantis instead of Mary Laier then I would be dead before I knew that I had been made. I had chosen the name 'Mary' because it was close enough to Marissa that it would not be too unbelievable that I answer to Marissa by mistake.

I glared at Zandor and tried to convey to him that I was not fond of that new nickname.

But his infuriatingly cheerful grin just grew wider. "I can tell that you don't like your new nickname, Siever. I assume you wanted something more grandiose. Like Mary the Crafter. Mary the Slayer. But instead you are stuck with Mary the Siever," he said with a mighty guffaw.

I closed my eyes and took a slow breath to calm my racing heart. Maybe I could kill all of them and claim to be the only survivor of a bear attack.

No, that would be too suspicious. If I only killed Zandor and maybe one of the others it would not be as strange. Though how would I kill them without the others noticing? I gave up that stray thought and forged ahead.

"What was that?" Valia suddenly asked.

"What?" Zandor asked, raising his pole-ax.

The woods were rather thick here so I left my rifle on my back and drew mothers revolver.

"I thought I saw something in the underbrush," Valia hissed.

"Probably just a wild animal," Felaan announced softly.

"Maybe," Valia whispered. "But I thought I saw the glint of an eye, too tall for an animal."

"What if it's a bear?" Zandor said a little too loudly.

"Then your loud voice will just piss it off," Wylah snapped.

To his credit, Zandor did not seem to care about her harsh tone like I had expected. He held his weapon level and his eyes were alert.

I heard a very slight rustling of leaves to my right and trained my revolver in that direction, a deadly spell forming in my left hand.

Then we neither heard or saw anything for a long while.

"Whatever it was, it seems to be long gone," I finally said.

"Probably, yes," Felaan agreed. "Shall we move on?"

"Yeah, but keep your ears open and your eyes peeled," I told them.

We kept walking in silence, straining to see or hear anything. Hands hovered by our sidearms, ready to draw a bead on a moments notice.

But nothing disturbed us, no crazed bear burst out of the underbrush to maul us. We arrived by the ley-line crossing in the late afternoon.

"We're here," I announced and brought us to a halt. I squinted up at the sun. There were still an hour or two until sundown. "Make camp, I will get right on opening this ley line crossing. If I work fast and undisturbed I might finish before true dark and then we can use the resulting magic storm to ward our camp during the night."

"Sounds like a plan," Wylah agreed. "Felaan, you're with me with the tents. Valia, Zandor, fire duty."

They got to work like an oiled machine but I had already discarded them from my thoughts. I used my magesense to feel deep into the earth for the ley-line crossing. Mother had been able to just reach down with her power and rip it open with brute strength, but she had been a goddess among mortals, an order of magnitude stronger than I was. I had to play to my own strengths: preparation and efficiency. I drew my wand from my belt, essentially just a materialised magic rod enchanted with focusing spells, and I used it to remove the layer of topsoil above the crossing. Wads of grass, earth and roots ended up in a pile to the side. Then I used the wand to draw runes and diagrams with firm flicks of my wrist onto the exposed ground. Each rune charred into the soil, melting it into a brittle glass-like surface. Perfect to coat with materialised magic to use as a substrate to the enchantment. Even as I drew the complex sigils with my right hand I kept tossing materialising spells into the grooves at random, binding them to strands of my Soul. The spells latched onto the glass-like surface and picked a random direction where it started to coat the glass in materialised magic. They would keep going until they either met another of their kind in which case both of them would disperse or until they climbed out of the furrow with the exact same result. The multitude of materialising spells took their toll on my Soul and my power was depleting rapidly. I was covered in sweat despite the evening chill and I was breathing hard an hour after I started. I laid the finishing touches to the spellwork and focused on maintaining the reinforcement spells. If mom had done this she would have just used the glass-like channels instead of reinforcing them like I did. But she was in the habit of returning to Dun Traelak once the crossing was open to seed the focusing tower. She could afford to since Malkareth was more than happy to carry her back and forth. I did not have the time to hike all the way back to camp, and then return here scant days later to seed the tower. No, I needed lay the groundwork for all three spells: opening the crossing, building the focusing tower and finally aligning it with the receiving tower back at camp. All of it to finish in due time without any interaction from me. And that was way more complex and required far more power. Hence the reinforced channels. I watched my reinforcement spells trudge along slowly. Maybe that was not enough. If Valia had seen something other than a wild animal, perhaps this place needed defending. We couldn't afford stationing soldiers up here on guard duty. This place would have to survive on its own. My mind was spinning, but from fatigue or ideas I did not know. My reinforcement spells would carry on by themselves as long as I supplied magic to them so I sat cross-legged on the ground and started scribbling in my spell book for defencive spells. I had several spell components I could assemble into defencive features, but they all had their perks and drawbacks.

I could use Arclite turrets, a somewhat recent creation of Kendall's. They fired searing beams of heat to incinerate their targets, but they consumed vast amounts of power when firing and quite a bit while idling. He hadn't been able to solve the leakage of power when I left Dun Traelak. Almost nothing could stand against the Arclite spell, not even magic shields. To defend against them you had to alter your shield specifically for the Arclite beam, and since almost no one knew about it yet all opponents would be defenceless against it. Using Arclite turrets here would cause significant drain on the magic fount that I was going to open, even if there were no attack. That would mean less magic delivered to camp.

I could also use more traditional automatic rifle turrets. Their idle drain was insignificant, but creating ammunition was time consuming. They also had a much lower rate of fire than the Arclite and there where plenty of counters. Even well made mundane armour would stop a bullet or two and a simple magical barrier against physical impacts would take some time to hammer through. But they would only reduce the magic delivery when restocking ammunition, which was a huge perk. So in regards to magic power supply the rifle turrets were the clear winner.

It would all come down to how frequent eventual attacks would be and how powerful the attackers would be. And that was a complete unknown for the time being. It might even be nothing. There might be only us out here, in which case I was worrying over nothing. That was what made the decision for me. Rifle turrets it is. Six of them mounted at equal distances halfway up the focusing tower would ensure that three turrets could fire at any enemy approaching from any direction and I could mount the ammunition factories to the side of the tower. A flare spell that would fire if either a turret ran completely out of ammo or was destroyed would signal camp that something was afoot and we could send a war party up here. Better add gun jams to the list of things that would trigger a flare. Skylander weapons rarely jammed, unlike black powder weapons, since Skylander flash powder burned completely without fouling but mechanical jams still happened sometimes. Though Arclite weapons had no moving parts and therefore would never fail to fire as long as there were power...

I threw my hands up in defeat and decided to postpone the decision about defences. Perhaps a simple shield spell would do the trick. It would be a constant drain on the fount, but far less than Arclite turrets and would be ease itself to set up. I waited for another quarter of an hour for the reinforcement spells to finish before I summoned a light spell to inspect the results. There were a few areas that had not been completed yet due to the random nature of the spells and I spent the rest of the hour touching up the designs. When I was finished I was exhausted and I had drained my personal reserves of magic power almost entirely. I hadn't dared to use the power in my powerstone since that was my backup power for materialising my armour. I was ready to keel over and I rocked back onto my heels and rubbed my face with my hands.

"You okay?" Zandor rumbled next to me and I felt a steadying hand on my shoulder.

"Yeah, just exhausted," I mumbled wearily.

"Come on, let's sit you down by the fire. Supper is almost ready."

"That'd be nice," I mumbled and let the big man lead me to the fire.

He thrust a mug of hot liquid into my hands and I sipped it and promptly scalded my tongue with a curse.

"Take it easy, Mary," Valia admonished me, "that's straight out of the pot."

"I noticed," I grumped and took a more careful sip of the tea.

"So, how did the spellwork go?" Felaan asked while stirring the pot.

"The groundwork is set, I just need to recover enough power to cast the spell."

"How long will that take?" Wylah asked with some interest.

"Usually I can fully recover within a day or two, but I am hoping that the Siever swarm will return soon so that the spell can work through the night."

"Hah! You called it 'The Siever Swarm'," Zandor pointed out smugly, "I win!"

I stuck my tongue out at him and drank some more tea.

"Supper's done," Felaan announced and started ladling out whatever he had cooked into the bowls we'd brought.

I was somewhat disappointed to learn that it was porridge with beef jerky again. My stomach turned at the thought of eating. Magic exhaustion always made me queasy but I forced the porridge down nevertheless. Even though it sickened me I knew that it would help me recover quicker. My eyelids were drooping and I felt myself nodding off every now and again and I was just about to give up waiting for the Siever Swarm and go to sleep when Felaan sucked in air through his teeth. We all looked at him but he was staring out into the night.

"Something's out there," he whispered and drew his sidearm. "Wylah, Mary. You stay here, cover us with your rifles. Zandor, you're with me. Valia, stay in the shadows. Flank anything that approaches the camp."

I stood up, using Blood Magic to wipe away my fatigue. I would pay for that severely later but there were no way that I would be able to handle my rifle when I was on the verge of passing out. Wylah and I tucked our rifles into our shoulders and stood back to back so that we could cover the perimeter of the camp. Valia faded into the darkness to my right and Felaan vanished soundlessly into the underbrush. Zandor clomped after him, his large frame and military training making it impossible for him to travel stealthily. I realised their tactic. Felaan would range ahead and Zandor would march straight on, herding whatever might be out there into the waiting Felaan.

Neither Wylah nor I spoke, just slowly rotating to pan our eyes across the dark landscape. Eventually Felaan and Zandor returned and Valia joined us.

"Found tracks," Felaan announced. "Looks human, but larger. And barefoot."

"Was it spying on us?" Wylah asked curtly.

"Perhaps," Felaan shrugged. "But we know noting about its intentions. We should treat it as a threat until we know more. We should double up on guard duty tonight. Valia and I will take first watch, then Zandor and Wylah is on the graveyard shift. Valia and I will take the morning pass."

"What about..." I started but Felaan cut me off.

"You are exhausted by your spellwork, you must rest so that you can finish it. The sooner you finish the sooner we can head back to camp."

"Fair enough, but I used Blood Magic to wash away my fatigue. I won't be able to rest now anyway. I might as well stand watch until my Siever Swarm returns."

Felaan grunted. "Fair enough. How long will it last?"

I shrugged. "It is like sleeping a full night, but I will totally crash later on and be passed out for a long time. I should be good to go until tomorrow night."

"Alright, then you take all three watches tonight?"

I nodded. Might as well.

"Then Wylah takes first shift with you, then Zandor and then me. Valia rests the whole night and takes double shifts tomorrow night. Sounds good?"

Everyone nodded without complaint and Wylah and I assumed guard positions at either end of the camp. I could feel magic power slowly trickle into me to replenish my depleted stores but it was a pitiful trickle. I waited for a boring hour, keeping my eyes peeled for whatever Felaan had seen the tracks off, before I heard a familiar buzzing sound in the distance. A Sieve Bug flew up to me, depositing its little charge of magic power into my tired body. At first they arrived one by one, then in pairs and soon there were a steady stream of them. Each delivered only a little magic, but accumulated they almost restored me fully. Each one flared and spat its charge and I greedily sucked it up before they landed on the hive core and crawled inside before they dissolved. Only their crystal cores remained, ready to be shook out again to gather more magic for me. As soon as the last one returned I shook the hive core again and sent the entire swarm out again. We would be staying here for a couple of days since I would pass out tomorrow night at the latest and stay down for at least a full day and a night so I would use the time to gather more power. Now refreshed I walked over to Wylah.

"My Siever Swarm returned, I'm ready to start the spell that will open the ley line. You good for keeping watch alone?"

She nodded without looking at me, keeping her eyes trained into the darkness with her back to the dying fire.

I didn't disturb her further and walked away. I summoned a weak light spell, just bright enough to get by, and got to work. The sigils and diagrams I had created around the crossing were ready, but they had to be activated in the correct order. Slowly, meticulously, I channelled power into the whole structure until just before the ignition point and then I gently tipped them over one by one in the correct order. They ignited and ripped power out of me in a torrent, but compared to the power my mother had spent doing the same it was barely a trickle. I could sense the spell reaching deep into the earth, snaking around the crossing, reinforcing it and shoring it up before drilling deep. Instead of just ripping the crossing open and letting the raw magic spill through the earth the spell formed a rune channel toward the surface, strengthened by the fount itself. Magic coursed through the rune, corroding and dissolving the earth stuck inside. A gently glowing hole formed in the earth in front of my feet and magic spewed forth like a geyser. I drank it in with relief and instantly channelled it into the spells so that they could finish. The entire structure settled and locked into the crossing, becoming self-sustainable. The pull on my Soul waned and I fell to my knees in relief. It was done. The crossing was open. But my work was not complete. This area would saturate with magic and the general magic field would be denser, but the power would drift away on the astral winds. I needed to focus it and project it toward camp. So that was what I started to do. Many of the spells I required I had already drawn, but I needed to expand those a lot. I used my wand to blaze more lines into the earth and I ended up removing even more of the topsoil to expose soft earth below. The reinforcement spells that I cast I bound directly to the fount instead of to myself and I could keep working without the tremendous strain on my own energies. In Dun Traelak the tower had been built out of rock, primarily because of two reasons. First and foremost: Grasax had lacked the knowledge to materialise magic. The second was, and I am certain that even Grasax hadn't considered it, doing so had reduced the power investment in building the tower. I had seen in mothers memories how the powerful liche had melted rock and moulded it with his power into the desired shape. I could not do that even if I had had the raw materials available, so I would have to resort to materialised magic bricks. It would delay the delivery to camp by a significant margin, but that could not be helped. I finished sketching the spells that would construct the tower. I was quite proud of those spells. Mother had used a spell that grew the tower from the top, adding each layer below the first and finishing with the foundation. It had been clever since the spell could self-adjust the tower foundation depending on how it settled into the ground. It also required an unfathomable amount of power to lift the tower to make space for the next layer.

My spell did it the other way around. A ring of materialisation spells started at the bottom and climbed on top of the area they had just created, shrinking and finishing with the focusing lens to create the artificial ley-line. It demanded more math up front, unlike mothers spell that could just be thrown down and left to its own devices. I had even devised a spell core that had all the necessary spells inscribed in it that would activate when the tower was finished, soaring up through the interior to burn the spells into the surface and bind them to the fount. My mothers had been required to return to the finished tower to inscribe the spells themselves. My tower was self-constructing. My mothers would have been so proud of me. Wherever they had gone after death, if they were watching me, I knew that they would be smiling. I heard movement in the underbrush to the side and I was instantly on alert, my revolver in my hand and trained in the direction the sound had come from. I stalked closer, finger on the trigger. A deer burst out of the underbrush and bolted and I lowered my revolver with a relieved chuckle. Just some wildlife, nothing to be scared of. Probably what Felaan and Valia had noticed earlier. But that did not explain the humanoid footprint Felaan had seen. I holstered my gun and returned to the blazing cauldron of magic. In the initial phases the spells only consumed about half of what was available from the fount and I diverted the rest into a powerful shield surrounding the camp. It was designed to repel any physical attacks but it would be possible to cross the barrier if you walked slowly. I started building a small manufactorium spell for ammunition before I set up spells to assemble four automatic rifle turrets. I'd have to place them on the ground until the tower was tall enough to mount it all to the sides. Now all I could do for a little while was to wait, and do the math for how to align the focusing lens toward camp. But first, nature calls. I walked into the woods a bit before I undid my belt and pushed my trousers down to my ankles and squatting. I didn't like tending to my needs like this but I had no choice. I much preferred the plumbing of Dun Traelak that mother had built. Struck by sudden home-sickness I sighed wistfully while I relieved myself. I suddenly wanted to joke around with Kendall and Alissa and play with the two young orphans that Uncle Kenny and his husband had adopted after the war. They were adorable, a boy and a girl. The boy had originally been born Azwaili but was now Bizhjerai and the girl had been born Bizhjerai. Both had lost their parents in the war, the boys' in a raid on a caravan by the Skylanders that mother and my brother had arrived too late for and the girl had lost hers in Utopeia. They had reminded me of myself at that age, lost and forlorn and adopted by noble and well-meaning parents. They had grown up so fast. They were both almost twelve years old by now. The same age I had been when my father died. I shook myself out of my reverie. I would see them again. Them and my uncles and Alissa. I just had to finish what my mothers started. I wiped myself as clean as was possible with a flex of magic, which admittedly was way cleaner than what was possible without magic, and pulled my trousers back up. I sat down next to my blazing spells, pulled out my spellbook and the special pen that mother had made for mom, the one with the internal ink container that ensured an even distribution of ink, and started doing the math. By midnight I had made all of the necessary calculations and I was fairly certain that I had gotten it right. It would be fine even if I was slightly off since the spell could handle a small measure of self-correction. Kendall had drilled that into me repeatedly. I could hear his voice clear as day even so many miles away from him.

"A mechanical construction must be precise. It can tolerate no deviations and if the parts are not aligned properly, the whole mechanism might jam. It might even tear itself apart. Magical constructions does not necessarily follow the same rules. Give your constructions a governing spell complex enough for self-adjustments. Make your creations almost sentient and they will serve you far longer than a steam engine."

I had taken his advice to heart. Some might call my creations over-engineered. I just called them thorough. If I built something that was made to last, I supplied it with enough spellwork to adjust and repair. Of course, a fatal damage would be too much, but my creations did not wear out. They maintained themselves. If a cog snapped, a materialisation spell was there to fix it. My most advanced creations contained all of the spells required to build them so even if they broke down completely they would just dissolve into magical power and then recreate themselves. Like my bike. Unless it was completely obliterated by a powerful magical attack it would always repair itself. I spent most of the rest of the night inscribing the alignment spells into the formulae and I was wrapping them up when dawn broke and my companions crawled out of their tents. I smothered a yawn, though I didn't feel more tired than I would after a normal day. I could manage a while yet.

"Good morning," I greeted the others when I rejoined them at the fire. Zandor was cooking breakfast, porridge.

"Good morning," he responded cheerfully. "I see you have been busy in the night."

"Yeah. Most of the spellwork is complete. Now we just have to wait for the tower to construct itself about halfway so that I can mount defencive turrets on the sides. Then we can return to camp."

"How long will that take?" Felaan asked softly.

I frowned, running some mental calculations. "A full day or two given the current flow of power. Why, what's wrong?" I asked when his dark face frowned.

"We might not have provisions for that long. We won't starve to death before we are back in the camp but I would rather not go hungry."

"Yeah, Zandor becomes very cranky when he is hungry," Wylah interjected flatly.

"I'm cranky?" Zandor protested and aimed a porridge covered spoon at his friend. "I'm not the one that threatened to twist my head around so that I could watch the birds while humping the ground!"

"Wylah gets hangry," Valia confided in me in a whisper and I could not help but smile at their antics. These youths were growing on me. Which was incredibly stupid of me since I was planning on betraying all of them.

Despite myself, I chuckled at the reserved woman. "We wouldn't want that now, would we?" I asked Valia with a smirk.

"So," Felaan said with a raised voice to cut through the chatter. "If we are to remain here for a few more days then I would like to go hunting."

"I actually saw a deer earlier," I said.

"Great, show me the tracks after breakfast and I will stalk it."

I nodded and accepted a bowl of breakfast.

"While Felaan is hunting, would you mind trying to teach me magic?" Zandor asked timidly.

I glanced at the budding focusing tower to gauge if I needed to intervene. "Sure, but don't expect immediate results. The first spell is difficult."

"That's fine. I'm just excited to try!" He said and it was clear that he was having issues with curbing his enthusiasm.

"Felaan, what about those humanoid tracks you found yesterday? Are you certain that it is wise to go out there alone?" Wylah asked her dark friend.

"I am well armed, and I will be vigilant. Since whoever, or whatever, left that footprint was barefoot I suppose that they are not well armed or armoured. I doubt they'd have rediscovered firearms before the art of cobbling."

"That makes sense," I agreed. "But what about bows?"

"They might be able to wound me, but my armour should keep my vital organs intact. I will try to not bleed out until I can return here for triage."

His face was impassive even as he spoke about his own hypothetical harm. He somehow reminded me of mother who had had the same casual attitude toward her own mortality.

"Wait, take this," I said and rummaged around my pack. I withdrew a crystal about the size of my thumbnail. "If you get hurt then you can use this to cauterise the wound."

He accepted it and rolled it across his palm with his thumb. "How does it work?"

"See the glyph on the flattened top there?"

I waited for him to nod. "Press your thumb firmly against it and it will trigger the spell. The magical power inside the crystal will power the spell, it will only draw a small amount from you to trigger. It will envelop your hand in a fire spell that you control with your fingers. Like this." I cast the very same spell around my own fist and extended my index finger. Then I slowly raised my thumb and a small jet of flame erupted out of my index finger. The jet grew in length and intensity the more I straightened my thumb. I lowered my thumb and shook my hand to dissipate the spell. "It will only last for a few moments, so you have to be quick. But it should let you deal with any bleeding, in a rudimentary way."

Felaan quirked his eyebrow, clearly impressed. "Thank you."

Zandor stared at my hand as if it was still on fire. "Did you make that stone yourself?"

"Sure did. It is not that difficult."

"That stone would have been worth a fortune in the Undercity, at least that is what I understood from my fellow Guardsmen."

"Yes, but the Skylands was a backwards place. While we did have a lot of magical knowledge, there were very little innovation or development. We just used magic like it had always been used. Ever since the fall magic have developed exponentially. Also the Skylands had outgrown the Great Geyser, there simply where not enough magic to waste on such frivolities."

"How did you learn all of this if you were trained by Varl? Wouldn't he have been as stuck in the old ways as any Skylander Mage?"

Shit. How do I answer that? I tried to go for a nonchalant shrug. "We travelled a bunch. The defeat at Utopeia changed Mathias. It shattered his confidence in the Skylander ways. We had refused to adapt, and were crushed for it. He became obsessed with developing his magic so we travelled a lot to study. We even visited Dun Traelak once. We learned a lot there."

"You went to Dun Traelak? But they are the enemy!" Zandor gasped.

I held up an admonishing finger. "They were the enemy, Zandor. They won the war. It is over. They might be the enemy once again, but they are currently not hostile. Neither are we. They crushed us once before, defeated us in our own game. If we want to avoid that next time, we must learn from them. Only by understanding our enemy can we hope to win."

"That makes no sense," Zandor grumped.

"In Dun Traelak I perused the liche's old library. In there I found a truly ancient tome, called 'The Art of War' by an ancient philosopher by the name of Sun-Tzu. In it he wrote, and I quote." I looked each of my companions in the eyes to make sure that they were listening. "If you know the enemy and know yourself, you need not fear the result of a hundred battles. If you know yourself but not the enemy, for every victory gained you will also suffer a defeat. If you know neither the enemy nor yourself, you will succumb in every battle'."

I let the silence drag for a bit. "In the war with the Clans, we did not know our enemy. We thought that we knew ourselves, but we truly only knew what we had once been. We went to war in a land we did not know, against a people we did not know. Without knowing ourselves within that context. And so we lost. If we are to have any hope in any future war, we must learn to know ourselves and our enemy."

"You've really thought this through, haven't you, Mary?" Wylah finally said, respect clear in her clipped words.

"The enemy killed my birth parents, and then my adopted parents. I have thought this through every day since."

I froze. I had said too much. I had never mentioned any adopted parents. I had told them that Mathias Varl had found me soon after my parents were killed at Utopeia. I waited with bated breath, hoping that they would not find the inconsistency in my tale. To my great relief they seemed to be pondering the old quote. I would have to choose my words more carefully from here on out. Felaan finally nodded and put his spoon down into his empty bowl. Wordlessly Wylah extended her hand to take it from him.

"Wylah, your rifle, please. For hunting," Felaan asked his shorter friend.

"Please, use mine," I interjected. "I will be working on the tower and wont need it. Wylah might if that barefoot invader shows up again."

I summoned my rifle into my hand on strands of magic and quickly ejected the magazine and racked the bolt. I snatched the ejected bullet with magic and let it hover next to me. "Let me just exchange these special shots and slide in normal kinetic rounds," I muttered and dug through my pack for my extra magazine. I slid it into the rifle and tossed it over the fire to Felaan. He caught it smoothly and racked the bolt, chambering a round before shouldering the weapon to look down the sights.

"Well made," he commented before slinging it across his shoulder.

It was strange to see my mothers favourite weapon on the back of a Skylander, but it looked good on him.

"Thank you. I made it myself as a replica of the weapon Kendall built for the Traitor," I lied. It was in fact the very same weapon. "It served her incredibly well for many years, it must have been a good design. So I chose to adopt it."

"Like with your revolver," Zandor observed.

"You've drawn a lot of inspiration from the Traitor," Valia observed.

"She was a powerful woman," I countered. "She destroyed Utopeia. She destroyed the Skylands. She utterly vanquished her enemies, she must have done something right."

"Can't argue with that," Zandor chuckled. "As long as you stand on the right side in any coming war."

Oh, believe me I will. It just so happens that the right side was whoever stood in opposition of the Skylanders.

Wylah gathered the bowls and took them to rinse in the stream and I led Felaan to where I had startled the deer earlier and showed him the tracks. Without a word he vanished into the forest in search for prey. I checked on my spells and saw that they were all coming along nicely so I returned to the fire to attempt to teach the rookies how to use magic. I yawned. The effects of my Blood Magic were starting to fade and I had a feeling that it was going to be a long day.

I was proven correct when I could barely keep my eyes open mid-afternoon. I had taught Zandor and the girls enough that they could keep practising on their own. The first step was to try to flex your 'extra limb' which mostly required you to find it. It was essentially trial and error, and you did not truly know when you had succeeded. You just sort of had a feeling, but without knowing how it was supposed to feel it was difficult to pinpoint. But I left them to it and crashed into Valia's bedroll. She had insisted that I not exert myself with a magical heating cocoon and rested properly and comfortably so that I could get back to work. Even though everyone appeared to enjoy our little camping trip they were all eager to return to camp and help rebuilding a new Utopeia. I didn't even know what the settlement was called, but I assumed that I would learn that at that introduction that Micah had promised to hold when enough newcomers had arrived. Or maybe he would hold it when I was out here and I would miss it. Either way, the name was irrelevant. I would make sure that it never had a chance to enter the annals of history. With that uplifting thought, I passed out.


---

## Chapter 6 — CHAPTER SIX Apparition {#chapter-6}

CHAPTER SIX

Apparition

I woke up slowly, groaning. My head was splitting, and my body ached all over. The 'hangover' from using Blood Magic. I spent a moment cursing mother. She had never had these kinds of issues with using Blood Magic. Her body just consumed her physical resources and was done with it. I remembered that after healing the gruesome wounds she had suffered in her duel with the liche, Grasax, she had lamented the loss of all of her body fat and much of her muscle mass. The loss of the mass of her breasts especially had devastated her since she believed them to be her most attractive features. Even though mine were as big as hers, at least proportionally since I was substantially shorter than her, my use of Blood Magic didn't deplete me in the same way that hers had. I would much rather have had her symptoms. At least hers gave her an excuse to pork out. I just felt like shit, and I couldn't even console myself with stuffing my face with delicious food. I rubbed my temples, hoping that my fingers would soothe the splitting ache. They didn't. I grumbled under my breath and slowly, laboriously, climbed out of the tent. The sudden sunlight stabbed straight into my brain and I recoiled from it.

"If I didn't know better then I would guess that you had a wild night yesterday," Zandor quipped.

"Ugh. How long was I out?"

"One night and most of a day. I would guess we have about three hours until nightfall," Zandor said after a squint at the sun.

"That was less time than I expected."

"You look like shit," Zandor said with a wide smile.

"Uh, thanks?" I responded sarcastically and tried to not throw up.

"What I mean is, have some water. You look like you need it," he said apologetically and offered me a skin.

I took a sip and almost vomited all over myself. But I swallowed it back down and forced myself to drink deeply. I needed it, even though my body rebelled at the mere thought of ingesting anything.

"Want something to eat?" Zandor asked helpfully and I had to choke back bile.

"I don't think I'm ready for that," I admitted. "Did Felaan find that deer? Is he okay?"

"Yeah, he's fine. He didn't find the deer though he did catch a few rabbits," Zandor responded, then his face fell. "Though we ate them all. Sorry. He went out to check his snares a little while ago, perhaps he can bring back some meat for you."

"I think I'll pass for now. I don't think I can stomach anything solid right now." I drew a deep breath of crisp air. "I should check on the spells."

Before he could respond I pushed to my wobbly feet and walked over to the spells. They were coming along nicely and the focusing tower was more than twice my height, which wasn't saying much. But judging by the rate of growth it would be about halfway finished by tomorrow morning. That would allow me to mount the defencive guns and we could be on our way. I just hoped that my math hadn't been way off and I would need to return here to realign the focusing lens. That would be a proper pain in the arse. Maybe I could do something about that though. A self-correcting focusing lens would be useful indeed. Perhaps if it could scan the horizon for a receiving lens and align with it... But that would require a fully functioning receiver, and that I did not have. It was a good idea though, something that I should incorporate into the next iteration of the focusing tower spell. So either we stayed here and waited for the focusing tower to be finished or we returned to camp to wait for it and hope that I had gotten it right. Though if we waited here and my math was on point then the tower might drench the camp in raw magic with potentially lethal consequences. Shit, I hadn't thought this through properly. We had to return back to camp so that I could gather up the power spilling out of the artificial ley line and channel it into something useful. It had taken the tower a little less than two days to grow to this size, and it had taken us a little less than two days to hike up here. The base of the tower was the slowest to build since it was larger and thicker than the top since it had to support the weight of the tower. But the focusing lens was a complex piece of magic machinery and would take at least a day to materialise judging by the flow of power of this particular fount. That would give us something between three and four days until I had to be back at camp. So we would have to break camp here tomorrow at the latest. Tomorrow the tower itself should be about halfway finished so that I could mount the defencive turrets and the ammunition manufactorium in the morning and then we could be off. I returned to Zandor by the campfire and sat down heavily.

"How did magic practice go?" I finally asked, anything to distract myself from the throbbing pain in my temples.

"I don't really know. I think I felt something a few times, but I am not certain," he responded hesitantly.

"Just keep going, you'll get the hang of it eventually. Ugh, I feel dirty and clammy all over. Is the stream deep enough to bathe in?"

"Not even close. But if you bring one of the bowls you can use it to scoop water to scrub yourself with. Gotta warn you though, it is icy cold."

"Anything is better than feeling this dirty. Maybe the chill will clear my head," I sighed and stood up.

The stream was indeed shallow and farther from the camp than I had anticipated. It had taken me nearly a quarter of an hour to get there. At least that would give me some privacy. I stripped naked and touched the powerstone on my choker to set my clothing to dissolve so that I could materialise a clean set after I bathed. Only my weapons belt and weapons would remain. Felaan still had my rifle so my only armaments were mothers revolver, her sword and the dagger she had used to transform herself into a liche. They were my second dearest possessions, superseded only by the memory core that contained her essence. I had left that safely in Dun Traelak. I let my mind drift while I scooped icy cold water over my head and scrubbed at my skin as best I could. A sudden sound snapped me out of my thoughts and I replayed my recent sensory inputs to figure out what had startled me. Then I heard it again. It sounded suspiciously like a footstep from a large man. I smirked and scooped another bowl of water over my shoulder and made sure that the water trickled down across and between my breasts.

"Didn't your mother teach you not to spy on bathing women, Zandor?" I asked coyly without looking.

I had expected a yelp and the rustle of a sudden retreat but I heard nothing.

"Zandor?" I asked and turned my head to search for him. No one else would have that heavy footfalls. What I saw was something that I really hadn't expected. Only a handful of yards away stood a creature. It looked human, except it had elongated arms and dull gray skin. Its forehead was broad and tapered into a mass of gnarled spines instead of hair and its chin were narrow beneath a large maw filled with sharp fangs. Its nose were squashed and flat and wide with two slits for nostrils. But the most startling feature of it was the incredibly large eyes that glowed with an inner violet light. It was my mothers eyes.

I was stunned to my core. It had been more than a decade since I had seen her eyes and I had been certain that I would never see them again in the flesh. Yet here she stood, deformed and grotesque. The creature, mother, tilted her head and yowled curiously at me.

"Mother?" I gasped and felt my eyes well with tears.

It yipped in response.

"Mother!" I cried out and ran toward her, arms open for an embrace. I was suddenly a little girl again, running toward her mothers comforting arms with the certainty that all would be right in the world once those strong arms closed around my shoulders. Mother hunched into a defencive stance, lips drawing back to show fangs in a threatening mother. I skid to a halt.

"Mother?" I asked uncertainly. Why was she so hostile? Didn't she recognise me? "It's me, Marissa. Your daughter," I tried to remind her.

It growled and raised one of its arms. For the first time I noticed the very long club. A simple branch with a large stone tied to the end with what looked like dried sinew. Another growl sounded from the underbrush of the forest and I saw the twin pinpricks of another set of shining violet eyes. Terrible grief and disappointment crashed into me, almost taking my to my knees. It wasn't my mother. It just had the same kind of glowing eyes. Another creature, almost identical except for the shapeless breasts adorning its torso, stepped into the open space before the stream. The first one rattled its club threateningly and I took an involuntary step back. I blinked a tear out of my eye. Mother was dead. Both of them were, their Souls scattered on the astral winds. These creatures just had her eyes. I called magic into my hands, preparing spells of defence and destruction. The creature was standing between me and my weapons, but I didn't need wargear to defeat these two savages. I had forged myself into a magical engine of destruction. Bolstered by its ally the first creature decided to attack. With a roar it charged and swung, tightly followed by its ally. I ducked the wild swing of the club and unleashed my spells. A tongue of flame erupted out of my right hand to envelop the head of the first one even as a shield sprang out of my left to absorb the body slam of the second. I grinned savagely as the first creature was about to die. But to my great shock its eyes flared brightly and my flames fluttered out, the magic fuelling them sucked into the creature. The others eyes also flared and it ploughed straight through my shield. Something cracked inside me and I was flung into the shallow stream. I rolled violently through the water and silt, inhaling mud and water before coming to a stop. Instinctively I rolled to the side, screaming in pain when my cracked ribs ground together, but it had been worth it. The giant club smacked into the mud beside me with a resounding thunk. I used Blood Magic to numb the pain and leaped to my feet, just in time to lean out of the way of a punch from the female. These assholes were fast, and strong, despite their wiry frames. And they seemed immune to magic, which meant that I was just a petite, unarmed and very naked girl. But my tumble through the stream had sent me a fair way downstream and the creatures had followed. I threw myself in a headlong dive beneath another swing of the club and took a rolling landing to end up on my feet again. I ignored the damage my numb and broken ribs could cause from such a manoeuvre. At least all of my bleeding would be internal. That's were the blood was supposed to be anyway. I used Blood Magic to pour power into my legs, just like mother had done in her mad dash toward Dun Traelak when she and mom was about to infiltrate that cursed place, and my legs powered me across the mud at breakneck pace. I twisted my hole body and dug my heels into the sand, sliding across it and scooping my revolver up as I slid past it. I twisted around, still sliding, and saw the creatures hot on my heels. I cocked the hammer with my thumb and raised the weapon, staring down the crystal sights. They lined up right between the eyes that were so hauntingly similar to my mothers. My finger caught on the hair trigger.

It's not her! I told myself harshly. It raised its club to smash me into a bloody smear. I just couldn't do it. What if it really was her?

Cold clarity filled me. If it was her, I knew what she would say. Do it. Kill me, little one. I would much rather be dead than ever harm you, Marissa.

I squeezed the trigger.

My revolver barked and purple flames shot out the muzzle. It kicked back into my hand with reassuring finality.

The back of the creatures head burst open, showering the female in blood, brains and skull fragments. The violet eyes in its eyes winked out instantly and it tumbled bonelessly into the mud, sliding across the wet earth to stop twitching by my feet. The female howled and leaped over its companion and I couldn't get the revolver up in time. It backhanded my weapon hand and splitting pain twisted up my arm. The bones in my hand broke and the gun fell from limp fingers. I dropped to the ground as if my bones had turned to jelly and the female stumbled over me with her momentum. I managed to get to my feet, cradling my shattered arm to my chest, and I stumbled toward the sole remaining weapon. Grasax' liche dagger. I numbed the pain in my right arm and snatched up the dagger in my left. I spun around to female who was staring at me warily. Every close encounter I'd had with her kind had ended in me breaking a bone. There was no way I could survive getting close enough to use my dagger. But my revolver were somewhere in the stream and out of sight. My magic couldn't touch these creatures. I only had the dagger. I gritted my teeth in a feral smile.

"Forgive me, mothers. It appears that I can't finish the mission you started. I have reached the end of my journey. At least I tried." I drew a deep breath and hefted the dagger. "I'll see you soon, and brother too. I love you."

I dug my toes into the silt and prepared to take this female into the afterlife with me. Her Soul would carry mine to whatever place the dead ended up. I would ride her shoulders to my mothers and brother. To my father and birth mother. To everyone I had lost. We would feast and fight and build until Uncle Kenny and Alissa and Marie joined us. The female leaned into her attack and her long legs coiled to power her into a headlong charge. There was no way I would be able to get out of the way. She would crash into me, crush me to a pulp. But not without my dagger ending up in her heart. Neither of us would leave this stream alive.

And then I had a stroke of genius. I summoned my power and wrapped it around the dagger before I used my power to fling it at her. The dagger flew and as I had expected the magic propelling the dagger was sucked into the female with a flash of her eyes. But the dagger kept going, straight into her throat. Her shining violet eyes widened and her hands scratched at the dagger. But it was stuck deep into her jugular and it must have opened her trachea for a gush of blood escaped her lips when she mewled pitifully. She died quickly, the glow in her eyes dimming almost completely when she fell to her knees. Then they went dark amethyst and she faceplanted into the stream.

I stood there, naked and shivering, for a long moment. Then I giggled before I burst into gut-wrenching laughter.

Not today, mothers. You will have to wait a little longer before we meet again. Tell brother that I love him.

My laughter was cut short by a familiar howl from within the woods. I cast about, searching for my revolver, but I could not find it. I didn't know what to do. I couldn't kill another one of these bastards without a weapon. My eyes locked on a largish rock and I wrested it from the ground with magic just as another of the creatures appeared out of the woods. My heart sank. It was carrying a spear. I was thoroughly fucked, and not in the good way. In the worst imaginable way. While I did enjoy getting impaled by the metaphorical spear of a male every now and again, this was not such a joyous occurrence. This one was going to hurt, a lot, and end in death.

"You wanna stick it in me, big boy? Well come over here and show me what you've got!" I taunted him nonsensically.

Instead of charging me he simply hoisted his spear above his shoulder and hurled it.

It was the exact opposite of what I had expected. I have no excuses. I wanted to blame my broken ribs, my shattered hand or the emotional trauma of having believed that my beloved mother had returned from the dead in the form of a misshapen beast, but that would just be excuses. I could have raised a shield to deflect the spear. I could have leaped to the side. I could have done any one of a number of actions but instead I just stood there, gaping at the rapidly approaching spear like an idiot. It impaled me through the hip and burst out of my back. There were no pain. I am sure there was, a lot of it, but I did not feel it at the time. My body just went numb and I fell to my knees. That was it. The end of my life right there.

Well, I guess I was wrong earlier. This was the time for me to join my dead family. End of the line.

The creature slowly stalked closer, keeping a wary eye on my gasping form. Eventually it came within reach and ripped the spear out of my body with a savage jerk. I grunted and clutched my healthy left hand over the wound, pinching it shut with primal instincts. Blood trickled out of my the exit wound in my back, covering my butt and thigh with disgusting stickiness. Still there were no pain, just a cold certainty that I would die. A decade of training to become the ultimate weapon. An agent of destruction that would bring low the remnants of the Skylands. To finish what my mothers had started. A decade of gruelling training, only to die naked and powerless to a barely sentient savage. I gritted my teeth and growled deep from my chest. A pitiful sound of defiance, the angry mewling of a tiny woman that thought herself to be powerful. The savage raised its spear for the death stroke, and I welcomed it. I was not worthy of being my mothers daughter. They had been powerful. They had been beautiful. They had been wise. I was nothing compared to them. I wasn't even fit to cower in their shadow. My first real fight and I had been mercilessly defeated. I raised my head and stared my death defiantly in the eyes. I would not die cowering like a Skylander. Despite my failings I was still Bizhjerai. I would die standing, proudly. I channelled power into my Bizhjerai rune and felt the familiar strength course through me. I shakily pushed to my feet and my left leg nearly buckled when my torn abdomen suddenly screamed in protest. I defiantly shifted my weight to my right leg.

"C'mon, you bastard. Do it. Kill me," I prompted the savage with a maniacal smile.

"Do it!" I screamed.

It stared at me with its head tilted curiously and then seemed to shrug and prepare to lunge.

"Get away from my daughter, motherfucker!"

A gunshot deafened me and the savage collapsed, brains violently evacuated from its skull.
