I stared at the falling corpse, dumbfounded. It struck the wet earth with a thud of finality and the strength fled my body. I collapsed onto my side, unable to remain standing.

"It's okay, baby girl," a familiar voice that I had not heard for over a decade soothed me. "Mommy's here."

Cold, gentle fingers turned me onto my back and I was shocked to my core.

My mother, Kyra Zorantis, the mighty Bás Marbhadair, knelt beside me and regarded me with worry writ plainly across her beautiful face. She looked exactly like she had all those years ago when she had left on top of my dragon brother. The time when she had lied to me and told me that she would return. I had known that it was a lie even back then. I had seen it in her eyes. My eyes devoured the sight of her. The scar that bisected her eyebrow and continued beneath her eye. Her shining violet eyes, glowing from the intense power contained by her flesh. The nasty burn scar on the side of her head and how she defiantly combed her violet hair away from it as if she was proud of the scars. Her left arm missing, replaced by the skeletal prosthesis Uncle Kenny had built for her. The glowing runes sealing the scar on her chest above her breasts, eminently encased in the crystal corset she favoured.

"Mommy?" I whimpered.

"It'll be alright, little one. The bad guys are gone. I'm here now," she soothed, cold fingers probing my wounds.

"It hurts so bad, mommy!"

"I know, Marissa, love. You did great, I am proud of you. Just relax now and let mother take care of you."

I knew then that everything would be alright. Mother was here. All was well. I relaxed into her arms and took comfort in her touch even though it was so terribly cold. I gazed up at her. Something was off about her, and I suddenly realised why. She was slightly transparent, and the colours were slightly off. It was as if she was made up of magic and illusion.

"You're an Archon," I whispered dreamily and reached up to stroke her face with my fingers. It did not feel like flesh, not by a long shot, but she was here in spirit. A decade of grief burst through the walls I had built around it when I finally touched her again. "I've missed you so much, mommy," I wailed into her shoulder.

"I've missed you too, little one. More than I could ever put into words."

Shining tears dripped onto me, sizzling on my skin without leaving a mark. "This is going to hurt, tuck your tongue in, make sure you can't bite it."

I did as I was instructed and nodded.

"Brace yourself," Kyra whispered and then unleashed her magic.

Fire burned through my wound, cauterising it and stopping the bleeding instantly.

"Listen carefully now, my baby. I've done what I can for your wounds, but I need you to apply Blood Magic to heal yourself. Do you understand?"

"Yes, mommy. I've missed you. Don't leave me again!"

Mother sighed. "I don't think I have a choice. Whatever you did to bring me back... It can't last. It takes a tremendous amount of power to sustain an Archon. I don't even know how I am still alive. The last thing I remember was seeing the Skylands fall and forming the memory core for you. Then nothing, until I appeared here."

"I don't want you do go again," I mewled when I could finally gasp in enough air to speak.

"I know, and I don't want to go. But wherever I go, I am watching over you. Never forget that, little one."

"I love you mommy!"

Cold and insubstantial lips pressed hard against my forehead. "I love you too. More than anything in this world. Now and forever. But you have to be strong, Marissa. Be strong for you mothers."

"I will..." I mumbled but darkness was claiming me. I fought it with everything I had left, but I wasn't strong enough. My mother was back, and I was about to pass out. I struggled to spend as much time as I could with her, but in the end my weak flesh betrayed me and reality slipped away from me.


---

## Chapter 7 — CHAPTER SEVEN Return to camp {#chapter-7}

CHAPTER SEVEN

Return to camp

I woke up with a groan.

"She's awake!" That was Zandor's voice and I heard scrambling of boots on bare ground.

Memories surged into my mind. "Mother!" I gasped and opened my eyes widely, searching for her.

"Sorry to disappoint you," Zandor said with a happy smile. "I'm glad that you're back with us."

I searched for mother, but she was not there. Had I dreamed her?

"How did I get back to camp?" I asked instead.

"We heard the gunshots, figured you were in danger. When we arrived you were laying there, naked and wounded. What were those creatures you fought?"

"I don't know," I answered and accepted the water skin that Valia silently offered me. I took a sip and coughed it all out when swallowing sent a sting of pain down my ribs.

"Their tracks were all over the place," Felaan told me softly. "We should stay away from there, remain here by camp where we have the power of the fount to protect us."

"They're resistant to magic. Absorbed as if it was water," I managed to get out between painful coughs.

"Shit!" Zandor swore. "We gotta get out of here, return to camp."

"Relax," I said. "They seemed primitive, barely sentient. They didn't have ranged weapons, and they died to gunshots just fine. The automatic gun turrets should keep them at bay." I glanced at the focusing tower. "As long as I mount them high enough before more of them arrive, that is."

"You're wounded," Wylah told me curtly.

"I'm fine," I snapped a little too harshly. "Just a couple of broken ribs and a shattered hand. I can fix that." I turned my gaze to Felaan. "Did you see any human tracks at the stream?"

"Yes, yours."

"No one else's? None with boots? High heeled boots?"

I distinctly remember seeing mother wearing her favourite boots with the absurdly high heels.

"No, just your booted footprints before you removed yours. Why do you ask?"

"I must have dreamed it," I muttered. "It doesn't matter."

"Tell us what happened," Valia asked me softly.

I gave them as detailed explanation as possible, but I of course left out everything about mother. Instead I told them that I had shot the last savage as well. Then I realised that my revolver had been knocked into the stream.

"It's all a little hazy," I lied, "must have received a blow to the head. Did you find my revolver?"

"Yes, it was lying right next to you," Felaan answered and gestured at the gun that rested by the fire. So mother had been real! She must have manifested and used her old gun to shoot that last savage dead. There could be no other explanation. A wide smile split my lips. Mother was still alive, somehow. I would figure this out and meet her again.

"What's so funny, Siever?" Zandor asked with a confused frown.

"Just glad to be alive, is all," I beamed at him. "When that first creature ate my spell, I was certain that I would die," I told them truthfully. "I was unarmed, unarmoured and without magic."

"Yet you managed to survive. Impressive," Felaan admitted.

"What's the plan? You've been down for almost a full day, focusing tower's coming along nicely," Wylah stated without emotion.

"We should head back to camp. First I will need to splint my hand and my ribs, shouldn't take more than an hour or two. Then I will mount the turrets onto the tower and we can be off. I'd rather not hang out here with potential hostiles."

"Agreed," Zandor said with emphasis. "What can we do to help?"

"Break camp and make sure that we have supplies for the home trip," I responded immediately. I glanced at the sun, it was rather early in the day. "I think that I can syphon enough power from the focusing tower to build us all a mount to get back to camp faster, but that will also take a while. Do you think we are safe here for a few more hours?"

"Haven't seen hide nor hair of these savages since we found you," Felaan shrugged. "If you say that these creatures die by the bullet and that your turrets can deal with them..." He trailed off and shrugged. Despite his confident words he hefted my rifle closer to his body.

"Better get to work then," I said. "Break camp, but remain vigilant."

I didn't make sure that they obeyed, I just closed my eyes and gazed inward. My heartbeat thundered in my ears, the blood rushed through my veins. Air soared through my lungs. I slid my awareness down my own body, rib after rib until I found the cracked one. It was a relatively clean break and it wasn't difficult wrapping it in a thin layer of telekinetic power to keep it firmly in place. I bound the spell to my Soul to maintain it and then I used Blood Magic to reduce the swelling and hurry up with fusing the bone. I sent my awareness down my arm to my shattered hand. That was way worse. Many of the small bones in my palm had broken in several pieces, and my index finger was snapped between the bottom two knuckles. I went over the breaks one by one, carefully aligning the bone as if it were an intricate puzzle. It was a good thing that I could numb the entire arm or the pain would be excruciating. Each time I aligned two pieces of bone I bound them together with strands of telekinesis bound to my Soul. It was intricate work and if I didn't do it properly the bone wouldn't set and my hand could be crippled. I would have to break it again to do it all over. I'd rather not. Luckily the bone hadn't been pulverised and I could fit the breaks back together rather nicely. There were some bone fragments shot through the flesh and I used Blood Magic to slowly eject them from my body. It would be easier to just reform the missing pieces. When I was finally done and the bones themselves were tightly bound together I used Blood Magic to reduce the swelling in my hand so that I could use it somewhat. It would still hurt like a motherfucker since I could not block the pain and maintain mobility. But I would just have to fight through the pain. I slowly returned to my proper senses and opened my eyes. Nothing made sense. I was awash in colours and shapes and my ears were filled with strange sounds. Slowly my brain grew accustomed to the familiar sights and my gasping breath gradually returned to normal. I blinked rapidly to clear my vision and I saw that the tents were gone and packed, the only thing that remained from our camp was the fire. I gingerly stood up on wobbly legs and fought through the pain of my rib. The sleeping bag my friends had stuffed me into slid down my body and I realised that I was still naked. A moment of panic gripped me. Had they seen the Bizhjerai rune tattooed on my shoulder? Ignoring the pain from my rib I twisted and looked down at my shoulder. I sighed with relief. I was covered in silt and dirt and my tattoo was obscured. Well, that was a wasted bath. I was even filthier now than when I had left to clean up.

At least one of my friends had had the sense to snap my choker into place and I touched the powerstone to summon my clothes. I chose to go with with the sturdiest corset that was imprinted into the gem. It would wrap my torso tight and keep my ribs in place, like a wrapping. I started walking toward the focusing tower while magical power billowed out of the powerstone to adhere to my skin, gradually materialising into clothing. It was a little weird taking a step only to find out that the heel had suddenly become just a little bit higher. I didn't wear boots like mother had with the very high stiletto heels. Mine were much shorter and much broader. More sensible. Though it had made sense that it should have been the other way around. Mother had been tall and didn't really need the boost in height. I was tiny and really did need it. I shook my head and reached up with both hands to brush my hair behind my ears and I winced when my shattered hand made contact with my skull. With a flex of my mind I gathered my hair into a high ponytail and bound it with magic. It would dissipate in an hour or two judging by the amount of power I had poured into the buffer spell but it was ease itself to cast again. I remembered with a fond smile how mom and mother had sat in the forest outside Dun Traelak, struggling to figure out how to connect a buffer spell to a simple light spell. And I had just cast a similar spell without thought, just to get my hair out of my eyes. The automatic rifle turrets were finished, as was the manufactorium spell for the ammunition and we had a healthy stockpile of bullets. Now I just needed to attach the turrets to the tower, imbue them with a scrying spell, a spell to reload and a couple of flares to signal to camp. I wanted to mount the turrets about halfway up the tower. I reached up and touched my powerstone and activated my Angel armour, and then I tapped it again to overlay with my construction gear. Power billowed out, consuming my current outfit to clothe me in my new regalia. It took quite a while, almost five minute, but I was finally clad in crystal armour. It was a set of light armour, similar to the Valkyrie armour. Complete with the skeletal wings with magical jets at each tip and the telekinetic membrane between them. Sort of like the wings of a bat. But what differed the Angel from the Valkyrie where the three tubular attachments to my back. The middle one was thick, providing the majority of the thrust. The two to the sides were there mostly for stabilisation but could also be used for sideways movement. The wings were for manoeuvrability. The first prototype that Uncle Kenny and I had built had had the manoeuvrability jets mounted on the hands since Uncle Kenny wasn't comfortable with the skeletal arms, but I had argued that freeing the hands would make the armour much more useful. And it had. While it drained too much magical power for prolonged flight, it was more than possible to fly a fair stretch before requiring a recharge. It was mostly used for personal transfer to and from Skyships but also used by boarding parties or rapid deployment of reinforcements on the battlefield. Though to be honest, it hadn't been used in actual combat yet.

The automatic rifle turrets were heavy and so there were simply no way for me to just hoist them into position due to the exponential drop-off of the nature of spells. That was that whenever you double the weight of an object, or double the distance from your physical body, it would require more than twice the power to manipulate it. So I had to turn to old-school mechanics. I triggered my Angel armour and I grimaced in pain when my weight was shifted and my armoured corset took my full weight, pressing against my cracked rib. I numbed the pain from my ribs and drew a relieved breath. I gently lifted higher, using the skeletal wings to stabilise myself. When I reached the appropriate height I spared a moment to twist in a circle to watch for trouble. The tree cover obscured my vision. There could have been an army of those magic-drinking savages out there and I would never know. I carefully guided myself closer to the focusing tower and raised my left arm. It was now covered in a heavy crystal gauntlet with a multitude of tools attached. With a thought a drill shot out of its housing to protrude before my fist and I used it to drill a hole into the crystal side of the tower at a downward angle. The drill retracted when I opened my hand and I reached down to my belt to draw a crystal rod with a stout ring attached to the end from it. I slid the rod into the hole I had drilled and hooked the ring into a carabiner on my belt. I gingerly cut the power of my Angel armour until my full weight was supported by the ring and my feet against the side of the tower. Perfect. No need to waste power hovering. I got to work casting a spell. Ten minutes later the spell was bound to the fount and carving its way down the tower, leaving machined grooves and notches in its wake. I activated my Angel armour and unhooked from the tower, swinging myself around a quarter of the way around and repeated the process. Twice more and I had four elevator shafts finished and I had made sure to make one of them a little higher than the others. I brought myself down to the ground and checked the power levels in my powerstone. It was getting low so I bound it to the fount to replenish while I worked down here. Unlike the Valkyrie armour, the wings on the Angel was dexterous and I could fold them tightly, tucking them behind the two side engines on my back to get them out of the way.

I walked up to the closest automatic rifle turret and got to work with attaching an elevator function to the back of it before I dragged it across the ground to mount it to the descending carving spell. Three more times I repeated the process, once for each turret, before I finally did the same for the ammunition manufactorium spell. That last one was the first one to be attached to the tower on the longest of the four tracks. While it trundled happily up the tower I attached each of the other three turrets before finishing with the one that was going to be directly below the manufactorium spell. I sat down on the ground and spent some time duplicating the schematics for my bike and bound them to the fount to materialise. I didn't bother to form a spell stone for each of them. The bikes would just have to serve until they broke down. With the bikes coming along nicely the turrets had almost finished climbing the tower. When they were finally in place I extended my wings and activated my Angel armour. I settled in by the manufactorium spell and hooked my belt to the ring I had placed earlier. The turrets were belt-fed and I spent some time modifying my spells. The manufactorium spell would eject four belts, evenly attaching new links with bullets on every fourth rotation of creation, on demand. I used the already produced stockpile to loop the belts around the tower and I fed them into the assigned gun. I tapped my lower lip as I hung there, considering what to do with the spent casings. Each casing was a not insignificant investment of magical power to materialise. I could probably loop the belt back to the manufactorium spell to reuse them to cut down on power drain. Though that might lead to a backlogged system if too many shots were consumed in too short amount of time to recycle them. But not if I proportioned the recycle hopper to be able to hold the sum of each turrets ammo capacity. It would also be significantly more complex than simply ejecting the casings and materialising new ones. And complexity brought errors. Since this tower should be self-sufficient, I should choose the solution that minimised risk of errors. Which meant that I should have gone for the Arclite turrets to begin with, but it was too late to change my mind now. I decided to just make each casing disintegrate after firing so that at least the power bound in the crystal could be reused even if the actual casing was not. Next I created a token that could activate and deactivate the turrets, just in case someone had to head up here for maintenance, and bound it to each turret. Next I added scrying spells to each of the weapons to send a scrying pulse once every few dozen seconds to sweep for movement. Anything that moved, died. I managed to cut down on the power requirements for the scrying spells by only having each turret cover an area that wasn't already covered by another one. A clever little spell linked the turrets together so that they could share target solutions if all threats appeared within range of several turrets. Then I added two flare spells, one that was bright yellow and would shoot into the sky to hover for a good hour. That would mean that the turrets were firing. A deep crimson flare would be fired if there were a malfunction or weapons jam. My work completed I descended to ground level and set my Angel armour to dissolve and my powerstone to clothe me in travel clothes. I paused, and then touched my choker again and added a layer of light armour on top of my travel clothes. Those savages might attack us again on the road and I wanted to be prepared.

I took a last long look at the mostly finished tower and listened to the faint clinking of crystal on crystal when the ammo was produced and added to the stockpile. Everything seemed to be in order. Just needed to test the whole thing. I walked out of the scrying range for the locater spells and activated them with my token. I used my own personal power to toss a boulder the size of my head within range. The three turrets that had line of sight to the boulder spun around and fired. With a sharp hissing sound the orange flare spell fired to arc high into the sky where it burst into flames and spat a thick smoke that caught the glow of the flare. Bullets zipped past the boulder, it was too small for the turrets to pinpoint it accurately. As soon as it thumped into the ground and stopped rolling the turrets ceased fire and resumed their original positions. The last crystal casings fell to the ground like a soft rain, already evaporating into magic power that was absorbed by the spells engraved into the inside of the tower. Satisfied with the whole setup I dusted off my hands, winced in pain from my shattered hand, and turned my back on the whole thing. My friends burst out of the woods, weapons at the ready and eyes scanning for threats. Wylah almost stepped over the invisible line that would see her shot by the turrets.

"Wylah! Stop!" I barked and the wiry woman froze. "Go any further and the turrets will shoot. They can't identify friend from foe, anything that moves will die."

She gulped and took a slow step backward, and then another one.

"We heard gunfire," Zandor panted, eyes still a bit wide with excitement.

"Just a live fire drill," I told him with a smirk and then I lowered my gaze. "I should probably have warned you before."

"Yes, you should," Felaan agreed without malice.

"Well, the bikes should be finished in a little while, should we eat and then leave?" I asked.

"Aye," Zandor answered. "I am famished."

"You are always famished," Valia pointed out.

We ate the dried remnants of whatever creature Felaan had caught last and washed it down with tepid water from our skins. I wished that I had brought mothers canteen that refilled itself by sucking moisture out of the air. It kept the water cool and pleasant, which was a nice touch. But I had left it in Dun Traelak. Only a few of them had ever been made and they had only been issued to the members of the First Expedition, so it would have been incredibly conspicuous to carry among other Skylanders. When we finished our meal I glanced over at the bikes and saw that the spells had disengaged and that they were fully materialised and charged. Even the extra power cores in the saddle bags seemed to be fully charged. We should have enough juice to take us all the way back to camp without having to resort to my Sieve swarm.

"Looks like the bikes are ready, shall we?" I asked and started walking toward them.

Something heavy slammed into me from the side, barrelling me to the ground. I cursed when I landed on my broken hand and struggled to get free, scrambling for my revolver. I looked up and saw Zandor on top of me.

"What the fuck?" I yelled into his wide-eyed face.

"You almost walked into the kill-zone," he panted and I was struck with how fresh his breath smelled, even after having consumed a large quantity of dried meat. Then his words sank in and I blushed.

"Oh, right. Thank you for saving my life," I grinned nervously and he smiled back, his green eyes twinkling. "You can get off me now, unless you want to take this further?" I teased with an arched eyebrow and squirmed under him, making sure to writhe my body along his.

I had learned that flirting with him made him extremely self-conscious and that it was the most efficient way to shut him up when he was in a teasing mood. His face coloured and he sat up, stammering out apologies.

"No need to apologise, Zandor. You saved my life. I owe you one."

He bowed wordlessly, still blushing. I accepted the hand that Felaan offered me, with my unbroken left hand, and he pulled me to my feet. I turned toward the tower and used the token to deactivate the defences and as I did I saw out of the corner of my eyes how Zandor adjusted his trousers. I couldn't suppress a smirk. Mother had once mused when I was a little girl and we had taken a bath together that I would grow up to be a sultry seductress if I kept developing the curves in the way she had noticed. Judging by Zandor's reaction, she may have been correct.

I saw Wylah place a large bundle into her saddlebag and I sauntered up to her.

"What's that you just packed away?"

"Savage head," she grunted and tugged the strap closed.

"A what now?"

"I want to show Micah those bastards that attacked you. Perhaps he knows what they are."

"Okay..."

I hadn't expected her to casually chop off one of their heads to bring with us as a grisly trophy, but if it made her happy...

I gave my friends a crash course in how to use the bikes and I guided them through a few practice laps around the tower before they drew up in a line before me. While they had been practising I had manifested my own bike and I straddled it.

"Remember, take it slow. Until you get truly used to handling the bike a single bump might send you into a spin and a fall can be lethal. I'd rather you go slow and make it back to camp in one piece than you go to quickly and someone gets hurt, alright?"

They all nodded with gleaming eyes, even the ever stoic Wylah, and gunned the whispering engines in anticipation.

"Very well, let's be off!" I said and followed my words with action.

I kept a rather slow pace at first, glancing over my shoulders frequently to check up on them. I wasn't used to looking behind me and I realised that whenever I did I pulled the steering bars a little to the side. Maybe I should figure out a way to look behind me without needing to turn my head. A scrying spell perhaps? One that projected an image onto the dashboard in front of me? That would probably work. I risked increasing our speed a little and I was not surprised when my friends gleefully accelerated to catch up to me. So far the forest floor was rather flat and we were able to make good time, but I couldn't help worrying about the rougher terrain a bit farther down the valley. Turned out that I worried for nothing. We screamed down the decline, wind in our hair and howling in our ears. I even saw Wylah drive her bike across an outcropping to leap a good distance before touching down. Her front tyre caught on something and her bike swerved. My heart leaped up into my throat but Wylah managed to stay upright. My heart slowly descended when she shot me an exhilarated grin before twisting the throttle and shooting ahead of me. I laughed and followed suit. Felaan overtook me, leaning in the saddle with the agility I had come to expect from him. Even Valia managed to keep up with us. Only Zandor didn't seem so enamoured with his new steed. He held the bars stiffly, his knees clutched tight around the saddle. It might be because he was a bit too large for the bike and he didn't feel secure. Or he might just not appreciate the speeds were travelling at. I choked down on the throttle until he caught up with me.

"You okay?" I screamed over the howl of the wind.

"Yeah!" He responded and gave me a very fake smile.

"Be honest!" I laughed. "I can't help you if you lie to me."

"It's a bit fast for being so small! I feel very unbalanced," he shouted back.

"It's proportioned to fit me. Once I get some spare time I'll tweak yours to better suit your gigantic frame, big boy," I screamed back and gunned my engine to catch up to the others. Zandor had enough sense to not push himself and I wasn't that worried that he would fall and injure himself. We rode until night started falling and I twisted the throttle to overshoot my friends in the lead. Once there I raised my fist above my head and started slowing down. They understood the signal and rolled to a stop in a cluster around me. We waited for a short while for Zandor to catch up.

"It's getting dark," I announced needlessly. "I think that if we keep riding we can reach camp before midnight and sleep in our own tents tonight. Or we could make camp here and strike back out tomorrow morning. What do you think?"

Wylah grinned ferally. "I say we keep going. This was fun!"

"I agree," Felaan chimed in calmly but with a glint of amusement in his dark eyes.

Valia just shrugged, going along with whatever the rest of us decided. All eyes turned to Zandor how looked down sheepishly.

"I'd rather wait until tomorrow. I don't cherish using this machine in complete darkness."

"Come on, Zandor," Wylah complained. "If we don't arrive too late, there might still be some stew left. Wouldn't you rather eat stew than more of that dried beef?"

Zandor rubbed his sparse beard growth. "Well, yes. But the darkness..."

"Mary can cast a light spell for us," Felaan countered and I nodded along. Even though the tent that had been assigned to me wasn't much better than sleeping on the ground I would still prefer that than another day in a bedroll.

With a flourish I cast a brilliant light spell into the air to hover above me. It illuminated a good distance around us as if the moon was shining brightly.

"Well, in that case I guess we can keep going," Zandor finally admitted.

Wylah pumped her fist once and immediately gunned her engine. The rest of us hastened to follow suit, but I let Zandor set the pace. The rest were wise enough to stay inside the area of illumination and they didn't even grumble much about the slower speed.

It was almost close to midnight when we rolled into camp. I was exhausted and couldn't wait to get some sleep. We parked the bikes in the outskirts of the camp.

"Stew, here I come." Zandor rubbed his hands together.

"I'd rather just pass out," I said. "I guess I'll see you guys tomorrow?"

"Definitely," Zandor said firmly. "Just because our mission is complete there's no reason to not hang out any more."

Wylah and Felaan nodded in agreement.

"I'm sure that Micah will ask us to accompany you when you go to open the other crossings anyway," Valia stated with a jaw-cracking yawn.

"Not that we wouldn't keep you company if he didn't," Zandor interjected quickly.

"Thanks, guys. Really appreciate it. But I really have to crash now," I said and left them with a wave. A few minutes later I was in my tent, my clothes dissolving while I slid into my bedroll. Soon the focusing tower would be complete and I could start building the receiving end. Then I could really get to work on this place.


---

## Chapter 8 — CHAPTER EIGHT You must construct additional pylons {#chapter-8}

CHAPTER EIGHT

You must construct additional pylons

It took two days for the first flicker of the redirected magic to reach us. Two days I spent planning with Micah. He made me describe my encounter with the savages again and again until it felt like a well-rehearsed chant.

"I don't know what they were. I have never heard about anything like them."

"Maybe they are an ancient type of living spell. Like the dragons, from the days before the Apocalypse. Or maybe they used to be human but adapted to survive among the wild magic that destroyed everything."

"Perhaps," Micah agreed. "But I fear that we may never know. Unless you think they can speak?"

I shrugged. They had only bellowed and grunted at me and their mouths did not seem shaped to form human words. That did not mean that they couldn't speak to each other though.

"Did you get any idea about how many of them there are?"

"I only saw the three that I killed. Judging by their primitive equipment I would guess that they are not that many. They won't be able to farm the land and there isn't enough wildlife in this valley to sustain a large population," I concluded.

"You are assuming that these creatures eat like we do." Micah mused while stroking his chin. "They could grow fungus in the caves like we did on the Skylands, or they sustain themselves on magic entirely. You said they drank your magic?"

I nodded. "Yes. Sucked it right out of my spells."

Micah was silent for a long moment, staring at nothing. "Damn," he finally swore. "That puts a real dampener on my plans. We will need to erect defences to our rear as well as to our front. We may be attacked from either direction at any time."

"The rear defences probably does not need to be as heavy. They did seem quite primitive."

"Yet we must assume that they are at least equal to us, even if it is unlikely. We just don't know enough about them." Micah sighed deeply. "Plan defences to our rear, Mary, similarly as we did for the front. I'll send scouts northward to learn more about these creatures. Perhaps we can simply wipe them out and be done with it."

His words soured my mood. I had almost forgotten what Skylanders were like, and he had just given me a stark reminder. A single encounter with an unknown people and his first thought was to casually commit genocide. Just like they had attempted with the Clans.

"Very well," I forced myself to say respectfully. "Anything else?"

"No. You did good, Mary. You should be proud of yourself," Micah said with a flicker of a smile on his stony face.

"Thanks." I managed to say the word without sarcasm and beat a hasty retreat before I said something that I would regret later. It was then that I sensed the spear of magic shoot by overhead. Finally! I hurried to the location that Micah had designated to be the main receiver tower. He had chosen an area in the middle of the camp, which was foolish, but I did not stop him. Dun Traelak suffered the same critical flaw since the tower had not been moved after mother conquered it from Grasax. Beneath the towers base there were a cluster of huge powerstones. If they ruptured the release of magical power would be catastrophic. Locating them in the centre would almost ensure the destruction of the camp. Perfect for my plans. I dashed into the open area and chased away a group of kids that were playing with a ball. I looked up at the vertical power spout and reached up with my own power. A thick tendril of it split off and changed direction and I was bathed in a shower of power. My shoulders relaxed as if I had sunk into a hot tub after a long day of horse riding. As if I had finally scratched that hard to reach itch. Like a sudden noise vanished and you just then realised how bothered you had been by it without knowing. I just stood there enjoying the sensation of the thick field of power. But it was just dissipating across the ground, the currents mildly corrosive to anything it touched. Now bathed in power I could get to work and I swiftly had several reinforcement spells trudging along scorched earth. My right hand was still a little swollen and more than a little tender but I could wield my wand with only a little discomfort. I drew the sigils on the ground, guided by my spell book, and soon it was finished. In the rich magic field the work went much faster than it had by the crossing and I wasn't even breathing hard when I was done. I bound the spells that would form the tower, a much bigger tower than the one to the north, to the artificial ley line and watched it work. Slowly the foundation formed out of nothing. It would take quite some time just to construct the framework and I decided that I could open more ley line crossings while I waited for the receiver. The more power we had available when the system finally went online the quicker construction of a proper town could continue. But Micah had announced that he would hold the introductory meeting today by noon and combine with a larger assembly for everyone where he would make an announcement. I couldn't leave before that. I squinted up at the sun. It was only an hour until noon anyway. I spent that hour going over my bike, making sure that everything worked. I knew it would since it could self-repair but Uncle Kenny had taught me that being meticulous could never, ever, hurt.

The time for the grand assembly arrived. People were slowly congregating outside of the camp to the north, inside the valley. I made my way over and somehow managed to score front row seats. Though, there were no seats. A front row standing spot, then. A tall wooden crate was before me, presumably so that Micah could stand on it so that he could be seen by everyone. I waited impatiently until everyone at the camp was assembled and Micah walked around the crowd, a tall woman trailing behind and to the left of him. Micah clambered onto the crate and nodded down at the woman. Magic flared through her aura and her hands shimmered and spat a small shower of sparks. When Micah spoke it seemed as if he were talking directly into my ears and I assumed that the spell the woman had used was some sort of sound-altering shenanigans.

"My friends!" He began. "A decade has passed since the Fall. The survivors of Utopeia, the sole remainder of our once proud people, has been scattered throughout these lands. A people without a people. A people without a home."

He paused and swept his eyes across the crowd.

"When Utopeia was destroyed, so was I. I believed in the Skylander dream, to find new lands and resources. Resources that would improve the lives of millions of Skylanders. The Skylands was not perfect, it could not be. Not when it had started to outgrow our supplies. Millions starved and fought for scraps to survive. But when we founded Utopeia, there was hope. Hope for all of those who were downtrodden. A hope that was stomped out by the Traitor. And that hope was all that sustained me. Without the Skylands, without Utopeia... All hope was lost. We scattered through these lands to find refuge. To seek a place to call our own after our own had so rudely been taken from us. We did not band together, for I feared that any congregation of Skylanders would be stamped out by the Traitor's kin. I tried to carve out my own niche, my own Skylands, where my family and I could live. The Skylands was destroyed, but I was still a Skylander! I thought that I could become just Micah, a simple farmer. But the vicious lies of the Bizhjerai reached even the remote corner of the world where I had settled down. I returned from a hunting trip to see my farm burned to the ground, my wife..." He choked on a sob and to my great surprise his usually so stoic face was streaked with tears. "My wife, my children. Murdered. It was then I knew that we Skylanders would never be welcome here. Never be allowed to exist amongst the others who already inhabited these lands. I don't know how many of you suffered similarly, and for that I am to blame. I was, to my knowledge, the highest ranking surviving commander of Utopeia. It was my duty to keep you safe, to guide you. And I failed in that duty. But I am here now. I will do my part. I sent out word that we are building a new community here. A new Utopeia. Not the Skylands, but the Groundlands. Where Skylanders can live in peace and prosperity!"

He shouted the last words and the crowd roared in jubilation. I lent my voice to theirs for appearance sake while I cringed on the inside. Groundlands. What an awful name.

"With the new magical supply lines almost coming online, thanks to Mary Laier, apprentice to the late Mathias Varl, we can soon start constructing homes and defences of dignity. No longer will we need to rely on such rudimentary materials," he said with a disdainful wave at the hide and cloth tents. "Our feet will once again trod the crystal highways, our houses will be insulated and heated by magic. Our Mages will have abundant magic to draw from. The source of Skylander might have always been magic, and soon it shall be so once more!"

Again the crowd cheered and as I glanced around I noticed that many faces were wet with tears of hope.

"Be patient, my brothers and sisters. Be patient, for soon our settlement will be built in earnest!"

Micah leaped down from the crate to the roar of the crowd and he took the two steps to stand before me.

"When will the magic supply become usable?" He had to yell to be heard over the crowd.

"Soon, a couple of days or so. But it won't be enough to quickly construct all that we need. That crossing was rather small and the power output relatively modest."

He digested that for only a moment. "Then your sole focus is to secure more power."

I sketched a salute and extracted myself from the crowd. I did not like large crowds, unless it was Bizhjerai. I trusted all of them implicitly. Here, I trusted no one. I made my way to the cluster of bikes and I drew my rifle from beneath the saddle. It still hurt to curl my shattered hand around the grip but I had healed enough to manage. I ejected the magazine and inspected the contents. It was full of specialised bullets, ready to be enspelled by the powerstones embedded in the stock. I dropped the magazine and let it hover beside me and I racked the slide. The chambered round ejected fluidly and I caught it with my magic. The magazine slammed home in the weapon and the bullet slid back into the chamber before I closed the breech. Everything was in order. I stowed the rifle on my bike and drew my revolver, opening the cylinder to verify that it was fully loaded. It was. I checked the speed loaders on my belt. All set. I tested the edge of mothers sword and it was as sharp as ever. I wrapped the fingers of my left hand around the liche dagger on my belt, stroking the gem adorning the hilt with my thumb. It was a habit I'd developed over the years. Mother had once told me that the gem was where the liche enchantment was anchored. She had been forced to enter that enchantment herself to eject Grasax. Her own Soul had been bound to it briefly when she turned herself into a liche in order to destroy the Skylands. Even though her Soul had been destroyed in that geyser, touching that gem made me feel as if I was caressing her cheek. Just like had as a child. But mother had shown herself to me. Saved my life. At least I thought so. Had I somehow used my magic on the brink of unconsciousness to kill that last savage? Had my weary mind just conjured an explanation out of my deepest desire? Probably. Even so it made me feel better to stroke the gem.

"Do you think that you can leave without us?" A cheerful voice boomed behind me.

I turned around and smiled at my friends. All of them were decked out in their light travel armour. Valia even had a pair of goggles strapped around her head, resting on her forehead to make her look like some sort of four-eyed monster. An incredibly cute and adorable monster, but a monster nonetheless.

"Not at all," I lied. "I was just waiting for your slow asses to show up," I answered Zandor with a grin. "Look, I even made your bike bigger," I said and pointed at it.

And I had. It was almost twice the size of what it had been and Zandor lit up when he saw it. He approached it reverently, slowly reaching out to caress it with his fingers. "I love it," he stated with a warm smile before beaming at me. "Thank you, Mary."

Inexplicably, I blushed with joy from his words. "Don't mention it," I tried to say nonchalantly but I could feel myself blush deeper.

Wylah had already straddled her bike and twisted the throttle as if she were already on the way. "Where are we going?"

"To open another crossing. It's a little farther than the first one, but the terrain is way flatter outside the valley mouth and we should be able to get there faster. Perhaps even before nightfall."

"It took us more than two days to hike up there!" Valia protested. "There's no way we can get there before nightfall, even with these," she continued and grinned down at her own bike.

"They'll surprise you," I said with a smug smile and mounted my bike. It whispered to life. "Let's go!" I shouted and twisted the throttle. I had intended to roar down the valley mouth but the mass of tents and returning people instead turned my triumphant dash into an awkward crawl.

Valia snickered behind me but I pretended not to hear her. When we finally left the camp behind I really let loose on the throttle and we were soon screaming across the plains. The mountain range had a rather odd shape. The mountains were high but the bottom appeared to have been sheared off with a gigantic axe. Only the signs of wear and corrosion from wind and rain during the centuries made it look a tiny bit more natural. Probably an effect of the wild magic that had destroyed the world. The land beneath the cliff was flat and grassy and we made good time. While we rode my friends pushed their bikes to their limits, accelerating and then sliding across the grass. Zandor surprised me by overshooting me with his front wheel raised to the sky and a wild laugh on his lips. Not to be outdone by the big man I did the same and after I passed him I applied the front breaks and leaned forward so that my rear wheel rose from the ground. I could just barely see his astonished expression before he zoomed past me. Such transpired the day and I led our little group toward the cliff wall just as the lowest part of the sun started to hide beneath the treetops. Just to show off my skills on the bike I used the front brakes to roll to a stop on the front wheel, swinging around to face my friends with a rakish grin on my face. I enjoyed their impressed expressions thoroughly.

"The crossing is nearby. Let us make camp here and have dinner, then I'll go and check it out." I said and shut off my bike.

"Alone?" Zandor asked, concern plain in his voice.

"Yeah. It's up there," I said and pointed up the mountain.

He raised his eyes to see where I was pointing. "Up there?" He asked, incredulous. "It's dangerous to make that climb!"

"I've got Angel armour;" I said nonchalantly.

"Angel armour?" Felaan asked, curious.

"You haven't heard of it?"

All of them shook their heads.

"Well, it's like the Valkyrie, but with the ability of actual flight. Mathias and I heard that the Bizhjerai had managed to figure it out. We reverse engineered it."

"Just like that, the two of you?" Valia asked dubiously.

"Yeah. He was rather brilliant," I said with a shrug.

"It's a true shame that he couldn't join us in the Groundlands," Zandor said wistfully. I couldn't help but cringe when I heard that awful name but none of my friends seemed to notice it.

"Yeah," I agreed before I clapped my hands. "Let's get to it!"

We quickly erected our tents, still just two of them since at least one of us would always be on guard duty. Though I didn't intend to sleep much. I had already used Blood Magic to wash my fatigue away. I wanted to work through the night, I could pass out tomorrow evening back at camp. We ate the standard ration for travelling, jerked beef and boiled oats, and washed it down with tepid water. While we ate I materialised my Angel armour and I could not help but notice the not so subtle glances Zandor sent at my body whenever part of my clothing dissolved to make way for the Angel armour. I smirked every time I caught him, pleased with his attention. I finished my food and Zandor hastened to grab my bowl for me.

"I'll take care of that for you, Mary," he said with a faint blush.

"Thank you, Zandor. Such a gentleman, right guys?" I asked the others.

Wylah rolled her eyes and Felaan just nodded. Valia looked between me and Zandor, her eyes shining with suppressed mirth. Her full lips twitched with movement and I just knew that she was struggling valiantly to not giggle. A sudden warmth filled my lower belly at the sight of her pretty face. No, not just pretty. With that expression and the flickering light of the flames illuminating her fine bone structure she was beyond beautiful. With a sudden thought I then realised that given the choice to bed either her or Zandor, I didn't know which one of them I would choose. My eyes twitched between them and the silence grew long. All three of us suddenly blushed as if we knew what the other two were thinking. For a moment I just wanted to drag both of them into my tent and have my way with them at the same time. Somehow I believed that if it hadn't been for the presence of Wylah and Felaan, they might have gone along with it. It had been deathly silent for too long, well into the territory of awkward. Especially with the amused way Wylah and Felaan regarded the three of us.

"You know, if the three of you want to use my tent I can sleep with Felaan tonight," Wylah deadpanned and finished her supper.

I felt as if my face would catch fire and Zandor sputtered something unintelligible. Valia hid her pretty face in her hands and shrunk in on herself.

"She's joking," Felaan assured us and I let out a relieved breath. "But just in case, Wylah will sleep with me," he said just as deadpanned as she had and looked at his stoic friend.

Something in the way they looked at one another at that moment tugged at my heartstrings. It was the same way my mothers had looked at one another. Tenderly, lovingly. Like two teenagers madly in love and not caring who saw them.

The moment between them was over almost before I had registered it. Was there something going on between them? If it wasn't then I was certain that it soon would be. I smiled at the thought. The two stoic warriors were perfect for each other.

Then my mood suddenly soured when I remembered that I was scheming to kill all of the Skylanders, this group included. I shouldn't get too attached to them. Couldn't get too attached to them. They were Skylanders. There were no innocent Skylanders. Certainly no lovable Skylanders. Not even me. I hardened my expression and I saw Zandor's face blanch in surprise at my sudden reaction.

"I should get to work," I said harshly and stalked out of the camp. I heard heavy footsteps follow me but I activated my Angel armour and soared up the cliff face, leaving Zandor behind. I did not wish to speak with him at the moment. I feared that it would have broken my resolve to do so. I flew past the ledge of the cliff and cut the engine to land on it in a perfect three-point landing. Something I had never managed to do when Lukas and I were practising back home. I looked around even though I was primarily using my Magesense to search for the ley line crossing. It wasn't within range but I knew the general direction of it. So I sent out a powerful scrying pulse. The return came from the north and from above. I looked up to inspect the mountain. It was rather steep and would be difficult to climb. It was fortunate that I wouldn't have to. I flew up the mountain, landing every now and again on an outcropping to scry again. When I finally arrived at the crossing I was about a third of the way up the mountain and I could just barely make out the Groundlands in the distance. At least I had line of sight to the target which would mean it would be simple to align the towers. While it was possible to burn the artificial ley line through solid stone, it was always sketchy to do so. The bore might upset the structural integrity of the rock. In the best case nothing happened, worst case could trigger a massive avalanche. I gauged the distance to the Groundlands. I shouldn't need to erect a focusing station to stabilise the artificial ley line, but it might be necessary. It depended on the quality of the crossing. My magesense couldn't reach the crossing itself, it was buried deep beneath the mountain I was on but my scrying told me in no uncertain terms that it was directly below me. It would take far more power to breach it than I had used to the north, but luckily I had brought extra power cores for this one. I started by flattening an area for the focusing tower. I used my power to break apart the rock and shatter it before shoving the gravel down the side of the mountain. I was sweating when I was done and the cold mountain air chilled me to the bone. I drew my wand to start drawing the pattern for the focusing tower but my teeth where chattering so hard that I couldn't keep the wand stable enough. I erected a shield around myself and started heating the air, waiting impatiently for my body to warm up. Luckily I was a tiny woman and the warmth quickly seeped into my stiff muscles. I got to work. The reinforcement spells I bound directly to a dedicated power core so it wouldn't put as much of a strain on me while I worked. I don't know how many hours I worked but the spells were complete sometime in the night. I had updated my schematics to include the gun turrets right off the bat and they would be constructed when the tower reached the proper height. I doubted that they would be required up here but once they were materialised and the buffer of ammo was produced they would hardly drain the magic output of this crossing at all. Unlike Arclite turrets which would continuously drain the output. I connected the power cores to the spells and then waited. I could only sense the spells reach a ways down into the mountain before they extended past the range for my Magesense. Mother would probably be able to sense the actual crossing, though it would have been at the edge of her range. I sometimes envied her her vast power, but it had come at a great cost. She had gained her power in a titanic duel with a vastly superiour foe. The liche Grasax. She had only won because she had risked everything. I closed my eyes and replayed the memories that I had absorbed from her memory core. How she had seen mom get stabbed through the belly with his staff. How she had fallen to the ground in pain. I had felt my mothers torment as the woman she loved fell to the ground, dying. I felt her despair when she realised that her foe was far more powerful than her, and more skilled at that. I felt how determination filled her and she hurled herself into the magical fount in a suicidal dive, drinking in the power to deny Grasax access to it. Too much power. I felt her strain to get rid of it, felt the madness fraying the edges of her sanity and boiling her body from the inside. Her skin and flesh ruptured, bleeding liquid magic and steaming blood. Her arm shattered and melted, her eyes searing in their sockets. She had almost died that day, suffering wounds that would kill her in time. Her beautiful Soul leaking out of the rents in her flesh and Soul both. Only an old spell in Grasax' library had saved her, along with the skilled hands of the tattoo artist Uralnor. He had spent many hours laboriously inking her skin with distilled magic to seal the wounds so that her Soul could remain in her body. The experience had left her body and spirit ravaged, damaged beyond repair. But her power capacity and channelling rate had stretched to accommodate the influx of power. It was a wonder that she hadn't Archoned on the spot, only the fact that she had been spending the power almost as quickly as she absorbed it had saved her life.

A sudden rumble pulled me back to the present and I jerked back when the ground before me ruptured violently. Crushed rock was flung high in the air and pattered across my shield like a hailstorm. Magical power erupted in a great torrent toward the sky before dispersing into the general magic field. I gaped in awe. This fount was almost three times as large as the one in Dun Traelak. It was the largest fount I had ever seen apart from the Great Geyser that used to keep the Skylands afloat. I grinned. This would be a great boon to the Groundlands. I could build so much with this. Which meant I could build a lot of sabotaged gear for the Skylanders. I quickly bound the fount to the focusing tower spells and I watched the first wisps of materialised magic form over the ground like a thin layer of violet ice.

My work here was done, now I just had to wait for the tower to finish. And maybe to add a focusing station to the artificial ley line, but I doubted it now that I had seen the intensity of the fount. But I did not really want to return to camp just yet. I still remembered the awkward note I had left my friends in and I had no desire to return to it. So instead I turned my mind to observing the construction of the focusing tower. No spell was ever perfect, Uncle Kenny had taught me. There were always, without exception, room for improvement. I planned optimisations for the next tower for an hour or two when a roar of engines and the sound of boots striking stone behind me pulled from my work. I spun around, revolver already in hand, and aimed at the figure behind me.

He rose from his crouch. Skeletal crystal wings folding behind his back and the Angel armour powering down. In his hand he clutched a staff topped by a powerstone. Even though his helmet was full faced and obscured his features I recognised him immediately. The power that wafted from him was unmistakable.

"Uncle Kenny," I gasped.


---

## Chapter 9 — CHAPTER NINE An unexpected visitor {#chapter-9}

CHAPTER NINE

An unexpected visitor

Kendall stared at me through the eye-slits of his helmet, azure eyes blazing with the remnants of his power. His staff flared and a shield erupted to encase us both.

"No one can hear us in here, Marissa."

"Uncle Kenny, I'm sorry," I began but Kendall raised his hand to interrupt me.

"I know why you are here. I've come to bring you home," he stated with finality. When I had been a child and I had heard that tone of voice, I knew that I had been in trouble. Despite my urge to hunch I straightened my spine and squared my shoulders. I needed every inch I could eke out of my short stature to compete with his tall frame.

"Mother..." I started.

"I know what she did, and I know that you want to finish it!" He spat vehemently. "But I knew Kyra better than anyone, except perhaps for Anjali. Neither of them would approve of the danger you have placed yourself in."

"I'm doing it for them!" I protested angrily.

"You're doing it for you!" He snapped back. "They're dead. Both of them." He said brutally. "A decade hence. Do you know what your mother told me the last time I saw her? The last time she spoke to me before she flew of to her death?"

"Yes," I breathed. It hurt me just to think about it. I had seen it in her memories, every time I made myself relive those years of her life.

"She asked me to take care of you. To make sure that you led a better life than she did."

"But the Skylanders deserve..."

"They deserve nothing. At the very least from you. They're but a shadow of what they once were, they're no threat to anyone. Let them live or die as they please. Come home with me?"

I steeled my resolve. "Not until I'm done here. I must do this to put their spirits to rest."

Kendall sighed and reached up to remove his helmet. His face was sad but determination burned bright in his blue eyes.

"They already are. Your mother already gave her life to eliminate the threat of the Skylands. She succeeded. Don't throw yours away in a vain attempt to destroy a shadow."

"I can't rest until all Skylanders are dead," I said with as much finality I could muster.

"Don't force me to subdue you and bring you home," Kendall sighed with tears brimming in his eyes.

I knew that I could not defeat him. Kendall was powerful and knowledgeable beyond belief. If he wanted to he could smother my magic with his strength, bundle me up and carry me home.

"I'll never forgive you if you do. I'll fight you all the way and eventually I will sneak away and return here." Each of my words struck him as if I had slapped him in the face and her recoiled physically. "You can't keep me locked up forever, not if you respect my mothers wishes. Eventually I will return here to finish what she started. But then you will have removed all hope of me succeeding. I have a chance now. I've earned their trust, Kendall. I can end this!"

Kendal shrank before each word until he hunched before me, leaning heavily on his staff. Tears trickled down his cheeks and I could not hold back my own any more.

"Kendall. Uncle. I love you as much as I loved my mothers. Don't make me hurt you like this."

"I must," he whispered. "I promised her that I would not let you come to harm. That you would have a better life than hers. It's right there, in Dun Traelak. With the Bizhjerai. Your people," he implored me.

"I know. Yet I chose to be here. Mother would want me to make my own choices. Do you remember her fighting with mom about how to raise Mal? How mom told mother that she must let him make his own decisions?"

I knew it was cruel to manipulate him like this and it hurt me more than it hurt him.

"Yes," he breathed. "I remember." He had lived her memories as well.

"That is exactly the same case with the two of us now."

"I see that, Marissa. I really do."

Relief crashed through me. I had convinced him. Then he suddenly stood up straight.

"But your mother was much stronger than I am. I can't do what she did. I can't let you walk into the vipers nest like this. If you won't come home willingly, then I will force you. Because I love you."

"No! Did you not hear me? I will escape, and I will return."

"Not if there is no place to return to. I will destroy the Skylanders for you. I will bring the Bizhjerai fleet and end them."

"No!" I exclaimed. "I need this settlement to prosper, to lure the rest of them here. If you destroy this place then those who remain will scatter to the winds! I will never be able to find them!"

"Precisely," Kendall said with a wan smile. "But you will be safe. That is all that matters to me. Now, last chance to come with me willingly. I have a skymmer nearby and the Ursa is waiting beyond the horizon. We can be home in a few days. Please, Marissa. Come with me."

I couldn't bear the pleading in the voice, nor the thought that he would stain his Soul for me. Kendall had always abhorred violence and I knew that he would not offer the destroy these Skylanders if it weren't for his intense love for me. I wanted almost nothing more than to make him happy. But I had sworn on the spirits of my parents, all four of them, that I would see this through.

"No," I said finally and Kendall squeezed his eyes shut and took a shuddering breath.

Then he snapped into action, his staff lifting to twirl above his head. He lowered it with a sweeping motion and an arc of power lanced out to wrap around me. I raised my shield and swatted his whip to the side and launched a trio of bolts of power at him. They would not hurt him even if his shield didn't absorb them completely. They would merely toss him around. I knew that I couldn't win this fight against him, even if I had been willing to kill him. Even if I knocked him unconscious and returned to the Groundlands he would come after me. He would bring the Bizhjerai fleet and assault the camp with infantry to get me out. Many of my people would die. I should just give up. It would be better that way. Even if I never fulfilled my oath to my parents. Kendall brushed my bolts aside and and lashed out with his whip again. If that thing curled around me he would use it to bundle me up and bring me home. Defeated, I let him. I dispelled my shield and felt the energy coil around my arms and I saw his eyes widen in surprise. Then they narrowed. He knew me well enough to expect some sort of deceit. When none was forthcoming he started to reel me in like a fish on the hook, preparing a proper binding in his other hand.

A sword sang and sliced the whip in front of me and the part that was still connected to Kendall sprang back at him, sending him stumbling. The coils around me withered and fell to the ground where they disintegrated in a shower of sparks. Kendall regained his balance and looked at the newcomer, a deadly spell already forming around the head of his staff. His face was painted with utter shock and he fell to a knee, leaning heavily on his staff while his face slowly paled.

"Kyra?"

I hadn't dreamed her. She was here. "Mother!" I exclaimed and surged toward her to embrace her and she lifted her arm without looking away from Kendall. I flowed into her side and felt the weight of her arm settle around my shoulders. She felt real, except for the fact that she was incredibly cold. Power wafted out of her, her status as an Archon rendering her unstable.

"Kendall. It is good to see you again. You've aged," mother told her old friend with a smirk.

Uncle Kenny tried to get back on his feet, failed, and gave up.

"Kyra," he croaked again.

"She's right, Kendall. About how Red and I used to fight about Mal. Red taught me that we can't force our own lives onto our children. They must make their own way in this world. Make their own mistakes."

I stiffened. Did she think that my current course of action was a mistake? Her spectral fingers stroked my shoulder consolingly. Her crystal claws made a scratching noise against my crystal pauldrons.

"You know how headstrong she is," mother said with a proud smile.

"She's just like you, Kyra. I can't believe that you are actually here!"

"Neither can I, old friend. I don't know how that is. The last thing I remember was disintegrating above the sinking Skylands. Except for killing that thing that threatened Marissa."

Kendall's mind seemed to focus on that one comprehensible tidbit. "Something tried to hurt you?"

I nodded, tightening the arm I had wrapped around mothers waist. "A creature that seemed to drink magic," I said and gave him a short description of the savages.

Kendall got a faraway look in his eyes. "Something about that sounds familiar. I might have read something about such creatures in one of Grasax' tomes from before the Apocalypse."

Then his face hardened. "But Kyra, you must talk sense into your daughter. Make her leave this foolish and dangerous plan behind!"'

"No," mother said with finality and Kendall recoiled as if struck.

"No?"

"No. I swore an oath to Red that I would let Mal live his life as he saw fit, and I will hold to that oath when it comes to Marissa as well."

I squeezed mother tighter, joy blossoming in my heart.

"But she will get herself killed!" Kendall protested, fresh tears springing from his eyes.

"Perhaps. But I trust Marissa. I believe that she will survive, and that she will succeed. If you want to save her, Kendall, then help her achieve her goals. You can't stop her by force, just as you couldn't stop me."

Kendall glared angrily at the ground. Then his shoulders slumped and his face seemed to age a decade in the span of moments.

"Very well, Kyra. I see that I can't win this one. You always were more stubborn than me, a trait you passed on to your daughter. I love you, Kyra. You were my sister by choice, and I will not fail you again."

"Oh, Kendall," mother whispered sadly and she suddenly dissolved into a stream of magic. She reformed in front of her old friend and gently cupped his cheek with her hand.

"You're my brother and you have never failed me. You were by my side through thick and thin. Without you, I would be dead. Truly dead instead of whatever shade I am now. I would never have survived the Undercity without your strength. I would never have survived myself. I owe you everything. Never believe that you failed me. For in fact, you saved me. I love you."

Kendall blinked away his tears. "Will I ever see you again?"

"I don't know, brother. But I hope so. Now go, it is dangerous for you here."

Kendall nodded and drew a shuddering breath before walking around the shade of my mother on wobbly legs. He gestured and power flowed into his palm, forming a small gem that he offered me.

"I will stand by with the Red Terror and whatever else of the fleet we can spare. Crush this gem and we will come and help you destroy this place."

I looked into his sad eyes and accepted the gem. "Thank you, Uncle. And I'm sorry."

He smiled wanly and reached out to muss my hair like he had done when I was a child. "Don't be. You must do what you believe is right."

He suddenly pulled me into his arms and crushed me to his chest before placing a kiss on the top of my head.

"Stay safe!" He whispered hoarsely before abruptly pushing me away. He took a step away and took a long look at the shade of my mother before igniting his Angel armour and soaring up the mountain.

"Now tell me what the fuck is going on, young lady!" Mother commanded me.

"What?" I sputtered, taken aback by her stern tone.

"I woke up and saw you attacked by some creatures. Then I wake up again and see you fighting Kendall? Kendall, of all people!"

I realised then that she had no idea what was going on or what I was about to do. She had just sided with me because she trusted me. So I told her everything while the focusing tower slowly built up behind me.

"Oh, my sweet child," mother moaned when I finally finished. "While I applaud your scheme, it is way too dangerous. You have to abort."

It felt as if she had stabbed me in the heart. "What? Why? I thought you would be proud of me!"

"I am. I will always be proud of you. As your mother I don't have a choice. But it is much to dangerous!"

"More dangerous than riding alone to take on the Skylands?" I asked with a quirked brow.

Mother blanched and turned her shining gaze to the side before it snapped back to me. "I had taken the necessary steps to prepare."

"As have I," I countered.

Mother regarded me in silence for a while. "I can't deny that. But your plan depends on too many variables."

"I'll play it by ear," I shrugged. "It will work out, I'm sure of it."

Mother sighed deeply. "You are so fucking stubborn, my beloved child."

"I wonder where I got that from."

She glared at me through narrowed eyes. "I wish Red was here. She always was better at talking sense into you."

A pang of grief shot through me at the mention of mom. "I wish she was here too," I whispered and couldn't keep a tear inside.

Mother's face suddenly twisted, and then she was sobbing violently. I crawled over to her and gathered her ephemeral form into my arms. "I miss her so fucking much, Marissa," mother sobbed.

"I know," I cooed and stroked her ghostly hair. "I miss her too. Every day."

"Why am I still here when she is not?"

I had no answer to that. I just rocked her gently and let her get her grief out.

"For better or for worse, I am here. Yet I don't know why," mother finally whimpered and pulled back from me.

"I have no clue," I admitted. "You seem to appear when I am in trouble that I can't get out of myself," I mused. "The people in the Cities, they tell stories of Guardian Angels that look after them. You are like my own Guardian Angel."

Her lips twitched in a faint smile. "An Angel. I remember Raistlin telling me of the strange beliefs of the Cities, and of Angels. Never would I have thought that I might be one of them considering all of the awful things I've done."

"That doesn't matter, mother. I love you regardless."

"Thank you, daughter," she said and gave my hand a final squeeze before standing up. "This is quite the spell you have constructed here," she said while she inspected my spellwork.

"Yup. It took me and Kendall years to build it, but it was worth it. You should see Dun Traelak now. Dozens of artificial ley lines meet at the tower and much of the mountain is excavated to make room for all of the people."

"Are the Clans still united?"

"Yes. Marie was elected Chief of Chiefs after you... After you and mom died. The Clans are still separate, but they live in peace. The distinction between the Clans blur more and more for each year that pass."

Mother smiled. "That's good to hear. What about Kendall? Is he still with that officer?"

"He is. They were married almost nine years ago now and they adopted two orphans from the war. He makes sure that the magic does not turn the Bizhjerai into Skylanders. He doesn't have to spend much effort in that regard."

"Is he happy?"

I nodded. "At least until I ran away."

"He will be fine once you return," she said decisively.

The focusing spell started draining more and more power from the crossing as it constructed the foundations and the general magic field started thinning. Mother flickered once and then stabilised.

"Seems that it is time for me to go," she observed calmly.

"No! Don't go!" I despaired, my heart leaping into my throat.

Mother shook her head sadly. "I'm afraid that I don't have a choice. I don't know what regulates my presence here, but I am fine with that. I've got to see you as a grown woman, Marissa, and you've become so much more than I could ever have dreamed of. I am so proud of you."

Her form blurred at the edges and she started fading along with the thinning magic field. "If I don't see you again, daughter, know that there is nothing where I came from. It's like sleep. Peaceful. Serene. And if we meet again, nothing would make me happier."

She was almost entirely transparent by then. And then she vanished.

"Farewell." Her word lingered, fading slowly to be replaced by the sound of the wind against the mountain.

"Farewell, mother," I whispered, cold tears trickling down my cheeks. I fell to my knees heavily, and cried.


---

## Chapter 10 — CHAPTER TEN There are no innocent Skylanders {#chapter-10}

CHAPTER TEN

There are no innocent Skylanders

I wasted hours crying next to the forming focusing tower. In the end I was not even certain what I was crying about. I was crying about hurting Kendall. I was crying because my mother was gone. I was crying out of happiness that her shade lingered, seemingly watching over me. I cried because she could not find the rest she deserved so much. I cried because mom wasn't there with her. Eventually my tears dried up and I brushed the frozen drops off my cheeks. I was frozen straight to the bone. This high up in the mountains the air was supremely cold and a light snow had started to fall to accompany my echoing sobs. But I was all out of tears for now. Better get to work. I forced my stiff legs to straighten out and I stood on wobbly feet, shivering violently. With a mighty effort I summoned the motivation to cast a few spells to heat myself up. There were another crossing further up the mountain, not too far away. I just hoped that none of the ley lines that crossed up there were directly connected to this crossing or opening that one would weaken this one. It would be a net gain regardless so I decided to power on. I activated my Angel armour and soared higher up the mountain, periodically sending out scrying pulses to verify that I was on the right track. Several times I had to adjust my course and I kept a wary eye on the power levels in my armour. I was running low. Finally I could touch down on the narrow outcropping that was the closest accessible spot to the crossing. I looked around. The outcropping was far too small to support a fully fledged tower. There were a small open area next to the crossing and I walked through the falling snow to look around. I would probably have to level a significant amount of rock to make room for the tower. At least I had had the wherewithal to bind my spare power cores to the other fount before my emotions got the better of me and I had plenty of magical power to spare. I cast a light spell to banish the night and a flicker of reflected light to my side drew my attention. I peered into the dark hollow in the mountain and saw the flicker again. It was unmistakable. Eyes.

The moment it realised it had been spotted the creature pounced. It was a cougar, flinging itself at me, fangs and claws extended. With a gesture I wrapped it in telekinesis, holding it immobile in the air. It was a beautiful animal, but I was too numb inside to admire it. Coldly I drew my revolver and aimed down the crystal sights. A squeeze of the trigger and the noble cat went limp. With a shoving motion of my hand I tossed it back into the cave whence it came and holstered my revolver. The cat already banished from my mind I turned my attention back to the foundation problem. There was nothing to it, I just had to get my magical hands dirty. I drew deeply on my spare power core and started splitting the rocks apart. The first rays of dawn stung my eyes when I finally finished, pushing with a mental heave the last rocks down the mountain. Far below me I could see the other focusing tower slowly forming in a maelstrom of magical power. I had cleared enough rock from the mountain to start drawing up the spells that I would need, but I needed a breather so I sat down on a boulder and drew a deep breath. In my palm I rolled the gem Kendall had given me. I knew what it was. It was the same kind of gem that I had made to contact Lukas and Raistlin to bring the big guns. I just needed to hide it. I sighed and then I stripped out of my coat, pauldrons, armoured corset and blouse. It was strange to see the Angel armour just lying there on the ground and I was suddenly wracked by a sudden burst of giggles. I was emotionally exhausted. Magically exhausted. Physically exhausted. No amount of Blood Magic could wash all of that away. I needed proper rest. But I still had work to do. A sudden cold gust of wind made me shiver. I had dropped my warming spells at my sudden bout of mirth. I reached behind me and unsnapped my bra, shrugging out of the garment. I could feel my nipples tighten instantly in the cold mountain air and I gasped at the sudden cold. I knelt on the frozen rock and drew mothers dagger. It was long and curved with jagged serrations down the back. It was not a suitable tool for what I needed right now, but it was the only tool I had available. Unless I made one out of materialised magic, but I just couldn't summon the willpower to do so. I reached up with my left hand to cup my left breast and I lifted it to expose the crease where it met my ribs. I aligned the point of the dagger and felt it dimple my skin. With a thought I numbed the entire area, and then I thrust the dagger deep. I withdrew the dagger from my flesh and felt warm blood run down my cold skin, but there were no pain. I felt blindly with my fingers until I felt the ragged tear in my skin and I gauged the width. It would be enough, I hoped. I took Kendall's gem in my fingers and pushed it into the wound. It was a tight fit, but I managed to get it in there. I pushed it as deep as I could reach with my finger before I used magic to telekinetically staple the entry wound shut. I dove into myself with my Blood Magic, finding the channel I had made and the gem resting there. I used telekinesis to push the gem farther up until it rested in the middle of my breast, resting half an inch from my breastbone. That should be secure enough. Using Blood Magic, I told my body to heal quickly.

Now I had two such gems in my left breast and if I ever let someone fondle it they was sure to feel at least one of them.

Not that I had any intention of sleeping with a Skylander.

On the other hand, I had felt really tempted earlier when Zandor, Valia and I had shared that moment...

No. I was planning on destroying this place, not finding a partner. I shook myself to rid my mind of those thoughts and I used handfuls of snow to scrub the blood from my skin. A slow trickle welled out between the staples, smearing along the crease of my breast, before dripping down my skin. I applied a telekinetic patch to increase the pressure and hopefully slowing the bleeding until it healed. I put on my clothes and armour and recast my warming spells. My teeth were chattering and my hands were shaking. But I would soon get warm again. I got to work on casting my second focusing tower construction spell in one waking period, fighting my thoughts and emotions all the way. I was so distracted by my encounter with Uncle Kenny and mother that it took me almost twice the time to emblazon the first spells as it should have. Luckily for me the rest of the process was more or less automated by now or I would never have managed to finish this tower. I sat down to wait for the reinforcement spells to finish.
