I gauged the power level of the power core and found that it would probably be enough. I might have to add some of my personal power to finish it all up.

I must have nodded off for I woke some unknown time later, yet again chilled to the bone and shivering. I recast my warming spells and inspected the sigils. Almost all of it was reinforced with materialised magic and I spent another three quarters of an hour finishing it up. I was beyond exhausted by then and ready to keel over. But if I did then I would not see another dawn. If I fell asleep up here in the cold without preparing warming spells I would most certainly freeze to death. And that would hamper my plans. Couldn't let that happen. I bound the sole remaining power core to the spells and sat back. I didn't bother scrying to check on the progress, I just stared dully at nothing while my mind went blissfully blank. Suddenly the ground shook and stone and gravel shot to the sky. I didn't even bother with a shield. Instead I just trusted my armour to take the brunt of the damage the falling rocks might cause. Magic flowed over me in a wave, reinvigorating my drained Soul. With a gesture I tied the fount to the spells that would construct the focusing tower and I remained seated. I felt the general magic field strengthen around me and I recast my warming spells idly. The sun rose fully above the trees, illuminating the forests and plains beyond the mountains. The view was breathtaking and I was struck by a sudden home-sickness. It reminded me so much of the view from the highest peaks behind Dun Traelak, close to the little valley where mother had once dreamed of building a cottage to share with mom, me and Malkareth. The cottage that was never to be.

"You work yourself too hard," mother said from beside me.

I was so worn out that I was not even surprised when she had suddenly appeared and I leaned my head against her cold shoulder. Her arm folded comfortingly around me.

"I have a job to do," I whispered and inhaled her scent. Even as an Archon she smelled just as I remembered. Sweat, magic and the lilac soap that mom had always favoured. It smelled like safety. Like childhood.

"And even so it is important to take care of yourself. If you keel over, then who will finish the job?"

"That's rich, coming from you," I snorted. I could practically hear her smile.

"Let's say that death has given me some perspective of how I lived my life."

"Do you have any regrets?"

"Plenty."

"If you could go back and do it all over, what would you change?"

"I would not have killed that child in the Undercity. Her death haunts me still."

"That is your sole regret?"

"I would also have become a liche much earlier, before Utopeia was established. And then I would have figured out a way to get there without Mal, and I would have killed them all before they caused so much suffering."

"Then that child would have died by your hand anyway. As would I."

"Fair point. I would have done it as soon as you, Kendall and your grandmother arrived at Dun Traelak. Without the ley line to the Skylands Utopeia would have been weakened. Maybe enough to prevent the war."

"That would have been nice," I murmured and inhaled her scent again. The silence stretched between us.

"How is mom?" Mother finally asked softly.

I sat up straight and faced her. I could tell by the way her shining eyes dimmed that she already knew the answer. "She died. Almost seven years ago. After you left she was crushed."

"Oh, Spirits," mother groaned and hid her face behind her hands. "I killed her."

"No!" I blurted. "She was devastated for years, but she found a new purpose to live. Me. Kendall's kids. She adopted a score of orphans from the war. They all became her grandchildren."

A sad smile stretched my lips when I remembered grandmothers last moments.

"She was out in the courtyard, playing with them all. I hadn't seen her smile that wide since you left. She was laughing, kicking the ball. And she scored!"

Mother laughed, imagining the scene.

"Then she said that she was tired and needed a rest. The kids kept playing and grandmother sat down on the ground in that sunny spot by the wall where you and mom used to sit and watch Mal's egg in the fount."

"I remember," mother said with a wan smile.

"She drew her last breath there, with a smile on her face. Watching over the kids that she loved."

A single shining tear trickled down mothers cheek. "I'm glad to hear that she passed peacefully, happy. I hate that I couldn't be there."

I reached out and clasped her crystal hand, squeezing it even though she could not feel it. To me, that arm was as real as her actual arm had been. "You gave up everything so that those kids could live happily. You made that possible, mother."

"I suppose."

We watched the sun rise together for a while more.

"So, I'm back here again," mother stated.

"Yes. And I am so happy that you are."

"Where did you hide the gem that Kendall gave you?"

"Inside my breast," I admitted and pointed at my left breast.

Kyra snickered. "Clever. They won't sense it in there. Good thing you have enough volume to mask it with."

I bumped her shoulder with mine. "I got those from you," I said with a snort.

Mother eyed me sideways. "You do remember that I am not your real mother, right?"

"Nonsense. You are the realest mother I've ever had. I don't even remember my birth mother."

"It's 'the most real mother', love. Not 'the realest'. I can't believe that Greg didn't teach you how to talk properly."

I giggled and poked her in the ribs. If she was feeling chipper enough to joke around, I was happy. I hadn't seen her smile or hear her make a joke since mom was killed.

"It's good to see you, little one. I've missed you."

"It's good to see you too, mother. I can't express how much I have missed you."

"To me it's only been a few days since I died, yet more than a decade have has passed."

"I hope that you get to see the statue of you and mom at Dun Traelak," I murmured into her shoulder.

Mother pulled back, a puzzled expression on her face. "Statue?"

"Yeah," I nodded. "Uralnor's grandson made it. Even though he was remarkably young when he last saw you it is very accurate. He even managed to capture your scowl."

"My scowl?" She asked, indignant.

I nodded vigorously. "Yeah, the scowl you get when you are about to do something dangerous."

"I don't scowl," mother protested, scowling.

"Okay, mother. He made that part up," I lied.

Mother nodded, and then her expression grew serious. "You are really planning to kill all those Skylanders?"

"I am," I affirmed.

"They are no threat to anyone," mother pointed out.

"Neither were all of those people on the Skylands. Yet you killed them anyway."

She sighed deeply. "Yes, I did. But they were a threat. As long as the Skylands was there, it would always be a threat. The Skylanders can't help themselves, they destroy and pillage everything they can reach."

"And so will these Skylanders. Just a few days ago Micah, he's the leader down there, decided to just genocide those savages that attacked me without investigating why or if they were a threat. Just kill them all."

Mother frowned, but said nothing.

"There are no innocent Skylanders," I stated, using her words against her.

"True. Not even me. I have deserved this fate many times over. I wouldn't wish it on you."

"But what about Uncle Kenny and his husband? What about Alissa? Are they not innocent? What about me?"

I couldn't believe that I had finally voiced my deepest doubts about my whole plan. If there were no innocent Skylanders, then did that mean that I would have to kill Kendall and Alissa? Would I have to commit suicide to avoid being a hypocrite? Mother thought about that for far too long and I started to dread her answer.

"You are not Skylanders. You are Bizhjerai."

"But we were all born on the Skylands," I protested even though I felt relief when I heard her words.

"Being a Skylander is not about where you were born. It is a way of life, love. You said it yourself. The Skylander Chief decided to just kill an entire tribe after a single encounter. They want to preserve the Skylander ways down there. Eventually they will grow powerful and seek to expand. We will have the same situation as with Utopeia in time."

"They have to be destroyed," I stated.

"Yes," mother agreed. "As long as they seek to take what is not theirs, to exploit everything for their own gain without regard to who they hurt, they will remain Skylanders."

I mulled that over for a moment. "It will take many years to hunt down those who do not come here," I finally sighed.

"Did you not listen to me? Those who do not come here must be those that no longer hold to the Skylander ideals. They've forsaken their own people. They are not Skylanders any more."

"That... That makes sense."

"Some of them will be, but they will be few. Powerless. Insignificant."

My doubts eased. "Thank you, mother. You are the only one I have ever been able to discuss this with."

"Anytime, little one. Anytime. At least I think so. I don't control when I can appear like this, but I hope that we will meet again."

"I'm certain that we will," I smiled.

Mother flickered.

"No! Don't go yet!" I cried out.

Mother smiled wanly. "Seems that I am running out of time. Take care of yourself, love. You are no good to anyone, especially yourself, if you work yourself into an early grave. Be safe, my child," she smiled and faded out of existence.

"I will," I whispered to the void where she had been.

I sat down heavily to collect myself. Even though her words had brought a sense of calm over my heart meeting her again had been emotional. I really needed a rest. I waited another hour to gather strength and make sure that the second focusing tower was coming along nicely before I stood up.

I stood with my toes peeking over the precipice and I soaked in the beauty of my vantage. Then I leaped. The wind howled in my ears, whipping my hair that extended past the rim of my helmet. I used my wings to guide my fall so that I didn't crash into anything. I didn't use the primary jets and let myself pick up speed. I activated the telekinetic membrane between the boning of my wings to catch air and transform my fall into a glide. Before long I saw our camp down below. I guided myself to land next to my friends who sat around the small fire. They looked up when I flared my jets and swung my legs down to absorb the impact. The ground struck my feet and my legs folded. Instead of a three-point landing I tumbled and rolled across the ground, through the fire, sending sparks and embers all over the place. My wings cracked and shattered and my helmet rang when it struck a large stone. I finally came to a stop against a tree trunk where I lay groaning.

"Mary!" Zandor yelled and came barrelling toward me. "Are you alright?"

"Just bruised. I guess I was more tired than I expected," I mumbled and accepted his large paw and he pulled me to my feet. I couldn't remain standing and I collapsed against him. His strong arms encircled me and I relaxed into his strength. He smelled like sweat, smoke and hard labour. A good smell. My knees buckled and Zandor scooped me off my feet. I groggily raised my arms around his neck and entwined my fingers in his green hair.

"Such a big boy," I giggled.

"She's delirious!" He snapped at his friends.

I was jostled gently as he ran back to camp and lowered me to the ground.

"Get her helmet off," Valia commanded softly and thick fingers probed at my neck. Eventually my helmet came off and Valia pulled my head into her soft lap. Her face was soft and angelic above me and her big platinum eyes was worried.

"Drink, Mary," she urged me and put something hard against my lips. She tipped the water skin back and tepid water spilled across my teeth. I coughed, spraying her face with water. She blinked but didn't recoil, the water dripping down her face like perspiration. A shimmering droplet came close to the corner of her mouth and her pink tongue darted out to absorb it. I was mesmerised and my hand rose of its own accord to cup her cheek. She leaned her head into my palm.

"So pretty," I mumbled and then I passed out.


---

## Chapter 11 — CHAPTER ELEVEN Hangover {#chapter-11}

CHAPTER ELEVEN

Hangover

I woke up with a start and instantly winced. The familiar Blood Magic hangover gripped my skull like a vice. I was inside a tent, and I was alone. I forced myself to move and I put my hand in the empty bedroll next to me. It was still warm. Someone had just been sleeping there. The bedroll was almost on top of mine so I assumed that whoever it had been had accidentally tossed it onto me when they left to begin their day. I was parched and I dimly remembered someone pouring water all over my face before everything went dark. I braved the bright light that spilled through the cloth of the tent and it felt as if someone had rammed two red-hot nails into my eyes and all the way to the back of my head. I fell to my knees with a pained groan. My left hand gripped the hilt of mothers dagger, my thumb making the familiar movements around the gem. Strangely it helped against the pain.

"Mary!" A female voice gasped and I heard the rustle of clothing before small hands grabbed under my armpits and helped me to my feet. "What happened?"

I cracked my eyes open and saw that it was Valia helping me. "I could ask you the same thing," I croaked through dry lips.

"Sit," she commanded and pushed me gently onto a rock by the fire. She fussed by the fire before pressing a tin cup of tea into my hands. "Drink that, slowly."

"Yes, ma'am," I whispered and obeyed.

"Don't you remember?" She finally asked cautiously.

I shook my head, dreading the manner of my humiliation that she was about to describe to me. What had I said when I was more unconscious than awake?

"You fell. You came in too fast with your Angel armour and couldn't absorb the shock so you fell. You rolled through the fire and your clothes caught."

"That would explain this shirt," I observed and pinched the soft cloth between thumb and index finger. It was a plain shirt, and incredibly tight. At least it wasn't too short for me. I assumed that it belonged to Valia. While she was taller than I was, most people were, I had a fair bit more stuffing in the hip and chest area.

"How did you even stuff me into this thing?" I asked idly.

"With a lot of cursing," Valia smirked.

"You shouldn't have bothered," I muttered and then realised what an asshole I sounded like. "Not like I don't appreciate it," I hastened to add, "but I don't mind sleeping in the nude."

"Don't sweat it," Wylah said curtly as she sat cross-legged on the ground next to Valia. "It was her pleasure," she added with an expressionless face and made grabbing motions with both hands.

Valia coloured sweetly and hid her face in her hands.

So she had gotten a bit handsy, had she?

"It was the only way to get those tits into that shirt," Wylah said maliciously with an amused glance at her friends discomfort. "Just grab a handful and stuff it in."

"Wylah, please," Valia pleaded but I wasn't paying attention.

Icy cold spread through my stomach. Valia had been groping my breasts? A part of me was delighted, another part was mortified. Had she felt the hard lumps of the crystals I had implanted? Had she seen the fresh wound beneath my left breast? My body was on high alert, ready to spring into action at a moments notice. But there were little I could do if they decided to question me about it. I had no armour, no weapons. All I had was this too tight shirt that just barely kept the sun from my privates and what little meagre magic I could summon in my hungover state. Even Valia would be able to overpower me with her pinky in my current state.

"Ah, the sleeping beauty awakes," Zandor announced as he walked into the camp. A gutted deer hung over his massive shoulder, congealing blood dripping down his stout chest. I had never been as attracted to a man as I was to him at that moment. He just oozed masculinity and capability, bringing food back to the table. Something primal twisted in my lower belly, a carnal appreciation of a provider. Felaan emerged from behind Zandor, the smaller man had been completely obscured behind him.

"Glad to see you awake," Felaan greeted me and flowed smoothly to stand behind Wylah. His hand fell to rest on her shoulder and her head tilted back slightly to rest against his thigh. Both movements were completely natural for both of them. Yes, there were clearly something there between them whether they knew it yet or not.

"What are you smiling about?" Zandor asked me cheerfully and dropped the deer roughly to the ground. "Apart from how lovely a day it is?"

"Just happy that we are done here. I just need to rest up a bit and then we can leave."

"Splendid," Zandor beamed and drew his big hunting knife. "I love the outfit you chose for her," he told Valia while pointing at me with the huge knife and then froze, blushed and promptly tried to hide behind the deer as he started skinning it.

"You three should just fuck and get it over with," Wylah said casually.

I was dumbfounded.

"Excuse me?" Valia squeaked, her face turning bright red.

"The tension is thick enough to cut with that thick, long, hard knife Zandor has," Wylah cooed huskily with amusement glinting in her eyes.

"While it is amusing to see you tip-toe around one another," Felaan continued smoothly, "it would be better for all of us if you just accept reality and enjoy yourselves. Who knows, we might all be dead tomorrow."

The sudden turn of the conversation made me deeply uncomfortable and I found only one way to redirect it.

"Look who's talking," I said and then cringed. Well done with the redirection of the conversation, idiot.

"What's that supposed to mean?" Wylah snapped, suddenly angry.

"Nevermind," I said and busied myself with drinking the last of my tea.

The silence was awkward, punctuated by the slicing and ripping sounds of Zandor skinning the deer.

Valia coughed daintily. "So, how did it go with the crossings?" She finally asked.

"It went well. I used Blood Magic so that I could work through the night. I opened both crossings which brings us at least a day ahead of the plan. Now we just have to wait for the receiver tower to finish. I can sleep off the effects of my hangover until then."

Zandor wrenched a hind limb around and popped it loose and handed it over to Felaan. I felt my eyes widen. He hadn't even cut into the muscles and tendons before he just casually ripped the limb off. And it didn't even look as if he exerted himself! The dark man accepted it and drew a smaller paring knife from his belt and started peeling thin strips of venison.

Wylah added branches to the fire while Valia started to nimbly weave slender saplings together into a sort of mesh for smoking meat. It was obvious that the four of them had butchered many an animal together. Soon Valia had a sort of loosely weaved basket and the branches Wylah had put on the fire were billowing a thick smoke. Valia doused the basked with water and placed it over the fire. Each thin slice that Felaan carved went onto the basket for drying and smoking. Soon the basked was almost entirely covered and a growing pile of venison slices formed on a flat rock Wylah had found. I watched with interest. While I knew that the Clans smoked meat daily, I had never seen it happen. It was just one of those things that happened in a castle that someone like me never even thought about. Such things had been taken care for me. Maybe I was a little bit spoiled. But I worked hard with Kendall to make improvements to the keep. The first strip of meat that was done was scooped off the basket with a stick and Valia juggled it between her hands until it cooled. She offered it to me.

"You need to eat, regain your strength," she said softly and blushed when Wylah made kissing noises from behind her.

But as opposed to earlier we all just chuckled without any awkwardness at all. I ate the venison and it might have been the best food I had ever tasted. I moaned in delight and couldn't wait for the next one. Valia had sat down with her back to me to keep watch on the meat and she just extended a piece over her shoulder without looking. Without thinking, as if it were the most natural thing in the world to do, I lowered my head and took it from her fingers with my mouth. My lips lingered a moment over her grease-covered fingertips before we both realised what had just happened at the same instant. Valia's head snapped around, her eyes wide, and I jerked backward and blushed. I still ate the venison. Valia's lips twitched upward but then she hastily turned back around.

"She's never fed me like that before," Zandor gruffed and peeled the last bit of meat off the last rib. Nothing remained of the deer apart from bloody bones, the pelt and the head. Zandor scraped the pelt clean and rolled it up before gathering the bones and head and throwing it into the forest. He looked magnificent with blood spattered across his chest and face and smeared all the way to his elbows.

"I'll go clean up in the stream," he said before giving me a stern look. "Don't eat all of the meat, little lady. I'm hungry too!" And then he was off.

"Little lady?" I asked the others in mock outrage.

"You are quite tiny," Felaan said absently while carving another thin slice from the rib he was preparing.

"I'm not tiny!" I protested.

"No, she's not," Valia supported me. Then the evil little bitch threw a mischievous smile over her shoulder. "I think 'petite' is the correct term."

I groaned and threw the last little bit of meat in my hand at her and to my surprise her head darted to the side, quick like a viper, and caught it in her mouth. She winked, swallowed and licked a bit of grease off her lip.

I picked my jaw up from the ground and held my tongue. I was not certain that I would have been able to make a coherent sound at that moment. Zandor soon returned and rummaged around in his saddle bags for a moment before returning with a pipe and a small bag.

"I didn't know you smoked, Zandor," I observed.

"It's a habit I picked up in the south," he said and took a pinch of the dark plant matter out of the pouch and stuffed it into his pipe. "I've heard that the Bizhjerai smoke some good stuff. I've always wanted to try it."

My shoulders tensed up and then realisation struck me. It was the mention of my Clan name that had made me realise it. Valia had stripped me naked. There was no way that she hadn't seen the Bizhjerai Rune on my shoulder. Hadn't she recognised it? Perhaps not. She had been quite young when Utopeia fell and she wouldn't have had the chance to see one up close since then. Valia chose that moment to turn her head around to smile shyly at me. I could see no guile in her eyes, no sign that she might be ready to betray me the moment we returned to the Groundlands. I returned her smile stiffly and a small crease appeared between her eyebrows as she frowned. I tried to make my smile more natural and her eyes twinkled before she returned her attention to what she was doing. I couldn't see what she was working on since her back was to me.

"Mary, look!" Zandor said and pulled my attention from my brooding. He held his pipe between his teeth and frowned at his extended index finger. A small jet of flame erupted from it and he used it to light his pipe. He puffed a few times until it was well lit before he took it out of his mouth.

"I've been practising!" He enthused.

"Good job," I encouraged him. "The most difficult part of learning magic is now over for you. Now there is just years upon years of studying so that you may learn everything that is possible to do."

"Nah, that ain't for me. As long as I can toss the odd fireball around and poke my enemies I'm good. I should probably learn to shield myself though, that seems handy."

"It is. And you should," I agreed.

Zandor extended his pipe in my direction. "Wanna try?"

My stomach rebelled at the thought. "Maybe another time. I'm feeling a bit too hungover to introduce new drugs into my system."

Zandor shrugged. "Just say the word. This lot here does not appreciate good herb." He took a deep pull of his pipe as if to punctuate his words.

Felaan finished with the last of the meat and he stood up. "I will also go and wash off," he announced and left silently. Wylah watched him go and then her eyes darted toward me. She cleared her throat loudly.

"I will, uhm, go and fetch more water," she said with a strange tone of voice.

"But Wylah, we have plenty of water," Valia said sweetly.

The taller woman glared at her shorter friend. "Someone seems to have spilled all of it."

"No, it is all there," Valia said just as sweetly, knowing full well what was going on.

"No. It's spilled," Wylah persisted.

Valia exchanged a mischievous look with me and then she extended her leg, kicking the pot of water over.

"Oops, how terribly clumsy of me. Wylah, would you be a dear and fetch us some more water? I'll owe you one," Valia said with a wide smile.

"Fine. If you insist," Wylah snapped and snatched the pot from the ground before walking in the direction of the stream with purposeful strides.

When she was finally out of earshot we all broke out in laughter.

"They are so adorable," Valia announced.

"I agree," Zandor said with a cloud of blue smoke. "I hope they can finally admit that they care about one another and just get on with it."

The earlier awkwardness returned and the three of us looked away from the other two.

After a long moment I cleared my throat.

"So, Valia. Have you also been practising with your magic?"

"Yes," she said, still refusing to look at me.

"Show me," I commanded but didn't really manage the tone of voice that I had hoped for.

Valia extended her hand toward me, fingers up, palm flat. Her face scrunched up in concentration and I could not help but smile at how adorable she looked. Then a light shockwave caressed my face and sent my hair flying.

Valia was panting from exertion, but her eyes were wide and her lips smiled.

"Very good!" I encouraged her. "I would gauge that to be on par with Zandor's progress, but geared more toward force than fire."

Valia beamed and the awkwardness melted away.

"Tell me, my student, will you pursue a higher education? Or are you happy 'poking at things' like that brute over there?"

"Hey! Brutes are useful," Zandor protested.

Both of us girls ignored him.

"I'd like to learn more. To do what you do, Mary. I'd like to be a Construction Mage," she said earnestly with a shy smile.

"And I'd be happy to teach you," I said with a warm smile. "But not today. Today your teacher is hungover," I said with a groan when my head suddenly throbbed. I lay down on the grass, tugging on the hem of the shirt to keep me decent.

"Now feed me more meat and I will consider when your next lesson will be," I said playfully.

I hadn't meant it as a command but Valia seemed happy to oblige and she plopped a slice of meat into my mouth, her fingernail lingering on my bottom lip. Her eyes smoldering while she stared into mine and I felt my nipples suddenly strain against the tight cloth covering them.

"Just pretend that I'm not here," Zandor quipped. "I won't mind if you'd like to go at it."

The moment between me and the other woman vanished and she turned away from me. I was sad to see her turn. My fingers found a pebble and I heaved it at Zandor.

"Shut your dirty mouth, you lecher," I admonished him with a mock scowl.

The pebble just bounced from his bare chest and he snorted a small cloud of smoke before knocking his pipe against his boot to empty it before grinding the embers with his heel.

Something about that action settled firmly in my nethers. Perhaps Wylah and Felaan had been correct. Maybe we should just fuck and get it out of our systems.

If this kept up it was going to be a long day. I settled back down, trying to relax and recover. Soon we would return to the Groundlands and whatever awaited us there. Back into the belly of the beast.


---

## Chapter 12 — CHAPTER TWELVE Construction Mage {#chapter-12}

CHAPTER TWELVE

Construction Mage

I much preferred to be out on an expedition with my friends than being in the Groundlands even though the tasks set before me here were more interesting. But there were little camaraderie here. Everyone was too busy with improving the camp. The receiving tower would finish up in a day or two and then the whole camp would be promptly covered with a magic containing shield and the general magic field within would be pumped into higher densities. Anyone with the skill to use magic but lacked the natural strength to accomplish much would be free to do what they could. I would be able to start construction on domiciles and defences. I had even prepared the spells that would construct turrets and mortars and bricks for the walls and housings. They were charged until just before the ignition point, the spells kept alive by my power cores so that they did not dissipate. As soon as the magic field gained enough strength I would activate them. When the magic field was dense enough the surplus power would be channelled into forming power cores deep beneath the earth for future use. It was later that night that the first signs of trouble appeared. The sun had just set and I was on the way to meet my friends for supper, something that we had started doing every night since we returned from the last foray outside of the Groundlands. The sky to the north lit up in an orange glow, the warning flare from the northern outpost. The automatic rifle turrets had engaged something. Was it the savages? I broke out into a run in search of Micah. I eventually found him in his tent where he was drawing something on a piece of parchment. Knowing him, it was probably schematics for our defences. You can take the general out of the war but you can never take the war out of the general.

"Micah!" I gasped as soon as I entered his tent.

"What is it?" He asked gravelly without raising his eyes from his parchment.

"The northern outpost has engaged something," I blurted.

Now he raised his eyes to look at me. "Interesting. The scouts I sent earlier found nothing but tracks. They don't appear to be numerous. Do you think they can damage the outpost?"

"No. Probably not. Unless they have more advanced magic or technology that we don't know about."

"As long as it is just the orange flare, I think that we are safe to ignore it. But just to be cautious, I will dispatch scouts to have a look at whatever it was that your turrets fired on."

"Sounds reasonable," I agreed. "You should give them this," I said and handed him the control token.

"What's this?" He asked and took the little crystal in the palm of his meaty hand.

"It controls the turrets. It will switch them on or off if the scouts need to approach the tower itself." I looked away sheepishly. "The turrets have no friend or foe recognition. Anything that moves gets shot at," I explained.

"Fair enough. What's the range of the turrets, and what's the range of this thing?"

Pertinent question. "About a hundred yards for the turrets. A hundred and twenty five for the token."

"Noted. I will ensure that the scouts are informed. Dismissed."

I nodded, still a little breathless, and retreated from his tent. Still feeling a little wary, I headed out to find my friends. Perhaps we should ride up there and take a look. We would be able to get there much quicker than the scouts if we used our bikes. I briefly wondered why Micah hadn't asked me to build more bikes and teach his scouts how to use them but I just concluded that he probably didn't find that to be a useful way for me to spend my time. As the only advanced Construction Mage in the Groundlands there were always more important things for me to do. I found my friends around our cook fire, we had all moved our tents closer to one another and shared a fire. Felaan's cooking was vastly preferable to Ms Vorka's, so I hadn't grumbled too much at suddenly getting crowded. At least I liked these people, despite my efforts at not getting too attached to them. They were all staring at the orange flare in the northern sky.

"The flare," Zandor greeted me and pointed needlessly.

"I'm aware," I responded snarkily but with a grin. "Micah are sending scouts up there. I was thinking that perhaps we should take the bikes and go there ahead of them. We are way faster than them."

Wylah grinned, she did love the bike I had made for her. Valia focused on stirring the pot and Zandor stared at the flare with a worried frown.

Felaan stroked his chin while he thought before he spoke.

"No. Let the scouts handle it. You said that the receiving tower will soon be done. You will be better utilised here."

He made sense, and I didn't like it. "But what if the tower is under attack? We need it!" I cringed at the whiny sound of my voice.

"Not really. We only chose that one because it was the closest, but it is less than a third of our projected power input. Even if we lost it there are more crossings to open, right?"

"Right," I agreed, trying and failing to not sound petulant.

"And besides, the flare is only orange. If it was red then possibly I would have gone along with you."

"Fine," I said and threw up my hands. I sat down on the block of wood that served as stools around the fire. "What's for dinner?"

Zandor put his large hand on my head, lightly scratching at my scalp. "You're adorable when you pout," he said in a rare moment of sincere honesty.

"I'm not pouting!" I pouted up at his towering frame but I could not keep my eyes from growing lidded and a small sigh of contentment escaped me when his fingernails scraped across my scalp.

"You are, and he's right," Valia smirked across the pot. "And to answer your question, it's venison stew. Again."

I sighed. "I never thought that I would say this, but I am almost getting sick and tired of eating venison."

"I saw boar tracks to the south-west when I ranged the other day," Felaan said. "I will try to hunt it for some variety."

He glanced at Zandor. "I might need help carrying the beast back, you want in, big guy?"

"Of course. I too would prefer to eat something else. I had my fill of monotonous meals when I was still on the Skylands," the large man answered with excitement shining in his green eyes.

We chatted while we ate, Valia, Zandor and I borderline flirting while Wylah and Felaan didn't even try to hide their feelings any more. After whatever happened between them at the stream on our last foray they had been more transparent.

That didn't mean much with the two stoic warriors but there had been some overt displays of affection instead of the natural comfort they had exhibited earlier. I was happy for them. They made a great couple.

Then my mood soured. I was planning to kill them, after all. They were Skylanders. They had come to the Groundlands to reestablish the Skylander ways. But I still liked them. They were friendly, helpful and earnest in their work. But they supported genocidal people. Would accept it because it made their lives easier.

"Why so glum?" Zandor asked me between spoonfuls of stew.

"I'm just thinking about those creatures. Micah wants to wipe them out."

"They attacked us, remember?" Zandor shrugged.

"I do. But we don't know what provoked their attack. They might have perceived our presence as an attack."

"Doesn't matter," he opined. "We weren't hurting them directly, and they tried to kill you. That's all there is to me."

I digested that in silence, picking at the remains of my stew. He was correct. They had attacked me first.

"But what if we were harming them directly? Perhaps the tower is built on a thoroughway for them and the automatic turrets are killing them? What if they used those ley lines for something and we are now tapping their resources?"

"Doesn't matter," Felaan stated with certainty. "They could have approached us to talk, if they are able. If they are not sentient, then they are merely dangerous beasts. Either way diplomacy will not work."

He did have a point there. But I still had one argument left.

"What if they are to the Groundlands what the Clans were to Utopeia? Our parents believed the Clans to be primitive savages, and yet our people was all but destroyed."

"If it weren't for the Traitor, the exact opposite would have happened," Valia pointed out and scraped the last of the stew into Zandor's waiting bowl.

"And that makes it alright? They lack the power to resist, and thus we can do whatever we wish?" I said unhappily.

"Well... Yes," Wylah said curtly. "That is the Skylander way."

I sighed deeply. I had my answer.

But I couldn't give up. Mother had also been a Skylander. As had Kendall and Alissa. They had all become Bizhjerai. There were still time for my friends to become something other than Skylanders. Perhaps not Bizhjerai, but something else. And then I would not have to kill them.

I woke up with a start. I knew instantly that it was in the middle of the night, and I was not alone in my tent. I didn't want to inform the intruder about the fact that I was awake by casting a light spell, which would also ruin my night vision, so I just remained there, still and breathing calmly while my eyes adjusted to the dim light of the moon that entered through the tent flap.

Slowly I could make out a shape standing over my feet, looking down at me. Finally the shape shifted and a narrow ray of moonlight fell across a face.

"Valia?" I asked with surprise when I recognised her. "What are you doing here?"

"I can't sleep," she whispered pitifully. "May I sleep here?"

"Of course," I said even though I did not understand.

I cast a dim light spell so that she wouldn't trip over something on the ground. She was wearing a short and thin shift that ended at the middle of her thighs.

"I only have the one bedroll though," I said and threw it open. I had fashioned my sleeping nook by opening the bedroll fully and used it as insulation against the cold ground. I didn't need blankets since I heated my tent with magic.

Valia crouched down to all fours and crawled over me, positioning herself between the wall of the tent and me without speaking. Her head ended up on my shoulder and her arm around my ribs. She was stiff as a plank and her fingers dug into my skin. But I was more durable than I looked and she didn't hurt me. In fact, I quite enjoyed the way she clung to me and the way that she hadn't flinched when she saw that I slept in nothing but my thong.

She shuddered silently and something warm and wet trickled onto my skin. She was crying. I curled the arm she was lying on around her and clutched her close.

I didn't ask what was wrong, nor did I particularly care. What was important was that she was in pain and in need of comfort. So I gave it to her gladly. I stroked her hair and cooed into her ear that everything would be alright. Over and over again until she fell into an exhausted sleep.

I must have dozed off after she did for I woke up when when a rush of magic swept over me, refilling my reserves. The receiver tower must have finished in the night and charged up enough to erect a magic containing field and starting to pump it full of magic. The magic levels were only slightly elevated but I could feel it growing denser by the moment. I had so much work to do now that I had the fuel to do so. Valia must have been jostled awake by my squirming for she groaned and opened her eyes. Our eyes locked and her face lit up with a lovely smile that I could not help but reciprocate. Then her face fell and she averted her eyes.

"I'm sorry about last night," she whispered.

I reached out and grabbed her chin, forcing her to look at me. "Don't ever apologise for having emotions, Valia. I am glad that I was here to help. I'm glad that you came to me."

We stared into one another's eyes for a long while.

"Thank you," she whispered. "Thank you for being there for me when I am weak. And for not asking me what was wrong."

"You will tell me when you are ready," I said with utter certainty. "Until then, I will be here for you."

Her eyes lit up with an inner fire and she abruptly leaned closer and pressed her full lips against mine. I was stunned frozen for a heartbeat before I found the presence of mind to kiss her back. It was over quickly and she retreated hastily, her cheeks colouring a deep crimson.

"I'm sorry," she apologised sheepishly.

"Don't apologise," I told her dreamily. "And kiss me again."

She smiled and leaned close again. When our lips touched this time there were no hesitation and her tongue thrust in between my teeth. I was a little surprised by her sudden aggression, but I was more than pleased. My hand snaked across her hip to press into the small of her back and her own hand slid up my ribs to cup my breast. Her thumb flicked across my nipple, teasing it into hardness. I moaned into her mouth and I felt her lips stretch into a wide smile. I slid my hand lower to grab a handful of firm ass, sliding the hem of her shift up to uncover my prize.

Her own small fingers hooked into the strap of my thong, tugging on it playfully.

"Mary! Micah wants you on those defences now that there's magic. No time to waste!" Zandor yelled from right outside my tent. "If you're not out here in a few moments I'm coming in to get you. Micah's orders." He paused and when he spoke I could hear the devilish grin that was on his face. "And you being indecent won't stop me. He said 'no excuses'."

If I hadn't been so frustrated by his untimely interruption I would have at least chuckled at his spot on impression of Micah's gruff voice. Instead I groaned and had to push Valia away. The smoldering look she gave me sent a jolt into my groin and my breath hitched.

"To be continued," I whispered and the smile on her face that my words evoked was the most beautiful thing I had seen in a long time.

"I'm coming," I called out to Zandor. "Unfortunately not in the way I need right now," I grumbled under my breath and Valia smirked at me while adjusting her shift. I lamented that I hadn't had the opportunity to see her more properly while I'd had it hiked up above her waist. Fortunately for me I hadn't dissolved my clothes yesterday and I quickly tugged my trousers over my legs, slung my belt around my hips before searching for my bra. I finally found it in Valia's hands and she had it pressed to her own chest and looked down at the not even remotely filled cups.

"What the fuck?" She mouthed at me with wide eyes and a lascivious grin.

I smirked and snatched the garment from her hands and put it on with practised ease before slipping on my blouse. With a last long look at my friend I rose to leave the tent before I halted and looked over my shoulder.

"I'm gonna do a lot of building today, wanna tag along? Start your training as a Construction Mage?"

Her face lit up like a sunrise and she nodded vigorously.

"Come find me once you're dressed," I said and paused. "Wear something sexy," I finished with a wink and exited the tent.

Zandor waited impatiently outside. "Took you long enough," he said before he had a proper look at me. His eyes dipped to my unbuttoned blouse and what it failed completely in obscuring. His eyes bugged out and his entire body froze.

I snapped my fingers in front of his face. Not so much as a flinch.

"Huh," I said to myself. "I think I broke him. Oh, well. I better go find Micah on my own then."

And with that, I left the human statue behind while buttoning my blouse. I was still a fair bit flustered after my encounter with Valia so I left the top four buttons undone to let the cool breeze inside. It was a little bit more risque than how I usually wore it but I was still in the mood. I found Micah in the command tent, as was usual after I had reduced the need for manpower at the lumber mill. I expected him to get his hands dirty once the construction really picked up its pace again.

"Construction Mage, reporting for duty!" I said and stood at attention and snapped a salute that would have been fit for the parade grounds. Unfortunately my squared shoulders strained the heroic button struggling to keep my blouse shut until I feared it would pop off and I relaxed my stance a smidgen. Micah gave me a quick glance before looking back down at the document in his big hands.

"No need for that, Mary, we're not a military unit."

"Fair enough," I said and assumed my normal straight-backed posture. I was too short to waste any precious inches by slouching.

"And for the record; if we were, your clothing is not up to regulations and I'd have you flogged for showing up with your tits out like that." The calm and measured way he said that left very little doubt in my mind that he was serious. He would have had me flogged without a second thought.

"Appreciate the leniency, Micah. What do you want me to start with?"

He finally looked me square in the eyes and gave me his full attention. "You're smart. And capable. You prioritise and get started. I will let you know if I need you to rethink your strategy."

I couldn't help but smile at him. I did prefer to decide what to do for myself. I found that that usually produced the best results. A task assigned by yourself to yourself were usually completed with more passion than a straight up order.

"Thank you, Micah. I appreciate your trust."

"Noted. Dismissed."

He was already back in his document when I left his tent and I reached up to my choker to manifest my Angel armour. I would need to move around in the camp quite a bit today and the Angel armour would make my travelling more efficient. I considered what the camp needed. Defences and housing, primarily. Defences meant walls, artillery and guns. Everyone already had at least a tent and while uncomfortable they would suffice as shelter until winter so I put housing at the bottom of my mental list. The artillery would be useful, but less so without walls. Most of the inhabitants who were capable of fighting already had some sort of weapon, whether it be a firearm or a close combat weapon, so more weapons were not as important as walls. Especially not against the most immediate threat, the savages. If they were numerous and came at us as one, they might be able to drown us in bodies regardless of how many guns we had. But a wall could change that, tip the balance of power in our favour. So walls it is then, I concluded just as my Angel armour finished forming up. So off to the brick manufactoriums I went. I soared above the camp and chuckled at the astonished looks and pointing from the people below as I whistled past. It was a short flight that was well within the range of the Angel armour and I touched down next to the faintly glowing skeleton of the first brick manufactorium. I invoked a complex spell from a powerstone attached to my utility gauntlet. It was one of my most commonly used spells that was applicable to almost any magical construction that would be fed by the ambient magic. I spent a moment scaling it into proportion with the manufactorium and I connected it to the ephemeral runes. The faint glow blazed into near blinding light and the entire thing started materialising. When it was finished it would look like a large cube with a crystal funnel on top and a brick-sized slot on each of the sides. The funnel was a physical manifestation of the power gathering spell to fuel the core of the cube which was the actual materialisation spell. It had four extruders that would construct inch-thick segments of bricks before pushing them out into the compartments on the sides where they would be fused together into a brick before being ejected through the slots. I had found that constructing the bricks in segments and then fusing them was somehow faster than making the bricks in their entirety. It also gave the added bonus feature of each side of the cube being able to independently produce bricks of different lengths. I frowned at the glowing spell for a moment. Currently it only supported creating bricks measured in whole inches, perhaps it would be better to make the slices thinner for more customisability? I shook my head. Later. This manufactorium would take a while to construct itself before the constructing scaffolding faded away upon completion. I would return later to hook up the outputs to something, probably a cart of some sort. That reminded me, I also needed to build some sort of power source for the carts since they would be too heavy for a man to pull once they were loaded up. And then I needed telekinesis wands for those Skylanders dedicated to construction that didn't know how to use magic themselves. I could work on that while the brick spells constructed themselves and started to produce their first stockpiles. I leaped into the sky again, carried high by the jets on my Angel armour. I had chosen to spread the brick manufactoriums out across the camp to reduce the complexity of logistics later, something that I believed to be a good idea in the early stages of settlements. Later on it would be more beneficial to have a centralised stockpile of brings somewhere that could be carted wherever they were needed. As I soared over the camp I spotted Valia struggling through the mass of people gawking at me. She spotted me at the same time and our eyes locked across the distance. She was heading the wrong way in the belief that I was to the north of camp, but now I was heading south. I waved at her to head south instead and she nodded up at me as I shot past her. I touched down next to another brick manufactorium and decided to wait for her. I wanted to show her the process. While I waited I considered my design. The funnel spell that drank magic from the ambient field was not the most efficient way to distribute power. It would work well in a dense magical field where there were few consumers since the field would seek to reach equilibrium, but when demand was high in an area there was a significant risk of starvation for some of the machines. If there were a cluster of machines in close proximity, the machines on the edges might deplete the incoming magic and the inner machines would stall. But that was not an issue. Not yet. When it became an issue I would have to split off some artificial ley lines from the receiver tower to feed the machines directly. It was a logistical headache, but it was unavoidable. Unless you had such a massive influx of magical power that it didn't matter. But if the field was that dense then there were significant risk of manifestations of Wild Magic near the source where the density would be the greatest. I had seen what Wild Magic could do. It had ravaged huge swathes of Utopeia. In fact, it had once nearly destroyed the whole world.

I saw Valia emerge from the row of tents and I waved at her. While I watched her approach a sudden thought struck me.

Why had there never, to my knowledge at least, been a severe Wild Magic incident at the Skylands? The monolithic city-state had been resting on the largest magical geyser in recorded history after all. It should have been rife with Wild Magic. On the other hand, maybe it had been, constantly. Those events would have been concentrated at the place where the power erupted from the massive ley lines, deep beneath the oceans. Probably in the air below the Skylands as well. But the density of the power would probably have decreased by dilution when it reached the Skylands that the likelihood of Wild Magic was significantly lower. And the strength of any such Wild Magic event would have been insufficient to even scratch at, much less breach, the Great Barrier. But that was all pointless conjecture. The Skylands had Fallen and the Great Geyser lay uselessly far below the ocean.

A monolithic tomb for two and a half million Skylanders. A necropolis that was the last monument to that fallen civilisation.

Unless a foolish Skylander took a Skyship out there in an attempt to rebuild the Skylands. It was entirely possible to do so, I realised with an icy sensation in the pit of my stomach. I could do it.

But why would I? Life had sucked on the Skylands except for the privileged few. Life was much better here. But if I ever needed to save those that I loved, if the Clans were ever driven into the ocean... Maybe it wouldn't be so bad to have a refuge waiting, a refuge that was almost impervious to attack. A place sheltered by terrain, elements and magic. I filed that thought away in the back of my mind when Valia sauntered up to me.

I looked her up and down and was a little disappointed that she was wearing her normal clothing. The only concession she had made to my request that she wear something sexy was that instead of buttoning up her blouse she had left it completely open and instead knotted it between her small breasts. Though it did have an appealing effect on her, showing off her narrow waist and slender ribcage. Her just-a-handful breasts were not large enough to produce any actual cleavage, but the cloth twisted and hugged them to really display their perky shape. And I just loved the way her hip bones peeked above the hem of her trousers.

"I don't own any sexy clothes," she pouted apologetically.

I couldn't help but grin and gave her a very obvious leer from head to toe. "This..." I said and gestured to encompass her whole outfit. "This works for me," I said with a wink and was awarded with a blush. But she didn't look away this time and her big platinum eyes captivated me when they caught the rising sunlight.

Finally she broke her hold on me by glancing at the magical construct next to me.

"Is this magic?" She asked with an awestruck voice.

"Yeah. You can finally see it now?"

I had assumed that she would be able see it now that she had learned to cast her first spells but I hadn't actually verified it with her.

"It's beautiful," she whispered by way of confirmation.

I looked at the construct again. I had seen so much magic in my life that it usually failed to awe me, but her fresh perspective brought something out of the dark recesses of my mind. I found myself admiring the clean lines of the spells and how the geometry interlinked in synergistic swirls. I compared it to the first few spells I had cast and saw the vast difference. Those spells had been either a cluttered mess, the spells interfering or leaking power, or they had been a disconnected shamble with very inefficient bottle necks where they interfaced. But this spell was neither of that. The spells that could boost one another were positioned just right and those that could interfere where kept the optimal distance apart. Of course, I had refactored this particular spell many a time, each time making it just a little bit better.

"I guess it is," I finally agreed.

"But it's so complex! How did you ever learn to build all of this?"

"Trial and error," I said with a grin. "Mostly error. And years of apprenticeship."

"What does it do?" She asked, unable to tear her eyes away.

"Bricks."

Now she really did look at me, her mouth slightly ajar with surprise. "Just bricks?"

I nodded.

"All of this," she gestured at the large construct, "just to make bricks?"

"Well, no," I admitted. "Most of this is a spell for constructing the actual spell substrate, and then a set of meta-magic to perform the actual enchantment."

She raised her hand and frowned. "Hold up. Spell substrate? What's that? And what is meta-magic?"

I rubbed my hands together. I loved teaching. "Micah expects wonders out of me today so I cant give you the full lecture right now. But here's the short version."

I sat down next to the construct and with an afterthought and a gesture I cast the power-funnelling spell out of the powerstone on my utility gauntlet. The spell flashed into life. I could use the next one as a practical demonstration for Valia.

Valia sat cross-legged next to me, close enough so that her knee was pressed into my thigh.

"Magic is a force," I started while remembering the explanation mother had given me as a child, later to be refined and expanded by Uncle Kenny. "Exactly how it works, we don't know. Maybe we did before the Apocalypse. But what we do know is that it somehow aggregates inside living creatures in what we call the Soul. This force, or power, is channelled through our bodies in response to our will."

"I'm with you so far," Valia said with a cute smile that I returned.

"Good, because here is where it gets weird. For simple things, like casting a light spell, pure will is enough and you just add magic and 'poof', instant light." I summoned a small glowing orb above my palm for emphasis before closing my fist and snuffing it out. "But human focus is very limited, each layer of complexity we add to a spell will diffuse the willpower of all layers."

"I'm not sure that I follow," Valia frowned.

"I'm happy that you admit it so freely. Alright, let's try this. Imagine a sphere. Now make it shaped like a head. Now give it ears. Make the ears different shapes. Give it hair. Make the hair a certain colour. Now highlight the hair in another colour. Give the face eyes. Make them slanted at different angles. Give them colour. Now change the colour of one iris. Add a nose. Shape the nostrils differently. Add a mouth. Make it smile. Give it teeth. Make each tooth unique. Let me know when you forget the shape you gave anything on the head."

Valia opened her eyes and squinted at me. "I think I understand your point."

"Great! The same holds for magic. If you fail to will any spell component to work in a highly specified way, it will at best just fail to ignite."

"And the worst case?"

"Wild Magic," I said gravely and Valia shuddered.

"That is why we use the sigils and diagrams. They each describe a well known concept and the glyph allows us to not have to bother about the specifics of the mechanism behind it. The glyph already contains that. We call that a layer of abstraction."

"So the sigils and diagrams actually mean nothing? They are just placeholders for intent."

"In the same way as a word actually means nothing. It is just a noise someone makes."

"Fair point."

"And here is where it gets weird. Let's use your analogy. A word can have a certain meaning to me, and another to you. Yet as long as I know what the word means to me, you will understand it."

I frowned a moment before I continued. "I'm sorry, that was an absolute shit analogy. Let me word it in another way. I can use a different layer of abstraction than you, and both of our spells will work since we are both well aware of what that abstraction is supposed to perform."

"That makes sense, given that the abstractions are just labels for something."

I gave her a significant look. "But how does the magic know what I want? If two different runes can achieve the very same effect, how does it know?"

Valia opened her mouth, closed it again and then shrugged.

"It could be sentient, but I have come across pre-Apocalypse research that unequivocally states that it is not. So how does it know? That is one of the fundamental unanswered questions of magic. How does it interpret our will? But at the end of the day, it does not matter. It works, and we just have to learn how to use it."

"Okay. This is all very theoretical," Valia complained gently. "Weren't you going to tell me about substrate and meta-magic?"

"Yes, but in order to understand substrates you sort of have to understand the need for sigils and glyphs and runes. So we sketch these runes with our power, each rune being a three-dimensional coil. Like a hose that's been twisted into a specific shape."

I waited for her to nod for me to continue. "Now, sometimes a spell can be so complex that even these layers of abstractions are not sufficient. Sometimes the sheer number of glyphs and runes are too great to maintain them in your mind. And that is when we write it down."

"I've seen that done before, though I never understood it," Valia admitted.

"Then you are familiar with the concept. Good. Now, what we write on is called the substrate, and what we write with is the medium. Now, different substrates will affect the quality of your spell. For instance, paper works well because the lines are clear and well defined, but it won't be perfect. There will be some leakage of magic power. Usually that just transforms into heat, for some reason, but if the leakage is large in a high-intensity spell it might cause Wild Magic. So a spell written on paper is liable to catch the paper on fire. Now, you can use magical ink, similar to materialised magic but in liquid form, to make the scroll more resilient but each use will degrade the paper until it finally burns."

"Got it. Magic leakage bad."

"Precisely. Magic leakage very bad. So instead of paper I can chisel my spell into rock. That will work better. In that case the shapes the grooves form are the medium, and the rock is the substrate. But the best substrate is a framework of materialised magic. It is nearly perfect in magical energy transfer with close to no leaking. In this case the materialised magic is both the medium and the substrate."

"So for a spell that is designed to operate at maximum power for an indefinite length of time, materialised magic would be the best building material."

"Precisely," I beamed. I was proud that she was such a quick learner. I had a sudden flashback to mother's memories when she had sat like this with mom to teach her magic. I returned to the present and saw Valia watch me with a slight smile on her full lips.

"Where did you go just now? You looked... Sad and happy at the same time."

"Yeah. I just remembered listening to two people I loved having this exact same conversation when I was a child."

"Varl and another apprentice?"

"Yes," I lied with a stab of pain in my chest. It hurt lying to her. When had that started to happen? I shook my head to clear it. "Now that you understand substrates, I will give you a brief overview of meta-magic."

"I'm all ears," Valia said and shifted a little closer to me.

I squeezed her thigh and almost jumped in surprise. When had my hand ended up on her leg? And it was quite close to her hip too. I was about to withdraw my hand when I realised that she had not removed it herself, in fact she had just glanced at it and smirked. So I let my hand rest there, forefinger curling lazily up and down across her inner thigh in an idle caress.

"Now, meta-magic is the art of casting spells that in turn cast spells."

She stared at me for a long while with near-dead eyes. "What?"

"Yeah, I get that it's mind-boggling, but that is what it is. For instance, this thing," I said with a gesture at the slowly materialising brick machine next to us. "The outer layer of spells are those that materialise the shell of the machine. But without the brick-making and logistical spells contained within it is just that: a shell. It still needs to be enchanted to be an actual machine. And since I don't want to return to each of these machines when they are done and enchant them individually... I just made a spell that would do that for me."

"Just made a spell?" Valia asked slowly before groaning and putting her face in her hands, her elbows resting on her knees. "You do know how ridiculous is sounds when you say those words so casually?"

"What? What did I say?" I asked worriedly. I did not enjoy hearing that I sounded stupid. I could tolerate it from most people since I knew that they were wrong. But not from her.

"You 'just' made a spell, that in turn would cast a spell. And not just any spell. A fucking enchantment!"

"Yes?" I queried. It wasn't that ludicrous to me.

She cracked her fingers apart and peered at me with one eye between them. "All Mages that I have spoken with struggled with creating normal spells! And you just made one up! And not a normal one, a very complex one."

Well, I'd had training from the most skilled meta-magic Mage that still lived. That in itself was not that difficult since as far as I knew out of all of them, only Kendall had survived the war. And he had trained the second meta-magic Mage in the world. Me. But I couldn't explain that to Valia no matter how much I wanted to.

"Well, Matthias always did say that I had a mind for it. It sort of comes naturally to me, I guess?"

"If you can teach me only a third of your skills, I will be happy with that," she smiled warmly.

I chuckled and blushed at her praise. "Well, I'll make you a meta-mage yet. But that is for the advanced courses, and those are expensive," I joked.

Valia threw her head back dramatically and draped the back of her hand across her forehead like a swooning lady. "But I am just a poor girl, how ever will I pay?"

I looked her body up and down and licked my lips. "I think we can make out a deal," I said lasciviously.

Out of all the reactions from her that I had expected, her shocked and furious expression was not among them. "Are you calling me a whore?" She demanded angrily.

I blinked. "I... No, I just... I meant..." I sputtered.

And then her smile broke through and she laughed loudly at me. "You should have seen your face!"

Damn was she an excellent actress. Her laughter slowly petered out and then she gave my pout a single glance and started laughing again.

Well, two could play at that game. With a wave of my arms I used telekinesis to tie her limbs and I hoisted her spread-eagled into the air. I stood up, milking every inch out of my short stature in an attempt to loom over her. But I still had to tilt my head backward to look up at her. I remembered how mother had loomed over people when she was angry and how intimidating she had looked. I wished that I was taller. I had found that it was impossible to be intimidating as a short woman without just being cute.

"You would do well by not mocking Mages in the future," I announced as ponderously as I could.

She had been staring at me impassively, even when she was lifted into the air, but then her eyes widened and her lips parted in a smile.

"Don't be gentle," she said breathlessly and I almost lost it. My knees went weak with desire and I almost expected liquid to trickle down my thighs. I lost my focus and Valia dropped lightly to the ground after which she strutted up to me and cupped my cheek with her hand.

"You may be a brilliant Mage, but you are so very easy to manipulate," she chuckled. Then she darted in and pecked me on my nose. "Hold that thought for later," she breathed into my ear before dancing back from my grasping hands.

I grumbled and accepted the fact that I might be the superiour Mage and warrior between the two of us but her flirting had me wound around her finger.

"So, what were you saying about meta-magic?"

I had trouble reconciling her sudden chipper and playful nature with the shy and reserved Valia I had come to know. In fact, this was the first time that we spent time alone together without the others, not counting this night of course, and I realised that for some reason she was more withdrawn when she was with them. Perhaps it was because Zandor took so much space and she retreated to let him, or there were something else afoot. I stashed that thought away for later digestion and returned my attention to her question.

I scrambled to reorder my thoughts and remember what we had been discussing. "Right. Meta-magic is the art of crafting spells that cast, or craft, spells of their own. It is possible to create a complex spell that can alter itself based on external sensory inputs to adapt. In the case of the brick-making spells the meta-magic is fairly simple. It is a spell that will enchant and activate the machines."

Valia looked at me gravely before lifting her hand and swiping it across her head, indicating that my words had gone straight over her head.

"It is advanced magic, I concede that. Don't worry about it, it will be easier to grasp once the basics sinks in," I smiled.

"I'll take your word for it," Valia grumped. "So, tell me about these machines."

I opened my mouth to speak but at the last moment I changed my mind about what I was going to say. "Actually, let's head over to the next machine and I'll walk you through the process of activating it."

Her eyes lit up with interest and she shot me a wide smile that hit me straight in the groin. I stepped back and spread my Angel wings, about to fly to the next machine template. Valia cleared her throat loudly and reminded me that she did not have an Angel armour of her own and couldn't follow me.

"Right," I said sheepishly and folded my wings back, tucking them behind the engine along my spine. "I guess I won't need this anymore," I said and touched my choker to change the armour into more casual clothing. Power instantly started billowing from my armour as it began evaporating to be sucked into the powerstone. I started walking and Valia skipped to catch up with me before threading her arm through mine. I led her through the camp and waited patiently for my armoured corset to evaporate and then I made sure that her forearm was firmly pressed into the side of my breast. When we reached the third brick making machine I reluctantly let go of her arm so that she could inspect the template closely.

She asked a bunch of questions and I did my best to explain them all. I felt that I failed with many of them, my explanations being far too advanced for her sparse knowledge of magic. I was a poor teacher. As I spoke I tried to remember how Uncle Kenny had explained things to me, but I could not remember how he had worded it. I only remembered what he had taught me, not how.

"This is the part that I want you to pay attention to," I said and pointed at the top of the template where the power funnel would be located. "This is where the spell that gather power from the ambient magical field will be located. You see the sigils here, here and here?" I asked and pointed them out for her.

"Yes, I see them."

"Those are the entry points for the power. See how the glyphs coil through the entire thing, like the blood vessels in a body?"

Understanding shot through her eyes and her whole face lit up when she saw what I was pointing out.

"I see it!" She squealed with girlish delight.

I admired her joy for a moment before I raised my left hand and invoked the funnel spell from the spellstone in my utility gauntlet and let it hover before me.

I basked in her awestruck expression for a moment.

"You cast that spell as if it was nothing," she breathed with reverence.

"I cheated," I chuckled and pointed at the stone in question. "This is called a spellstone. It is a special kind of materialised magic, and within it I have embedded a spell template. The stone keeps it stable even without a supply of magical power and it is extremely quick to just copy it out of the spellstone. Like a shortcut, or a stencil when doing paperwork."

"Handy," Valia commented.

"Very. Even though this spell is not that complex, using a spellstone will make it even quicker to cast, and it ensures that it will work."

"What do you mean? The spellstone have some sort of verification spells?"

"Not at all. Whenever you cast a spell, there is a chance for error since you may forget something or hastily connect sigils in an incorrect way. But if you have a spell that you have tested and verified to work, copying that one will guarantee that it will work since the first one did. It eliminates the human propensity for errors."

Valia nodded slowly, mulling that over. "Does that mean that someone that doesn't understand a spell can cast it if they have a spellstone and the capacity to channel enough power to fuel it?"

"Precisely! Beauty and brains, you really are something else," I said with a wink and smirked when she blushed.

"But if the sigils is just an abstraction for intent and I don't know what the spellstone will do, how does the spell work if I can't possibly know what my intent should be?"

"That is precisely the great conundrum of magic. And the answer is still: 'we don't know'. Perhaps it is the intent of the Mage who created the spellstone that is somehow conveyed to the spell, or it is something else. The important thing is that it actually works."

Valia sighed. "I guess I will have to get used to the fact that we don't really know how it works, just that it does."

"Trust me, it will be way easier and cost you fewer sleepless nights if you just accept it and move on," I said with a dry chuckle. "Now, look at this funnel spell here," I said and gestured at the spell framework I had summoned. "See how the outputs of the spell sigils down here on the bottom almost lines up with the inputs on the brick machine?"

Valia closely inspected the interfaces of each spell and nodded. "I can see it, but this spells is way to tiny to fit on that thing," she said with first a gesture at the funnel spell and then at the brick machine.

"Right, but that is not that important. The importance is the relative distances between the various points in the interface. Scaling a spell up or down is rather simple, but scaling it wrong will make it not fit anymore," I explained.

"If you ensure that your various spells have matching interfaces, creating new spells by combining the components will be much quicker."

"Like assembling a gun using modular pieces from different types of guns."

"Yes. But with magic," I spread my fingers and waggled them when I said the last word. "Now, what I want to do is for you to invoke this spell right here," I said and stroked the spellstone on my gauntlet. It popped off smoothly and rolled into my hand. Valia extended hers with an excited gleam in her eyes and I placed the little stone in her palm. Our fingers curled around one another's, the stone cradled between us, and she gave me a sultry look. Our touch lingered, and then reluctantly parted with wordless promises of a naughty future.

"Just channel power through the stone, but not too much. Gently reach for it and caress it until it awakens, " I breathed. "But not too much or it will ignite."

Her lips parted and her tongue slid out to wet her lips before she stared at the stone and focused. I waited for a while, not wanting to disturb her, and just admired the gentle sweeps of her face. The golden shimmer of the morning sun on her metallic hair and the way her nose shaded her lips and cheek in sharp contrast. I felt an almost irresistible urge to reach out to touch her hair, to finally find out if it was as soft as it looked or if it was cool and metallic. But I didn't want to break her focus so I resisted the urge with a mighty act of willpower. Finally the spellstone in her palm started glowing faintly.

"That's it," I cooed softly. "Gently now increase the pressure. Coax it, stroke it. Be patient," I whispered.

Slowly the glow increased in intensity and then the spell suddenly erupted out of the stone, igniting with a flash and hungrily devouring the power of the magical field, spitting it out in little concentrated jets from the output sigils.

Valia made a surprised sound and her focus wavered, but the spell was self-sustaining by then, feeding itself from the power it gathered and sparks shot out of the output jets as the uncontained magic started misfiring. I lashed out with my power, disintegrating the spell structure and it fell part in a bright shower of embers.

Valia gave me a frightened look, shock writ plainly on her face. I took her hand in mine gently and squeezed it reassuringly. A moment later I felt her squeeze back. I wasn't taken aback at all by what had happened. No one ever managed to do this on their first attempt, especially not with such an advanced spell and meagre experience that she had.

"Like I told you, gently. A spell is like a cock, it will explode early if you are too eager," I said with suppressed mirth.

My quip had the desired effect and Valia giggled and then laughed, her shocked and disappointed expression melting away.

"I don't get how you do it so quickly, Mary," she admitted. "You just wave your hand and the spell is there. I thought that I was going slow, and then it suddenly exploded."

"That's what she said," I joked again but then grew serious. "It's like finding the clutch on your bike. You have to find the sweet spot before applying the throttle. Do you remember how difficult that was in the beginning? How the engine stalled the first few times?"

She nodded her pretty head and understanding lit up in her eyes. "I think I understand. With experience you know where that sweet spot is and you can just go straight to it."

"Exactly. Try again. If you fail, try again. Now, I really have to get to work so I want you to practice and then come find me when you've succeeded and I will give you your next task."

I waited for her to smile and nod before I started turning away. Then I stopped and turned back. "On second thought, you should practice with this one instead," I said and handed her another spellstone and returned the one she had been using to my utility gauntlet.

"This spell will not be self-sustaining like the other one and will merely fade away when you cut the power."

"Ah, good catch. Than you, Mary," Valia smiled radiantly and made my knees week.

Before I could lose my resolve to get to work and instead chose to stay with her I forced my wobbly legs to walk away. I touched my choker to manifest my Angel armour. My belly rumbled and I remembered that I had skipped breakfast today. Even though it was mid-morning I decided to go for a snack while waiting for my armour to manifest. It wouldn't take long to do so, but I hated just waiting around so I made my way to the closest camp fire to see if there were any left overs. There were, and there were no one there so I swiped a piece of bread and dried venison and munched on it while I waited for my armour. My belly somewhat sated and my armour finished, I took to the skies. I had five more brick machines to start, for a total of eight, and then I needed to create the required logistical tools to actually move the bricks to where they were needed. Luckily, I had brought a whole slew of spells with me in various spell stones or in my spell book.

I did not really wish to use the spells in my book since they took much more time to cast since I had to recast them every time. If I invoked the spell from the book it would degrade and eventually burn. And I most certainly did not want that. I preferred to use the spellstones, it was quicker and much easier but most importantly it was not as error prone. The only reason why I even had a spell book instead of just a pouch of spellstones was a pure logistical one. A spellbook could store a far larger quantity of spells than the same volume of spellstones could. And creating a spellstone was not a quick process at all. It took a significant amount of power and time to create and you had to make sure that it was perfect or the results would be, you guessed it, unpredictable. So it was only worthwhile for spells that were used often, or used primarily in circumstances when you did not have the time to cast it. Like in combat, or as a component in a spell that mass produced something. There were, however, a type of blank spellstone that just stored the last spell cast into it. My rifle, the one that Kendall had made for mother all those years ago, had a few of them embedded in the stock. Those took even longer to create but where endlessly more versatile. I had two of them in my utility gauntlet if I ever needed to repeat a spell multiple times in quick succession. I landed by the next machine template, waved my hand to invoke the funnel spell before quickly adjusting the scale. Then I quickly slotted it home, bound it in place and where off to the next.
