Half an hour later I watched the final brick machine flash into life. They would start producing bricks sometime in the afternoon and we would have a small stockpile come morning to begin construction.

Now, for the logistics problem. The bricks had to be moved to where they were to be used and there would be a lot of them needed for each construction site. How should I solve that problem? I had earlier considered carts powered by magic engine to shift the bricks to the construction sites. That would work, but it would require manual input from a human, both in loading the carts and offloading them. Those people tasked with logistics where people who could not build, which limited the rate of construction. The less people doing things that were not building, the quicker we could build. So, what could be a method of logistics that did not require any people to operate? That was an interesting problem, one that I did not have a ready solution for. So I did what I always did in these situations, I skipped the problem to tackle the next one on my to-do list and letting the problem stew in the back of my head. The next issue with construction work is that each brick have to be placed manually. Each side of each brick was enchanted with a spell that when lined up with another brick the two would fuse into one. That got rid of one of the most time consuming parts of construction with normal means: the application and mixing of mortar. So for these bricks, the slowest part would be fetching the bricks from whatever mode of logistics I would choose and aligning it to another. I suddenly started second-guessing myself. Was my brick-producing idea really a good one? The problem I had intended to solve with it was the fact that materialising an entire building in one go was a slow process and one that required a unique spell for each type of structure. The spell would inevitably be a mess that conflicted with itself more than it synergized. On top of that, the old way of materialising huge chunks of magic was also a very slow one. Producing the same volume of bricks as a given solid block of materialised magic would be much quicker. But then I hadn't taken into account the fact that you also had to assemble the damned bricks. The old method required no human input after the initial spell had been cast.

That was the moment that I realised how wrong I had been. In the old method, even though the actual build time was slower, it required no human time apart from the Mage that designed and cast the spell. My solution required a crew of people to assemble which brought its own issues with miscommunication, mistakes and general tardiness. The accumulated man-hours for the working crew would most likely far supersede the time spent by the singular Mage. I slapped my hand over my forehead and cringed hard enough to almost break my own neck. Then ideas sprouted in my mind and I had to sit down, vision swirling. This sometimes happened to me. Inspiration struck me so hard that I almost fainted. My thoughts churned and they both made sense and didn't. I knew that I was on to something, but I didn't at that moment know exactly what I was onto or how all of my disjointed thoughts would fit together. But I knew, just knew, that I would be able to merge the two styles of construction. I could use the old way of monolithic design of buildings and use my much quicker method for assembling them. I would use the brick method not to replace the old one, but to optimise it. One by one my thoughts ordered and I finally had a somewhat clear view of what I needed to do to accomplish that vision. But I would need some time to research and experiment and design a new spell. I needed a workshop. I didn't trust my wings in my current condition so I stumbled toward the fringe of the camp in search of an open space that I could use. I didn't even consider the fact that Micah had asked me to work quickly, that the people were in dire need of defences and housing. My mind was totally focused on creating the optimal construction spell. I stumbled out of the row of tents and found myself close to the western wall of the valley mouth and for the first time I spotted an opening in the cliff face. I walked over and cast a light spell inside. It was a cave. Not a deep one, more of a hollow actually. But behind the opening it opened up into a roughly circular space. Not big, but perhaps big enough. This would be perfect for a workshop!

I gleefully rubbed my hands together and took a few deep breaths to calm down. I would need some basic things first, like a workbench, a magical containment field and an artificial ley line to really power up the interior. The ley line should be the first order of business. By that point I had calmed down enough to hazard a flight and soon after I touched down outside the receiving tower. Only one artificial ley line connected to it from the north, but the other two should connect any moment now to further strengthen the Groundlands. I opened my spell book and spent an hour casting a satellite spell for the tower that would hover beside it to leech of a powerful and highly focused ray of power to fill my little workshop with an intense magic field. Not too intense in order to avoid events of Wild Magic, but intense enough that I wouldn't have to worry about running out of fuel for my research. The spell ignited before me and rose into the air, already materialising itself out of the power it leeched. I leaped into the air again and was momentarily blinded by a golden reflection off to the side but I paid it no mind. I landed outside my cave and spent a quarter of an hour casting the much simpler receiving satellite and tossed it into the air. I went inside my cave and got to work, drawing spells from my spell book to furnish it. A wall to narrow down the opening with a door in it, two large workbenches and chairs as well as several light spells to illuminate it all. After a moment of thought I also added a bed. Might as well move in here instead of using my tent. With a chuckle I thought of Zandor and Valia and made the bed much bigger. Finally I threw up the blueprints for ventilation and climate control. Now all I had to do was wait for the artificial ley line to connect and my little den would start growing out of nothing. Which gave me some idle time to refine my thoughts.

Valia found me there with my back to the wall and my legs folded beneath me and my nose deep in my spell book where I was refreshing my memory of the old construction spell. I knew it by heart, but I wanted to make sure that I fully understood it.

She was breathing rapidly, as if she had been running all over camp to find me and I realised that she had. That light that had blinded me earlier had been the midday sun reflecting from her hair.

"Here you are," she huffed. "You totally ignored me earlier when I tried to find you," she complained.

"I'm sorry," I said sincerely. "I didn't see you."

"I waved," she pouted.

"Sorry. I was just so engrossed in my thoughts. I had an epiphany!" I said excitedly.

"Oh?" She queried and sat down on the ground in front of me. "Tell me."

So I did. After only a few sentences her eyes glazed over but she kept nodding along and made encouraging sounds. I was glad that she did, for merely vocalising my ideas got my thoughts churning and I found several flaws in my current plan that I would have to account for. When I finally stopped speaking I was breathing hard. I had barely paused to draw breath.

"I did not understand any of that," she admitted without shame.

"You will, in time," I said with a wink. Then I decided that I should take a break from my churning thoughts and let them settle down so I focused on her. "I suppose you came to find me because you mastered the use of the spellstone?"

"Mhm," she nodded and showed me with a proud smile that she could invoke the simple light spell in the spellstone without igniting it.

"Great job, I'm proud of you! It takes most people a few days to learn that, and you did it in one morning."

Valia preened and smiled happily when I praised her.

"Are you ready for your next lesson?" I asked and she nodded vigorously.

"Alright, take the spellstone for the funnel and invoke it. The sweet spot will be a little different for this one so be careful."

Valia frowned in concentration but in only a few heartbeats the spell bloomed out to hover before her. She smiled proudly at me.

"Well done," I complimented her and quickly invoked the same spell without a spellstone of my own. "Now, watch me rescale this and attempt to do the same."

I used my fingers to illustrate what I was doing, pinching and spreading the sigils and glyphs apart to become larger but proportional.

Valia did her best to imitate me but it took her a little while to figure out that the motions were not the only component of rescaling the spell. You had to match them with the correct intent and willpower. But eventually her spell framework started to shift in motion with her gestures.

"Like this?" She asked uncertainly.

"Yes, you just need to refine your control a bit," I told her and pointed at a problematic area of her spell. "See these two sigils and how they don't line up anymore? If you were to ignite the spell in this current condition then this entire part of it," I indicated the lower right third of the spell, "would just fizzle out of existence. And the output from these sigils would leak over into this part."

"What would that do?"

I shrugged. "Most likely just disintegrate, possibly set fire to something along the way." I pointed out a few other areas of her spell that had gone all misaligned. "Remember to keep each segment in proportion to the others. That's crucial."

She glared at the spell and then reached out to adjust it. "Like that?"

I pointed out two places that she had missed and she quickly corrected them.

"That looks about right now," I said, "try to ignite it."

She did, and the spell flashed into life, greedily sucking power out of the ambient magic field and ejecting it in concentrated jets from the outputs. It was not a perfect spell and neither had I expected one. A few of the connections within the spell itself were leaking power in visible flashes and sparks, multicoloured embers falling to the cave floor. But it was a very good result for her first try.

"Excellent, very impressive for your first go at it. You see these areas?" I pointed out the problematic areas.

"Yes. That looks wrong to me."

"Because it is. While the overall spell works, it is leaking power there which will reduce the overall efficiency. That in itself is not a problem but the leaking areas will erode and degrade over time. If this was to be enchanted into a machine, that machine will in the worst case break and require maintenance."

"And in the worst case?"

I shrugged. "Depends on the machine. If it is a high intensity spell it may explode, or summon Wild Magic. If it is a crucial machine, like the generators for the Great Barrier on the Skylands, it could kill a lot of people."

Valia shuddered at the thought.

"Exactly. And that is why you never listen to the Mages who claim that a spell just have to be 'good enough'. Never settle for 'good enough'. Always strive for perfection and know that you will never achieve it."

She nodded thoughtfully.

"Now, I've set up this little workshop because of that epiphany I told you about. It's gonna take a while for me to figure it out, I think, but in the meantime we should utilise the machines already set up to start construction."

"Walls?"

"No. If we start building the walls with the bricks it will be tricky to adapt the method halfway through..." My voice trailed off when I had a sudden idea. "Or maybe not. We don't really need a full curtain wall, not yet. We can use the bricks to build pillboxes with overlapping fields of fire. That would require far fewer bricks. A number of cleverly placed bunkers might actually be a more efficient defence than a tall wall."

"Perhaps," Valia agreed. "I'm no architect, or a strategist so I wouldn't know."

"I should probably bounce the idea off Micah," I agreed and pushed to my feet before offering my hand to Valia. "Shall we?"

"You want me to come?" She asked with some surprise.

I couldn't stop my thoughts from going to a dirty place and I grinned at her. "Multiple times," I said huskily.

Her eyes widened slightly and her lips parted.

"But to answer your actual question, yes. You are my apprentice, you should follow me everywhere."

She took my hand and I pulled her to her feet with a grunt and off we went to find Micah. He wasn't in the command post so we had to ask for directions. We were led on a wild goose chase for almost an hour before we finally found the old pink-hair mending a barrel for an old woman. He looked up and took in both of us with a grunt.

"You've brought your friend," he greeted us.

"Apprentice," I corrected him with a smile at Valia.

"Good. We need more Construction Mages. You alone are not enough. You should take more apprentices, teach them the basics."

"I agree," I said even though I didn't. I didn't want competition. If I taught more Mages how to build that meant that I would not be involved in all of the building projects. Which meant that I couldn't sabotage it.

"What's the status of the construction of our defences?" Micah asked as he strained to tighten the binding of the barrel. With a final grunt of effort he slapped the barrel with a resounding thunk before tipping it back upright.

"Thank you, lord Micah," the old woman gushed.

"Just Micah, please. I ain't no lord."

Then he gave me his full attention and I cleared my throat.

"Eight brick machines are currently being erected, we should have enough bricks to start laying foundations tomorrow. Though I have an alternate idea."

Micah started walking and gestured for me and Valia to fall in beside him.

"Tell me."

"The brick method I described to you may not be the best solution for our needs."

"But you said that it would be much more efficient than just materialising the whole thing?"

"I did, but I had a bit of a revelation earlier today. When I said that it would be more efficient I was only thinking of absolute time, not aggregated man hours. While my method will produce building materials for the wall quicker than the old method, my method require workers to assemble the final product."

"I see. We don't have many workers currently."

"Precisely. But I have an idea that might alleviate that problem. I think that I can combine the two methods for an even better one. One that combines the speed of the brick method with the no-human-required features of the old."

"Alright. What's the catch?" He grumbled disgruntedly.

"I don't know how to do it, precisely. Not yet. I've set up at little workshop in a cave to the west and I'd like to experiment with this knew concept."

"We may not have time to wait for you to figure it out. We need defences as soon as possible."

"I completely agree, and I propose and interim solution. You said it yourself earlier, we can't defend against a full scale attack even with a full curtain wall. So why build one straight away? Let's use the brick machines that I have already set up to build armoured pillboxes along the defencive line with overlapping fields of fire. We can also use surplus bricks for housing. In the meantime I will work on this new conceptual spell for building the curtain wall."

"Or we could just use the old-school method and just get it done."

I ground my teeth. I didn't like that at all. I wanted to optimise it, not just stick to old and obsolete spells just because they worked. But I knew that that argument would not sway the old man.

"Yes, we could. But it might take years to fully build a curtain wall with our current influx of magic."

Micah shot me a glance before spoke. "Aren't two more focusing towers about to connect?"

"Even with those, two full curtain walls with the old method will severely drain the input and leave very little for other uses."

Micah rubbed his large paw over his head. "Then open more ley line crossings. Increase the power supply."

I fumed silently. That was a poor solution. Use an inefficient solution and solve the issue by throwing more power at it. That was the exact same mentality that had caused the Apocalypse and starved the Skylands of magic. I wanted to yell at him that research was important. By delaying construction now to invest in more efficient methods, we will be gaining time and power in the long run. But I knew that I wouldn't win this argument so instead I just dipped my head and accepted.

"Then I better get to it," I bit out and without waiting for a reply I left with Valia in tow.

She seemed to sense my mood and didn't speak until we were back in my workshop. The artificial ley line had completed and the chamber was swiftly filling up with magic, all the furniture materialisation picking up speed.

"What's wrong?" Valia finally asked.

"Just frustrated that he won't see the reason of my plan. He only see the short term gains, not the long term costs."

"I hear you."

"Thanks. We should probably get to work with the wall enchantments."

"We?" She asked with raised eyebrows. "I am not skilled enough for this," she protested.

"Then watch, and learn," I forced myself to smile at her, even though I did not feel like smiling. "I just need to clear my head, hold on."

I closed my eyes and took a handful of long and slow breaths. "Alright, let's get to it."

We stepped outside and found Zandor heading our way and I waved him over so that he fell into step beside us. "Great. Zandor, I have a job for you."

"For me?" For some reason he seemed surprised at that.

"Yes you, big guy. Follow."

To his credit, he held his questions until we walked up to the closest brick machine. "These are making bricks, or they will be once they finish," I explained to him. "There are seven more spread out throughout the camp. I'm putting you in charge of using the bricks for construction of housing to replace the tents and lean-tos."

"What do I use as mortar?" He asked immediately. I liked that he didn't argue.

"Nothing. Each side of the bricks will be enchanted with a spell, when it lines up with another of it's kind it will fuse into one. Remember this so that if you pile them up in a cart or something you make sure that they are not aligned. Wouldn't want them fusing in transit now, would we?"

"No, that would be bad," Zandor agreed with a grin. "Why me though? I'm no architect."

"Because I haven't had time to enchant any telekinesis wands to use to lift the bricks and you are the strongest man I know. You are going to need to use those thick arms of yours to pile those bricks on one another. I'm sure Micah won't mind if you rally up some more workers, maybe someone who has built houses before to help you with the design."

Zandor saluted smartly. "Yes, ma'am!" Then he looked at the partly assembled machine. "When will the bricks start popping out?"

"I think you will have a small stockpile by tomorrow."

Zandor pumped his fist in victory. "Off-day, yes!"

I rolled my eyes at Valia with a grin and she shook her head slowly at the large mans childish antics. She did grin though.

"We gotta go and get started on that wall," I told him. "Let me know if you need any magical help with the construction."

Valia and I left him behind and walked over to the valley wall not too far away from where my workshop was situated. The old spell for materialising magic was one that I had stored in a spellstone and it was incredibly easy for me to cast. The only issue was that I had to describe to it how the material to be produced would be like. And in the case of the curtain wall that was a very large description, and I had to get all of it correct for it to work properly. Then with an evil grin I decided to teach Micah a lesson. He had chosen to disregard my advice and given me an order that I believed was wrong. Time for some malicious compliance. I sketched up the first segment of wall, including the salients that Micah and I had devised earlier, until I had a repeatable segment of wall. I apologised to Valia that I would be forced to leave her behind and I spent the rest of the day hopping across the valley with my Angel armour, repeating the same section of wall over and over. I bound each segment to its own input spell so that the wall would build in segments instead of from one end to the other. It would be more efficient to do so, but it would also drain our power more in a shorter amount of time. The absolute time for constructing the wall would be quicker than doing it all in one go but the total drain on our magic reserves would be far greater.

Night was already falling when I finished the northern side but I used Blood Magic to wash away my fatigue and repeated the same thing on the south side of the camp. Then I realised that I had forgotten gatehouses and had to demolish a part of each wall template to construct a rudimentary placeholder. I wasn't an architect so I would leave the design of the gatehouses to those who actually knew how to build them. I finished just when dawn broke and I staggered back to my workshop, exhausted. The magic field in the camp was almost entirely depleted by the multitude of materialisation spells and progress would be slow. But that was what Micah had asked for. The artificial ley line feeding my den was prioritised though and the thicker magical field was a balm on my tired Soul. The furniture inside was finished and I was looking forward to pass out on my new bed. I didn't make it all the way and I stumbled and fell by the entrance. Strong arms scooped me up before I could hit the stone floor and I mumbled something into Zandor's broad chest. I heard Valia murmur something with a concerned voice and Zandor's rumbling bass made my entire mind vibrate. I was put into my bed and was out like a light before they even tucked me in.


---

## Chapter 13 — CHAPTER THIRTEEN Turncloak {#chapter-13}

CHAPTER THIRTEEN

Turncloak

I was alone when I woke up, and surprisingly naked beneath the blankets. I sat up and rubbed my gritty eyes, swallowing back the bile from my usual Blood Magic hangover. I desperately needed a drink of water, but I hadn't installed any plumbing in my little workshop. That would be the next item on my to-do list I decided. Though, there were no proper water supply for the camp. All through the days there were a steady stream of people carrying pails of water from the river that had carved out the valley over the ages. That should probably be the top item on my to-do list. After securing more power for the camp that is. The two new ley lines should have connected by now but the wall project would be soaking up most of that. I had to talk to Micah about prioritising. I searched the room for my clothes and couldn't find them. I reached up and idly stroked the stone on my choker and found my jaw dropping when I sensed the power levels within. Then I realised what had happened. Valia must have activated my stone somehow to dissolve my clothing. I was impressed that she had figured that out on her own. I activated my casual clothes, I was way too hungover to fly, and waited around until my clothes were mostly formed. Only the corset remained unfinished but I was decent enough. I stepped out into bright sunlight that stung my eyes painfully and made my head throb but I fought through my nausea with pure determination and made my way to the nearest water barrel. I gratefully splashed cold water on my face and forced myself to drink heavily. At least the water was fresh and cold. Feeling slightly refreshed I stood up and raked my wet fingers through my hair, slicking it back across my scalp. The pressing need for water taken care of I focused on the camp. As I had surmised, the ambient magic field was severely depleted. I looked at the closest brick machine. It was almost stalling, only three of the four brick making sections working at a time. The walls were barely visible thresholds of materialised magic, just a few inches high. But at least they spanned the entire valley.

"Excuse me, do you know where Micah is?" I asked a passing woman and she waved in the general direction of the camp without looking at me.

I didn't bother thanking her and started walking deeper into camp. A few minutes of walking later I rounded a tent and stood face to face with the epitome of masculinity. Zandor was loading up a cart with bricks, and he was doing it with style. Not any flourishes or embellished movements, but with precise and controlled twists of his whole body. He really abused the bio-mechanics of his skeleton to aid him in his movements and whenever he couldn't his muscles coiled under his sweat-slicked skin. Contrary to the other men that was working with him, he was taking four of the large bricks in a single go where the others only took one. With almost casual grace the big man deposited his load into the cart and stood up straight to wipe his forehead with the back of his hand. He only managed to smear his dark green locks across his forehead in a way that struck me right in the nethers. He raised his eyes and that was when he saw me. His face broke into a boyish grin and he raised his hand to wave at me. I obliged and approaching him. Something was different about him, and then it suddenly struck me what it was.

"You shaved your beard!" I exclaimed, happy that he had gotten rid of that patchy thing.

Zandor rubbed his chin thoughtfully. "Yeah, Felaan mentioned that I should just give up and accept the fact that I will never have a glorious beard."

"I think it makes you look handsome," I said honestly with a smile.

I hadn't expected the sad expression that descended on his face.

"You didn't think me handsome before?" He asked, crestfallen.

I stumbled on my words but he couldn't hold his grin back.

I stared at him through narrowed eyes. "Valia told you to do that, right?"

"Yes," he admitted. "You should have seen your face. How are you feeling?"

"As if last night was really wild, but without the good memories."

"That bad, huh?"

"Yeah. But I'll get over it. How is the construction going?"

"It's going great. I found this guy who used to study architecture in one of the Cities. He's going mad with power and has us build all of these strange stuff."

"Strange stuff?" I asked with an arched eyebrow.

"You know, buildings that aren't square."

When he saw my blank expression he huffed and squatted on the ground and started drawing in the mud with his thick finger. He sketched out what looked like a regular house with some extensions.

"So, he has you building houses?" I asked for clarification.

"I guess," Zandor said and scratched at the back of his neck. "But they ain't square," he finished softly, grumbling.

"Alright then, big guy. You guys carry on," I said and patted his curly locks as if he were a disgruntled boy. Damn was his hair soft and curly and I couldn't help marvelling at how his hairs felt as they slid across my palm. Warm and just a little bit damp from his sweat. I was standing so close that I could smell him. He smelled like hard work, earth and materialised magic. My fist clenched in his curls and I realised that I had hunched over him slightly to bring my face closer to his scent and that my boobs where practically in his face. And that he had frozen stock still. I immediately let go and took a long step back, feeling my chest, neck and face heat up.

He too were red in his face but his lips were stretched into a wide grin and his eyes were locked to the space that my breasts had just occupied.

"I, uhm..." I started but couldn't finish. "I must, ah, see Micah," I stuttered. "You should get back to work."

"About that," Zandor said and stood up, towering over me. "We are nearly out of bricks and the machines aren't working at full capacity..."

I cringed. "Yeah, that's on me. Or Micah really. He forced me to start building the walls and they soak up most of the power, starving the brick machines."

Zandor brushed his hands off on his trousers. "Well, then there's not much for me to do here. I'll go with you to find Micah and he can give me my next assignment," Zandor said cheerfully. "Shall we?" He asked and offered me his arm with a glint in his eye.

"Shouldn't you put on a shirt?" I asked uncertainly. I wasn't prude, but it felt weird to walk by a shirtless man among all of the others.

"Nah. I'm all sweaty, might as well dry up first," he answered carelessly and retrieved his shirt and tucked it into his belt before offering me his arm again. I took it hesitantly and off we went. I had to take almost three steps for every one of his but he slowed down when he realised that I was almost jogging to keep up. Zandor pointed out the foundations for the buildings they had been constructing and I was surprised by the amount of progress. The teams were working efficiently and gladly and I even heard a group of men singing a jaunty tune while they laboriously lifted and placed the bricks. Perhaps my brick-method was superiour even if it was in the grand scheme of things less effective than the old method. The old ways didn't bring people together like this. I couldn't help but smile at the cheerful men and women that worked, the sheer joy of building something tangible with your own hands. It was nice just having a walk with Zandor and I didn't know if it was the fresh air, the physical exertion of our fast pace or just his presence but my hangover faded remarkably quickly and I felt almost normal when we finally found Micah. Of course he was with one of the construction teams, getting his hands dirty and singing the same song as they did. As soon as he spotted us he carefully placed the brick in his hands and watched it fuse for a moment before he walked over to us.

"Sir," I began, "I have finished the task you set before me and the wall is currently under construction."

"Cut it out with the 'sir' shit, I've told you that before. I ain't no lord."

"And then you overruled me, sir. That strikes me as something a lord would to. Sir," I said insolently.

Micah's jaws tensed in anger and he had to visibly calm himself. "And I assume that you are now here to remind me that you warned me that our magical supplies would be exhausted by doing so and to tell me that the brick machines are stalling."

I hadn't considered the fact that he might already be aware of it, and that had indeed been my intention. I tried not to deflate visibly.

"Yes." I stated. I noticed that Zandor was shifting his weight from foot to foot and I had the notion that he wanted to get out of Micah's line of sight so that he wouldn't share the pink-haired man's ire but that he also didn't want to leave me on my own.

"Well, I expected so. Now, listen to me. Carefully. I overruled you because I must see to the bigger picture here. You are a genius in your field, Mary. Magic. I am a leader. I am responsible for these people. If we are attacked, people will die. If we have walls, people will still die. But far less than would have if we hadn't had those walls. I understand that it galls you to do something inefficient, something that will bite us in the ass in the long run. But if we don't sacrifice the long term efficiency for the short term gain, there might very well not be an ass to be bitten once the long run comes around. I trust you to know how to do things, but sometimes the need is so pressing that we can't do it perfectly. We may need those walls at any moment. We all need them raised as soon as possible, nothing is as important as that because peoples lives depend on it. Their lives do not depend on some future hypothetical gain in time or power. Do you understand?"

I did. And he was correct. I had only thought about the theoreticals of my spells and how they could or should work. I hadn't considered the practical aspects of their application. But my pride wouldn't let me surrender that easily.

"I do. But that logic will justify any shitty short-sighted solution to any problem. When the long run comes around then we will be sitting on top of a steaming monument to compromise," I countered.

"But at least we will be sitting!" Micah finally snapped before taking a deep breath with his eyes squeezed shut. "Alright, Mary. How about this. I will only overrule you if I believe it to be strictly necessary for our survival or any other dire, and I do mean dire, need. Apart from that I will not get in your way. I might give you a certain area to focus on, and you may go about it in your own way. Deal?"

"Deal," I agreed immediately and when Micah smirked smugly I knew that he had been playing me. He had never intended to get in my way after this and he had pushed and prodded me until I would readily accept this mutually beneficial agreement. The agreement that he had intended all along. I cringed and looked away.

"Well played," I complimented him and he accepted it with a shallow bow.

"Are you happy now?" He asked with twitching lips, almost smiling.

"Sure, why not. You got everything that you wanted, I got most of what I wanted. Now, the walls are going along slowly, we need more power. We also need a steady supply of clean water and plumbing. If more people come to join us then disease will run rampant in the camp if we don't have water."

Behind Micah the sky suddenly bloomed orange. "The flare," I breathed. Something had startled the sentry guns up there.

Micah cast an unconcerned look over his shoulder. "It's been doing that a few times a day lately. It hasn't turned red yet so we shouldn't bother."

"Did the scouts you sent find anything?" I asked, concerned.

"Only a lot of tracks. They estimate a few hundred of those creatures that you fought have been in the area, but it is difficult to tell them apart since they apparently are not using any footwear. It could also be just a handful of very active ones."

"Were there any corpses near the tower?"

"A dozen or so," Micah said with a shrug. "I've sent more scouts to find where they are coming from. If they find the nest I'll send a larger force to exterminate them."

My belly twisted at his callous words, reminding me of my mission to the Groundlands.

"But back to your earlier statement. I agree with both of your observations. Power and water. Do you know how to purify water?"

"No," I admitted. "Boiling it works, but given the volumes of water we are speaking about that will require a not insignificant amount of power to do so."

Micah nodded. "I will look around the camp. There are quite a few people that were well educated on the Skylands. Perhaps they will know. In the meantime a cistern and plumbing should be your next priority. Or perhaps guns?"

"The guns we currently have should suffice to fend off any small or medium scale attack. As you like to say: 'a full scale attack will defeat us regardless in our current state'."

"Fair enough," Micah conceded. "Plumbing and power it is then. I'll leave it to your discretion in which order." He said with a wry smile.

"Thank you, sir," I said sarcastically and sketched a sloppy salute.

Micah turned to Zandor. "And why are you here, young Zandor? Moral support?"

"She don't need no moral support, Micah, she's the strongest woman I know."

My back straightened and my shoulders pulled back when his words reached my ears.

"I'm here because the builders are out of bricks. We want something to do."

Micah stared off into the distance for a short moment while stroking his stubbled chin.

"Food supplies are running low," he grumbled.

Zandor scratched at the back of his head. "Most of the able hunters are already out," he finally answered.

"I know. But the local wildlife won't support our numbers for much longer. We need alternate food sources. There are many farms in the lands to the south, most of them are small and isolated but any little bit would aid us. Organise a party and visit them. Make them trade with us, offer them Skylander magitech to increase the yield of their crops in exchange for a cut."

Zandor nodded slowly. "I guess I can do that." Then he turned his head to look at me. "Do we have any magitech suitable for agriculture that we can offer them?"

I thought it over, trying to remember if I had any such schematics in my spell book. "I think I might have some, but those will require even more power to materialise and the farmers will need training in their use."

Micah waved my concerns away. "If it will secure our food supply then that is a small price to pay. Here, let me show you on my map where the scouts have marked the farms."

Zandor and I had a look at the map and I was pleased when I saw that there were several farms en route to the ley line crossing that I had planned to open next. It was a big one with five ley lines overlapping. I looked up at my handsome friend and plunked my finger down on the map.

"Here's a ley line crossing I'd like to open. What do you say, big boy? Road trip, just the two of us?"

"You got it, let me find the others and we are ready to go," he answered and started to walk away.

I hurried to catch up. "I was thinking that just the two of us should go, if we all go we may intimidate the farmers," I said. Even though I would have loved Valia to come with us, I had given her some magical exercises that I wanted her to focus on. Besides that, I was yearning to spend some alone time with Zandor.

"Fair point, sounds fun," he replied with a wink.

I started to respond but was interrupted by a blue-haired woman who strode up to me with determined steps.

"There you are!" She exclaimed with a tight smile. "I'm glad to see that you made it here in one piece, young lady! How was the trip? Have you been here long?"

I felt the colour drain out of my face when the woman draped her arm around my shoulders and hugged me close, her hand holding my shoulder like a vice.

"It was alright," I answered and then bit my tongue. My thoughts where churning, trying to figure out what to do.

"I'd love to speak to you a bit more. In private, right now," the blue-haired woman said with a smile painted on her face and a placid look in Zandor's direction.

"Everything alright, Mary?" Zandor asked me cautiously. He must have noticed how I tensed up.

I tried to smile warmly at him. "Why don't you gather some travelling supplies, Zandor, and I will meet you by the bikes after I've caught up with my friend," I said.

Zandor gave me a long searching look before he finally nodded and walked away, looking over his shoulder every few steps.

"Come, I have a place where we can talk," I told the woman.

I led her in silence to my workshop and thankfully Valia wasn't there. I closed the door firmly and activated the privacy enchantment. No one would be able hear us from outside, nor could they pierce the enchantment without at least alerting me. I spun to face the woman who wore an amused smile on her pretty face.

She had aged gracefully and the lines on her face only accentuated her fine bone structure. I almost hadn't recognised her with her hair in that strange blue shade instead of her usual green tresses.

"Are you here to try to bring me home like Uncle Kenny, Alissa?"

Auntie Alissa chuckled. "Not at all. I'm here to help you. Kendall was here?"

"You didn't know?" I asked, surprised.

"No. I realised what you were up to just days after you vanished. Your friend Lukas filled in the blanks for me."

"That bastard!" I growled. "He promised he wouldn't betray me!"

Alissa held up her hands. "Don't be angry with him. I had to twist his arm a fair bit before he caved."

My anger was suddenly replaced with worry. Auntie Alissa could be quite vicious when she was worried. "Did you hurt him?"

Alissa waved my concern aside. "Nothing that won't heal. I didn't scar him."

I let out a sigh of relief. "So why are you here?"

"Kendall tried to bring you home?" She asked instead of answering my question.

"Yeah. He waylaid me in the mountains when I was separated from the others. He even fought me when I refused to come home."

Her blue-dyed eyebrows climbed her forehead. "Kendall fought you? And you're still here?"

I nodded and shrugged.

"How the fuck did you win that one?"

I opened my mouth, closed it. Then opened it again, closed it again. How would I explain to her what had happened without sounding like a raving lunatic. When I couldn't think of anything I just went with the truth.

"Mother," I said simply.

"Kyra?" Alissa asked, deadpanned.

"Yeah. She's been appearing a few times. Once to save my life, once to stop Uncle Kenny and once to just chat."

"Just to chat? A dead woman?" Alissa still couldn't believe me.

"Yes. That's what happened. I can't explain it. She seems to be an Archon, and she can't stay for very long. I don't know how it happens, she appear to fade away like any Archon that runs out of power but then she returns."

"Kyra yet lives..." Alissa murmured, her eyes overflowing with unshed tears.

"At least she exists. Wait, you believe me?"

"Of course. I have never known you to lie," Alissa stated and then amended herself. "At least not when sweets are not involved. I know that you wouldn't lie about something like this. And if you are still here after meeting your mother, then I know that she approves of your mission. So I'm here to help."

My heart swelled with love for this woman who had been like a mother to me after my mothers died. And then the fear came crashing down.

"Alissa, you have to leave, you must return to Dun Traelak! If anyone here recognises you... They'll kill you!"

"I know, dear one. But they won't." She said the words with such confidence that I almost believed her.

"You think that hair-dye will keep you from being recognised?"

"That and the fact that I've aged a decade. Besides, I may be famous... Well, infamous I guess it actually is. I'm not as famous as your mother was... Is. Everyone knew the great Dragonbane by sight, but I was never that. People knew my name because I was part of the First Expedition and then later because I'm the Turncloak, the Traitor's pet, but they never really knew my face. I'll go by the name Alana Kelvari and that will be the end of that."

"But what if you get caught," I started but she interrupted me.

"I wont, but if I am... Then I'll die. There are worse fates. You can't talk me out of this, just like Kendall couldn't talk you out of it."

"You're doing this to guilt me into going home," I accused her with a petulant wave of my finger.

"Not at all," Alissa scoffed. "Even if you left I would stay here and fuck with these people. They attacked my people, my family! They attacked the Bizhjerai, they killed my friends. I won't let that stand."

Something about the furious glow in her eyes convinced me that she was telling the truth. Then the smoulder simmered down and she smiled.

"I'll stay in the shadow and watch your back, maybe pave the way for your actions if I can."

"What are you planning to do, Auntie?"

"I don't know yet, but I know that I won't do anything drastically stupid. I may have been a mercenary like your mother but I was not the front liner that she was. I've always worked best in the shadows. You tell me your plan instead and I will try to fit inside it."

So I did. I only had time to give her the short version. That is helping to build the Groundlands and use it as a beacon for any Skylander who wanted to preserve their old way and in so doing preparing a grand trap for them.

"Evil, vicious, brutal. I like it," Alissa smiled. "I'll do my best to fit into it. Now, your big handsome friend must be waiting for you, better not keep him waiting."

I couldn't hold back any more and rushed my aunt and embraced her tightly. "I'm so glad to see you, auntie, but I am so worried that you are here."

She hugged me back and stroked my hair. "Then you must understand what Kendall and I are feeling," she said with a smile in her voice before she pushed me back to hold me at arms length. "I like what you've done with your hair. Red and violet, like your mothers," she smiled.

"Yeah," I murmured in response.

"Before you go, tell me what you have done about your Bizhjerai Rune?"

"My Rune?"

"Oh, my dear child. I hope that you have disguised it somehow? If any of these Skylanders were to recognise it..."

I squirmed guiltily. I hadn't done anything about it. Alissa rubbed her eyes wearily with her fingers.

"Good thing I found you then, here," she sighed and offered me a small spellstone.

I cast my senses in it but only got the impression that it had something to do with colours.

"What does it do?" I asked, curious.

"It's a little something Kendall helped me make a few years ago when I went on that covert mission to the Cities. It will disguise your Rune. Don't worry, it's not permanent. Just press it to your rune and activate it. Do it now, Marissa."

I grumbled but did as she asked. I turned my back and had her help me in removing my corset before I took off my blouse.

Alissa sighed wistfully. "To think that I was once that young and... Perky. Sometimes I miss the good ol' days."

I ignored her and pressed the spellstone to my Bizhjerai Rune and activating it. It dissolved into a faintly glowing liquid and seemed to seep into my skin, running down to form mesmerising patters all across my shoulder and down to the back of my hand. It started creeping down the slope of my breast before it finally halted. It was beautiful, all sharp angles and swirling patters. I strained my neck to see my Bizhjerai Rune and saw that it was completely subsumed in the pattern. It was still there clear as day but if you did not expect it to be there your eyes were thoroughly fooled by the overall pattern.

"How long will it last?" I asked, not wanting to get caught off guard if it suddenly vanished when I was depending on it.

"As long as you keep feeding the spell power it will last. If you stop it will fade in a few days, a week at the most."

"Thank you, Auntie."

Alissa shook her head at me. "Now go," Alissa told me gently with a jerk of her head in the direction of the door. "And make sure to get a piece of that hunk before the shit show starts," she finished with a wink.

"Auntie!" I protested as if I were still a child. I feared that I might always be one in her eyes. Once we stepped over the threshold Alissa seemed to melt into the shadows of the cliff wall. I only sensed a short flare of magic that I could have easily mistaken from something else. She even vanished from my magesense. It always freaked me out when she did that. I schooled my features in a calmer mask and made my way to our bikes. Zandor was there, waiting for me and pacing back and forth with a worried frown on his brow. As soon as he saw me relief cascaded down his features and he took two long strides to stand before me.

"Everything alright? You seemed a bit rattled by that woman," he said while looking me all over for injuries. As if Alissa would have hurt me. Even so, his close perusal of my body ignited a slow smoulder inside me.

"Yes, everything is fine. Alana helped my mother and me out of Utopeia when the Clans broke through but we got separated before we reached the forest. I hadn't seen her since and neither of us knew if the other had survived," I lied. "She wanted to greet me and catch up but I didn't have time. I'm sure that we'll meet again later."

"That's nice, I'll have to thank her for saving you. Did she really recognise you after all these years? You where just a little girl back then," Zandor frowned thoughtfully.

I pointed at my hair. "She said these oddly coloured streaks were unmistakable," I said with a shrug.

That wasn't so far fetched. While most Skylanders had hair-colours that were far from usual Outside, my plain brown hair streaked with two vibrant and very distinct colours where most certainly not common on the Skylands.

"Fair point, those streaks are quite eye-catching," Zandor agreed, the tension leeching out of his shoulders by the moment.

"Did you find the supplies?" I asked, shifting the subject.

"Yeah," Zandor replied with a slap on his bulging saddle-bags. "There's a lot of meat and oats here. We can make your favourite, venison porridge, for days as long as we find water."

"Great," I said sarcastically, "can't wait to feast on that sludge."

"Chin up, little one, this is the exact problem that we are out to fix, remember?"

"Little one?" I asked, indignant.

"Well, you are quite tiny," Zandor pointed out as he towered over me. "I like that in a woman," he added with a feral grin.

I was surprised at his sudden aggression and remembered that I had been similarly surprised when Valia bloomed when we were finally alone. Apparently the two had a lot in common.

"That's quite a thing to say to a woman," I huffed before reaching out to put my palm on his thigh. "Luckily, I'll forgive you for I prefer my meat... Well built and firm," I said with a squeeze of his powerful leg.

Zandor blushed and sputtered and I let him off the hook by sliding past him.

"Are we ready to go?"

Zandor hastened to mount his own bike and start the engine. "We are. I've memorised the route to the closest farm and if we ride hard we should be able to get there before nightfall, maybe even be able to persuade the farmer to let us stay there for the night."

"Sounds great," I agreed. "Do we have enough power cores to last us to the ley-line crossing? We can recharge them there for the return trip."

"I'm not sure," he admitted while scratching at the back of his neck. "We have two each, will that suffice?"

I made some mental calculations. "Probably. I might have to send out my Sieve swarm to top us off. Yeah, I'll just go ahead and do that when we are out of range of the Groundlands."

"Hah! The Siever strikes again," Zandor laughed.

I cringed. I didn't like how much 'The Siever' really did sound like 'Deceiver'. But it was too late to do anything about that at the moment.

"Very well then, let's ride!" I exclaimed and gunned the engine, hurtling down toward the south.


---

## Chapter 14 — CHAPTER FOURTEEN Intent does not matter {#chapter-14}

CHAPTER FOURTEEN

Intent does not matter

Both Zandor and I were frustrated when we finally arrived by the ley line crossing and I killed the engine of my bike with a scowl. Five farmsteads we had visited and we had been chased off the grounds by hounds from one of them, had a crossbow pointed at us at another and the wife of the third farmer had tried to seduce Zandor. We had had to leave that one quickly when the farmer started reaching for his axe. Of course, they were no threat to us but we were not here to kill anyone. We just wanted to trade. Two of the farmers had at least not been openly hostile but they had seen no reason to trade with us. They could provide what they needed themselves, and what they could not they traded for in a nearby village. So far we had come up with nothing, but at least there were three more farms to visit on the way home. I angrily stalked up to the ley line crossing and cast my senses down into the earth. The crossing was not that deep, but it was bountiful. By my quick estimate it would almost double the power influx that we currently had. Zandor walked up to me with an uncharacteristically sour face.

"How's it look?" He grumbled.

"Great. This will empower the Groundlands twofold," I answered.

Zandor scratched at the back of his neck and looked around. "Where's a good place to set up camp? I don't want to get in the way."

I scanned the area and pointed at a spot that wasn't too far away. "Over there, I guess. There's no water around, right?"

"Don't know, I'll have a look," he shrugged and tromped away.

I sighed and got to work. I had optimised the spell to open a ley-line crossing and had drawn up a high-quality scroll to invoke it from. I hadn't had time to create an actual spell stone, nor did any of my blanks have the capacity for such a complex spell. I unrolled the scroll and let my eyes flicker across the symbols and sigils and felt the power tear out of me. The runes and glyphs exploded into the air in a maelstrom of magic. It felt as if my Soul was torn from my body, but there were no pain. My Soul was still firmly entrenched, only the magical power was leeched away. I fell to a knee, gasping. I laboriously transferred the spell to one of my power cores and the burden on my spirit dwindled immediately. It was a few more moments before I could stand. I raked my hand through my sweat-dampened hair and observed the spell work for a while. It didn't take long before the ground exploded upwards, clods of dirt and stones disintegrating in the concentrated jet of magic. It was thick, almost half of my height across, and powerful beyond any I had yet seen. Power washed over me, seeping into me like invigorating water to a parched man. My thumb idly stroked the gem on my knife and I thought of my mothers, quietly embracing in the glow of the fount at Dun Traelak just days before they marched on Utopeia. That had been the last time I saw them being affectionate to one another.

"This reminds me of that night before we left for Utopeia," mother said from beside me.

"I was just thinking the same thing," I whispered without turning toward her.

"I've been noticing a pattern," mother announced and this time I really did turn to face her.

"A pattern about what?" I queried.

"Whenever I wake up, there is a concentration of magical power in the field."

It all suddenly made sense to me. How she survived, how she kept appearing and why she couldn't stay long.

"The dagger!" I hissed, my hand clenching around the hilt. That's why I always felt her presence when I touched the gem. Because she was in there!

"I see you've drawn the same conclusion that I have," mother smiled proudly. Then her expression turned sad. "I am not an Archon, as I originally believed. I am still a liche."

"Grasax survived the death of his body within the dagger," I remembered.

"Yes. I had to oust him to finally kill that evil bastard."

I thought back to my many times of reliving her memories. "But his presence in the dagger made itself known, he leeched magical power constantly in order to break out. Even when he was too weak to be conscious. Why doesn't that happen with you?"

Mother was silent for a long time before she heaved a heavy sigh. "I think," she began, "that is because Grasax craved life and immortality more than anything else. He had so many projects to work on, so much to learn about magic and the world."

She fixed her glowing violet eyes on me. "I don't. My life ended the day Red was killed. I have no desire to return to whatever vestige of life this enchantment might grant me."

"But you're back now," I said and was not the least bit ashamed over how tiny my voice sounded.

"I am. I am asleep when I am not with you, unknowing of what transpires. But something seem to wake me up, not only the sudden influx of power into the enchantment that brings me back from the void, but something else. I believe that something is you. I've appeared when you've been in danger, or when you needed your mother."

I considered her words carefully. She was right, of course, as she always was.

Mother made a point of looking around before looking back at me. "There is no danger here, dear one. What is troubling you?"

"What makes you think that I am troubled?" I asked in denial.

"I'm here, am I not?" Mother smirked.

"Fine, you're right. What's not to be troubled about? The Groundlands revert to the Skylander ways more and more for every day that pass. Even my friends, who are good people, seem to be glorifying the old days even though they were mere children when the Skylands Fell."

"Sometimes good people with good intentions will do bad things," mother mused loudly. "Intent matters little, outcome does," she declared with finality.

Her words struck me hard. "I'm planning on betraying the trust of the Skylanders, kill them all. Just like you did. Does that mean that I am a bad person? Am I in the wrong? Where you?"

Mother turned back to me and took my hands in both of her cold ones. She squeezed my hands comfortingly. "Of course. I was not, am not, a good woman. I never claimed to be. I did horrific things, and I want to believe that I did some good things. A horrific act can still be a good act, in one way." She paused and looked to the stars. "Oh, how I wish Kendall was here right now. He was always the more philosophically inclined of the two of us."

I smiled fleetingly. "He almost bored me to death on several occasions with his long rants."

"Me too, little one. Me too. But do you know what he would say right now?"

I shook my head slowly.

"Imagine a warlord being told a prophecy that a young man with raven hair and blue eyes would one day become a powerful tyrant who would torture and murder millions during his reins. Assume that the prophecy is true. Since the warlord does not know the exact individual the prophecy warns about, he sets out to murder every boy with raven hair and blue eyes. Would you deem the warlords actions horrific?"

"Yes," I said without hesitation. "He would be killing hundreds of thousands of innocent boys."

"I agree," mother said. "But he would save millions of people from torture and death. Is that not a noble act?"

I didn't like it, but she was correct once more. "I suppose so," I conceded.

"But you don't know the warlords intent, what if his intent is not to save the people but to stop the usurper from taking his power? Does that make his actions more horrific?"

"Yes!" I spat vehemently. "In that case he is just a power hungry and murderous madman!"

Mother gave me an amused glance. "Is he though? His actions are the same. The outcome is the same. The only thing that has changed about the situation is what you perceive to happen inside the warlords head."

I had no answer to that.

After a while, mother sighed and turned her face to bask in the invisible glow of the magic fount. "Some actions are horrific, some are good. But good actions can lead to horrific events, and some horrific actions can lead to wonderful events. There is no way for us to know."

She turned to look me deeply in the eyes. "Your wish to kill all of the remaining Skylanders... Does it make you a bad person? Are you in the wrong? Perhaps. Was I in the wrong destroying the Skylands? Perhaps. But didn't my actions stop a war and brought peace and prosperity to the Bizhjerai? If you turn from the path you have chosen, what will the Groundlands grow to be? A second Skylands? A new tyrannical monolith that will consume all that its shadow touches? Perhaps. Perhaps not. There's no way for us to know."

She reached out and placed her crystal claw on my forehead. "You can only do what you believe to be right in this moment. You can't do more than that. Your intent matters little. Only the possible outcomes of your actions."

Her crystal fingers slid down my face to cup my cheek and when I opened my mouth to speak her crystal thumb slid across my lips to silence me. Mother tensed and her head twisted to the side, showcasing her burn scars on the side of her face, and she looked over her shoulder.

"Someone's coming. Be safe, my daughter," she said quickly before evaporating from sight.

I stood there, shuddering in a storm of conflicting emotions.

"Found water," Zandor announced. "What's wrong?" He asked once he could make out my face in the twilight and he rushed up to my. "You're shivering and you're pale! Mary, what's wrong?"

"I'm fine," I stuttered before taking a deep breath and putting my Mary mask back on. "This one just took a lot out of me. I'll be fine once I've absorbed enough power from the fount."

I tried to smile reassuringly at him but I could tell that he was not convinced. Even so he led me to the little stream he had found and set up camp by. With a wave of his hand he lit the firewood he had piled up beneath the little pot.

"I'll just head back and fetch your bike as well. Are you sure that you are okay?" He asked again and I just nodded and shooed him away. He returned shortly thereafter and sat down on the other side of the fire.

"You're getting better with your spell casting," I complimented him.

"Thanks," he answered with a proud grin. "Though I fear that Valia has far surpassed me already," he complained light-heartedly.

"Well, she tries," I shrugged. "I thought you were happy with just throwing some fire around?"

"I thought I was, but seeing you two... It's kinda made me think."

"What? You? Think? Get out of here," I teased him.

"Ha-ha, very funny," he said and made a face before he took on a thoughtful expression. "Valia is way more clever than I am. I don't think I'm cut out for construction like you two are. But I'd like to learn more magic outside of combat. I enjoyed putting my back into building those houses. It felt nice. But as it is I'm just a strong guy, I'd like to be more."

I gave him a thorough look. I had never seen him this serious before. "There's more to magical construction than design and spellwork, you know."

"There is?" He sounded as if that thought had never struck his mind.

"Yes, like piling bricks. That is part of magical construction. Or operating a magical cart to shift goods. You don't need to know a lot about magic in order to do that."

"I'm not following," Zandor admitted without a hint of shame.

I remembered then that he had never seen proper magical construction on a large scale. Or, he had in Utopeia, but he must have been too young to truly appreciate it back then. He was only five-odd years my senior after all.

"What I do, what I'm training Valia to do, is to construct tools. Our creations on their own are useless without people to use them."

"That makes senses," he agreed.

"Good. Now, the more advanced in magic the user is, the easier it is for us to create the tool. The magical gap we must overreach is smaller."

Zandor stared thoughtfully into the slowly bubbling pot for a while. "You mean that if I learn more about magic, you can build me awesome stuff?"

I turned his words over in my head a bit before I answered. "Yes, I guess you can put it like that."

"Awesome! What should I practice?" He asked while ladling porridge into the usual bowls.

I accepted the portion he offered me with a smile. "Have you learned anything else besides using fire?"

"No," he admitted sheepishly.

"Well one thing you could do is to try to learn using telekinesis. That's useful for a bunch of things."

"Moving things with magic? I can see how that might be useful," Zandor mused and took a bite of porridge.

"For instance you can use your fire magic and your telekinesis, combine it with metallurgy and you would quickly become a master blacksmith who would be in high demand anywhere in the world."

"Why would I want to work with metals? Wouldn't a tool made out of materialised magic be way superior?"

"In a way," I admitted. "But materialising magic takes far more magical power than forging metal with magic. You might not always have access to much magic."

"But the Groundlands have ample magic," he pointed out.

"Perhaps you will not stay in the Groundlands forever," I said hesitantly.

"Nonsense, the Groundlands is the only place for a Skylander to live," Zandor scoffed and my heart sank.

I didn't dare push any more in that direction and we fell into an awkward silence. Soon enough my jaws were prised apart by a mighty yawn and I decided to go to sleep even though it was still rather early.

"Good night, Zandor. Thank you for erecting my tent."

"Don't mention it. You had more important work to do," he smiled before wishing me a good night.

I stripped off my clothes and climbed into my bedroll and tried to go to sleep. But my mind would not calm down, my thoughts kept churning. Mother's words kept echoing through my mind. Was I on the right path? Was I an evil person for planning the things I planned to do? To murder the Skylanders for being Skylanders? I had seen what Skylanders did. I had heard the Skylanders in the Groundlands glorify the olden days. Micah's casual mentions of killing the savages as if they were mere vermin. I didn't like it. But to kill them all? I didn't know. Eventually I could not hold the frustrated tears back and I buried my face in my pillow to smother the sound.

It proved to be a futile exercises for I heard a rustle by my tent flap.

"Mary, are you alright?" Zandor whispered into the darkness of my tent.

I couldn't respond, only mewl pathetically. More rustling followed and then a big body lowered itself to the ground behind me. Strong arms gathered me into his chest and he just held me there while I cried myself to sleep.


---

## Chapter 15 — CHAPTER FIFTEEN Ambush {#chapter-15}

CHAPTER FIFTEEN

Ambush

I woke up and realised that dawn had been hours ago judging by the intensity of the light that spilled into the tent. But I was still cradled in Zandor's big arms and his comforting scent filled my nostrils. I was still cocooned in my sleeping bag but even though Zandor had slept shirtless beside me he was still warm and comfortable. I wriggled and snuggled closer to him, burying my face in his chest. Zandor woke up with a grunt when my nose tickled his chest and his arms tightened around me.

"You alright?" He mumbled into my hair.

"Yes. Thank you," I whispered even though I did not know if I was indeed alright.

"Want to talk about it?"

"Not now."

"Okay," he murmured and just held me for a long while. I almost dozed off again and would happily have done so, but we had work to do.

But before I could muster the motivation to withdraw from his warm embrace, I wanted to thank Zandor again. His comfort had meant so much me and I wanted him to know that. I scooted back a little from him and lifted my head to his.

"Thank you," I said again and reached my neck up to brush my lips against his.

I pulled back and saw that his eyes were fully open and were devouring my face with a deep seated hunger. Before I could finish the smile that was creeping over my face he lowered his head and kissed me. Soft at first but when I pushed back with my own lips his ferocity grew. He crushed my body against him with his strong arms and my leg tried to lift to drape over his hip but my bedroll kept me locked down. I moaned into his mouth when his hand slipped down my back to grab a handful of my ass, his fingers digging into my flesh with male dominance. I shuddered at the thought of being pinned down beneath him, at his mercy, and desire flooded my whole body. But the fucking bedroll were keeping me pinned down so I could do nothing about it. I couldn't even let my own hands roam across his body. I could feel something hard growing, prodding my belly and my smirk broke our kiss. I squirmed, grinding myself across him and I was rewarded by a gasp of pleasure.

"Mary," Zandor mumbled, his hand clenching in my hair and pulling gently.

I obliged and tilted my head back, exposing my throat and Zandor did not hesitate. It was as if a bolt of lightning shot from his lips and into the sensitive flesh of my neck just below my jaw before it shot down my spine to come to rest as a roiling ball of heat in my nether region.

"Ah, what have we here, boys? A camp, in our lands? Come out here!"

Both Zandor and I froze at the unexpected sound of the rough voice. Zandor pulled back and gave me an astonished look. I cursed whatever being might be responsible for cock-blocking me this time.

"Wait here," Zandor whispered and before I could protest he was outside the tent.

"Morning, fellers," I heard him say. "I'm just passing by, I didn't mean to trespass."

I quickly disentangled myself from my bedroll and scrambled to find my blouse.

"Well this here is our land, mister," the first voice announced haughtily in a menacing growl.

"Well, in that case I will make haste to pack up my camp and be on my merry way. Good day to you, sir," Zandor said respectfully in a perfect imitation of the local dialect.

I found my blouse and stuck my arm inside one of the sleeves.

"Don't get me wrong here, mister. These lands are dangerous, there's raiders afoot, you see. Me and my friends here were concerned for yer safety is all."

"Well I kindly thank you for yer concern, friend," Zandor drawled.

"We've been keeping these parts safe for a while now. Safety ain't cheap you know," the first voice carried on.

So that was how it was. They were here to rob us in the guise of 'protecting us'. Sadly for us, and them, we weren't carrying much that were worth robbing. And if we had nothing to pay them off with... Then they might just kill us and then loot our little camp.

"I imagine it ain't, sir," Zandor replied.

I started buttoning up my blouse but had only gotten two of the buttons when I heard a new voice.

"Boss! What the hell is this? Some sort of crystal contraption!"

Shit! They had found our bikes. I scanned the inside of the tent for my weapons belt.

"Crystal contraption... Some sort of bullshit going on back there..."

I assumed that they had found the forming focusing tower before coming here.

"You are one of them 'Skylanders' ain'tcha?"

"You are mistaken, friend," Zandor tried but I knew that there would be no reasoning with these men. They had intended to rob us either way.

Summoning a spell into my left hand I cocked the hammer of my revolver and dove out of my tent.

I don't know what surprised the mounted men the most, the gun in my hand, the spell in my other or the sudden appearance of a petite woman wearing nothing but a thong and a partly-buttoned blouse with her tits practically falling out of it. But surprised they were, surprised enough for me to have ample time to hurl my spell at the leader and squeeze off a shot at his second. The leader screamed when he was smothered by tiny lightning bolts of power, his entire body going stiff as a plank and slowly tumbling off his horse. His second took the bullet in the chest and the spell embedded in the crystal munition triggered. His torso exploded from within, ripped clothing and flesh and ribs and mangled organs sprayed out of him while his head and shoulders tumbled off his horse. His hips and legs went down the other side. The panicked animal bolted and I had to roll to the side to avoid being trampled. The frightened animal ploughed into my tent and went down in a tangle of lines, cloth and limbs. The other men stared in horror at the mangled remnants of the man I had killed before drawing single shot smoothbore pistols and swords from their belts. I saw two men aim their antique guns at me and I summoned a shield to protect me. The lead shots flattened against my shield in a flash of sparks and I responded in kind with a much more efficient outcome. The two men tumbled dead off their horses and the remaining man swung his mount around and fled. He couldn't outrun my final bullet though and he rolled across the grass before coming to a stop. The animal trapped in my tent was screaming and when I approached it I saw that it had broken both of its front legs. I winced. I had been around enough horses to know what that meant. I cocked the hammer of my revolver and quickly put it out of its misery.

"You okay?" I shouted to Zandor.

"Yeah, they were focused on you," he stammered before hurrying over to his tent to retrieve his own weapons. "We should leave before more of them arrive," Zandor said.

"I agree, but we need to secure the crossing first," I said while scanning the horizon. "I doubt that they can really harm it for there shouldn't be any Mages in these parts but I'd rather be safe than sorry."

Zandor nodded absently and looked at my trampled tent.

"Leave it," I said. "We don't have time to move the horse. There's nothing in there that isn't replaceable."

Except my dagger I realised with a sinking heart. I dashed over to my tent and saw that the area where my weapons belt had been was not covered with dead horseflesh. I used my magic to cut and burn the cloth away and to my relief I found my belt there. I swiftly looped it around my hips and stood up.

"Break camp, I will secure the crossing so that it should be safe until it constructs its own defences," I told Zandor and started jogging away.

"Uhm, Mary?"

I stopped and looked over my shoulder. "Yeah?"

"Even though I would love it if you always dressed like this, given the circumstances..."

I suddenly remembered that I was almost completely naked and actually blushed.

"You got a point there, but my clothes are all flattened by horse. I'll have to reform them from scratch, which will mean that I'll be naked over there. No peeking," I admonished him and started walking again. "Unless you really want to," I added flirtatiously and straddled my bike. I made sure to tilt my hips forward to really accentuate the curve of my spine and rump while I rolled past him. With a smirk I reached up to set my choker to reform my clothes and some light armour. I doubted that this particular gang had enough men left to assault us, but there might very well be more gangs in the area and sound travelled far across the hills.

The fount seemed to be undisturbed, the tracks of horses ended far beyond what would have been the sensible range to approach, but it would take a while before the tower constructed its own defences. I didn't really want to hang around here long enough to protect it until then, and it would take too long to build extra turrets before we left. But there were some quick defences that I could make. I spent a few minutes sketching explosive spells onto the ground and bound them to be powered by the fount. Anything that stepped on them would be blown to bits. Simple, effective, brutal. The only real downside was that they would not reset themselves. Once ignited, they would consume themselves. As a final touch I cast my wall template spell, scaled it down, and laid out a perimeter wall around the tower. I bound the spells to the fount and they immediately started manifesting. It would delay the activation of the tower by some time, but it would be worth it. The walls would allow the elevated automatic rifle turrets on the tower to rain death from above without allowing any attackers to approach the tower. If they managed to hide behind the walls they wouldn't be able to climb them. If they did then there would be no place to hide from the bullets on top of the walls. I smiled grimly at the thought. Maybe I should include the walls in the standard schematics for the tower.
