The few birds he offered would mean nothing to the Groundlands so I just thanked him and declined. What the Groundlands needed was a steady supply of a large amount of food, not a few birds. We rolled into the Groundlands when the sun was setting and we wearily located Micah to report our lack of progress. The big pink-haired man stood up straight when we found him hunkering over a pile of documents on his desk.

"The fount is open and is currently constructing the tower. I had to add extra defences to this one so it will take a little longer. Though it's a strong fount so it will still build quicker than the others."

"Extra defences?" Micah asked with raised eyebrows.

"We ran into a gang of raiders. They tried to rob us."

Micah shook his head in disapproval. "No law and order around here it seems. That will have to change now that we're here." He turned to Zandor. "What about the farmers?"

Zandor shook his head sadly. "None wish to deal with us. They say they are content with what they have and all of their trading are with a nearby village."

"Fuck," Micah sighed. "So no new source of food?"

"No," Zandor affirmed with a downcast look on his face.

"Well then, here's your new orders, Zandor. Tomorrow, after you've rested, gather a party and appropriate those farms. We'll install our own people to work them and use our own machinery to increase the yields."

"You mean just taking their land from them?" I asked, aghast.

Micah gave me a stone cold stare. "Yes. They were given the chance to trade with us, they refused. We need that food or people will start to starve."

"Those farmers will starve if we drive them off their land!" I protested angrily.

"You're right. Zandor, kill them. It's the merciful thing to do."

"Merciful?" I spat bitterly, not believing what I was hearing.

"Yes. They are an obstacle to the new order around here. This is the Groundlands now. They were given a chance to adapt, they wouldn't. I now have to choose between the lives of some backwater farmers and my people and I choose Skylander lives over theirs."

I knew then that I couldn't win him over with moral arguments. He was a creature driven by pure pragmatism. "If you kill them you might provoke the village to attack us. They will fear that we will do the same to them."

"You're right," Micah said and stroked his chin. "I will send a larger force and wipe out the village as well. I am sure that they have stockpiles that will keep us fed until the new and improved farms are up and running. Dismissed."

I couldn't believe it. I should have seen it coming, but I hadn't. The cold indifference with which he ordered the deaths of innocents were chilling. Mother's words echoed in my ears. He was doing what he believed to be right in this moment. He had to care for his people and he was willing to take horrible actions to do so. I almost respected him for that. But I could not stand idly by while this transpired.

"With all due respect, sir," Zandor hissed and for the first time I saw the big lug being furious, "I won't do this."

"Are you disobeying a direct order, boy?" Micah growled and pulled himself up to his full height. He was a hair shorter than Zandor but he still managed to loom over the younger man. But Zandor wouldn't be cowed.

"I am. This is the exact same behaviour that provoked the Clans into a war. It is the reason the Skylands have fallen! I won't let the same happen to the Groundlands, Micah. We can do better this time!"

"The Skylands fell because we were betrayed!" Micah roared angrily. "The Bizhjerai would have stood no chance against us if it hadn't been for the Traitor! These people have no Traitor to help them. There is no risk in our doing this and only risk for the whole of the Groundlands if we don't! We need to farm that land to sustain our numbers, boy! We are barely scraping by as it is, what do we do when more Skylanders arrive and we have exhausted the local wildlife? What then? Huh?"

Zandor returned the older mans challenging stare. "That may be true. But that does not give us the right to simply take their land or kill them! Let me return out there, try again. There are more farmsteads that we haven't visited yet!"

Micah sighed deeply. "Don't bother, I already know what their answers will be. Fine, have it your way, boy. I won't order you to do this, but know that I have lost a lot of respect for the both of you. I thought you both believed in the Groundlands. Now get out of my sight!"

I seethed and was about to snap something foolish at the large man but Zandor's large fist clamped around my upper arm and he dragged me out of the command post. I writhed and hissed but he wouldn't let me go. Briefly my magic surged but I let it go almost instantly. Zandor was my friend and I let him drag me down the muddy street.

"Pick your battles, Mary," Zandor said gently and released my arm.

I took a deep breath and released it slowly.

"That was very noble of you, Zandor," I whispered and reached up to squeeze his bicep. "I appreciate how difficult it must have been for you to oppose him. I know how much you look up to him."

His jaw tensed. "I did. I'm not so sure any more. He seems so stuck in the old ways... I can understand why he wants to wipe out the savages to the north, but these people... They're just farming the land, disturbing no one..."

"I know," I said and patted him on the lower back. "Except for those raiders," I murmured.

"That's it!" Zandor exclaimed and snapped his fingers.

"What?" I asked intelligently.

"Those raiders," Zandor said excitedly. "If we hunt them down and stop them from tormenting the area, it may prove to the farmers that we are trustworthy!"

My eyes widened in realisation that he was correct. "You're a genius, Zandor!" I complimented him and gave him a spontaneous hug. "But I fear that Micah won't let us do it," I continued.

"Fuck Micah," Zandor snapped. "Let's get the gang together and just do it. I'm sure that none of the others will object. Judging by how easily you dealt with those morons that tried to rob us I think that you might not even need the rest of us along for the ride."

I chuckled fondly. "I'll always need you by my side, Zandor," I said with a longing gaze up at his face.

He looked back down at me but then his brows drew down. "What about Valia?" He asked seriously. "I've seen the way you two look at each other."

"I..." I began and had to pause to gather my thoughts. "I like her too," I admitted. "I thought you did too?"

My heart fluttered in anticipation of his words. Would he condemn me for being a lecher, or would he say the words that I hoped that he would say?

"I do. I feel like the worst man in the world but I want you both," he admitted and closed his eyes and turning his head aside.

I reached up and grabbed his chin and forced him to look down at me. "Then let us be the worst people in the world, together," I mumbled.

Slowly he nodded and smiled. He offered me his hand and I took it. We slowly walked through the camp toward my workshop, my intestines coiling with anxiety. What would happen when we got there? ## Chapter 16 — CHAPTER SIXTEEN Raiders {#chapter-16}

CHAPTER SIXTEEN

Raiders

The morning after we had quickly found Wylah and Felaan, in Wylah's tent no less, and brought them aboard our plan to hunt the raiders. I was in charge of preparing the bikes while the others gathered the necessary supplies for our journey. Less than an hour after dawn we were hurtling down the mountain from the valley that housed the Groundlands. We had traveled as quickly as possible to the campsite where Zandor and I had been assaulted and we found the corpses were still where we had left them, buzzing with flies and ripped by carrion. The whole area reeked of death and decay and I was forced to raise a magical filter to even be able to breathe. Zandor hung far back upwind with Valia while Felaan and Wylah prodded around by the corpses with impassive expressions on their ever stoic faces. They squatted next to each other and pointed into the grass and mud, conversing softly. I was hoping that they would be able to find the tracks left by the mounts and we would be able to trace them back to whatever place they had come from. The pair returned to the rest of us.

"We found tracks, we think they came from the south west," Felaan said and pointed in that direction.

"But the tracks are old and muddled by the tracks of wildlife," Wylah supplied.

"The terrain out there is less forested and more hilly," Felaan continued as if they were speaking as one. "Even if we are not able to follow their tracks we have an advantage."

"Oh, yeah? What's that?" Zandor asked curiously.

"Her," Felaan stated and pointed at me.

"Me?" I said, surprised. I was not a tracker. I was not even a wilderness person. I was a builder, sometimes fighter.

"Or more specifically, the birds eye view. Her Angel armour will let her see farther than any of us if she just flies straight up a bit," Wylah explained.

I nodded slowly. What they said make sense. "Alright, but I won't be able to keep up with you in the air. I don't have enough power to sustain flight for prolonged periods of time."

"You can ride double with Zandor," Valia proposed. "His bike is big enough that your cute little ass won't be too much of an encumbrance," she teased me with a playful thwack on my behind.

Wylah raised an eyebrow and Felaan actually smirked.

"Finally," he whispered out of the corner of his mouth to his lover whose lips twitched in response.

"So everyone agrees?" Zandor asked with a clap of his hands. "We ditch Mary's bike and then come and get it on the way back?"

"No need," I announced and reached into my bike to finger the power core. "Just hold up a while and I will have it squared away!"

I also reached up to touch the powerstone on my choker to fully manifest my Angel armour.

A quarter of an hour later and I was bedecked in my full Angel regalia and my bike was nothing but a power core in the grass. I slid it into my satchel and then turned to face my friends and offered them an elegant curtsy.

Valia applauded politely but with a warm look in her eyes. Zandor snorted at my theatrics and offered me his arm like a proper gentleman.

"Thank you, sir," I said coquettishly and looped my arm in his.

He led me the single step toward his bike and I heard Valia snicker behind me.

Zandor mounted the huge beast I had made for him and then helped me up into the saddle behind him. I locked my arms around his thick waist and the engine whispered to life between my thighs. He kicked the bike into gear and off we went.

A few hours of boredom, induced of seeing nothing but Zandor's broad back, later it was a welcome relief when Felaan circled his hand above his head, the signal that we had agreed would mean that it was time for me to take to the skies. I drew a deep breath to steady my tingling nerves before I spread my skeletal wings and flared the jets. I lifted off the bike and soared toward the sky. The relief on my aching nethers was sublime, but I was so tender and sensitive that the gentle caress of the wind was enough to bring sharp jabs of pain. As I climbed higher I gently reached down and touched my thigh. Yup, I'd made a slippery mess all over myself. And over the back of Zandor's bike. Even though I was alone in the sky I blushed with embarrassment. But I had a job to do so I scanned the area for as far as I could see. I saw something in the distance but I could not make out what it was so I gunned the main engine on my back and shot toward it, using the smaller jets on my wings to parry any stray gust of wind that sought to knock me down. When I drew closer I saw that it was an old abandoned campsite. I looped around and zoomed past my friends before I swooped down to fly alongside Felaan and Wylah.

"Campsite," I called out. "Straight ahead!"

Felaan nodded and kicked his bike into high gear. I angled my wings to gain some altitude and we arrived at the old camp at the same time. I swung my legs down and landed, but my wobbly legs folded and I tumbled into a boneless heap on the ground.

"Mary!" Valia cried out and sprinted over to me, skidding to a halt by my side. "Are you alright?"

"Yes, I'm fine," I said dreamily and fought my heavy eyelids.

Valia obviously didn't believe me and ran her hands down my legs to make sure that I hadn't broken anything. Her fingers brushed the mess in my groin and she took a closer look. Her eyes widened and her lips curled.

"Shit! You weren't just messing around earlier," she whispered.

"Rough landing," I lied with affected sheepishness and Valia helped me stand. The folds of my armoured skirt fell down around my hips to conceal the source of my embarrassment and I had to lean on the slightly taller woman for support.

Felaan were already poking around in the burnt-out fire and Wylah were scurrying around, studying the ground as if she were reading a book. It all looked like a mess to me.

"This is their camp alright," Wylah concluded after a few minutes.

"Five days old," Felaan agreed.

"This looks like as good a place as any to set up camp," Felaan continued. "You three set it up, Wylah and I will see if we can find a track leading from this place."

The two stoic warriors stalked out of the campsite in some sort of obscure searching pattern and left me with my two lovers.

Valia jerked her head in my direction and addressed Zandor. "That one won't be of much use tonight," she snickered. "Seems setting up camp is our job."

Zandor nodded amicably and got to work. He quickly handed me the pot and the ingredients for supper and I used magic to reignite the leftover firewood that was in the already established fireplace. I mixed up the usual porridge and dried venison and then busied myself with telekinetically stirring the porridge while Valia and Zandor quickly set up camp with practised movements. The simple fire spell made for a grand entrance and he looked fearsome indeed. I imagine several of the raiders shat their trousers when they saw him stalking through the flames. He lashed out with his halberd, skewering someone I couldn't see. Valia darted in beside him, her small revolver speaking rapidly. It was as if they had kicked a hornets nest. A crossbow bolt struck Zandor in the chest, but his crystal armour deflected it as if if where nothing. The crossbowman stood up to reload and I put him down with an explosive round to the shoulder. The resulting explosion ripped his arm clean off, exposing his ribs, lungs and heart to the elements. Gunfire from the north of the camp heralded Felaan's flanking approach.

"Kill any who attempts to flee," I called to Wylah and without waiting for her affirmative I stood up and leaped into the air. I soared with my Angel armour and a breath later I landed on the battlefield, revolver ready for battle. One man raised his sword in a challenge but I simply shot in him the face with a normal kinetic round. A man peeked around a water barrel with a small crossbow and snapped the bolt off at me but my shield absorbed it without issue. I lashed out with a spell, splintering the water barrel into fragments. The man stared wide-eyed up at me and then his eyes crossed when they focused on the barrel of my revolver. I put him down without a second thought and moved on. But there were no more men fighting us. I heard a single report of a rifle in the night and knew that Wylah had taken down the only runner.

Sudden silence descended on the camp, punctuated only by our own heaving breaths and the moans of the wounded. I looked at Zandor and Valia over my shoulder and saw Felaan silently skulk in from the shadows to join them.

"No prisoners," I said grimly and led by example by striding up to a man crawling on his belly away from us. I lined up my revolver with the back of his head and put him out of his misery.

Behind me I could hear footsteps and the sound of Zandor's halberd sinking into flesh and the odd gunshot.

Eventually, there where silence once more and Wylah joined us.

"Anyone got away?" I asked her, wanting to be prepared if reinforcements were to suddenly bear down on us.

"Of course there were not," Felaan scoffed gently and Wylah confirmed by shaking her head.

"Well done, everyone."

"What now?" Zandor asked, for once without a grin on his face.

"Now we tell the farmers that we rid them of this menace," I told them and slammed my revolver back into its holster low on my thigh.

"How do we prove it to them?" Valia wondered.

That was an excellent question.

"Bring them their heads?" Wylah suggested, her large hunting knife already drawn.

"No," I said while shaking my head. "That will probably just scare them."

I let my eyes roam over the scattered corpses and noticed something odd. I crouched next to the man that I had murdered and tugged at his sleeve.

"They all have this red cloth badge sewn onto their shoulders. Must be a gang marker. Let's gather these," I said and drew my own knife to remove the cloth.

In short order we had gathered twenty one badges and I stuffed them into my pouch.

"What about the bodies?" Valia enquired.

"Leave them to the carrion," Felaan responded.

"But we should clean this place out of supplies," I added. "In case they have something the Groundlands can use."

It was almost midnight when we concluded that everything of use lay in a big pile in the middle of the camp. Zandor had used his strength to drag the corpses in the immediate vicinity away, leaving bloody tracks in the mud all over the place.

"There's no way we can bring all of this back to the Groundlands," Valia commented with a gesture at the large pile.

"Unless she's got a wagon or something stuffed into her pouch," Zandor said with a wave of his finger in my direction.

"I don't. Lets just sort through it, anything too big we leave here. Most of this junk is low-tech. Grab the swords and axes, those are always useful. Anything edible that we can take with us as well," I ordered and my friends got to work.

Within an hour we had loaded our bikes up and I torched the camp with thick streamers of magic fire, aided by Zandor and Valia.

Then we were on our way.


---

## Chapter 17 — CHAPTER SEVENTEEN Epiphany {#chapter-17}

CHAPTER SEVENTEEN

Epiphany

"Somethings wrong," Zandor shouted at me, slowing his bike down. I matched his speed and looked ahead.

"I agree."

There were far too many people at the farm closest to the ley line crossing I had opened a few days ago. It was powerful enough that the walls were complete and the tower nearly so. A small group of men and horses had been lying dead within the perimeter, the result of the orange flare we had seen late yesterday evening. We slowly approached the farm until I recognised one of the men at the farm.

"Fuck!" I exclaimed.

"What?"

"Those are Skylanders. I recognise that asshole that took my weapons when I came here."

"Brengar?"

"That's the one."

"Shit," Zandor spat to the side. "That can't be good."

We approached the bustling activity until we were spotted and Brengar came running with another man, bolt-action rifles at the ready.

"Halt!" He cried, his weapon raised halfway.

"Fuck off, Brengar," Zandor snapped. "Where here to speak to the farmer."

"Good luck with that," Brengar laughed. "He's over there in the grove, with his wife and daughter."

With a sinking feeling in my stomach I guided my bike to the little grove. The smell hit me first, confirming my suspicion. The caw of ravens came next.

The farmer lay on his back, a bullet hole in his head. His wife lay on her side next to him, her white blouse dyed red from her blood. Their little daughter was piled onto their legs like so much trash.

I drew my revolver and swung my bike around.

"Mary?" Valia queried.

"I'll kill him!" I hissed but was suddenly knocked off my bike by a heavy projectile. I struggled beneath Zandor but he somehow managed to snatch my revolver from my hand without harming me.

"You can't kill him!" He told me sternly. "Micah won't forgive murder, no matter how useful you are!"

"He murdered these people!" I protested angrily.

"I know! But it's done. Vengeance won't solve anything."

I went limp, defeated. He was right. But I would not forgive them, none of them. And I most certainly would not forget. There were no innocent Skylanders. Micah had promised me that he would not harm the farmers!

Or had he?

He had actually only said that he would not order us to do it. That bastard hadn't even waited until the dust of our departure had settled before he sent another group out.

I no longer had any hesitation if my plan was just. This was the proof that the Skylanders that came to this place were not the Skylanders that were capable of change. They could have lived wherever they ended up, assimilated into the local communities to live good and peaceful lives. But they didn't. They came here and they killed and they destroyed and they consumed everything!

Mother, I will kill them all.

"Okay, I'm calm. I won't kill Brengar," I told Zandor. And I meant it. I wouldn't kill him. Not yet.

Zandor looked searchingly into my eyes for a moment longer before he stood and offered me his hand. He pulled me to my feet without any apparent effort before he offered me my revolver back. I slammed it into its holster and I leaped back on my bike.

"We're done here," I said and gunned the engine.

My friends did not reply, they merely fell in line.

We powered through the night to reach the Groundlands and we rolled through the partly constructed rudimentary gatehouse I had set up. My eyes widened. The Groundlands had almost doubled in size in the few days that we had been gone. It must be nearly six or seven hundred Skylanders here now.

"What in the fuck?" I murmured. I spotted a flash of blue hair and saw Alissa beckon me over from in between two tents. "Guys," I called out loudly, "I just spotted my friend. I need to talk to her. Great job out there. Go, rest. I'll catch up with you later."

They waved in acknowledgement and I peeled off toward Alissa. I stepped off my bike and almost went in for a hug but her tiny head-shake reminded me that we were in public.

"Where did you go?" She mumbled so softly that I almost couldn't hear her.

"We went out to hunt down a gang of raiders as a gesture of goodwill for the farmers. So that they might reconsider trading with us," I answered just as softly.

"It was a good plan," Alissa returned after a short delay. "But that bastard sent out his kill-teams just an hour or two after you left."

"Shit..."

"I sabotaged one of them, messed with their horses," Alissa whispered apologetically.

My stomach twisted. "Did you kill them?"

Alissa cast me a mortified gaze. "Of course not. I put burrs in their feathering."

My belly untwisted. That was uncomfortable for the horses and made them jittery, almost impossible to ride, but it didn't actually harm them.

"Clever," I complimented my aunt.

"Thanks. But it didn't delay them more than a few hours."

"Hours?" I asked incongruously, eyes widening.

"Yeah," Alissa chuckled. "These idiots are the worst horsemen I've ever seen."

"I almost messed up," I suddenly confessed. I hadn't even known that I was about to say the words.

"How?" Alissa asked me and pointed with her whole arm up at the top of the cliff edge on the end of the valley. "Pretend to look where I'm pointing and reply something clever."

I shaded my eyes with my hand and peered in the direction she was indicating before nodding sagely. "That is a perfect spot for a pillbox," I said loudly. A little too loud and a passing woman gave me an odd glance before hurrying off.

"I almost killed one of the Skylanders that was in one of the kill-teams."

"Why didn't you?"

"Zandor stopped me," I admitted.

"Good man. You should keep him close."

"Yes, he is. And I will. But I really want to kill that man, he tried to steal my weapons when I first came here."

"The ugly one that's usually on guard duty?"

"Sounds familiar. Name's Brengar."

Alissa nodded and spat. "I know him. Tried to finagle me into bed in exchange for entry. Told him to get fucked."

"You've always had such a way with words," I almost finished the sentence with 'Auntie' but just barely managed to bite my tongue. Even though we were whispering that was a risk too great to take.

"Shut up, kiddo. So what's the plan now?"

"What's done with the farmers is done. Can't help them now," I sighed. "But I won't forget. Next on the agenda is plumbing and water supply."

Alissa nodded thoughtfully. "Makes sense. Without it disease will run rampant."

"That's what I told Micah," I agreed.

"Any plans on how you are going to 'fix' it?" She asked with just a tiny inflection on the word 'fix' that told me that she actually meant 'sabotage'.

I shook my head in the negative.

"Let me think on it, I might have a solution for you," Alissa murmured. "We've been standing here too long already. Be careful."

"You too," I said but Alissa had already vanished and I was left standing there blinking in surprise. One moment she had been next to me, and in the other she was simply gone without a trace. Only the bootprints in the mud next to me convinced me that she had actually been there. I shook my head and returned to my workshop. I had a lot of things to build, a lot of things to sabotage.

I stood up from my crouch and let my eyes roam across the vast scaffolding of magic that I had erected.

"And this is supposed to solve our water problem?" Valia asked from next to me.

"Uh huh," I responded absently, searching for flaws.

"What is it?"

"A dam," I responded while refusing to be distracted.

"What's a 'dam'?"

I sighed and gave in to the inevitable and regretfully turned to face her head on. The regret faded the moment I laid eyes on her pretty face. I'd rather look at her than a bunch of magic constructs any day of the week.

"It's a structure that traps water." Before she could ask the inevitable follow up question I carried on. "The stream you see here, only a certain volume of water flows through a particular place at any one given time, right?"

"Right."

"So for any given moment we have a given upper limit to how much water we can consume?"

Valia nodded that she was still with me.

"Let's call that volume of water x. Now, the camp demands a certain flow of water that ranges between zero and some value z depending on the time of day."

Valia frowned and thought about it. "Because no one uses water at night when we sleep, but a lot of people use water for cooking or washing during the evenings?"

"Precisely. Can you guarantee that x will always be greater than z?"

"No?"

"And what happens to the volume of water that is x minus z?"

"It runs down the mountain to a lake or a river or something," Valia said as if I was an idiot for having to ask that question.

"Precisely. That is water that isn't useful to us. The dam will stop the water from flowing away from us."

"So instead of only having x water, we will have x and whatever have accumulated while x minus z was greater than zero," Valia concluded with a satisfied grin.

"Good girl," I complimented her and leaned to give her a quick congratulatory kiss.

A splash from the water interrupted us and I turned my head to see Zandor withdrawing his spear from the water, a wriggling fish impaled on the point.

"I don't understand why you bring letters into your numbers. It makes no sense," he grumbled while turning around with a confused frown. Then he quickly killed the fish by sticking his thumb into its mouth and bending its head backwards until its spine crunched. "But what happens to the fish that live in the stream when the dam goes up?"

He gave me sad eyes, feeling bad for the fish even though he had just callously killed one of them without a second thought.

"They'll have a big pond to live in instead," I said dismissively, not really caring about the fish.

"But these ones prefer running water. I see them playing in the currents," Zandor said and made a wiggly motion with his hands.

"They'll have to move upstream, I guess," I shrugged.

"Fair enough," Zandor nodded and threw the fish onto the little pile he had been building for the last hour.

"Perhaps there will be bigger fish in the pond than these small ones," he grinned. "I want to catch a big one!" And with that he waded back into the water, his spear raised high and ready to pounce.

Valia watched for a moment with a warm smile. "He's so adorable."

"Yeah," I sighed, also watching Zandor stalk for prey.

"Do you see all of this shit?" I asked Valia with a wave of my arm toward the magical template and had to elbow her gently in the ribs to draw her attention from our lover.

"Yeah, I see it," she sighed wistfully with a last glance at Zandor's shirtless back.

"Do you see any issues?"

Valia had advanced significantly with her magic over the last few weeks and she had even corrected my work more than once. She couldn't do what I did, not by a long shot, but she could sometimes spot my mistakes.

After a few minutes of intense scrutiny she threw up her hands. "I can't find any issues, but that does not mean that there are none," she said, defeated.

"A very profound insight, let me give you the answers. Of course there are issues with this spell."

"Tell me," Valia said with a hungry expression. She did love to learn.

"I don't know, I can't see them either," I shrugged.

"Then how do you know that there are issues?"

"There's always issues," I told her with a wink. "No spells are perfect. Never forget that."

"Why not? Certainly there must be a spell that can no longer be optimised further," Valia protested with a cute frown.

"Sure. Any spell have a theoretical optimal state. But as long as there are humans casting the spells, they will never reach that point. Because humans are error prone and a single human will never be able to foresee every issue that may arise."

"That makes no sense," Valia protested thoughtfully. "Certainly a person could spend enough time on a spell to figure out what may go wrong."

"You'd think so, wouldn't you? Let me posit you this," I began. "Imagine you are creating a spell. Then you find a flaw in it, so you correct it. Repeat this until you find no more flaws. You have fixed all issues that you can think of. Is the spell perfect?"

"I want to say yes, but the way you say this makes me believe that the actual answer is 'no'."

"An astute observation, my pretty. So, what about the issues that you can't think of?"

Valia opened her mouth but then closed it again.

"Can't fix those problems, can you?" I persisted with an evil smile.

"Alright, you've made your point," Valia grumbled.

"That's why the optimal state is an hypothetical. You can't even prove that any state is optimal."

"But what if I have a spell that no one can find a fault in?"

"That only means that no one can find a fault in it. That in itself does not prove that there are no flaws. What if there comes a Mage a hundred years later and finds a fault in it?"

"I guess..."

"As long as a human have conceived of a spell, there will be issues with it. Simple as that. Humans can do a lot of things. We can even do a lot of things well. But none of us can do anything perfectly."

"That's depressing."

"Perhaps. But that is reality."

Valia was silent for a long while and I took the opportunity to activate the whole dam spell. I couldn't figure out if there were any issues with it and since there were no skilled Construction Mages in the Groundlands that I could consult I would just have to ignite the spell and let it play out.

"What if a human didn't create the spell?"

"What?" I blinked at my taller friend.

"You said that as long as it is a human that created the spell it will be not be optimal. What if a human didn't create it? What if a spell created it?"

"Spells can't create spells," I protested.

"But they can cast spells," Valia persisted. "You told me that with meta magic a spell can enchant an object."

"True," I drawled. "But that spell to be enchanted was pre-designed by a human."

"Is that really necessary, though? What if you designed a meta spell to not cast a specific spell, but to achieve a particular effect. And then kind of work it out?"

"And how exactly would that work? Just trial and error?"

I froze, mind suddenly going blank. Why wouldn't that work?

"Yes?" Valia asked uncertainly and I saw her mentally preparing herself for another lecture.

"Actually, that might work," I whispered.

Why had I never thought of that before? Had I walked onto the path that I had been so disgruntled by in the past? To get stuck in the rut of 'this is how it's done'? Only someone with very limited knowledge of magic could have ever thought about questioning the whole concept of meta magic like that. A little smile curved my lips when the sudden memory of mom doing the exact same thing to mother shot through my mind.

"I need to head back to my workshop," I said suddenly. "You keep an eye on Zandor, make sure he doesn't drown himself or something," I told her with a quick kiss to her forehead.

Before she could respond I was soaring through the air, my Angel armour bringing me back to my workshop in less than two minutes. I had had to leap higher than normal since the walls were coming along nicely. They were not high enough to be actual walls yet, but they were a little taller than Zandor was by then. I folded my wings and barged through the door, armour already dissolving.

I had furnished my lab quite a bit in the past weeks and I had whole slew of apparatuses to help out in my research. It was getting quite cramped in here.

I sat down heavily in my thinking chair, my chin in my hand and I stared at nothing while my thoughts whirled.

What was a spell? It was intent and power. That intent could be formalised with a glyph, a rune or a sigil. In essence, all three of those where the same. Only their complexity told them apart. Glyphs where the least complex type of spell component, something that described a simple and straightforward concept. Like heat, or light. Runes were similar, but more advanced. Usually they combined at least two glyphs. Sigils were the most complex of the components, usually a group of at least two runes that synergized to create a new concept. Diagrams where the most complex of all that could be any collection of glyphs, runes and sigils that worked toward one purpose. But all of them were essentially the same. A three-dimensional squiggly line of power channels. And a squiggly line is just a collection of points in three-dimensional space...

Did that mean? Yes... The set of all sets of permutations of three-dimensional coordinates given a certain volume should include all spells that can be expressed within that volume. And a whole lot of bullshit. The only issue was how to filter out all that bullshit. Each set in that super-set would be a potential spell. Most of them would not be actual spells, and a whole bunch would be flawed variations of the same spell but there would also be a whole lot of legit spells in there.

My eyes opened wide with realisation. Within those sets where the optimal spells! If only I could figure out a way to filter out all of the others. But how?

Well, there were always the brute-force method. Just straight up trial and error. That would be incredibly time and power consuming. Perhaps so damned slow that it would take lifetimes to struggle through it.

So how to prune away the sets that would be a waste of time?

Before I could even begin to think of a spell that would search through possible spells I had to reduce the number of sets in the super-set. Perhaps by instead of thinking about it as a set of sets of coordinates I should think about it as a set of equations that described a continuous line in three dimensions. That would reduce a nearly infinite number of shapes that was guaranteed to have no effect. I chuckled dryly. That would just leave me with a slightly smaller infinite number of potential spells. Large adjacent chunks within this set would just be variations of the same spell, ranging from almost useless spells to the optimal before petering out into unusable again. That meant that if I ordered the input variables in some pre-defined way, if the spell found an effect that was unwanted it could probably skip ahead a fair bit in order to arrive at the 'next' spell instead of the 'same but less optimal' spell.

But that also meant that I could run such a spell for a long while, cataloguing all identified spells for later perusal. I shook my head to clear it. Best not get carried away. Let's focus on the case where it identifies a particular spell for a particular effect. I decided to use the same spell that my mother had used when teaching mom magic. The first spell almost all Mages learned. A light spell. If my meta spell found an effect that was not light, it could skip ahead a bit and check again. But what if it skipped over the light spell? Then it would never find it. That was a bummer.

I guess there were nothing to it than to actually iterate through all possible spells. Then all I required was a way to figure out if a potential spell was an actual spell. For my light spell proof of concept, that wouldn't be an issue at all. All I needed to do was to surround the experiment surface with a light-receptive spell that would give some output when light struck it from the inside. I just had to shield it from light from the outside so that it wouldn't intervene with the test results. I stood up and rubbed my hands together excitedly before I walked over to my drawing board and reached for a sheaf of paper. I had my own spell that would materialise the paper out of magic, along with the ink required to make high-quality scrolls. They were also easy to wipe clean, the ink would just seep out of the paper and evaporate with a single cleaning spell. Now I just had to figure out how to formulate an equation that would describe all possible three-dimensional curves. This was going to be fun!


---

## Chapter 18 — CHAPTER EIGHTEEN Unexpected assault {#chapter-18}

CHAPTER EIGHTEEN

Unexpected assault

In the end, it was not fun. I enjoyed it for a few hours, but then frustration started to seep into me. At some point Valia had joined me but she had promptly giving up comprehending my notes with a single quick look at my paper over my shoulder, and a significantly longer look down my cleavage, before she went to bed. Not even her seductive posing and sighing could distract me from my work. When Zandor arrived he didn't even try to look at what I was doing. He just saw me hunched over my desk, bid me goodnight and went to join Valia. To my surprise, I heard no distinctive noises from the bed, apart from Valia's soft snores. I had been prototyping for a few hours when I saw the fatal flaw in my latest attempt and I angrily swiped my hand through the spell construct to destroy it. I rubbed my eyes and tried not to curse too loudly. Then I heard the rustle of sheets and a yawn. I looked over my shoulder to see Valia moving in the dim light cast by my light spell. I had kept the light level low so as not to disturb them. What time was it even?

Valia bounced out of bed and skipped past me, only pausing long enough to press a quick kiss onto the top of my head before she vanished into the small washing area I had installed. A short while later I could hear her shut the lid on the toilet I had built. It didn't have plumbing, not yet, so it was essentially just a hole in the ground with a one-way airtight shield spell to keep any odours at bay. Valia washed her hands in the basin and then rejoined me in the larger room with a relaxed expression on her face. Zandor had started moving by then.

"You should really install a window in that wall," Valia commented idly. "It's almost impossible to tell the time without the sun."

She cracked the door open and stark daylight blinded us all. She quickly shut the door with a wince.

"You've been working all night, haven't you?" She asked me accusingly.

"No..." I started to lie but she raised her hand to cut me off.

"Don't lie."

Zandor got up out of bed, distracting the both of us for a moment until he pulled on his trousers.

"Well?" Valia asked me with an arched eyebrow. The effect was somewhat lessened since she was still turned toward our big lover, her eyes fastened to his thick torso instead of at me.

"I did. But I think I'm really onto something here, I just can't quite reach it," I whined.

"Because you don't sleep enough, Mary. You know that your brain does not work well if you don't sleep."

"I know," I groused. "But I just can't let it go..."

"Sure you can," she said happily and snatched the paper from in front of me. I grasped for it but she deftly dodged my attempts to recover it.

"I'll let you have this back once you've rested," she told me sternly.

"I'll use Blood Magic to wash away my fatigue, it'll be alright now give me that," I said and reached for the paper but she snatched it out of reach.

"Oh, no you don't," Zandor joined Valia in her attempts to thwart me.

"It's not worth it, Mary."

"Just a little?" I tried to mollify them and then my body betrayed me by letting my heavy eyelids almost close completely. With a supreme effort of will I forced them open.

"No," Zandor said resolutely and crossed his magnificent arms across his equally magnificent chest. "You do that and you'll be awake all night, then you'll sleep all through tomorrow and tomorrow night and saving no time at all. Then you'll wake up in the evening feeling hungover and broken and your day and night cycle all out of whack. It ain't worth it."

I scowled at him, but got nothing. He knew he was right. Valia knew he was right. Even I knew he was right. But I didn't like it.

He pointed at the bed. "Bed, now."

"Yeah," Valia chimed in. "It's time for your nap!"

Zandor smiled evilly. "If you don't complain we will bring you breakfast in bed in a couple of hours," he tempted me.

I conceded defeat and went to lie on the bed. "Fine. I'll lie here if that makes you stop tormenting me," I pouted. "I'll even close my eyes if that makes you happy. But I'll be fucked if I fall asleep!"

I was snoring before they even left the room.

The door was flung open after what felt like mere moments. I sat up with a start. My mouth tasted foul and my thoughts were sluggish. I glared at the door, wondering why it had been opened so swiftly and without concern for my sleep. It was Zandor and he was carrying a tray laden with food. That's why the door had been opened so violently. He had no hands to open it with so he had merely kicked it open. And as was usual for him he had used way too much strength. I rolled my eyes at him but the concerned look on his face made me take pause.

"I fell asleep," I consoled him. "It was good to have a nap," I said with a playful stretch of my body. I made sure to curve my spine and pull my shoulders back to accentuate my figure. Perhaps I could entice him into some midday play. When my shenanigans failed to draw his attention I knew that something was very wrong.

"What's up? You look as if you've seen the Traitor herself?"

He placed the tray over my knees and I instantly went for the cup of water. I took a sip and swished it around my mouth to clear the foul taste of sleep.

"Brengar's dead," Zandor said suddenly and sat down on the edge of the bed.

"Can't say that I'm saddened to see him go," I shrugged and bit into a loaf of bread.

"He was murdered," Zandor clarified.

"I can't say I'm surprised. The man was an asshole, he probably pissed someone off that he shouldn't have."

"Maybe..."

"But?" I prompted.

"It's just the manner of his death. It was so needlessly brutal. I get why someone would want to kill the man, but not like this..."

Zandor had seen plenty of death in his short life, but this particular one seemed to disturb him more than I would have thought that it would. I knew for a fact that Zandor had detested Brengar.

"How'd he die?"

"From what I've heard... He was attacked in his tent. His vocal cords were cut with a stiletto, his hamstrings severed. The tendons in his armpits as well. He was completely incapacitated. Then he was dragged from his tent and onto the wall where his belly was opened and then he was hung down the inside of the wall. His guts spilled onto the ground at the sudden stop," Zandor said with a shudder. "Again, I get why someone would have murdered him but this method... Only a monster would go to such lengths to torment a man they planned to kill. Why not just slit his throat and be done with it?"

I had gone completely still at his description.

"I know, chilling isn't it?"

It was. But not for the reason he believed. I knew of only one person that would be so brutal, so methodical. Only one person with motives and skills to commit such an act without being found out. Aunt Alissa. What if someone else connected the murder with the methods of Alissa Turncloak? What if she was found out? They would never let her go. They'd kill her. I had to find her, tell her to return to Dun Traelak!

But I couldn't be seen searching through the camp. I'd just have to carry on as normal and if I saw her I had to try to get her somewhere where we could talk in private.

I forced my spinning belly to calm down and took another bite of bread to buy me some time to figure out a response. I swallowed too early and winced when the partially dry bread slid painfully down my throat.

"Yeah. We better keep a watchful eye. I'll add some extra layers of security to this place," I told him reassuringly.

Instead of being calmed down his head snapped around and he fixed me with a ferocious look.

"Do you think this murderer will go after you? Do you think they seek to weaken the Groundlands by taking out the most useful Construction Mage?"

"No," I replied quickly. Perhaps too quickly. "If that was their intent then they wouldn't have revealed their presence by murdering the most useless Skylander around."

Zandor calmed down a little. "Fair enough." He rubbed his face and sighed. "I gotta go. Micah wants me to escort Wylah and Felaan. He's sent out tracking parties to find out if the murderer left the Groundlands or if he is still here."

"He probably left after a display like that," I said, emphasising the gender pronoun slightly. If they believed that they were hunting a man instead of a woman then Alissa might be just a little bit more safe from pursuit.

"I guess," Zandor agreed. "Now eat up, Valia told me to make sure that you ate but I really have to go," he said apologetically.

"That's fine, you go do your job, Zandor. I appreciate it."

I pouted for a kiss and he happily obliged. When he pulled back I snaked my hand behind his neck and pulled him back for a second lingering kiss before I winked and sent him on his way. I finished the whole tray of food while refusing to think about this new development. It was currently out of my hands, no reason to worry about even though I desperately wanted to. I left the tray on the floor beside the bed with a shrug and after a longing look at my schematics I left the workshop. I had work to do that was not research. With the new massive artificial ley line we had enough magical power to materialise the walls at full capacity as well as producing bricks and still have power left over. Even with the construction of the dam there were still a small power surplus. So either I could start setting up construction of guns, or plumbing to actually use the water that the dam would aggregate or I could head out to open one of the ley line crossings in the mountains to the east. Either of them was viable. The plumbing wouldn't be useful until the dam was finished and a small reservoir of water had formed, and it might be wise to wait for the majority of housing to be finished so that I would know the layout of the pipes. Or perhaps it would be better to lay the foundations so that it would be easier to build the houses with pre-existing plumbing. I shrugged. I'd let the architects deal with that decision. I had already manifested my Angel armour while I ate so I took to the skies. I was surreptitiously looking for Alissa while in the guise of looking for Micah. As it turned out, Micah was meeting with Archibald, the lead architect of the Groundlands. I landed a few paces from them and politely waited for them to notice me even though I wanted nothing more than shooting that pink-haired bastard in the face. He had ordered those farmers killed out of hand, because it was convenient. They only forced me to wait for a short while before they beckoned me over. I explained the plumbing issue with Archibald and to my complete lack of surprise he had an answer ready. He had been a prominent architect on the Skylands and had overseen the construction of buildings for almost fifty years.

"We'll build the houses with plumbing and make sure that each row of buildings connect in the same direction. That will make it easier to run the mainlines up and down the streets."

I nodded. "Makes sense to me."

"Are you planning on burying the pipes?"

I hadn't even thought about how the pipes should be run through the Groundlands. In Dun Traelak they were all above ground, running along the outsides of the buildings.

"I don't know, what do you think is best?"

"Buried, of course."

"Then I guess we'll bury them," I concluded.

Archibald nodded and that was that. I sort of liked Archibald. He was alright for a Skylander. No nonsense and straight to the point. Too bad that he had come here to further the Skylander ideals and that I would have to kill him at some point.

"So, what's next, Micah?" I asked the big man. "More power for construction, or weapons?"

"Do we need more power?"

"Not yet, but soon. If I set up more factories and we run out of power then overall production will suffer."

Micah stroked his chin as he contemplated. "I say guns. That is worrying," he said with a gesture at the orange flare to the north. It had been an almost constant presence for a week now. The savages must have sent at least a hundred of their own to their deaths by now, assuming that they only assaulted it one at a time. One every hour for a week. But I doubted that they were that stupid. There could be hundreds or thousands of them up there. But the scouts still had not found any group of them larger than a dozen so there couldn't be that many of them. Unless their main population was further up into the mountains and those we encountered were just their border patrols. We just didn't know.

"Do you think they are mounting an assault?"

He shook his head. "I don't know. But what we do know is that their presence around the focusing tower is intensifying. That much we can tell from that flare. And that increase in activity worries me. I'd rather have some big guns to deal with them. The more artillery we have, the fewer of our people will die if they actually come here."

"Fair enough," I agreed.

Without further words, I leaped into the sky. Higher and higher I flew until I could see the entirety of the valley. A lot of Skylanders had arrived lately and there were close to two thousand people in the Groundlands. The space between the two curtain walls were far from full, but real estate planning was starting to become an actual problem. Where to have manufactoriums and where to have housing were not easy choices to make. Currently most housing was situated to the west near my workshop while the bulk of manufacturing was to the east. So I guess that the gun manufactoriums should be in the east as well. I drifted closer to the ground and to the east to better see the layout of that particular area. A few minutes later and I had made my decision. I dropped to the ground and considered what kind of weapons we needed. The savages had not displayed any type of armour or technology, only a strange ability to absorb magic. So Arclite weaponry might not work on them since they might just suck the power straight out the beams. Traditional munitions would have to do. The southern wall on the other hand could use some more modern magical defences.

But should it? Arclite weapons were mine and Kendall's invention. The Skylanders didn't know about it. If I suddenly built them, they would wonder what it was and how I knew about them. Sure, I could possibly explain it as something that Matthias Varl and I had invented but did I want to? No, I decided. Even though Arclite weapons were easier to sabotage, just disrupt the magical power flow, than traditional guns I didn't want to risk that powerful spell to fall into the hands of Skylanders. Now I just had to figure out how to sabotage traditional guns in a way that didn't impede their normal mode of operation and that would allow me to trigger the fault remotely. All the while appearing as a simple design flaw in case another Construction Mage arrived that might find it. I was more than a little surprised that out of the almost seventeen hundred Skylanders that had arrived since I came here none were a proficient Construction Mage. There were a few that dabbled or had been apprentices or journeyman back in the day, but none that were masters. That was suspicious. I shrugged that thought away. An anomaly of statistics, probably. Nothing more. I got to work.

Night was falling when I finally finished and I wiped my brow with the back of my hand. It had been a long day, but a productive one. I decided to walk back to my workshop instead of flying. The amount of power I had channelled that day had made me pleasantly tired and I was covered in a sheen of perspiration. The cool night air felt glorious against my damp skin and I popped a few buttons on my blouse to cool down faster. I'd like to be cold and freezing when I crawled beneath the blanket to snuggle up to my warm lovers and let their bodies warm me up again. I passed a man who looked as exhausted as I felt and I gave him a fleeting smile as we passed one another. He just gave me a short glance and a nod, and then he did a double take. I expected his grin to turn into the usual leer men had when they saw a pretty woman but instead his face whitened and his eyes widened. I looked behind me to see what had scared him so.

"Betrayer!" He choked out and time seemed to freeze. He must have recognised me for who I was, somehow. Had he seen me as a child and connected that child to the woman I was today? Had he travelled to Dun Traelak and seen me? It didn't matter. The only thing that mattered was that he had somehow recognised me, he was armed, and I was turned away from him. I had foolishly let go of my shield to let the cool air glide across my skin and no shield I could raise would be strong enough and quick enough for it to stop a bullet if he decided to draw and shoot me. It didn't stop me from trying. The first vestiges of a shield bloomed out of me while my right hand reached for my revolver and my left conjured a fireball. It felt as if I was swimming in molasses and my head slowly pivoted to face him. He was raising his single-shot pistol to aim at my chest. I swore internally for even though his weapon was a single-shot muzzle-loader my magesense told me that it was rifled. There where no way that he would miss at this distance. If it hadn't been rifled I might have had a chance. Might have. My fingers curled around the grip of my revolver. Flames flickered around my hand and a blue sheen shimmered in front of my face as my shield started to manifest. Three ways to thwart him, three ways that where too slow. My legs tensed in preparation for a sideways dive, but I knew that that effort too would be far too slow. His hand suddenly jerked sideways and his finger squeezed on the trigger, the sharp report of his gun shocked me out of my stupor. I drew my gun and raised it. The flames finally ignited fully and my shield manifested. But it was all in vain. A shadow flitted across my peripheral vision and blood sheeted out of his opened throat in a wet wheeze and he stumbled sideways, clutching fingers to the gushing wound. He managed to take two steps before he tumbled bonelessly to the ground to land face first in the mud with a wet squelch. Alissa looked at me and wordlessly jerked her head for me to get out of there. I nodded in thanks and banished my fireball, holstered my weapon and strengthened my shield before I burst into a sprint. If Alissa hadn't been there, watching my back, I might very well have died that night. I didn't stop running until I stood outside my workshop. With sweat pouring down my skin, breathing heavily and heart beating rapidly, I opened the door as softly as I could and sat on the floor with my back against the wall. I curled my arms around my legs and rested my forehead on my knees and shook silently as to not disturb my sleeping lovers.


---

## Chapter 19 — CHAPTER NINETEEN An old man come to visit {#chapter-19}

CHAPTER NINETEEN

An old man come to visit

"So here I am again," mother said from beside me.

"It would appear so," I agreed.

We watched the budding focusing tower that was slowly building from the latest ley line crossing that I had opened. After I had finished the gun manufactorium spells the magic power supply had reached its limit and I had headed to the east with Zandor to secure more power. Once again I had flown higher into the mountains where he could not follow, and now mother had joined me.

"I suppose that our hypothesis that I only manifest in a very dense magical field is correct," mother observed casually.

"It would appear so."

"What's troubling you, my child?"

"Am I really that transparent?" I said with a wan smile and turned to hug my mother.

"You will always be transparent to me, love," she murmured into my hair.

In her embrace it felt as if a weight had slid off my shoulders.

"Alissa is here," I said.

"Good. She will have your back," mother answered with a nod of approval.

"But it's dangerous, she should go home!" I protested.

Mother just smiled indulgently, waiting patiently for me to make some connection that she already had.

And then it hit me.

"I sound like Uncle Kenny..."

Mother nodded, her shining eyes twinkling.

I sighed. I guess Alissa had the same right to be here as I did.

"But there is something else bothering you," mother prodded me.

"The Skylanders... I was sent out to negotiate trade with the nearby farms. They refused."

"So the Skylanders killed them and seized their farms," mother concluded without hesitation.

"Not only that, they also wiped out a whole village just in case they were angered by the murder of the farmers."

"Of course. That's the Skylander way. Don't pay for things that you can steal."

I nodded slowly. I had already known that, of course, but to see it happen with my own two eyes... That was something else entirely.

"What about you, mother? How are you adapting to being an incorporeal liche?"

Mother turned away from me and stared into the swirling vortex of magic. "I don't know, honestly. Most of the time I am not even aware. It's as if I'm sleeping, no dreams. I fear that I am stuck like this forever," she finally admitted.

"I am crushed by grief, still. I understand that it's been many years for you, but to me Red died just days ago. But I am glad that I am here to help you out," she said with a warm smile.

"But?"

"But one day you will be gone," mother finally said with a quivering voice. "And what then? Years will pass and I might wake up every now and then. My existence will be a series of disjointed moments of wakefulness, unable to partake. Unable to actually live. I exist, but do I live? No."

She turned back to face me, her back ramrod straight and her glowing gaze intense.

"I will help you in any way I can, but when you are done here and these Skylanders are dead I need your help."

"Anything for you, mother. Anything," I promised her sincerely.

"I'd like to join Red. I saw her Soul spread out into the magic field, to become one with everything. I want to become one with everything as well. To become a part of her once more. When the dust finally settles, I want you, no I need you, to find a way to wrest me out of this gem and let me go."

Tears were silently spilling down my cheeks but I didn't protest. I understood, even though I didn't want her to go. I had just recently had her return to me. I didn't want to be the one to finally kill her. But I would. Because she wanted me to. And I loved her so very much.

"I promise, mother."

"Thank you, daughter. That is all I ask."

We stood silently for a while.

"It's odd," mother finally commented. "Sometimes when I am awake like this, when all is silent, I can almost feel her next to me. As if she is out there in the magical field, watching over me."

I didn't answer, the sudden shearing in my heart wouldn't let me. I knew that it wasn't possible. An unfettered Soul would not survive, it would evaporate and disintegrate into the magical field.

"I know it can't be true," mother admitted. "I saw her Soul spread apart. I felt it. But still, I can feel her even now. Perhaps there is something to the old Bizhjerai legends about the ancestor spirits remaining. Perhaps the Spirit is a part of the Soul, something indestructible that carries on after death."

I didn't have the heart to voice my doubts. She was in enough pain already. If deluding herself like this could bring comfort to her troubled Soul, then let her.

"Perhaps," I agreed instead. "That is a comforting thought."

"You don't have to humour me, little one," mother said wryly. "Pay no attention to the ramblings of the dead woman. Now tell me, how goes your plan?"

I told her everything.

"Seems like you have everything well in hand, love. I have only one question."

"Yes, mother?"

"What about stragglers? Even during the chaos that I unleashed on Utopeia, thousands still managed to flee and survive."

"The curtain walls. When I destroy the Groundlands, I will make sure to keep the walls intact, the doors locked and that the stairs onto the top of the wall collapses. I hope that there will not be time to fashion ladders to climb them."

"What about Valkyries? Or these Angels that you spoke of?"

"I will have to think about that. I hadn't considered that, thank you for pointing that out. Worst case I will have to hunt them down myself."

"Don't forget the Skylanders that are working the farms outside of the walls."

"Oh, they will be on the inside. Y can count on that," I said with an evil smile.

"Well then, there is nothing more that I can teach you," mother said with a proud smile.

"There is always something else you can teach me, mother. Never doubt that," I responded gently.

"Don't be so certain of that. You are only a few years younger than I was when I left the Skylands. Sooner than you think you will be older than me."

I hadn't considered that. It was a strange thought. Mother had always seemed larger than life, older and wiser and always had an answer to everything. But soon I would be older than her. No one in the history of the world had probably never been this close to their parents in age. Even if they were adoptive parents.

"Ah, the spell is devouring the power already, I feel myself weakening. I think I will retire earlier this time, perhaps the power already stored in the gem will allow me to manifest in a weaker environment. Let's call it an experiment," she said with a smile.

"I'm looking forward to it," I grinned at mother and embraced her.

And then she was gone, my arms suddenly encircling nothing. I drew a shuddering breath. Seeing her was bitter sweet. I had my mother back, but not all of her. It was rough seeing her, for I knew that she detested what she had become. What she had turned herself into in order to defeat the Skylands and save countless people from its tyranny. To see her so wracked with grief over mom tore me apart, ripped and prodded at old scars until they bled and chafed raw once more. I shook myself to gather my willpower before I leaped higher into the mountains to open another ley line crossing. There were two more in this general area and I was going to open both of them. My revised spell didn't require as much effort on my part with setting up the initial spell enchantment on the ground as long as I was willing to leave a power core behind to fuel the initial opening. I would swing by on the way back to collect the recharged cores. It took my only a short while to set up the second crossing and I didn't stay to observe it. I was confident enough in the spell by that point that I just left it to open the crossing. When the crossing opened up with its usual violence I basked in the wave of power. I looked around expectantly but mother did not manifest. But I got the sense that she was there anyway and I noticed a powerful current of magic being pulled into the dagger that housed her. She was charging up. She showed some initiative and drive to do something. That was good. Perhaps she would embrace her liche status and rejoin society. As long as there was a powerful source of magic for her to draw upon to sustain herself then maybe she could remain awake and present indefinitely. If she wanted to. I hoped she would. I would love to have one of my mothers back, even though it still grieved me that one of them were irrevocably dead. After a little while the gem on the dagger started to glow gently, the glow slowly intensifying.

"Should I leave you up here to gather strength?" I asked aloud, wondering if she could hear me even though she had not manifested her astral body.

She didn't respond with words, perhaps she could not use them without her astral body, but I had the clear impression that she declined. I leaned back against the cold rock and basked in the waning sun. When it finally set I leaped off the mountainside and soared on skeletal wings toward our camp and Zandor. It had been a productive day and soon the Groundlands would have more than enough power once again.

I fell from the sky to land lightly next to Micah. I had been installing the anti-air guns on the cliffs when the alarm was suddenly sounded and I had immediately flung myself off the cliff to find out what was going on.

"What's going on?" I asked the big man with the pink hair.

"I have no idea," he rumbled and set off in a sprint toward the southern wall.

I hurried to follow but his much longer strides quickly left me in the dust. I had to use quick jets of my Angel armour just to keep up. While Micah vanished into the stairway that let him ascend the partly constructed walls I decided to cheat and just flew up there. I landed next to a sentry who had a pair of binoculars pressed to his eyes.

"What's going on?" I reiterated my previous question to Micah.

"Skyship on the horizon," he said and gave me an excited grin. But in his eyes I could see trepidation.

With a lurch in my belly I quickly scanned inward and I let out a relieved breath when I saw that all three of the crystals embedded in my breasts were all intact. For a moment I had been worried that they had inadvertently been crushed to summon my allies. Then my insides twisted again when I realised that if that Skyship was not one of my friends, then it was most likely an enemy. One of the Cities perhaps? Come to investigate or possibly trade.

If it was here to attack us then we were in a heap of trouble. The current power storage of the Groundlands, the magical power stored in the power cores deep beneath the tower, was not strong enough to maintain a bombardment shield for more than a few hours. Even less if they destroyed the focusing towers first. I should have added anti-air guns to them as well. On the other hand, a dedicated and clever opponent would be able to overcome any defences that was worth the investment of power. The anti-air guns that I had been mounting might be able to fend off a single medium-class Skyship. As long as it was not outfitted with a bombardment cannon. If it was, then we were thoroughly fucked in the ass. And not the good kind of fucked in the ass either. The bad, explosive and unwanted kind. Micah came bursting out of the stairwell and despite his age he was not even breathing hard.

"Skyship approaching, unknown origin," I reported curtly.

"Hostile?" He grunted in response before grabbing the binoculars out of the sentries hands and having a look for himself.

"Unknown. Shall I rally the anti-air gunners?"

"Yes. And prepare the bombardment shield, just in case. But don't activate it unless they fire upon us."

I nodded and took to the skies. A short hop and a glide later I strode into the boxy extension to the receiver tower and found the on call Mage already there.

"Be ready to activate the bombardment shield at the first sign of combat but preserve power until then," I ordered the tall and lanky woman with the cerulean hair.

She would have been attractive if she hadn't been so skinny. I preferred women with either curves or tone, not just skin and bone. She gave me an impassive nod and poked a stray strand of hair into the messy bun on top of her head. I returned the nod and immediately left. I leaped up onto the western cliff face and met the gunners jogging up the switchback road I had laboriously carved out of the rock. When they finally arrived they were to a man red in the face and huffing and puffing for breath.

"Skyship's inbound. Track target and be ready. Fire on my mark only, got it?"

I was answered with a chorus of "Yes, ma'am!" and a bunch of loud wheezing noises. I guess that was good enough. I flew across the valley mouth to land on the eastern cliff face with a similar arrangement of anti-air guns. The men were already here though they where still breathing heavily.

"There's a Skyship approaching. Track the target, but do not fire unless they fire first. The other gunners are cued to fire only if we do. Take your positions," I ordered and the men and women hopped to with alacrity. "You there," I said and pointed at a stout woman wearing a pair of binoculars around her neck. "Give me your binoculars."

She complied without a word and I used the binoculars to scan the horizon until I found the Skyship. The sun was reflecting off the sky-borne beast and I couldn't make out any details. No markings of allegiance that I could see. I couldn't even see what kind of engines it were using. But something in the glare of the sun seemed off to me. It didn't reflect as if it where an armoured metal hull. All I could tell was that it was rapidly approaching and I estimated that it would arrive within the hour. The following hour was nerve-wracking, but I could eventually see the Skyship when a cloud hid the sun. The armour of the Skyship glinted violet, and thick streamers of magical plasma propelled the whole beast forward.

Stunned, I lowered the binoculars.

"Skylander Airship," I whispered.

The colour and engines made the ship unmistakable. It was of Skylander design. That meant that it must belong to one of the Clans, but why where they here? Had word reached Dun Traelak that the Skylanders were building a settlement? Had they come to destroy us? It was too soon! There must still be thousands of Skylanders out there in the world that hadn't managed to arrive yet. This place must survive for a while longer to act as a candle to the moths. But could I help the Skylanders fight my people? Could I kill my own kindred for the sake of revenge?

I limply returned the binoculars to the middle-aged woman when she asked for it and her surprised gasp when she beheld the Skyship pulled me out of my inner turmoil.

"It's the Prowler!" She exclaimed. "I recognise it from Utopeia, it yet lives!"

Her words were met by a cheer from the other gunners. I wracked my memory, or rather my mothers memories, of the battle of Utopeia. There had been no Skyships that survived the battle for Utopeia. Only the Hand of Glory had remained after the siege of Dun Traelak and that massive beast had been killed by mother, brother and a small flight of Greater Dragons. But wait! There had been one Skylander ship that had been mortally wounded by the Fists of Heaven during that last battle outside the walls of Dun Traelak. It had withdrawn from the fight and limped away and had been subsequently forgotten. Could this be that very same Skyship? Had it been prowling the world ever since in search of enough magical power to repair? Perhaps. But what did the sudden return of the Prowler mean? I fought to contain my anxiety while I and the entirety of the Groundlands waited for the Skyship to approach.

"Be ready," I ordered the gunnery crew when the Skyship drew closer. The crew hopped to without complaint, probably because they believed that the older woman had been mistaken and that the Skyship might be of the Bizhjerai fleet. But the Skyship sailed within range of our guns, which meant that the bombardment cannon fitted on its prow was well within range of the Groundlands, without firing. It had started descending a while ago and now it was far below cruising altitude.

"It's going in for a landing," the older woman cried in excitement.

"Keep tracking it," I ordered them and ignored the baleful glare the woman shot me. "It might be a trick," I cautioned them all.

Soon enough the Skyship touched down on the northern side of the Groundlands and people spilled out of its innards to moor it properly. The engines cycled down and soldiers streamed onto the walls. Mortars and field guns that had been spaced along the inside of the wall all turned to trace the crystal behemoth that had suddenly appeared behind us. Micah wasn't taking any chances. Good.

A man jogged from the Skyship toward the wall and he stopped well within firing range. I assumed that he shouted something to the men on the wall and within a few minutes I saw the sun reflect off a familiar pinkish hue. I supposed that Micah and the man exchanged words before the man ran back to the Skyship. The area around the ship was a hive of ordered chaos and it looked to me as if they were mooring her securely for a prolonged stay. A procession of men and women in combat armour marched from the Skyship and through the unfinished gatehouse on the wall.

"Keep your guns on the ship, fire if fired upon," I ordered and leaped from the side of the cliff.

My Angel wings caught the wind and I glided toward the command post. I arrived well ahead of Micah and the soldiers from the Prowler and I entered the building to wait for them. I had to admit, I was quite curious about what was going on. As well as a fair bit nervous. I hadn't forgotten that man that had recognised me and tried to kill me on site. I had thought that I had changed enough in ten-odd years that being recognised would not be a problem, but I hadn't considered that a Skylander might have visited Dun Traelak to have seen me since. Perhaps one of these new arrivals had done the same? When time dragged on and they didn't join me in the command post I was getting worried. But then I suddenly heard Micah's booming voice from outside and realised that he was using the raised stage outside to make an announcement, probably to introduce the newcomers to the curious crowd that inevitably would have formed. I shook my head at my own folly of waiting in here and I walked over to the door and stepped through. What I hadn't considered was that the particular door that I had chosen led me right onto the stage and hundreds of eyes shifted in my direction when I suddenly appeared. Micah looked over his shoulder at my disturbing entrance. Beside him stood a wizened old man clutching a crystal staff as if his entire balance depended on it. He looked like a kindly old grandfather with a pleasant face and rheumy blue eyes that seemed to overflow with liquid but without it spilling over into tears. His face was heavily lined, at least the parts that were not obscured by a scraggly gray beard that matched the colour of his robes.

"Ah, Mary," Micah introduced me loudly. "This will be a joyous day for you!"

He gestured at the old man beside him, waiting expectantly. His face grew gradually more and more confused at my continued blank expression.

"I would have thought that you would be happier to encounter your presumed dead master," Micah said softly so that the crowd couldn't hear him.

"Master?" The old man said. Even though his voice was old and frail it still carried across the crowd on a stream of magic. I wouldn't have been surprised if his little spell had been entirely accidental. The man literally oozed power.

"Mathias Varl," Micah said and repeated his gesture at the old man.

My heart sank into my pelvis. Shit. I had chosen Varl as my supposed master since he had been old even when Utopeia fell and I hadn't heard anything about him since. I had assumed that he was dead, a perfect cover for my skill with Construction Magic.

"I do not know what fairy tales this slip of a girl have fooled you into believing, Micah, but I have never laid eyes upon her before in my life," Varl announced with a disdainful sniff.

"That's not Varl," I protested but it sounded weak even to my own ears.

Micah rounded on me, his eyes narrowing.

"I knew Varl personally before the Fall. I tell you unequivocally that that is him. Who are you, Mary? Really?"

I opened my mouth to answer, but I had nothing. How could I possibly recover from this?

"Take her away for questioning!" Micah roared and shocked guards hesitantly climbed onto the stage. My hand dropped to my revolver, my other curling into a spell.

But it would be useless. I was facing the entirety of the Groundlands. If I resisted now then I would die. At least imprisoned I might have a chance to find a way to talk myself out of this. I raised my hands and showed them to the wary soldiers and I could see them relax a fraction.

"Please, allow me, Micah," Varl said and waved impatiently for one of his servants to approach. The tall woman handed him a set of cuffs and I felt my eyes widen. I knew what those were. I had seen them in my mothers memories. Mom had used them on her when she first captured mother, though this particular set was made out of materialised magic instead of metal. How had Varl managed to reverse engineer those? Had he infiltrated Dun Traelak to learn from the Bizhjerai?

I stared in horror as the soldiers snapped the cuffs onto my wrists and ankles and felt unfamiliar dread when the collar snapped shut around my neck. My magic was torn from me in a violent vortex and my vision dimmed. I fell to my knees and coughed, retching. My mind spun and my ears rang. A burning pain spread across my body. I had felt that pain before. Or rather, mother had. It was the pain from when your body was reaching its capacity for channelling magic. My skin would soon char, my flesh melt and my bones crack into dust. I felt myself weakening rapidly, power leeched from my limbs until it finally stopped when I was on the brink of passing out. The pain remained, the vertigo almost felled me. But I remained on all fours, panting and dribbling onto the stage.

"Take her away for questioning," I heard Micah order. "We will get to the bottom of this, Varl. I promise you that."

"She was the Construction Mage you told me about? The one who helped you set all of this up?" I heard Varl ask when I felt strong hands under my armpits.

I was violently hoisted to my feet and I heaved again. I had though that my belly was empty, but I was proven wrong by a thin stream of bile and partly digested bread that dribbled down my cheek and trickled into my cleavage.

"She is," Micah affirmed.

"Then I would verify all of her work. Do not trust a liar."
