Wylah had picked the tunnel that didn't have such a steep incline. One by one my friends ran around the corner. I could practically hear the savages breath down my neck. A premonition made me twist to my right and a spear thrust past me where my neck had just been. The motion put my full weight on my broken femur and I cried out in pain as my leg folded. I fell, careening forward in full tilt. I would smash my head against the wall and there were nothing I could do about it. At least my helmet would keep my skull intact but the shock would be funnelled into my neck. I didn't know if it could take that much punishment. Something slammed into my side and I was wrenched sideways. Instead of slamming into the wall at top speed I instead struck it at a sideways angle and I spun to the floor. Whatever had struck me was wrenched from me and I heard a soft curse from beside me. Valia. She had dive-tackled me to keep me from smashing into the wall. I groggily rolled to my back only to see a savage towering over me. It's spear was already launching toward my chest. The stone tip struck my armoured corset with tremendous force. The spear snapped, my corset cracked. But it had saved my life. I had barely even felt the impact. I lashed out with my leg and the tip of my solid boot struck the savages oversised genitals. It made a pathetic mewling sound and fell to its knees while clutching its gonads. Valia thrust her sword beside me from her prone position and it bit deep into the calf of a female savage that fell screaming to the floor. Valia bounced to her feet and lashed out with her sword, making another savage flinch. I struggled to my feet but I couldn't see the rest of our friends through the press of savage bodies.

"Zandor!" I screamed at the top of my lungs.

"We're cut off," came the panicked reply.

"Keep running," Wylah screamed, her higher pitched voice cutting through the dim. "We'll go this way! Meet up by the bikes. Just go if you can't lose them, the Groundlands must know of this!" A short pause. "Zandor, Felaan! We can't get to them, let's go!"

"Shit!" I cursed. "Valia!"

"Yeah," my lover replied as she used her magic to throw dust into the eyes of the leading savages. They blinked and growled and swung blindly. Clever girl. "Let's go," she yelled and started running. I hobbled after her as best I could.

I don't know how long we ran, I lost track of time. The smoke was thick and it was difficult to breathe. Pain shot through my body from my leg with every step. Several times I nearly fell but managed to keep running by a sheer force of will and a liberal application of Blood Magic.

Suddenly we burst out into open air and skidded to a halt. I cast about, searching for a way to go. But there where none. We were on an overlook, only cliff edges on all sides. It almost looked as if the tunnel had been carved to this place for the vantage point. And the view was breathtaking. The sun was rising in the distance, painting the sky red and casting beautiful shadows across the valley. Had we really been inside those caves the whole night? Screams from the tunnel drew me back to the present. We had nowhere to go. We were done. Valia seemed to have understood the same for her shoulders suddenly relaxed and she stood up straight before turning to me. Her eyes regarded me lovingly.

"You should go. Just fly away. At least one of us will live."

"No!" I protested.

"Don't be foolish, Marissa, I would gladly give my life for yours," she said and cupped my cheek with her gloved hand.

I could just stare at her, stunned.

"I've known since I saw your Bizhjerai Rune. When I tucked you into bed after you exhausted yourself on the plains. I've known a long time who you are, and loved you anyway. You are a wonderful person Marissa. I don't hold your upbringing against you. You're with us now, that's all that matters."

If I had had the wherewithal to speak at all then I would have told her that I was not allied with the Groundlands at all. That I planned on betraying and destroying all of the Skylanders. But I couldn't get the words out.

"Just go, love. Just fly away," Valia urged me with a wet glint in her platinum eyes.

"I can't," I managed to choke out. "I don't have the power to fly. Besides, I won't leave you here. Either we both find a way out, or we both die here!"

Valia reached up and pulled down my mask and then she did the same to her own. Her smile was sad but proud.

"Okay," she said and then she leaned in and kissed me.

The night went still. The sounds of the approaching savages faded away into nothing, the fear and hopelessness dwindled until all that remained in my mind was this lovely woman. She had known me, the true me, for months and accepted me for who I was. The Traitor's daughter. Brought up among the enemy yet welcomed back into the fold. She had seen Marissa and found her desirable. Valia puckered her lips and dragged her tongue across my teeth before she pulled back slightly.

"Forgive me," she whispered.

Before I could even begin to react she had placed all of her weight on me and she heaved with her legs. I didn't stand a chance against her larger weight and we fell backward over the edge of the cliff. The air rushed past me and sang in my ears. I could only stare at Valia, aghast. She was so damn beautiful. Her golden hair swirled behind her, shining metallic from the reflection of the rising sun. Her lips were stretched in a wan smile and her eyes stared into mine. Then I felt the press of her power on my Soul. I couldn't stop myself from drinking it. Primal instinct took over and I consumed her power and made it my own. She didn't hold back, I could see the pain in her eyes when she pushed past her limit.

"Valia, no!" I screamed but the wind ripped the words away from me before she could hear it.

Faster and faster the power flowed until she could expel no more. Her eyelids slid shut and then she jerked and forced herself to remain awake. With supreme effort she curled her legs below her and put them against my stomach. She blew me a kiss and then she pushed off, launching us apart.

"No!" I wailed in denial, but it was done. I pushed power into my choker and my Angel armour cycled up. The jets fired and I rotated myself to shoot toward Valia.

I knew that I would barely be able to slow down myself. With her extra weight my armour had no hope of coping. She knew it too for she shook her head vigorously when I approached.

"No!" She cried and tried to swim through the air away from me.

I reached for her and she lashed out at me, striking my grasping hand.

"Please!" I saw her shout but I could not hear her words. "Don't!" She pleaded.

I didn't listen. Instead I bowled into her and wrapped my arms around her. She squirmed and fought but I was stronger than her after she had given me all of her strength. She suddenly went lax in my arms and I fired my engines at full throttle to slow us down. Her arms circled around my neck and I felt her tug on my Soul. She must have been desperate for magical power and couldn't help herself. Despite needing all power that I had to slow us down I couldn't deny her. I let her have a trickle of power. Then she did something. I didn't know at the time how she had done it, and she probably couldn't have done it if I hadn't been in such distressed and weakened mental state. She wrested control of my Angel armour from me. She invaded my choker and ripped the connection out of my mind. Searing pain lanced from my shoulder and my left arm went slack. She fought out of my weakened grip and kicked off from me.

In the blink of an eye she had fallen away from me while my armour kept braking me. I fought to regain control and quickly retook it. But the damage was done. I looked down and saw Valia falling below me, her big eyes staring at me and her beautiful face framed by her whipped golden hair. Her lips were curled in a loving smile and then the tree tops swallowed her up.

An incoherent howl of loss rang in my ears and it took my a moment to realise it was my own voice.


---

## Chapter 23 — CHAPTER TWENTY-THREE Planning betrayal {#chapter-23}

CHAPTER TWENTY-THREE

Planning betrayal

I dropped down through the trees, not caring about how the branches snapped at me or how my armoured limbs cracked them. My jets set fire to the foliage and I dropped to the ground illuminated by the rapidly immolating treetops. But I had no attention to spare for the destruction of my descent. Not even the pain of my broken femur that brought me to the ground could draw my attention of the bundle crowned with gold.

"Valia!" I gasped out. She didn't answer. My fingers dug into the soil and I laboriously dragged myself through the dirt and decomposing plant matter toward her. She wasn't moving and she lay oddly on the ground, as if she had sunken into it. I pulled her into my lap and and found that she had indeed made a little crater in the soft ground, but it had also made a crater into her. Blood and gore dripped out of her mangled backside across my legs, but I did not care. I cupped her cheek, turning her face to mine. Her eyes were closed, peaceful. But her face had the unnatural slackness of death and my fingers that cradled the back of her head sank into the mushy fragments of her skull beneath her scalp.

I was numb, felt nothing. It was simply too much. First Alissa, now Valia... Possibly Zandor, Wylah and Felaan as well. I lowered my forehead to hers and closed my eyes. She was still warm and for a moment it felt like any of the other times when we had rested foreheads like this. But then the moment dragged out and I did not feel her warm breath on my face and neck. She was gone.

"Fuck, why Valia?" I whimpered.

But she didn't answer. Yet I knew why. She had known that we would both die if we remained. She had known that she would die regardless, so she had made sure that I survived.

"Why didn't I teach you how to use one of the new Angel armours?" I whispered.

Because it took time to learn how to use them. Time that we did not have in the Groundlands.

I should have made time! If I had then she would have been able to fly away with me. Her death was my fault, because of my poor judgement.

I sat there for a long while, cradling my dead lover to my chest. I didn't care if the savages found me now. I'd kill as many of them as I could before they finally got me.

But no savages came. Eventually I reached up to my shoulder and curled my fingers around the hilt of the knife she had stabbed me with in order to break out of my grip. With a grunt I ripped it free and gently slid it into the sheath on her belt. Flames burst out of the stab wound as I cauterised it but I felt no pain.

Finally I stood and lifted her body in a bridal carry. Despite all of the blood that had left her body she still weighed more than me and my broken femur protested angrily. I gritted my teeth and boosted my strength with Blood Magic and then I started walking. One painful step at a time, left foot, right foot, left foot. My mind was blank, my heart was numb. An eternity later I walked past the focusing tower and replenished my magical power. I gently placed Valia on the ground and cast a few simple spells. A thin tarp of materialised magic formed along with a strong rope and I wound Valia in a violet shroud before I picked her up again. I had to sling her across the back of my bike like so much meat but I absolutely refused to leave her behind. I didn't know what the savages ate and I would not risk them consuming what remained of my lover. I had just tightened the final rope across her back when I heard footsteps approaching.

I spun around, revolver in my right hand and a spell in my left. I cocked the hammer with my thumb and grinned.

Time to fight. Time to die.

A large form burst out of the foliage and I raised my revolver to kill it.

"Mary!" It cried and I blinked, hesitating with my finger on the trigger.

"Zandor?" I queried, releasing the hammer and dismissing my spell.

My lover took three long strides and embraced me, crushing me to his chest. I buried my face in his shoulder and breathed in his scent.

Sweat, blood, and something comforting. Something durable.

After a brief moment he pushed me back and let his eyes roam from the crown of my head to the soles of my boots.

"It's not my blood," I said before he could ask.

"Then who..." His eyes darted over my head to the bundle on my bike.

"No!" He gasped and suddenly his knees folded. His knees struck the ground with a dull thud and his large hands wrapped around my hips for support. "No, no, no, no, no!" He whimpered, face pressed into my belly.

I put my hand on the top of his head and nodded sadly. I suddenly noticed that Wylah stood a way from us, staring into nothing with a blank expression on her face.

"Felaan?" I asked her quietly.

She just shook her head in response, her eyes shining wet for a brief moment. "We have to go," she snapped, the lost expression on her face gone almost before I had registered it.

"Zandor," I whispered and pushed gently on his shoulders. "We must go, it's not safe here."

He nodded, sobbing, and pushed to his feet. He stumbled as if he were drunk and then staggered toward his bike. He paused for a moment by Valia's wrapped body, resting his large hand on her shoulder.

Then we were ploughing through the forest as fast as we could ride and then shot out into the more open valley.

Neither of us had slept for almost three days but none of us even suggested that we stop.

When we were almost halfway to the Groundlands the daylight turned from the soft golden hue of the sun into an actinic white for a moment. I had seen such a light before, in my youth. During the siege of Dun Traelak. I looked over my shoulder and saw a pillar of dust and debris flung high. The artificial ley line overhead winked out of existence. A few moments later the crack of a thousand lightning bolts rolled over us followed by a gust of wind. Thousands of birds took flight and the trees swayed in the stiff breeze.

The savages had destroyed the focusing tower, somehow. This area of the valley was lost.

I did not care.

The response from the Groundlands was immediate. The atmosphere of settlement and hopeful construction switched overnight into a mode of total war. The discovery of the egg chambers, and the sheer numbers of savages, implied a hidden strength in these strange creatures. I also had a creeping suspicion that the destruction of the focusing tower, their ability to devour magic and the large number of dormant eggs were connected somehow. Micah ordered full prioritising on the northern defences followed by the development and production of weapons that would allow us to sweep the tunnel system clear. We were to treat the savages to the north as a foe that were capable of destroying the Groundlands until we learned otherwise. Varl and his Mages had already started, throttling down the ley lines feeding the southern wall and were designing weapons suitable for tunnel warfare. I didn't participate in any of that apart from showing them how to modify the ley lines. I spent most of my time in my workshop, focusing on my meta-magic spell. It was the only thing that could keep the grief at bay. Since it had been Valia's idea, working on it made me feel connected to her. As if she was looking over my shoulder. But she didn't. She was dead.

Zandor, Wylah and I had had a private service for our fallen lovers. We hadn't been able to recover Felaan's body and none of us had been comfortable with burning an effigy in his place. So we just cremated Valia in true Skylander fashion. Incidentally, it was also the ancient Bizhjerai tradition. I had hoped that I would never again have to burn the body of a dead loved one after mom's funeral, yet here I was again. None of us said a single word while we watched the flames consume Valia. None of us flinched from the disgustingly pleasant smell of cooking flesh. I just leaned back into Zandor and waited for the tears to come. But they didn't. I was empty inside.

Later when I asked Zandor how Felaan had died he had given me the short version. The very short version.

"He gave his life for Wylah when she stopped to help me after I fell," was all he managed to say before he broke out in heartbroken sobs.

I didn't have the heart to ask him again and just cradled his head to my bosom and let him exhaust himself.

For a week I isolated myself in my workshop, alternating between working maniacally at my meta magic spell or sobbing in Zandor's arms.

But it wasn't the grief that was the worst part of it. It was the guilt.

When I had killed Alissa I had had to suppress my grief and anguish since I was supposed to hate her and I hadn't been able to mourn her properly.

But now that Valia had died as well I had a legitimate excuse to mourn Alissa as well. Even though I mourned them both, just that tiny little sense of relief that I could now mourn openly, filled me with self-loathing.

A part of me was actually relieved that Valia had died so that I could properly mourn.

And that was killing me inside.

Zandor did what he could to comfort me, but I was at the time not in a state to be recipient to his actions and kind words. I neglected him and his own grief in the most horrifying way.

I was selfish and blinded by my own emotions.

During that week of isolation the Prowler returned with another batch of Skylanders and that brought our population to almost nine thousand. There couldn't be many more to bring here. The steady stream of newcomers at the gate had dwindled to almost nothing and the Prowler accounted for basically the entire influx of people. Soon it would be time for me to put my plan into fruition, yet I had no idea of how to do it without rousing the suspicion of Varl's Mages.

I had prepared a dozen explosive power cores but I didn't really know what to do with them. I had linked them all to a crystal similar to the ones embedded within my breasts so that when I destroyed it the power cores would detonate.

I leaned back in my chair and watched idly as my meta-magic spell cycled through the endless array of potential spells.

Mother had told me something simple. To remove the obstacles that could stop the Skyships from pummelling the Groundlands into dust. I made a mental list of those obstacles.

Shields, anti-air guns, Angels, the Prowler.

Then there where the same issue that had allowed all of these Skylanders survive Utopeia. What would stop them from just running away and getting lost in the forests?

Did it matter?

At Utopeia, less than one out of a hundred had made it out alive by all estimates. That left roughly ten thousand Skylanders alive. If the same was true for the Groundlands then only a thousand Skylanders would survive. Even fewer would make it back to civilisation and even if they did I doubted that they would congregate once more to build a settlement. At least not if Micah perished here. He was the driving force behind all of this.

I decided that that was the least of my problems. If a thousand Skylanders survived, fine. First I should focus on how to kill the remaining nine thousand.

There was a giant dome shield that covered the main settlement as well as several satellite shields to defend the gun emplacements on the cliff wall. The attacking Skyships could weaken them by destroying the focusing towers that I had erected but the Groundlands had a vast capacity of stored power. In addition to that Micah could order the production of all non-essential material to halt to extend the supply. The most efficient way to get rid of the shields, which also had the added benefit of crippling production, was to destroy the receiver tower.

I suddenly realised the greatest flaw in my design. The central tower was both the receiver for all of the artificial ley lines and it was the primary nexus for the main shield dome. The entirety of the Groundlands was terminally dependent on that tower. I had made the same error as the Skylanders that designed the Skylands. The same mistake mother had made when she and mom adapted Grasax' shield tower to also receive the artificial ley lines.

That must be my primary objective. To destroy the shield tower. But how? It was heavily guarded at all times and there where thousands of armed Skylanders living their lives nearby. There was no way that I could assault the tower myself, get inside, destroy it and then flee. Especially not if I planned on surviving it. If Alissa was still alive then perhaps she could have done it stealthily but I had never walked the same dark path that she had.

But maybe I wouldn't have to sneak inside. Perhaps I could bluff. I was still the only one in the Groundlands that truly understood the complex system of spells that made up the tower. I could claim that I had sensed something inside that required my immediate attention. That might very well get me past the guards. But not without a chaperone. Micah had made it very clear that I was not to work on anything without oversight from a Mage that he or Varl trusted. I would have to bring at least one of them with me into the tower. But while inside I was rather confident that I could kill a Construction Mage. Maybe even two. I was far from certain that I could do it silently but once I was inside it would hardly matter.

I found myself warming up to this plan. Bluffing my way into the tower, kill my chaperone and then overload or destabilise the power cores beneath the tower. That should cause a huge explosion and Wild Magic that would destroy the tower, bring down the shield and starve the Groundlands of magical power. The magic field would still be dense enough to operate the guns though, especially with the local power buffers that Varl had had his Mages install. That led me to the second part of the plan. I couldn't destroy all of the anti-air guns, there where simply too many of them. But the ammo caches... If I could destroy those then the guns would not have enough munitions to wear down the shields of the Skyships. I quickly dredged from my memory the layout and layers of the munitions supply system that Micah and I had designed when we were still on good terms. There were a central munitions store for each munition type and then a smaller and more localised magazine near each cluster of redoubts. Each redoubt would then have its own smaller buffer, and then finally each gun would have a cache.

There where too many to destroy them all, but I could certainly get rid of the main munition store and probably the the shared magazines of the redoubts on either side of the valley.

That would still leave plenty of munitions but hopefully that would not be enough to bring down a Skyship. It was the best I could think of and I mentally committed to my plan.

That settled, I stood to go and relieve my bladder. On my way to the water closet my eye fell on the wall. I stopped hesitantly. Something was off here. I walked closer to the wall and knelt, staring intently at it. A section of the wall seemed to be outlined by something. As if a segment of the wall had been removed and then replaced. Yes, someone had definitely messed with my wall. I had grown it it all out of one piece of materialised magic myself and I knew that there were not supposed to be a fake panel here.

Feeling curious for the first time since Alissa's death I reached out with my magic and probed around the edges of the wall. It was indeed only jammed in there. I curled my magic around the edges and ripped it loose.

Behind the false wall was a compartment and the only thing inside was a large jug. I thumped it with my palm and could hear a liquid slosh inside.

Alissa's words echoed in my mind. 'I should have killed you after I found that poison in the nook in the wall.'

This was what she had tried to tell me. She had hidden poison in my wall. But what should I do with it? Pour it into the cooking stations at meal time?

An evil smile curled my lips.

I should administer it into the plumbing system. Lace the drinking water with poison. That would weaken the entire Groundlands, paving the way for the Bizhjerai assault.

The only issue was the dosage. I had no idea what poison this was nor how potent it would be. I realised that it didn't matter. I would plug it into the water system and take a guess at the poison to water ratio and overdo it. If there was only enough poison to harm only a fraction of the Groundlands then so be it. I replaced the section of wall carefully and then got to work. I would have to release the poison in advance of the fleets arrival, but I would require water for myself and Zandor. That was easily remedied by installing a water tank in my workshop with a closeable valve that could shut off the external supply. It only took me a few minutes to cast the appropriate spells. My plan was set. This was it. Time to execute. I made some calculations and made some rough estimations. I had a choice to make. Did I want the Prowler to be present for the battle, or not? If it were away on an assignment then the assault on the Groundlands would be much easier and less risky but then the Prowler would most likely escape my justice. If it was here then it would most certainly be destroyed before the Skyships turned their attention to the Groundlands.

I quickly used the toilet and then sat down at my desk, pulling a fresh sheet of paper to rest before me. I sketched out a rudimentary map and noted down the distances involved before I made some calculations.

Raistlin's steam-powered Skyship would be slower in arriving than the Bizhjerai fleet. Since the Prowler had been gone for a few days and this trip would be longer than the others then I supposed that it would be at least a week before it returned.

I made a schedule and marked down the days where I would crush the appropriate crystals, when I would administer the poison and when I would place the explosive power cores in the magazines. The destruction of the shield tower would be the crowning jewel. The one placed last on my schedule. I stared at the schedule until I had it burned into my memory. Instead of wiping the page clean with a spell I opted to burn it to ash, just in case one of Varl's Mages knew how to undo such a spell. I didn't think it was even possible, but fate favour the paranoid.

Now all I had to do was wait.


---

## Chapter 24 — CHAPTER TWENTY-FOUR A savage horde {#chapter-24}

CHAPTER TWENTY-FOUR

A savage horde

Zandor's arms around me had been slack for almost two hours and the slow steady rhythm of his breathing told me that he was deeply asleep. I wiggled my arms a little to test him but he just grumbled in his sleep and resumed snoring loudly into my hair. I struggled a bit more as I usually did when I had to relieve myself. He had grown accustomed to the movement and his grip around my waist relaxed without him waking up. Quietly I stood and quickly dressed in my trousers and blouse. I didn't intend to be gone long. I jimmied open the secret compartment in the wall while thanking myself for the forethought of greasing it. As quietly as I could I hoisted the large jug of poison onto my shoulder. Before we went to bed I had turned the valve on the pipe behind our own personal reservoir and there were nothing more to do here. I left as silently as I could and then I skirted the outskirts of the Groundlands. With the latest shipment of Skylanders the Prowler had brought the edges of the town had crept ever closer to my workshop but there were still a fair stretch of land between my home and the closest tent. The core of the Groundlands was now all materialised magic brick houses but the newest arrivals had to make do with hide and cloth tents until more dwellings could be constructed for them. Micah and Varl were fools. Even though they had both made sure that that I knew that they did not trust me, they hadn't placed a night sentry by my door to keep tabs on me.

I paused suddenly.

Or maybe they had? A hidden one whose mission was to catch me doing something that I shouldn't. I continued walking, determinedly not looking around. If there were one then I was already spotted. Either he had already dashed away to notify the guards or he was still following me. If he was... Then I had to figure something out.

What should I do if I was being followed? I couldn't kill him. That would be too suspicious.

Then what could I do that would make my mission out here understandable? Why would a young woman walk in the night with a jug of liquid on her shoulder?

Should I try to find a secluded spot and pretend to drink out of the jug? Pretend that it was moonshine and that I was drinking to cure my grief?

Possibly. But why would I bring so much? No matter how experienced a drinker I could convince them that I was there was just no way that a girl as small as I was would be able to survive downing this entire jug. What could I possibly use as a disguise to explain away the fact that I should have just poured a more reasonable measure of moonshine into a smaller container for my nightly binging?

And what if they demanded to taste my moonshine?

I steeled my resolve. My paranoia was getting out of control. I kept walking, pretending to be oblivious of the possibility of being followed, until I found the section of the northern wall that I had prepared. The construction of it had lagged behind the rest because of what would appear to be an inconsistency in the spellwork. It just so happened to be shaped in a way that would allow me to walk through a gap in the wall far away from the sentries by the gate. I walked sedately through the gap and then I instantly dodged to the side and deposited the heavy jug on the ground. My fingers curled around mother's dagger and I slowly drew it, ready to pounce the moment someone appeared through the wall. At this point I'd rather take the chance of killing the guard than letting him squeal on me. I waited, breathing as slowly and calmly as I could while I strained my ears for any sound of footsteps.

Long moments passed and I heard nothing. I was about to relax my guard and berate myself for a paranoid fool when I heard the soft scrape of leather on crystal.

Someone was walking through the gap!

A second sound reached my ears, and then another. Whoever it was was doing his utmost to walk silently.

That must mean that I'd had a minder all along. The only question was if this was the only one or if there where more. Or if this one had already sent word to Micah and Varl.

I didn't have time to explore the implications of that any further for a head popped around the sharp corner. My hand lashed out and I thrust the dagger upward. I felt the slight wet resistance and heard a wheeze of pain. My other hand clamped down on the neck of the person and I heaved, adding some Blood Magic strength to my limbs. The person stumbled forward, confused and in pain from my initial stab to the throat.

But it was not a mortal wound. I hadn't meant it to be.

I glided in behind the person, stabbing my dagger into the back of his knee and ripping it to the side. His hamstring snapped with an audible crack and I quickly repeated the process with the other leg before he fell.

Panicked wheezes was all he could do with his split vocal cords. He landed heavily on his face and I pressed my knee into the small of his back and I grabbed a flailing wrist. Twisting the arm to expose the armpit I stabbed into it and cutting through the muscular attachment of the triceps and biceps. The limb I held went slack. I treated the other arm the same.

I couldn't imagine the pain the man had to be in. Or maybe I could. I'd had my hand shattered, my ribs broken. My femur and broken nose was still not completely healed.

And my heart had shattered six times. First my birth mother had died. Then my father had been killed. Then my mom had fallen in battle. Then mother had given her life to take down the Skylands. Then I had been forced to kill Alissa. And finally Valia had sacrificed her life to save mine.

Oh, I knew pain.

Now that I had my victim completely immobilised and unable to voice any alarm I flipped him over. And blinked in surprise.

It was a woman. If her face hadn't been twisted in pain and her light application of makeup hadn't been smudged by frightened tears then she would have been very pretty. Right in my strike zone.

Large blue eyes beneath a cute bob of rose hair with a saucy sideways cut to her fringe. Regal cheekbones, a bold nose and thick lips drew my mind to places that it shouldn't have given that I was looking at a woman in severe pain.

"Sorry about this," I murmured to the panicked woman. "I know you were just following orders, but that excuse has never worked before."

I steeled my heart and grabbed her collar. "I imagine this isn't how you thought this night would end, nor did I, but I guess that's life, right?"

I was freaking out, and I couldn't stop it. I don't know why I was blabbering to her. I used my Blood Magic enhanced limbs, already lamenting the further damage that wreak on my figure.

I could count all of my ribs just by touch and my breasts and hips were down to half volume. I had been quite proud of my hourglass figure, and now I had lost it with all the prodigious use of Blood Magic recently.

"Just lay here for a while, I'll be back soon. I promise," blabbered and left the woman lying helplessly in the concealed gap in the wall.

I hoped that no one would find her while I was gone. That would be awkward.

I giggled to myself and retrieved the jug of poison and set off at a brisk walk toward the dam and the water filtration plant.

One of Varl's Mages had had knowledge of how to purify water by using several layers of different grains of sand to filter the water. She even had presented plans for a spell construct that would flash-boil the water in real time as it flowed through the pipes but she hadn't yet managed to work out the thermal equalisation equations. I had seen the obvious flaws in her math but I had neglected to point them out to her. I'd be damned before I taught Skylanders something that I didn't have to.

Since they hadn't asked me specifically if I could improve her design I had stayed quiet.

I tittered maliciously as I walked, recalling the moment and her obvious dismay at not being able to figure it out.

Oddly enough, the dam was not guarded from this direction. All of the guards were posted to the north in their fortified emplacements, always on the lookout for the savages.

They didn't imagine someone from the Groundlands would infiltrate their facility.

I pushed the door open and quickly hurried over to the mainline that fed the Groundlands with water. There was a standardised connection valve here that I had added for no obvious reason at the time. I had just thought that it might be useful in the future, perhaps to introduce medicine to the water supply. Luckily for me it would work just as well with poison. I had already added an adaptor to the jug and it was ease itself to connect it to the valve with a push and a twist.

I turned the dial to one per mille. For every liter of water there would be a milliliter of poison added. I didn't know if that was too much or too little, but hopefully it would have the desired effect. I trusted Alissa and trusted that she would have adjusted the potency of the poison to debilitate the entirety of the Groundlands. She had known the proportions of this endeavour and she had been very good at her craft. I don't know how often she had applied her skills in subterfuge for the Bizhjerai but it would not surprise me if she had assassinated a good number of people to strengthen our position in the world. While my mother had been pragmatic, she had always been more direct in her approach to her enemies. Kendall was too noble and good-hearted to turn to assassination and mom would never even have considered such dishonourable ways. But Alissa was a creature of shadow. Good and well-meaning at heart, but cold and ruthless when she had to be. Fuck, I missed her reassuring presence.

I hurried back to the wall, taking care to hide in the darkest shadows so that no sentries could see me.

To my relief the crippled woman remained where she were.

"Glad you waited for me, hold on just a little longer," I whispered to her.

I dashed over to the closest tent, the one with the loudest snores coming from it, and I inspected the lines that kept it standing. I found the one that whose absence would be missed the least and cut it from the tent to bring with me.

It was a little too short for my purpose but it would just have to make do. With my enhanced limbs I hoisted the woman onto the wall and I quickly tied a hangman's noose from the rope and fastened it to an outcropping on the wall that would become a crenellation.

"Sorry," I mumbled while I lifted the womans head by a fistful of rose hair and I threaded the noose around her neck and tightened it.

Her eyes were wide and pleading and her mouth formed words that could not make a sound.

One by one I undid the latches on the front of her corset until it flipped open and I ripped her blouse open with a quick yank.

Surprisingly full and shapely breasts spilled out of the tight garment.

"What a fucking waste," I muttered.

I drew my dagger and took a deep breath. I didn't like this kind of savagery. I preferred to kill quickly and cleanly when I had to.

But desperate times call for desperate measures.

I plunged my dagger into the side of her abdomen, just below her ribs. I pulled and sawed and tugged across her taut belly before I finished with a downward slash.

Her eyes were lost in maddened pain, the tendons in her neck bulging with a silent scream and a wet wheeze. Blood oozed out of her wounds.

I couldn't bear looking at her face any longer. With a grunt of effort I heaved her over the side of the wall on the inside of the Groundlands.

The rope snapped taut with a twang but the fall was too short to break her neck. With her severed tendons and muscle attachments she couldn't struggle. But I could still hear the wet splash of her intestines coil out of her and slap against the wall beneath her feet.

I climbed down from the wall and without looking back I returned to my home. I ripped off my bloody clothes on the way and set them to dissolve and I ran the final stretch in the nude. I couldn't let Zandor find any blood on the floor come morning or he would get suspicious. With a chill in my heart I climbed into bed next to my lover and felt his arms close reassuringly around me.

But sleep would not give me relief that night.

Any day now would be the day. Four nights ago I had dug the last two crystals out of my breast and crushed them in order to summon the Bizhjerai fleet. Raistlin's captain, Ioana, should be on her way. Lukas and Kendall likewise.

I just hoped that they would catch the Prowler here as well. I had been told that the Prowler would remain in the Groundlands for a while, being retrofitted from a passenger carrier to a cargo hauler to buy supplies.

I had no idea what goods Micah hoped to trade with and I was fairly certain that the Groundlands did not possess much in monetary riches. Perhaps he meant to buy on credit, or steal. I didn't know, nor did I care.

All I knew was that it would take time for the farms the Groundlands had appropriated to deliver in anything close to the scale that was required by the Groundlands.

The northern wall had been finished yesterday and the pre-constructed cannons and supplies were being installed. Thousands of Skylanders were working on the project and the length of the wall was lit up with spells being cast. Even though all of Varl's Construction Mages were assigned with the task of outfitting the wall, it was not enough. Manual labour had been conscripted to speed up the process. That was all fine by me since any and all effort spent on the northern wall just meant that the southern wall was neglected. And the southern wall was hopefully the one that would be assaulted by my allies. I was in my workshop and trying to figure out how to place my explosive power cores in the munitions magazines when I heard the alarm.

With my heart in my throat I hurried outside, my armour already materialising around me. Had my allies already arrived? Was this the day?

I blinked in the bright sunlight and looked to the south. The skies were clear. Confused, I looked to the north. A red flare hovered above the gatehouse. The signal that an attack from the north was imminent. So the savages would attack today.

"All hands to the northern wall!" Someone shouted and I saw thousands of armed Skylanders rushing to obey. Men and women piled onto and into the walls, manning loopholes and guns all along the length of it. Mortar crews prepared their weapons behind the wall and runners carrying munitions topped off the local ammunition stores.

With sudden realisation I finally digested the command to send all hands to the wall. I spun around and shot high into the air. True enough, the anti-air guns on the cliff face was manned by only skeleton crews. The munitions depots were unguarded.

An opportunity had presented itself. After quickly dropping back down I retrieved my explosive power cores before I sped toward the ramp and landed lightly, tucking my wings tightly along my back before I hurried toward the munitions depot. I looked around for sentries and seeing none I dashed into the armoury. Shells and large calibre bullets were stacked everywhere, boxes and belts piled in neat configurations. I approached a tall pile of boxes and I cracked one open. It was full of belts of air-burst flak shot and I buried a power core near the bottom. I hid three more in the main depot and then I scurried off to find the two lesser depots that would feed the two redoubts that where situated up here. That one was guarded by a single man that stood by the edge of the cliff, his hand shading his eyes from the sun. I sneaked behind the depot and around so that I wouldn't be spotted before I slipped into the armoury. A single explosive power core should be enough to take this one out. I didn't dare plant any bombs in the redoubts themselves since they were still crewed. Instead I sneaked back onto the ramp where I launched myself into the air again. The space above the wall was teeming with Skylanders in Angel armour and I decided to blend in with them on my journey across the valley. I cast a glance to the north and felt my eyes almost pop out of my head in surprise. Almost the entire valley width was covered in an undulating mass of gray. Savages. Not just hundreds. Thousands. Maybe even tens of thousands. How the fuck had they managed to find sustenance for that many? The Skylanders were barely ten thousand strong and had already hunted the valley near dry. I had seen no signs of farming to the north. There were just no way for them to be able to breed to these numbers.

Then it all clicked into place in my mind.

They didn't eat. Not food anyway. They ate magic. I had seen them, time and time again, devour my magic yet I had never seen any of them cast a single spell. Where did all of that magic that they absorb go?

It sustained them. Before the Skylanders arrived then the magic field must have been just dense enough for a small population of them at a time. Just enough for them to survive and lay their eggs. Then we had come and ripped open the earth to unleash the magic contained within. When they destroyed the focusing tower that must have saturated the magical field in their caves to a degree that all of those hoarded eggs would have hatched.

Just how quickly did these creatures grow?

Very fucking quickly must have been the answers if the vast majority of this horde had been hatched in the time since Valia died.

And now they had come here, to the greatest concentration of magic for miles and miles around. I hovered in the air, gazing at the massive horde in awe.

Perhaps I wouldn't need my allies to destroy the Groundlands after all. Perhaps the savages would do it for me.

A choice appeared before me. Should I trigger my plan now? Destroy the shield, deprive the Groundlands of its power and let the savages sweep over this area like locusts?

Or should I wait and hope that the savages would weaken the Groundlands to make them easy pickings for my allies?

I should wait, I decided. Best case the Groundlands died today beneath the tread of thousands of giant feet. Worst case the Skylander artillery and guns turned this horde into so much minced meat without a single scratch.

Regardless of what happened today, it wouldn't change my plan. My allies were summoned and they would arrive at any moment. Today, tomorrow, the day after? Didn't matter. If the Groundlands was reeling after a major battle with the savages then all the better. I resumed my flight and zoomed across the valley while the savage horde approached. They'd be here within the hour if they maintained their current pace. My feet struck soil and I crept up the ramp of the eastern cliff face. It appeared to be as weakly guarded as the other side. Unfortunately for me the sentry on this side was more diligent in her duties than the one on the western side had been. She leaned on the wall beside the only entrance with one foot propped up against the crystal surface behind her, smoking something that smelled sickly sweet out of a pipe while her eyes calmly regarded the approaching horde. By her relaxed posture and lined face I deemed her to be a veteran from the war. Her clothes where tidy and clean and despite her relaxed posture her back was ramrod straight. So probably not an old mercenary then. Probably not even a Guardswoman. Special forces then. A formidable opponent.

She was wearing a revolver and a sabre on each hip and she seemed well accustomed to her weapons.

Shit.

I walked up to her casually.

"I need to get inside," I greeted her.

Her gray eyes regarded me coolly for a long moment and then she spat to the side.

"No."

"No?" I queried as pleasantly as I could.

Did I dare cast a subtle spell? Or would she notice? Was she trained in magic at all?

"No traitors allowed here," she said rudely. "Now get lost, bitch."

"I'm not a traitor," I told her, still with that tone of voice that dripped honey. I cast a small spell that didn't have any effect, just to test her.

Not as much as twitch in reaction. Either she couldn't sense my magic or she had a damn fine control of her features.

"You have the Bizhjerai Rune on your shoulder. Traitor."

"I didn't have a choice," I tried to argue with her while slowly extending ethereal tendrils of magic towards her face.

"You always have a choice," the woman scoffed. "You chose to betray your people and join with the enemy. Now begone before I throw you down this cliff myself."

I took a step closer and gently eased my tendrils down her throat. "Do you really think that it is wise to argue with a skilled Mage on a mission?"

She suddenly launched off the wall and was all up in my face, towering over me. "I don't care on whose orders you are here. You ain't getting in here without Micah's personal say so. And don't threaten me. Mage or no you are just a slip of a girl. I've eaten bigger girls than you for breakfast."

I suppressed a snicker at her unintended innuendo, and the implication that she was into fat chicks. Instead I just smiled sweetly up at her and then solidified my tendrils within her lungs. Suddenly, she couldn't breathe.

Her eyes widened and her hands dropped to her weapons. I just reached out and trapped her hands on them, keeping them in the scabbard and holster respectively.

Then the old crone surprised me by headbutting me. I hadn't even considered that she would be stupid enough, or willing to disregard pain enough, to headbutt someone wearing a helmet.

I could feel the crunch of fragile bones in her face and I staggered away from her. My helmet had absorbed all of the force but her unexpected move had startled me and her revolver came sailing toward my face. I lashed out with a magical fist of force, snapping the bones in her forearm. The gun fell from listless fingers and I danced away from her wild slash of her sword. I kept out of the way, never relenting the magic I had sneaked into her lungs. It took much longer than I had anticipated, almost three minutes of awkward dancing before she fell to her knees. The whites of her eyes where red from blown vessels and her face was turning blue. She swiped with her nails at me when I got close enough but there where no force behind her blow. I stomped on her hand, breaking her fingers before I shoved her weapons back into their places on her belt. Her eyes were still tracking me even though she was too weak to move by then. I grabbed a fistful of her greying teal hair and dragged her toward the cliff edge. Then with a final heave I hurled her from the edge. Hopefully whoever found her would assume that she had been on the edge watching the approaching horde when a gust of wind had pushed her over the edge. A flash of inspiration struck me and I used my magic to break off a large piece of the rock above where her mangled corpse lay. It would look as if she had been standing there and the rock split beneath her weight. Hopefully no one down there had seen me throw her off the cliff.

I quickly planted my explosives and left for the smaller magazine by the redoubts. Curiously, there where no guard there and I looked around in search of one.

Then the wind suddenly twisted and a foul stench reached my nostrils. What the fuck? Then I heard the unmistakable sound of a gastronomic distress and I nearly gagged.

Must've been a rookie overcome with nerves when he saw the vast horde that descended upon us. Off into the bushes to attend natures sudden and merciless call.

His distress was my gain though and I hurried to plant the last of my explosive power cores.

Two out of three steps of my plan was carried out. Now all I needed to do was wait and figure out a way to gain entrance to the shield tower when the time came. But first I had to survive the coming battle. Perhaps if I partook in the defence of the Groundlands I might regain some trust from Micah and Varl. But considering the fact of all that I had already achieved hadn't been enough I doubted even this would. I sneaked down the ramp until I judged it safe and then I took wing on the updraft and glided toward the northern wall. The horde was substantially closer and the wall was manned. Hundreds of guns and mortars studded the wall and thousands of rifles, pistols and revolvers were ready to unleash death and destruction the likes of which these savages had never seen. Seeing the vast arsenal of magic and technology that faced the horde their oppressive number suddenly struck me as woefully insufficient. They probably didn't even have ladders to scale the wall. They would be slaughtered. Unless they had some ace up their sleeve that I didn't know about. Which was entirely possible. I saw a particularly large man near the gatehouse and I landed beside Zandor. He looked down at me and smiled yet his eyes remained as sad as they had been since he first saw Valia's wrapped body.

"Took you long enough," he teased me but I could tell that his heart was not in it.

"Had to put my make up on," I responded with forced lightness. "Perhaps one of these dashing attackers are in the market for a new lady friend."

Zandor didn't respond. Instead his eyes just stared implacably at the horde. But he wrapped his arm around my shoulders and I took comfort in the solid weight of it.

"Do you think they can harm us?" He finally asked.

"I don't see any ladders or any other means of scaling the wall. I think they'll be crushed utterly."

"Good."

I had never before seen Zandor rejoice in the prospect of death and destruction. Even when he hunted he killed with regretful respect. But since Valia and Felaan died... Something inside of him had hardened. The last dregs of youthful innocence had died with our friends. He had become more like me. I was not entirely certain that I liked the change in him. But I still loved him. Just as much as I had loved Valia. Before she died I had even entertained day dreams of having his children some day. I'd dearly have liked to raise a daughter together with Valia's son by Zandor. Our second child would have been a boy and his older sister would have taken care of him and protected him along with his older half-brother. But now there would be no large family with lots of children. Valia's death had driven a wedge between us, I realised while I watched the savage horde approach.

No, not her death. My reaction to it. I had pushed him away to wallow in my grief and despair. I had left him alone to cope with not only the death of his lover, but of two of his oldest friends.

What a selfish cunt I had been. But I didn't know how I could make it up to him. I didn't even know if I could.

But I was going to be fucked if I didn't try.

I wrapped my arms around his thick waist and buried my face in his armoured shoulder.

"I'm sorry," I said into his breastplate.

"For what?" He asked, concern tinging his voice as he turned his attention from the encroaching horde and down to me.

"For everything," I said. "For how I've been acting since..."

My words was drowned by a thunderous roar. The first artillery piece had spoken.

Thousands of Skylanders all along the wall listened to the high-pitched whine of the shell as it soared on a high trajectory toward the enemy.

Thousands of breaths where held in expectation.

Then a plume of soil and dust was thrown into the air by a fireball, well short of the approaching army.

The collective exhale sounded almost like a spring breeze. A lone voice called out orders that propagated down the wall. Distance estimations. The gun crews all around us got to work adjusting the aim of their guns.

Again we waited in trepidation. That first ranging shot had given us the information we needed. The savages didn't seem to care at all about the violent eruption until they reached the blast crater. Then they just parted to march on either side of it as if they believed that it would erupt again. As if they didn't connect the explosion to the bang they must have heard from the wall.

They didn't know shit about guns, I realised. Only the ones that had fought my friends and I knew about the terrible effects of our weapons, but even they did not know about the great range of them. We had used them almost exclusively in short distances. The only exception were the rifles that Wylah and I used and the savages that had witnessed that had all died.

They were marching unwittingly to their deaths. But even with their lack of experience and knowledge, even they should have known that they can't get up onto the wall.

And then the sound of the sky splitting apart shook me to my core when the guns of the Groundlands unleashed the fury of a decade in a single massive volley.

The thunder carried on for almost half a minute, and then kept on going when the explosive rounds landed amongst the savage horde.

Bodies where torn apart in fireballs and thrown into the air. Airburst munitions showered the creatures below in a deadly shower of shrapnel that instantly transformed them into unrecognisable heaps of meat.

Other, more esoteric, shells with embedded spells detonated. I saw one nova burst cut through dozens of savages with the coruscating ring of magical power. Some shells unleashed a thick smoke that ate at the flesh of everything it touched like acid.

I even saw one that erupted in some sort of weightless liquid that wrapped around the savages like a second skin before rapidly solidifying, trapping the still living creatures within an airtight shell.

The enemy roared in fury and their steady march turned into a headlong sprint. They were fast. Much faster than their size implied and the artillery crew struggled to compensate. The order to fire at will was passed down and the disciplined volleys stretched into an everlasting crescendo of thunder. To my horror, most of the shells went wide, impacting behind the horde. The gunners had been thoroughly surprised by the sheer speed of these creatures. For a moment I believed that they would actually reach the wall after all. But then the mortars behind the wall opened up with hollow coughs and the entire front line of the horde was mangled. But the horde still surged forward. They had already lost hundreds, maybe even more.

But they where adapting. They were spreading out to make sure that fewer of them where struck by the same shell. Now that they were closer I saw they had in fact brought ladders, they had just been hidden well in the tight press of bodies. They were crude constructions made out of roughly hewed wood and lashed together with what might have been tendon or possibly tough bark. Either way, they would serve their purpose if they came close enough.

I was no longer convinced that the Groundlands would hold. If the savages gained a foothold on the wall then our guns and artillery would be useless. It would be hand to hand combat from there and despite their losses the savages still outnumbered us three to one. And they were all larger, stronger and probably faster than the average Skylander. And worst of all: our magic was useless against them.

Nervously, I unslung my rifle and took aim. When the horde was a hundred yards from us I fired and felled a female in the front line. All along the wall small arms fire joined their hail of death to the big guns. By my estimate thousands of the savages where put out of the fight, either killed outright or wounded beyond fighting capabilities. Yet still they came on. I fired, again and again and beside me Zandor did the same with Valia's revolver. It looked almost like a child's toy in his massive fist. They were within pistol range now I realised when I saw him fire and I slung my rifle and drew my revolver. I had quickly reloaded it with area of effect spells after I had landed on the wall and I squeezed them off at the closest ladder bearers. The ladder itself was ripped apart by the firestorm blasts and I quickly realigned my aim to take out another. But there where hundreds more of the ladders.

I reloaded with standard kinetic rounds and prepared to fight in hand to hand combat. I didn't want to face these creatures again and I briefly considered if I should just make a run for it and let the Groundlands live or die by its own merits. But the choice was taken from me when a powerful voice called an order, overpowering the smattering of guns.

"Angels, to me!"

I couldn't ignore that order. Not without making everyone suspicious. A thrown javelin whistled past my head and embedded itself into the chest of the Skylander behind me who gurgled and fell to the wall. I glanced down at him. The javelin had pierced his neck. There would be no saving him. Without sparing him a second glance I gave Zandor a final look before I engaged my engines and left the wall behind to join the rapidly growing cluster of Angels behind the wall.

It was the leader of the Angel Corps that had called for us.

"They're within minimal mortar range by now," he called out with his powerful voice. "All of you, grab as many mortar shells as you can carry and fly out there. Drop them on their heads and thin their ranks. Hurry!"

It was a good plan. The mortars where useless now that the horde was packed against the wall and raising their ladders. Of course, they could still be fired at such a high trajectory that they would strike just outside of the wall, but given the inherent inaccuracy of smooth bore weapons the shells were as likely to drop down on top of the wall as it was to actually strike the enemy. But having Angels drop the shells was a much safer strategy. The group where already dispersing, leaving space for the newcomers from other areas to listen to the colonel. I ran over to the nearest mortar team and gathered as many shells as I could carry in my arms. With a thought I soared over the wall and remembering the javelins and rocks that the savages where so fond of hurling I summoned as powerful a shield as I could muster to cover myself from the sides and from below.

One by one I took the mortar shells into my right hand and used a spike of telekinesis to strike the primer in the shell to light the propellant flash powder and the fuse before I simply let the round go.

I maintained a rather quick speed along the length of the horde and dropped my bombs. The Angel air strike proved devastatingly effective against the enemy that had packed itself tight against the wall. Everywhere I looked I saw savages go down with bloody holes ripped in their flesh from small arms fire and here and there I saw large swathes of them get ripped apart by the dropped mortar rounds. I even saw a few of Varl's Construction Mages on the wall doing the same as I did, striking the primers of mortar rounds with their magic before hurling them into the sea of gray flesh down below. The death toll was immense and yet I doubted that more than a handful of Skylanders had fallen to the javelins and other improvised projectiles. The horde was thinning visibly by the moment, and yet they did not break. If this had been a human attack then they would have probably have routed in that first massive barrage.

If the wall had been outfitted with my automatic rifle turrets then they savages would not have stood a chance. As it was the most advanced weapon on the wall were the bolt action rifles that the special forces had been outfitted with on the Skylands and while the rate of fire of those where impressive, there where far too few of them. Most were armed with rifled muskets or revolvers.

But even single shot smoothbores where a grave threat to the savages. Their superiour size and strength meant nothing to a human armed with a firearm. A large calibre slug would put that giant down almost as easily as it would kill a fellow human.

The real slaughter would begin when the savages got onto the walls. Which they almost had in several places. I zipped closer to one such place and flung my final mortar round at the base of the ladder. The whole thing went down, the savages on the ladder falling into the cloud of dust. I zoomed over the wall to gather more mortar rounds and I met several other Angels doing the same.

"Focus on the ladders!" I called out to them. "Destroy them and they'll have no chance!"

I didn't know if they heard me, nor did I really care. I gathered a new load of mortar rounds and went ladder hunting.

I lost myself in my work and I don't know how many ladders I destroyed. When I ran out of mortar rounds I attempted to use magic to set fire to the wooden ladders but the sheer number of javelins that where thrown at me to be deflected by my shield dissuaded me from any further attempts. I went back for another round of mortar rounds and when I crested the wall again I blinked in surprise. There where almost no savages left standing and even as I watched more and more of them dropped dead, riddled with bullet holes.

A single segment of wall was fighting a small beachhead of savages that had managed to get onto the wall and push the defenders back. Before I could get there to aid them a tall man with a revolver in each hand shouldered into the front line and killed seven savages with twelve shots. I blinked in surprise. Was it over? I could still hear scattered gunfire but wherever I looked I saw only Skylanders shooting at wounded savages on the ground, putting them out of their misery.

The battle was won with relatively few casualties. But it was far from over. Thirty thousand corpses had to be cleaned up and many of those were not actually corpses yet and those might still be dangerous.

But I was done for the day. I had done my part. Even though the savages had failed to severely weaken the Groundlands today they had bought be a golden opportunity to plant my sabotage. That fact alone made the sacrifice of thirty thousand creatures worth it.


---

## Chapter 25 — CHAPTER TWENTY-FIVE Betrayal {#chapter-25}

CHAPTER TWENTY-FIVE

Betrayal

It was four days later and the pyres were still burning. The whole of the Groundlands was covered in a thick and greasy fog that covered everything. Yet less than a third of the bodies had yet been disposed of and the decaying remains of the rest beneath the merciless sun was permeating the air with the sickly sweet odour of rot. I was on edge at all times and I barely slept, fearful that someone would stumble on the explosives that I had planted. The enchanted trigger stone I had hidden in the compartment in my wall where the poison had been. Speaking of the poison, I had not yet seen any overt signs of people falling ill, which bothered me a bit. Perhaps I had diluted the poison too much, or perhaps it was a poison that accumulated in tissues before the effects became obvious. Either way, I had been hoping for a more significant effect. It wasn't until two stinking days later that I found some satisfaction. Everyone around me was looking pale and were sweating, moving slowly and carefully. The latrines were overflowing. It must have been one of the accumulating poisons. I applauded Alissa for her choice. Not many of her lessons in subterfuge and assassination had stuck with me, but I did remember that such poisons were usually more difficult to administer but would be near impossible to trace back to the source and it was almost impossible to reverse the effects in time once the symptoms started to appear. The Groundlands slowly ground to a halt, only me and Zandor that I knew of remained operational at full effectiveness. Far from everyone was bedridden, of course, and those that could still stand and work were sent on guard duties. The Prowler had returned the evening before last and the retrofitting of the Skyship into a cargo hauler had been paused while this 'sickness' rampaged through the settlement. To my great astonishment the last census had counted almost eleven thousand Skylanders currently in the Groundlands. That was more people than had been estimated to have survived Utopeia. Many of these must be the children of the survivors and the non-Skylander spouses that had been brought here along with their husbands and wives. It didn't matter to me though. If you married a Skylander then you obviously shared your spouses ideals, and that made you a Skylander.

Everything was coming to fruition, my carefully laid plans were coming to a close. But where were my allies? I had expected them to arrive days ago yet the horizon remained conspicuously empty of Skyships.

Until that night, of course.

The peal of thunder roused me from my bed. I hadn't been sleeping. As was usual as of late I couldn't sleep in anything longer than short spurts when exhaustion drove me under and I was invariably awakened by a nightmare where someone I loved died by my own hand.

So I was already awake, if not alert, when the thunder crashed. But this was no thunder. This was a sound I was intimately familiar with. I had heard its like day and night for months during the siege of Dun Traelak in the war.

The Groundlands was almost as powerful as Dun Traelak had been back then and the shield would be able to stave off my allies for months. But I wasn't planning on letting it carry on for that long. I was out of Zandor's arms in a flash, Angel armour already forming around my naked body.

"Wha?" Zandor groaned and rubbed at his eyes.

"We're being attacked," I told him. "That was cannon fire."

"Cannon fire?" He asked, rapidly coming fully awake.

"Yeah. I better get out there," I told him while intending to make a beeline for the shield tower and get inside, somehow. Perhaps I could actually force my way inside now that the guards were weakened by the poison.

"I gotta get to the wall," Zandor mumbled and stood up, reaching for his trousers on the floor.

My heart sank in my chest. I didn't want to lose him too. But neither could I stop him from defending the wall, not without coming clean with him. Though on the other hand the wall might be the safest location for him right now. My allies would be focused on destroying the anti-air guns that I could hear opening up before reducing the settlement itself to rubble. The walls were irrelevant in a fight like this.

I used my magic to rip open the secret compartment and I retrieved the little crystal that would trigger my explosives.

"What's that?" Zandor asked and yawned.

"Secret weapon," I grinned mysteriously.

I took three steps to stand before him and I kissed him firmly, lingeringly, before I pulled back and gazed into his eyes.

"Don't die, Zandor," I begged him. "Please don't die."

He grinned but there were real concern in the depths of his expressive eyes. "I won't if you don't," he quipped.

"Okay," I said with a sad smile.

"Now go, Mary," he shooed me off with a swat on my ass. "They need you out there."

Without looking back at him, I was not entirely certain that I would be able to carry on with my plan if I had, I left him there and I set a course for the shield tower.

The Groundlands was a hive of ordered chaos. Men and women were arming and armouring themselves and rushing into battle positions. While the activity was frantic, there was no panic. They all trusted that the shield would hold long enough for them all to get ready to fight and die. There where no cause to worry about anything. Not yet.

I caught a glimpse of Micah in the distance, shouting orders and bringing a bubble of calm with him.

"Get the Prowler ready to take the fight to them," I heard him shout at Varl that trailed him like a shadow. The old man nodded and hurried off, his entire body crackling with power.

I dipped in behind a row of tents to stay out of his sight. The cliff sides where lit up by the violet muzzle flashes of the anti-air guns and I saw the air bursts illuminate the outline of several Skyships in the night sky. They had used the cover of darkness to get close, but the shields still held. One of the Skyships were taking direct damage and in the light of the explosions I could see that the hull was grey metal and way bulkier than a Skylander Skyship. It had to be Raistlin's Fist of Heaven that he had promised me. The big prow cannon on the ship thundered and the shot exploded across the shield and lit up the Groundlands as a miniature sun. When the blast faded everything was inky darkness, my night vision ruined. After that I studiously refused to even glance upwards and my eyes slowly adjusted. I could have cast a light spell, but that would have told everyone that I was here and where I was going. I carefully threaded my way through the Groundlands, avoiding anyone that might be able to order me into battle somewhere in a way that I could not avoid without rousing suspicion. I managed to make my way all the way to the shield tower where I encountered four guards blocking the door.

"I have to get inside," I told them with affected urgency. Or maybe not that affected.

"No one but Varl enters," the big man that appeared to be the sergeant snapped.

"Varl didn't build this thing, " I snapped angrily. "This is the first time that it is truly tested. I have to make sure that it is sound."

"Only Varl enters. He may decide if you may join him inside," the sergeant told me impatiently before turning to one of his subordinates. "Fetch Varl," he ordered the woman and she nodded once before dashing away.

"Varl has other orders," I called after her but she ignored me completely.

"Wait over there," the big man told me and pointed a way off.

I sighed and did as I was told. Though I had no intention of waiting for Varl to show up. I only waited long enough for the guards to stop scowling at me and turn their attention back to the battle in the sky. As soon as their eyes shifted off me, I moved. Blood Magic fuelled my limbs and I blurred forward. To their credit, they reacted quickly and their hands grasped for their weapons before I had taken more than two steps. But they were not quick enough. The sergeant died with the tip of my sword through his neck, his second following by having her face caved in with a blunt fist of force. Using telekinesis in that way drained me severely, but the expenditure had been worth it. Time suddenly slowed down when my magesense zoomed in on the final guard. His single shot pistol was already aiming at the side of my helmet. His shot would probably not pierce through it but it would undoubtedly knock me senseless long enough for him to stab me half a dozen times with the dagger he held in a reverse-grip in his left hand. Nothing I could do to stop the shot would be quick enough. No dodge, no magic shield. Not even firing my Angel jets to twist myself out of the way would be quick enough.

A roaring behemoth slammed into the man and his single shot went wide. I stared, gaping, as Zandor wrested the man to the ground and wrapped his huge paws around his neck. Zandor lifted the mans head and shoulders into the air and then slammed him hard down into the crystal paving stones. Again and again. On the thirds strike the man went entirely limp and the back of his head looked suspiciously flat.

I couldn't believe what I was seeing. Zandor had killed a Skylander. One of his own. Zandor slowly stood up, breathing hard.

I smiled at him, my heart overflowing with gratitude and relief. He had come to aid me. I wouldn't have to leave him behind when I killed everything that he held dear.

Zandor smiled radiantly at me, and then he stepped in between me and the door.

"I can't let you do this, Marissa," he told me seriously.

I shouldn't have been as shocked as I was. Valia had figured out who I was before she died. It shouldn't have surprised me that Zandor had as well. The big man had such a carefree attitude that it was easy to forget that he was much more clever than the way he presented himself.

"How did you figure it out?" I asked to buy me some time.

"I saw your tattoo, same as Valia did," his voice choked when he said her name.

"Why didn't you tell me earlier?"

Zandor shrugged uncertainly. "We thought that it might freak you out if you knew that we knew. We spoke a lot about it but we decided that it didn't matter. We loved you, that was all that mattered. We knew you, and we loved you anyway."

"Yet here you stand in my way," I observed as my heart broke.

"I do. Even if I hadn't promised Valia that I would if it came to this, I still would. We hoped that you were genuine. Hoped that you would join our people for real. You're a Skylander Marissa, why do you seek to destroy us?"

The hurt in his voice almost broke me, but my mother's memories flashed through my mind and reinforced my conviction.

"All Skylanders do is destroy. You ruined the Skylands, almost destroyed the Clans, and it wouldn't have stopped there. You know it."

"That was the old Skylander ways," he protested. "We have learned from our past. We can be better," he said adamantly.

"Like you were with the farmers?" I spat acidly.

At least he had the decency to look away. "You know I didn't support that," he whispered sadly.

"I know," I reassured him warmly. "And I love you for it, Zandor. But it still happened. The Skylanders did that. I can't let history repeat itself over and over. The Skylanders have to be stopped!"

"You're a Skylander!" He snapped angrily. "Will you kill yourself when this is over?"

"Perhaps," I replied calmly and I could see that he had not been expecting that response. "I don't know if I am a Skylander any longer. Was my mother a Skylander when she died? Or had she become Bizhjerai? Are the survivors of Utopeia that are not here in the Groundlands still Skylanders? Or have they become part of the native population wherever they settled?"

Zandor didn't answer, so I continued.

"Don't you see that being a Skylander is not a matter of who you parents were? It's a set of ideals, ideals that justifies killing those who are weaker and taking what is theirs because it is convenient. It had to be so on the Skylands. But out here... Out here there are resources enough for everyone. We don't have to keep acting like we did. But we do. And that is inexcusable."

We stared at one another for a long while before Zandor finally sighed.

"You're right, Marissa. Absolutely correct. But I still believe in the Groundlands. I believe that we can change, with time. We just need to be allowed the time to grow into this new world."

"And how many people will suffer while you 'grow', Zandor? Micah already ordered the deaths of the farmers and their entire village. Almost two hundred innocent people have already died while the Skylanders have been 'changing'. How many innocent lives is it worth, Zandor?"

His lips thinned and he couldn't meet my stern gaze. He knew that I was correct. Finally he answered. "So you'd rather kill eleven thousand Skylanders to save a few hundred farmers at the edge of civilisation?" He asked bitterly.

"That question, and your answer, is the reason why I must do this, Zandor.

The needs of the many supersedes the needs of the few, is that it? Because there are more Skylanders than farmers in this region, that gives you the right to just kill them? What if there had been ten thousand farmers? Ten thousand is still less than eleven thousand? Huh?"

Still no answer.

"No, Zandor. I have spent more than a decade thinking this through. There's no argument that you can present now that will sway me from my path. I will get into that shield tower, one way or another," I warned him.

I didn't have time to debate with him. Varl was probably on his way here even as we were speaking.

"I won't let you," he said with heavy finality.

"You would kill me over this?" I asked sadly.

"No. I will never harm you, Marissa. I love you. I still do. I disagree with you strongly on this, but I still love you. But I can't let you destroy the Groundlands before it has had a chance to change."

"You know that you can't defeat me, Zandor," I tried to threaten him.

"I know. But I don't have to. I just have to keep you outside of the tower long enough for Varl to arrive."

I sighed, and lashed out at him with my magic. I didn't attack him. I just carefully wrapped him up in telekinetic bonds so that he couldn't move.

Then he surprised me by slashing his hand through the air and magic flared around him. My bonds disintegrated when he cut them and I winced and staggered at the sharp pain in my brain from the feedback.

"I've been practising, Marissa. You won't be able to hold me down. If you really want to get into this tower, then you will have to kill me."

"You'd really force me to kill you, Zandor?"

He nodded soberly. "I believe in the Groundlands. I believe that our people deserve a future. I know that we have done horrible things, but we can change. We can thrive here. A world in which the Groundlands is destroyed is not a world that I want to live in. So you have a choice now, Marissa. Kill me and destroy the Groundlands. Or walk away and let us be. We can still be together here. We can live happily together. Either outcome is fine by me."

"There's a third option," I whispered and in a lull in the bombardment he could hear me clearly. "Step aside and let me do this. We can be happy in Dun Traelak."

"That's not an option for me," he said resolutely.

I sucked in a deep breath and slowly let it out while hardening my heart.

"Alright. I love you Zandor. Never doubt that."

"And I love you too, Marissa."

Then I shot him.

My hand dipped to my revolver and I fired from the hip, the kinetic round slamming into his thigh and piercing through his light armour.
